# Kuki Shūzō, *The Problem of Contingency* (偶然性の問題)

## Preface

### The book

Kuki Shūzō's *The Problem of Contingency* (偶然性の問題, *Gūzensei no mondai*), published by Iwanami Shoten in December 1935, is probably the most systematic treatment of contingency in the history of philosophy, as John C. Maraldo has observed (Heisig, Kasulis and Maraldo 2011). It begins from a simple definition. Contingency is the negation of necessity: the necessary is what is necessarily so, and the contingent is what happens to be so, an existence that does not hold a sufficient Ground within itself and so contains the possibility of not being. Kuki places contingency at the point of contact between being and nothingness, and for that reason he holds that it is a problem for metaphysics, which alone takes nothingness as its question. Probability theory and quantum mechanics, he argues in the Introduction, deal with contingent cases and acknowledge contingency as a principle, but neither can grasp the contingent as contingent.

The book then divides contingency into three modes, following the three relations of judgement. Categorical contingency (Chapter 1) is the contingency of the individual thing in relation to the general concept: the four-leaved clover, the exception to a law, the mark that does not belong to the essence. Hypothetical contingency (Chapter 2), the longest part of the book, concerns ground, cause and purpose, and it is here that Kuki gives his best-known definition of contingency as "the encounter of two independent elements" (獨立なる二元の邂逅). Disjunctive contingency (Chapter 3) concerns the whole of which each actual thing is one possible part, and it leads Kuki through modal logic, probability, time, wonder, art and fate to the "metaphysical absolute" and to what, following Schelling, he calls primal contingency. In the Conclusion the argument turns from the logical to the existential: contingency is the Encounter of I and thou in the present, and the book ends with an imperative drawn from a Pure Land text, "Having met, do not pass by in vain."

The book draws on an unusually wide range of sources. Aristotle, Leibniz, Kant, Hegel, Schelling, Cournot, Boutroux, Windelband, Rickert, Husserl, Heidegger and Bergson stand alongside the *Book of Changes*, the *Liezi*, Buddhist sutras, the *Man'yōshū*, Japanese newspaper reports and Kuki's own etymologies of the Japanese words for chance. It was the expanded form, about five times as long, of a doctoral dissertation, 偶然性 (*Gūzensei*, "Contingency"), which Kyoto Imperial University accepted in November 1932 with Tanabe Hajime among its readers (Maraldo 2008: 40; Nara 2004: 174). A French translation by Omodaka Hisayuki, *Le problème de la contingence*, appeared in 1966, and Massimo Scaligero reviewed it in *East and West* the following year (Scaligero 1967). Excerpts from the 1932 dissertation are translated in Heisig, Kasulis and Maraldo (2011).

### Kuki Shūzō

Kuki was born in Tokyo on 15 February 1888, the fourth son of Kuki Ryūichi, a Meiji official in the Ministry of Education and a diplomat who served in Washington, and who was made a baron in 1896 (Nara 2004: 171; Pincus 1996: 27-28). Okakura Kakuzō (Tenshin), the champion of Japanese art, was close to the family and mentored Kuki in childhood. Okakura and Kuki's mother, Hatsu, had a love affair that broke up both households, and Kuki later called Okakura his "spiritual father" (Pincus 1996: 28-29; Nara 2004: 123 n. 2). He studied at the First Higher School, where he turned from law to philosophy, and from 1909 at Tokyo Imperial University under Raphael von Koeber. In 1911 he was baptised a Catholic (Nara 2004: 171).

In 1921 Kuki left for Europe, where he stayed until the end of 1928. He studied in Heidelberg with Heinrich Rickert, reading Kant's first *Critique* with him in private tutorial, and with Eugen Herrigel. From the autumn of 1924 he lived in Paris, attended courses at the University of Paris and visited Henri Bergson, whom he later called the greatest philosopher of the first half of the century (Pincus 1996: 57; Nara 2004: 125 n. 11). In Paris he wrote poetry, including a poem titled "Contingency", and the first draft of *The Structure of Iki*. In 1927 he went to Freiburg to study phenomenology with Edmund Husserl and Oskar Becker, and at Husserl's house he met Martin Heidegger, whose lectures and seminars on Kant, Schelling, Leibniz and Aristotle's *Physics* he attended at Marburg in 1927 and 1928. Back in Paris in the second half of 1928 he met the young Jean-Paul Sartre, then a student at the École Normale, once a week for some two and a half months to discuss French philosophy from Descartes to Bergson; Sartre later recalled that Kuki "was very enthusiastic about Heidegger". The meetings are often described as language lessons, but Sartre denied having been Kuki's tutor, and Kuki's notebook of their conversations, which mentions Breton's *Nadja*, dates them to after his return from Marburg (Light 1987: 3-4, 12-13, 27 n. 5). Heidegger later told a Japanese visitor that Sartre had come to him with a letter of introduction from Kuki, but Simone de Beauvoir told Light that Sartre had no such letter, and Sartre met Heidegger only once, in 1952 (Light 1987: 27 n. 6). In August 1928, at Paul Desjardins's invitation, he lectured on time at the *décades* of Pontigny, sharing the podium with Alexandre Koyré and Raymond Aron; the lectures were published as *Propos sur le temps* (Pincus 1996: 58-59).

On his return to Japan in 1929 Kuki was appointed to Kyoto Imperial University, after Nishida Kitarō, who had read the Pontigny lectures, recommended him to Tanabe (Pincus 1996: 59; Nara 2004: 173). He taught French philosophy, Husserl, Heidegger, Bergson and contingency, including a class on Boutroux's *De la contingence des lois de la nature* in 1930, and became professor in 1935 (Nara 2004: 173-75). *The Structure of Iki* (いきの構造) appeared in 1930, a study of Heidegger in 1933, *The Problem of Contingency* in 1935 and *Man and Existence* (人間と實存) in 1939. In 1939 he made the tour of Manchuria and China expected of a holder of a Kyoto chair (Pincus 1996: 212). He died of peritonitis on 6 May 1941, at the age of fifty-three, and is buried in Kyoto beside Hōnen-in, under an epitaph written by Nishida (Pincus 1996: 212 n. 5; Maraldo 2008: 37).

### Kuki and Heidegger

Kuki was a year older than Heidegger and had already spent six years studying philosophy in Europe when they met (Parkes in May 1996: 85). Of the Japanese visitors of the 1920s he seems to have made the strongest impression on Heidegger, and his 1933 study was the first book-length account of Heidegger's thought in any language (Parkes in May 1996: 96). Heidegger's "A Dialogue on Language between a Japanese and an Inquirer" (1959) opens with a remembrance of "Count Kuki", his visits and his silence in seminars. Reinhard May has shown, however, that the Dialogue was reconstructed from a 1954 conversation with Tezuka Tomio, who had not known Kuki, and that the gloss on *iki* it gives cannot be squared with Kuki's own account (May 1996: 15-19).

The relation in *The Problem of Contingency* is one of use and departure. Kuki takes up Heidegger's vocabulary, Dasein, care and anticipatory resoluteness, and discusses them directly in Chapter 3. But he refuses Heidegger's grounding of time in the future. For Heidegger contingency appears as the openness of future possibility, which authentic resolve makes necessary; for Kuki the temporal character of contingency is the present, the moment of encounter, and the nothingness that contingency faces is the "deep past" of primal contingency, re-produced in the present encounter with another (Mayeda 2001: 38-41). The emotion that answers to contingency is accordingly wonder, set against the anxiety Heidegger assigns to possibility (Chapter 3, §10). Hiroshi Nara argues that by the end of his years in Europe Kuki's thought had moved closer to Bergson's than to Heidegger's (Nara 2004: 97, 102).

### Kuki and the Kyoto School

Kuki taught alongside Nishida and Tanabe, and he is often counted among the philosophers of the Kyoto School, but most scholars place him at its edge: Maraldo calls him "something of a black sheep" on its fringes (Heisig, Kasulis and Maraldo 2011; Maraldo 2008: 40, 53). His interest in French philosophy set him apart from a faculty oriented to German idealism, and he did not write for the department's journal (Pincus 1996: 4 n. 5, 22). The philosophical difference lies in nothingness. For Nishida and Tanabe nothingness is absolute, the ground in which opposites are determined; for Kuki it is relative, the non-existence that every contingent existence holds within it, the possibility of the negation of being (Maraldo 2008: 40; Heisig, Kasulis and Maraldo 2011). Where Nishida and Tanabe look to a self-determining absolute, Kuki asks that contingency be accepted and valued, and where Watsuji Tetsurō stresses communal life, Kuki insists on individuality and on the duality of the encounter. Tanabe, as a reader of the dissertation, criticised it for giving too little place to teleology (Maraldo 2008: 40).

### Politics

Kuki's place in the politics of the 1930s is contested. Leslie Pincus reads *The Structure of Iki* as laying "the groundwork for a Japanese version of national aestheticism", and points to essays of 1937 on the Japanese character, and to a newspaper article urging that the war in China teach "the spirit of Japanese philosophy", as moving towards the official ideology of the time (Pincus 1996: 210, 220-31). Harry Harootunian finds in the theory of contingency "a powerful critique" of identity and necessity that "risked sliding into an argument for necessary exceptionalism", although Kuki "never went so far", and notes that his writings reduce imperial aggression to "incidents" (Harootunian 2000: 249). Hiroshi Nara, Graham Parkes and Jon Mikkelsen dispute the case against him, and Nara concludes that "an incontrovertible basis to inculpate Kuki is lacking" (Nara 2004: 6). Readings of Kuki as a theorist of Japanese uniqueness also need to weigh what he met in Europe. In Paris he wrote "Yellow Face" (黄色い顔, 1926), a satire of European race theory (Marra 2008: 63-65; Marra 2004: 55-57), and Yuasa Yasuo traces *The Problem of Contingency* itself to Kuki's experience of being singled out by race in European society (Yuasa in Parkes 1987: 158). A judgement of his writing on the supposed uniqueness of the Japanese character that leaves out the attitudes of Europeans towards the Japanese in those years is therefore one-sided. Omodaka's introduction to the French translation read Kuki's ethics through bushidō and Nietzsche; Mayeda rejects that reading and finds in the book an ethics of responsibility for chance encounters (Mayeda 2008: 8-9). The record also includes his help in finding Karl Löwith a post in Japan after Löwith had to leave Germany (Pincus 1996: 212). *The Problem of Contingency* itself argues for the individual against the general concept and ends with the ethics of an encounter between I and thou; the essays on the Japanese character followed it within two years.

Pincus calls Kuki's wartime record "ambiguous", and his death on 6 May 1941 ended it before it was tested further (Pincus 1996: 212). He died seven months before Pearl Harbor, and so before the period in which Japanese intellectuals were most pressed to take a public position. Between November 1941 and November 1942 four younger philosophers of the Kyoto School, Nishitani Keiji, Kōsaka Masaaki, Kōyama Iwao and Suzuki Shigetaka, held the three roundtable discussions published in *Chūōkōron* and collected in 1943 as *Sekaishiteki tachiba to Nihon* (The World-Historical Standpoint and Japan), the last of them on "The Philosophy of Total War". In July 1942 Nishitani and Suzuki also took part in the symposium on "Overcoming Modernity" (Calichman 2008: ix-xii). Kuki took part in neither, and how he would have answered that pressure cannot be known. The case against him rests on what he wrote in 1936 and 1937.

### This translation

This is a preliminary translation, made from the fourth printing of 1948. The Translator's Note that follows explains how it was made and the conventions used, and Appendix A records the translation choices for other translators. Page numbers in the margins of the text are those of the 1948 printing; Appendix C relates them to the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū*.

---

## Translator's Note

This is a preliminary translation of Kuki Shūzō's 偶然性の問題 (*The Problem of Contingency*), made from the National Diet Library's digitised images of the fourth printing (Iwanami Shoten, 1948; first published 1935), and much work remains to be done on it. It has not yet been checked against the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū* or against Omodaka Hisayuki's French translation, *Le problème de la contingence* (University of Tokyo Press, 1966). The attributions and dates given in the footnotes still need verifying, the readable version needs a full revision for consistency across its chapters, and the passages Kuki quotes from other authors have been translated from his Japanese and not from the original texts.

This is what I call a vector translation.[^tn-1]

Two conventions are used in the book to identify my changes to the text. In the readable version every footnote is the translator's, since Kuki gives his own references in the body of the text, and each begins "Tr.". To mark anything I have inserted into Kuki's text itself, whether a supplied word, a gloss or a note, I considered Kuki's own sign for contingency, )(, which he introduces as the sign of "the Encounter of two independent elements" (p. 73) and which pictures two pieces meeting at a seam, much as a kintsugi repair does. Instead I chose tortoise-shell brackets, 〔 〕, as Japanese scholarly editions use them for insertions made by an editor or by the person quoting, so that the convention comes from the editorial tradition in which Kuki's own works are published, and because Kuki never uses them himself.

One of the issues in translation is where multiple source terms share a single English word, to deal with this in this translation capitalisation is used to distinguish them. Where his German sources have *Grund*, Kuki writes both 理由 (*riyū*) and 根據 (*konkyo*). For the encounter of independent series of events (Cournot's *séries indépendantes*) he writes both 遭遇 (*sōgū*) and 邂逅 (*kaikō*), and for modality both 樣相 (*yōsō*) and 樣相性 (*yōsōsei*). 理由 is "ground", 遭遇 "encounter" and 樣相 "modality"; 根據 is "Ground", 邂逅 "Encounter" and 樣相性 "Modality". The capital marks the term Kuki uses for his own metaphysical determination. 根據 is the ground that existence has, or lacks, within itself (p. 1), and the word in which he gives Hegel's account of the contingent (p. 324); 理由 is the ground in the relation of ground and consequent on which Chapter 2 is built. At pp. 146 and 148 Kuki writes 遭遇または邂逅, *sōgū* or *kaikō*, and then defines contingency as 獨立なる二元の邂逅, "the Encounter of two independent elements"; 遭遇 is also his word for Cournot's *rencontre* (pp. 10, 179). 樣相性 is modality as a category, and 樣相 a modality or the modalities. Where 根據 or 邂逅 is a verb the English is in lower case, with the Japanese given. Headings are set in sentence case, and no sentence opens with the lower-case term of a pair, so that a capital is never ambiguous.

In making it I have relied on the work of others, and I am grateful to them. My greatest debt is to Takeshi Morisato, whose partial translation of the book, circulated as a work in progress, gave me a first English text against which to test my own reading of the Introduction. I have followed his decision to keep Kuki's two pairs of terms apart, rendering 存在 and 非存在 as existence and non-existence and 有 and 無 as being and nothingness, and his marking of the pages of the *Zenshū* fixed the first points of the page concordance. Graham Mayeda's doctoral thesis, *Ethics and Difference: Time, Space and Ethics in the Philosophy of Watsuji Tetsurō, Kuki Shūzō and Martin Heidegger* (University of Toronto, 2001), reproduces Kuki's Japanese alongside references to both Omodaka's French translation and the *Zenshū*, and these made it possible to tie the 1948 pagination to volume 2 of the *Zenshū*. Mayeda's chapter "Is There a Method to Chance?" and John C. Maraldo's "The Contingencies of Kuki Shūzō", both in Victor Sōgen Hori and Melissa Anne-Marie Curley (eds), *Frontiers of Japanese Philosophy 2: Neglected Themes and Hidden Variations* (Nagoya: Nanzan Institute for Religion and Culture, 2008), gave me English versions of passages from the Introduction and the Conclusion against which to check my own, and Maraldo's rendering of Kuki's terms confirmed the choices adopted here. The errors that remain are mine.

::: right
David M. Berry, University of Sussex, UK
:::

[^tn-1]: Tr. By a vector translation I mean one made by directing a swarm of AI agents through the text under human guidance, in a manner close to vibe coding. A corpus of existing translations and of scholarship that quotes the text, for this book chiefly Takeshi Morisato's draft, the passages translated by Graham Mayeda and John C. Maraldo, and the *Sourcebook*'s version of Kuki's 1932 dissertation, fixes the key terms and serves as the reference against which the work is checked. The agents work from the page images in parallel under a shared brief, and for each section they produce a literal version, kept close to Kuki's sentences for checking and quotation, and a readable version, which is the translation printed here. Other agents check the readable version against the literal one, against the corpus and against the images, and return their corrections to be applied, so that the text is revised through repeated rounds of checking, a kind of hermeneutic circle. The decisions about terms, register and conventions are mine. The method is set out at https://vector-humanities.github.io/vector-translation/.

Readable working translation, Japanese to English, not for publication. The register is interpretive: Kuki's compressed sentences are joined into continuous argument. Kuki's own references are inline, as in the original; every footnote is the translator's (identifying names, glossing Greek, Latin and Chinese terms, explaining term choices). For quotation, use the literal version (`Kuki_Problem-of-Contingency_literal.md`), which is published alongside this one, and the page images (`source/guzensei-pages/`); a transcription of the Introduction is in `source/Kuki_Guzensei_transcription.md`.

Source: 九鬼周造『偶然性の問題』岩波書店, 昭和23年 (1948 printing; first published 1935). NDL Digital Collection pid/1037966. Translated from the page images, not the OCR.

Conventions:
- The appendices (A, notes on translation choices; B, contents of the 1948 printing; C, concordance with the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū*; D, index of persons and works cited) and the glossary are at the end of the book.
- `[p. N]` marks the start of page N of the 1948 edition. `[コマ N]` gives the image number (`source/guzensei-pages/page_NNN.jpg`).
- Kuki's emphasis marks (圏点) are rendered in italics.
- Kuki's citations are kept in his form. Quotations from Shestov, Trendelenburg, Hegel, Lotze and others are translated from Kuki's Japanese, not from the original-language texts, and should not be cited as translations of those authors.
- Kuki's 甲, 乙, 丙 are rendered A, B, C. Kuki's 我々 is "we"; his 思ふ is "I think".
- Translator's notes and glosses are footnotes beginning "Tr.". Any insertion into Kuki's text is set in tortoise-shell brackets, 〔 〕.

## Author's Preface (序) [コマ 3]

I first set out my views on the subject of contingency at the autumn public lectures of Ōtani University in 1929,[^ap-1] and I took the same subject for my lectures at Kyoto Imperial University in the academic year 1930. My interest in the problem is therefore of some standing, but circumstances did not allow me to concentrate my thinking on this single point. It is, however, a problem that touches the core of *Existenz*, and it will give me no rest until at some point it has taken some ultimate form. For the time being I intend to make it public in roughly this form.

I wish also to express my deep gratitude to the seniors and friends who gave me their sympathy and instruction as this book took shape, and above all to Dr Tomonaga Sanjūrō and Dr Tanabe Hajime.[^ap-2]

August 1935, the Author

[^ap-1]: Tr. Ōtani University, a Buddhist university in Kyoto. The dates are given in the Shōwa era in the original: Shōwa 4 (1929), Shōwa 5 (1930), Shōwa 10 (1935). The preface stands before the contents in the 1948 printing, on an unnumbered page.
[^ap-2]: Tr. Tomonaga Sanjūrō, historian of Western philosophy at Kyoto Imperial University; Tanabe Hajime, Nishida's successor in the chair of philosophy there and a reader of Kuki's 1932 dissertation.

## Introduction (序説) [コマ 7-12; pp. 1-11]

### 1. Contingency and metaphysics

[p. 1] [コマ 7] Contingency is the negation of necessity. The necessary means being necessarily so, that is, an existence that in some sense possesses its Ground within itself.[^i1-6] The contingent means what happens to be so,[^i1-1] an existence that does not carry a sufficient Ground within itself. This is to say that the contingent is an existence that contains negation, an existence that is able not to be. In other words, contingency arises when, within existence, an inseparable inner relation to non-existence comes into view and is witnessed. It is a limit-existence, interposed at the surface of contact between being and nothingness, a state in which being is rooted in nothingness, the figure in which nothingness encroaches upon being.

In contingency, then, existence comes face to face with nothingness. To go beyond existence towards nothingness, to go beyond [p. 2] [コマ 8] form towards what lies beyond form, is the core meaning of metaphysics.[^i1-2] Metaphysics certainly takes "true existence", *ontōs on*,[^i1-3] as its problem. Yet true existence becomes a problem in the original sense only in its relation to "non-existence", the *mē on*, so that the existence with which metaphysics is concerned is existence enveloped by non-existence, that is, existence surrounded by nothingness. It is here that metaphysics, which is to say philosophy in the eminent sense, differs from the other disciplines. The other disciplines take existence, or a fragment of being, and treat it only as given existence, as a given fragment of being, whilst of nothingness, and of the relation between being and nothingness, they seek to know nothing at all.

The problem of contingency is consequently a problem of metaphysics in the strict sense, since it cannot be separated from the question of nothingness. It follows that the disciplines other than philosophy as metaphysics do not take up contingency as a problem in its proper sense. We might think that probability theory, a branch of mathematics, makes contingency its own problem, and it certainly deals with contingent cases. The aim of probability theory, however, is never to grasp the contingent as contingent, in its contingency, and it does not seek to elucidate the meaning of contingency in and of itself. Its concern is exhausted by [p. 3] the quantitative relation between all the possible cases of an event's occurring or not occurring and the contingent cases in which it does occur. Moreover, since a theoretical quantitative relation acquires empirical validity only when the number of observations is made infinitely large, probability theory seeks to do no more than determine, at the macroscopic level, the ideal constancy of the quantitative relations among contingent events. The contingent variability that resides in the microscopic details is left untouched. Yet it is in the movement of the details that the contingent is contingent. Probability theory, in short, is no inquiry into the contingent itself, and it cannot even be called a "calculation" of "the contingent", for the contingent as such cannot be calculated. It would be wrong to deny that probability theory, casting a sidelong glance at contingency from a fixed angle of vision, allows us to witness something of its structure, but it permits no grasp of contingency as a whole.

Some may think that quantum theory takes contingency as its problem. Quantum mechanics asserts that, for quantum phenomena, the two conditions of position and velocity cannot be determined at the same time, and in doing so it merely permits a certain degree of contingency to hold sway. Its so-called uncertainty principle[^i1-4] does no more than acknowledge contingency as a principle, and for quantum mechanics itself [p. 4] [コマ 9] quantum contingency remains, to the last, in an "unknowable dimension". We cannot ask quantum theory to reflect in principle, across the whole of existence, on its own principles. Contingency can become a datum of science in principle, then, but it has a primordial character by which, through its very contingency, it resists being treated by science as an object. Only philosophy as metaphysics can take up the contingent as contingent, in its proper aspect, as a problem.

At the same time, every discipline, for the very reason that it seeks to investigate the necessary or probable relations among things, cannot in principle be separated from the problem of contingency. Whenever a discipline reflects in principle on its own labours, it necessarily confronts the problem of contingency in its proper aspect, though only at that moment. This is because every discipline is, at its foundation, connected to metaphysics. The problem of contingency, in short, insofar as it concerns nothingness, that is, insofar as it can be fully grasped only on the horizon of nothingness, is a problem of metaphysics in the strict sense. [p. 5] Whether it is a problem that can be completely solved is, naturally, another question. We must nonetheless pursue contingency as a problem of philosophy as far as it will go. To borrow Shestov's words,[^i1-5] we belong among "those who do not wish to give up the hope of discovering in this world something other than statistics and 'necessity'" (L. Chestov, *La Philosophie de la Tragédie*, Paris 1926, Préface, p. XV), and we wish to bring the ontological structure and the metaphysical grounds of contingency to as much clarity as we can.

[^i1-1]: Tr. Kuki glosses 偶然 (*gūzen*) as 偶々然か有る (*tamatama shika aru*), "happening to be so", against 必然 (*hitsuzen*), 必ず然か有る (*kanarazu shika aru*), "necessarily being so".
[^i1-2]: Tr. 形而上 (*keijijō*), "above form", is the term from which 形而上學 (*keijijōgaku*), metaphysics, is built; Kuki plays on it here.
[^i1-3]: Tr. ὄντως ὄν, Plato's term for what really is; μὴ ὄν, what is not. Kuki gives them as glosses on 眞の存在 and 非存在.
[^i1-4]: Tr. Heisenberg's uncertainty principle, published in 1927.
[^i1-5]: Tr. Lev Shestov (1866-1938), Russian religious existentialist philosopher, who wrote in exile in Paris.

[^i1-6]: Tr. Ground: 根據 (*konkyo*). Kuki also writes 理由 (*riyū*) for *Grund*, rendered "ground" in lower case: the ground in the relation of ground and consequent (Chapter 2). See the Translator's Note.

### 2. The essence of necessity and its three modes

Since contingency is the negation of necessity, any attempt to grasp the meaning of contingency must begin by elucidating the meaning of necessity. What, then, is necessity? As we have already said, it means being necessarily so, which is to say the impossibility of the opposite. That the opposite is impossible means that a thing holds the ground of its existence within itself, [p. 6] [コマ 10] that a given self maintains itself just as it is given. When the self maintains itself in this way to the end, it takes on the form of self-identity, and consequently the concept of necessity presupposes identity. This is why the form of the law of identity, "A is A", expresses necessity in its strictest form, and why necessity is, in the end, nothing other than the qualitative determination of identity stated from the standpoint of modality.[^i2-1] Trendelenburg too says:[^i2-2] "The necessary is, by its concept, unchangeable, and was therefore already called ἀΐδιον (eternal) by Aristotle and *aeternum* (eternal) by Spinoza; in the necessary, the *identical* appears. Everything necessary is self-identical and, as such, holds fast to itself" (Trendelenburg, *Logische Untersuchungen*, II, 3. Aufl., S. 210). Elsewhere he writes that "behind *identity* stands necessity, back to back" (ibid., S. 175). Hegel similarly describes "*necessity* in itself" as "the one essence, *identical* with itself but full of content", and the "*necessary*" as "in itself absolute relation, that is, the developed process in which the relation also sublates itself into absolute *identity*" (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, [p. 7] 1906, §§ 149, 150).

The determination of "identity", and with it of "necessity", is found in three places, in conceptuality, in groundedness and in wholeness. This is to say that it is grasped in (1) the relation between a concept and its marks,[^i2-3] (2) the relation between ground and consequent, and (3) the relation between whole and part. Lotze identifies three forms that lead to necessary knowledge of the relation between two terms, A and B. In the first, we form a generic judgement (see p. 22) and seek a B that is already thought within the generic concept A, so that B necessarily belongs to every species of A. In the second, we form a hypothetical judgement and show that when a condition x is added to A, a B arises in that A which would not otherwise exist, so that B holds necessarily of every A on which the same condition acts in the same way. In the third, we form a disjunctive judgement, reducing a problem to a strict "either the one or the other", and so gain a firm hold on the state of affairs, since a single experience then suffices to decide, in each case, which of the two predicates, B or C, is posited of A with necessity. For Lotze the ways [p. 8] [コマ 11] of arriving at necessary knowledge are limited to these three, and there is no other (Lotze, *Logik*, hrsg. v. Misch, Leipzig, 1912, S. 65). Hegel likewise names three absolute relations in which necessity is realised: (1) the relation of substantiality and accident, (2) the relation of causality and (3) the relation of reciprocal action. For substance to fulfil the concept of necessity it must be grasped as cause and its accident as effect, which means that the relation of substance and accident must pass over into the causal relation. And for the causal series to be truly infinite it must leave the straight line and take the form of a circle, so that causality becomes reciprocal action. In this way Hegel sets out the inner connection among the three kinds of necessity (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, §§ 150-156).

Let us call these three modes of necessity (1) categorical necessity, (2) hypothetical necessity and (3) disjunctive necessity. Distinctions in the relation of judgements are usually named with the standard logical terms *teigenteki* (定言的), *kagenteki* (假言的) and *sengenteki* (選言的), but we deliberately use *teigenteki*, *kasetsuteki* (假說的) and *risetsuteki* (離接的).[^i2-4]

[^i2-1]: Tr. Modality: the mode in which something is, as necessary, possible, actual or contingent.
[^i2-2]: Tr. Friedrich Adolf Trendelenburg (1802-1872), German logician and Aristotle scholar.
[^i2-3]: Tr. Marks (徵表, *Merkmale*): the characteristics that define a concept.
[^i2-4]: Tr. The characters of 假說的 stress supposition; those of 離接的 join separation (離) and connection (接). English has one word for each pair, so "hypothetical" and "disjunctive" cannot mark the change.

### 3. The three modes of contingency

[p. 9] Since contingency carries the sense opposed to necessity, there should be three modes of contingency corresponding to the three modes of necessity, namely (1) categorical contingency, (2) hypothetical contingency and (3) disjunctive contingency. It is only by distinguishing contingency into these three modes, I think, that its meaning becomes clear, both in its multiplicity and in its unity. Theories of the contingent have always lacked clarity, and whilst this is partly due, of course, to the difficulty of the problem itself, much of it, I think, comes from the fact that, at the very point where the problem is posed, the modes of contingency have not been distinguished on any clear principle, and grasping them as a unity has not been consciously taken up as a theme.

A division corresponding to these three modes can in fact be found in Aristotle. As I shall discuss in detail later, Aristotle's *symbebēkos* (συμβεβηκός, *accidens*) corresponds to categorical contingency; *automaton* (αὐτόματον, *casus*) and [p. 10] [コマ 12] *tychē* (τύχη, *fortuna*) correspond to hypothetical contingency; and *endechomenon* (ἐνδεχόμενον, *contingens*) corresponds to disjunctive contingency.[^i3-1] Milhaud,[^i3-2] in an essay on chance in Aristotle and Cournot, identifies three aspects of chance that the two theories share: first, "encounter" (*rencontre*); second, "rarity" (*rareté*); third, "a possible" (*un possible*) (Gaston Milhaud, *Études sur la Pensée Scientifique*, 1906, pp. 137-158; *Revue de Métaphysique et de Morale*, 1902, pp. 667-681).[^i3-3] Now "encounter" means the encounter of one series of grounds with another series of grounds, and so is nothing other than hypothetical contingency. "Rarity", meaning what belongs to a thing only rarely, must be categorical contingency, which lies in the relation between a concept and its marks. And "a possible", meaning one possibility among several equally possible disjuncts, is nothing other than disjunctive contingency. Rickert[^i3-4] likewise lists three meanings of the contingent: (1) what is not lawlike, (2) what has no cause, (3) what is not essential (H. Rickert, *Die Grenzen der naturwissenschaftlichen Begriffsbildung*, 3. Aufl. 1921, S. 286-287). His examples of what is not lawlike are that Saturn, and not the earth, has a ring, and that Frederick [p. 11] the Great won the battle of Leuthen, and he is therefore pointing to the disjunctive isolation of disjunctive contingency over against the universal inclusiveness of law. What has no cause, as what lies outside causality, is hypothetical contingency. What is not essential, as what does not belong to the essence of the concept, is categorical contingency. These examples, I believe, make it reasonably clear that the division of contingency into categorical, hypothetical and disjunctive corresponds to the matter itself.

As I shall show later, categorical contingency remains throughout within the conceptual standpoint of logic, hypothetical contingency appears most markedly in connection with causality in the empirical world, and disjunctive contingency comes to the fore in relation to the metaphysical absolute. One might therefore name the three after their predominant feature, as logical, empirical and metaphysical contingency.[^i3-5] Since contingency belongs at its root to logical Modality,[^i3-6] however, naming of this kind is, strictly speaking, inappropriate. In what follows let us examine the problem of contingency under three heads: categorical contingency, hypothetical contingency and disjunctive contingency.

[^i3-1]: Tr. *Symbebēkos*: what belongs to a thing without belonging to its essence. *Automaton*: spontaneous chance. *Tychē*: luck or fortune. *Endechomenon*: what admits of being otherwise.
[^i3-2]: Tr. Gaston Milhaud (1858-1918), French philosopher of science.
[^i3-3]: Tr. Kuki gives the essay title only in Japanese, アリストテレスおよびクールノーに於ける偶然 ("Chance in Aristotle and Cournot"). The French title has not been checked.
[^i3-4]: Tr. Heinrich Rickert (1863-1936), German philosopher of the Baden school of neo-Kantianism.
[^i3-5]: Tr. Kuki's 優勢的命名法, naming from the predominant feature (*denominatio a potiori*).

[^i3-6]: Tr. Modality: 樣相性 (*yōsōsei*), modality as a category. 樣相 (*yōsō*), a modality or the modalities, is "modality" in lower case. See the Translator's Note.

---

## Chapter 1. Categorical contingency (第一章 定言的偶然) [コマ 13-30]

### 1. Concept and categorical contingency

[p. 13] [コマ 13] The structure of a concept rests on witnessing some universal identity common to a range of individual representations. The constitutive content of a concept is accordingly the totality of essential marks abstracted as an identity, whilst its possible content arises by giving access to the non-essential marks that abstraction has placed outside the circle of that identity. What characterises an essential mark is that to negate it is to negate the concept itself, since the constitutive content of the concept and the totality of its essential marks are one and the same. The relation between an essential mark and the concept, insofar as it is determined by this identity, is consequently necessary. The relation between a non-essential mark and the concept depends on whether or not [p. 14] [コマ 14] access is given, and because it lacks an identity of its own it is contingent. Essential marks are therefore called necessary marks, and non-essential marks contingent marks. Categorical contingency is nothing other than the contingency of contingent marks.

Take, for example, the concept of a triangle. For this concept, being a portion of a plane bounded by three lines is a necessary mark. It makes up the constitutive content of the concept, so that to negate this property is at once to dissolve the concept of the triangle itself. Because the concept of the triangle and being a portion of a plane bounded by three lines are bound together by identity, we call the relation between them necessary. Whether an angle is right, obtuse or acute, on the other hand, merely forms the possible content of the triangle, and is for the concept of the triangle a contingent mark. The concept is related to these marks only by giving them access, and since the two lack an identity of their own, we call the relation contingent. Even that the sum of the interior angles equals two right angles is, insofar as it does not [p. 15] share an identity with the concept of the triangle, something contingent for the triangle, and this is why non-Euclidean geometry can conceive of the sum of a triangle's interior angles as less than, or greater than, two right angles.

What confers reality on a concept is substance. Substance is that which, by the law of identity, maintains itself unchanged in time, or, to borrow Kant's words, the meaning of substance is "the identity of the substratum" (Kant, *Critique of Pure Reason*, B 229).[^c1s1-1] Hegel too speaks of the "absolute identity" of substance (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 150, S. 202). Over against substance we think of attributes, the various determinations of a substance that signify its mode of existence. This is to say that the relation between substance and attribute corresponds to the relation between concept and mark, and consequently we can think of essential attributes and of non-essential, or contingent, attributes. A striking example of a concept made real, in which the meaning of substance is stated clearly, is of course the Platonic Idea. The Idea, as concept, is "the common" (τὸ κοινόν) in relation to the many individual things, and so "the one" (μονάς), and it is at the same time the "true existence" that forms an independent world of its own. The individual thing participates in the Idea on the one hand, [p. 16] [コマ 15] whilst on the other it is rooted in "non-existence", understood as space. Contingency in this sense means that participation in the Idea is rendered incomplete by non-existence, that is, it means the gap that lies between archetype and copy. Aristotle called contingency of this kind *symbebēkos* (συμβεβηκός, *accidens*). The *symbebēkos* is "what belongs to something and can truly be asserted of it, but neither *of necessity* (ἐξ ἀνάγκης) nor *for the most part* (ὡς ἐπὶ τὸ πολύ)" (Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, Δ 30, 1025a). Aristotle thereby made clear the distinction between "by itself" (καθ' αὑτό, *per se*) and "by accident" (κατὰ συμβεβηκός, *per accidens*). What is "by itself" is "the essence", or "everything that belongs to the essence", or what holds "when something has received a thing directly into itself or into one of its parts" (ibid., Δ 18, 1022a). For example, "man lives *by himself*, because the spirit, in which life directly resides, is a part of man" (ibid.); that is, "that he is a living being is not *by accident*" (ibid., E 2, 1026b). But "that a certain man is white is *by accident*, because it is so neither *always* (ἀεί) nor *for the most part*" (ibid.). [p. 17] And "matter (ὕλη) is the cause of the accidental" (ibid., E 2, 1027a), so that "the accidental is something close to non-existence (ἐγγύς τι τοῦ μὴ ὄντος)" (ibid., E 2, 1026b). Aristotle also says that the sum of the interior angles being equal to two right angles is accidental to the triangle, though in a somewhat different sense. Contingency of this kind can happen to be "eternal" (ἀΐδιον), and it belongs to its object "*by itself*, but is not *in its essence* (ἐν τῇ οὐσίᾳ)" (ibid., Δ 30, 1025a). The concept of an "accident by itself" (καθ' αὑτὸ συμβεβηκός) (ibid., B 1, 995b) contains, as it were, a contradiction, yet in a certain sense it can be compared with what Husserl calls the "contingent a priori" (*kontingentes Apriori*) (Husserl, *Formale und transzendentale Logik*, Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. phän. F. X, S. 25-26).

[^c1s1-1]: Tr. The page number is faint in the scan and reads most like 229; an earlier draft had 231. Check against the edition before citing.

### 2. The contingency of synthetic judgements

Categorical contingency, in short, is the contingency of contingent marks in relation to a concept. [p. 18] [コマ 16] If we regard concepts as the elements of judgement, a judgement is an unfolded concept, and the contingency grounded in the concept must consequently appear in some form in the judgement as well. When a concept is made the subject of a judgement and the mark that occupies the place of the predicate is a contingent mark of that concept, contingency appears in the connection between subject and predicate. And since a judgement is the joining of subject and predicate, contingency in their connection means that the judgement itself has the character of contingency.

If we draw the distinction between analytic and synthetic judgements, the analytic judgement, being based on an analysis within the identity of the subject concept, must have as its predicate a necessary mark of that concept. The synthetic judgement consists essentially in a synthesis of the subject concept with its possible content, so that a contingent mark of the concept comes to occupy the place of the predicate, and the synthetic judgement therefore has the character of contingency.

In the *Critique of Pure Reason* Kant says that in analytic (affirmative) judgements "the connection of the predicate with the subject is thought through *identity*" (B 10), and in the *Logic* he likewise says that "analytic propositions are those whose certainty rests on the identity of the concepts (of the predicate with the notion of the subject)" [p. 19] (Kant, *Logik*, hrsg. v. Jäsche, 3. Aufl., § 36). The analytic judgement, that is, is nothing other than an analysis, within the identity of the concept, of a concept grasped in its identity. "All bodies are extended" asserts that if the concept of body is a×b, extension b is contained within it, and so long as identity forms the basis we can "be conscious of the *necessity* of the judgement" (*Critique of Pure Reason*, B 12). The analytic judgement reaches the limit of its character in identical judgements such as "a body is a body", which carry over unchanged the necessity that belongs to the law of identity, "A is A". In a synthetic judgement, on the other hand, the connection of predicate and subject is one "thought without identity" (B 10). "All bodies are heavy" asserts that gravitation, c, is also joined to the concept of body, a×b, and since the predicate c is something other than the concept a×b, the connection of weight with body is "merely *contingent*" (B 12). Synthesis (σύνθεσις), after all, means leaving self-identity behind and coming to stand, by chance, *together* (σύν) with something else.

[p. 20] [コマ 17] Of course, with regard to a particular judgement of perception, the judgement arises from analysing the concrete content of perception given to the perceiving subject, so that a judgement such as "this man is yellow" ought to be called analytic, and the connection of "this man" with "yellow" is then necessary. We accept the distinction between analytic and synthetic judgements on the assumption that the subject is taken as a general concept, and seek only to point out that the former carries necessity whilst the latter, insofar as it means an empirical synthetic judgement, carries contingency. In the empirical synthetic judgement "some men are yellow", that is, the connection between the concept "man" and the predicate "yellow" is entirely contingent. In other words, when the concept of man was constituted, the mark of yellow skin colour was abstracted away as a contingent mark and placed outside the conceptual identity of man, and when a synthetic judgement empirically joins man, as concept, with yellow, the connection between them is naturally contingent.

### 3. The contingency of particular judgements

[p. 21] When the subject and predicate of a judgement show a necessary connection grounded in the identity of the concept, the judgement can take the form of a universal judgement. Where there is only a contingent connection, the judgement ought to take the form of a particular judgement, and the particular judgement accordingly shows, within the judgement itself, a contingency grounded in categorical contingency. When we say "all bodies are heavy", for instance, it should strictly be presupposed that weight is a necessary mark of the concept of body, that is, that a necessary relation holds between weight and body. Where this necessity is denied, the particular judgement says "some bodies are heavy" or "some bodies are not heavy", and so declares that weight is no more than a contingent mark of the concept of body. And since weight is only a contingent mark of body, even if we say "all bodies are heavy", the universality here is no strict universality, being "merely comparative" (Kant, *Welches sind die wirklichen Fortschritte, die Metaphysik* [p. 22] [コマ 18] etc., Beilagen I, Abschnitt I). In the *Critique of Pure Reason* Kant used the universal judgement "all bodies are heavy", but in the *Prolegomena*, in order to state the contingency more precisely, he changed it to the particular judgement "some bodies are heavy" (*Prolegomena*, § 2, a).

According to Lotze, the universal judgement (*das universale Urteil*) merely asserts a general fact and is therefore only assertoric (see p. 198). "All men are mortal" (*alle Menschen sind sterblich*) says only that, as a matter of fact, none in the end remain alive, and the necessity of dying is left ungrounded (*konkyozukeru*, 根據づける). The generic judgement (*das generelle Urteil*), by contrast, also shows the Ground of its necessary validity and can therefore be called apodictic (see p. 198). When we say "man is mortal" (*der Mensch ist sterblich*), we assert that it is contained in the character of man that whoever shares in this character cannot escape death. The universal judgement, in short, cannot express necessity, and to express necessity one must choose the generic judgement (Lotze, *Logik*, hrsg. v. Misch, 1912, S. 93). That is what Lotze says. As Benno Erdmann has argued, [p. 23] however, whilst the two kinds of judgement do need to be distinguished, since the universal judgement thinks the subject from its extension and the generic judgement from its intension, Lotze exaggerates the difference between them. It cannot be said that the universal judgement asserts no more than a general fact, nor that "all men are mortal" means only that all men have so far died, for to say that all men in general are mortal is already more than the assertion of a mere fact (Benno Erdmann, *Logik*, 3. Aufl., S. 456). We may therefore take it that necessity is expressed by the universal judgement and contingency by the particular judgement.

The contingency of the particular judgement is expressed symbolically in the signs of symbolic logic. In Couturat's notation (L. Couturat, *L'Algèbre de la Logique*, 2e éd.), the universal affirmative expresses the relation of inclusion between a and b directly with the sign <:

> a < b

The universal negative, "no a is b", means "every a is non-b", which is to say that the universal negative is the same as a universal affirmative with a negative predicate. [p. 24] [コマ 19] Since the negation of a term is written with the sign ′, the universal negative is written:

> a < b′

The particular judgement is the negation of a universal judgement, and to negate the judgement as a whole we need only negate the copula <, which is shown by striking it through with a vertical bar, giving the sign ≮. The particular affirmative, as the negation of the universal negative, is therefore written:

> a ≮ b′

and the particular negative, as the negation of the universal affirmative:

> a ≮ b

What grounds the relation of inclusion between a and b? It is the relation of identity between a×b, that is ab, on the one side and a on the other. The universal affirmative can therefore also be written:

> a = ab

The non-inclusion of a and b in the universal negative can be thought as the inclusion of a in b′, [p. 25] and so as an identity between a×b′, that is ab′, on the one side and a on the other. The universal negative is written:

> a = ab′

Since the particular judgement is the negation of the universal judgement, the particular affirmative and the particular negative can be written:

> a ≠ ab′
> a ≠ ab

These two deny, respectively, the identity of ab′ with a and the identity of ab with a. Since the modality of identity is necessity, the negation of identity is contingency. The categorical contingency that underlies the particular judgement is thus symbolised most clearly in the form ≠, the negation of the sign = which expresses necessity as identity.

The relation between the particular judgement and categorical contingency becomes clearer still when we consider the relation between inference and the particular judgement. Among the immediate inferences there is one called "conversion by accident" (*conversio per accidens*): where "all A are B", we can say "some B [p. 26] [コマ 20] are A". This mode of conversion is said to be "by accident" because contingency intervenes between A and B. So long as the universal judgement "all A are B" is used, there must be between A and B a necessary relation in the form of some relation of identity. That necessary relation, however, is the necessary relation a < b, that is a = ab, and since there is no absolute relation of identity, no absolute necessity, of the form a = b, the relation b < a, that is b = ba, does not hold. We cannot say "all B are A", only, as a particular judgement, "some B are A", because, as the signs b ≮ a′ or b ≠ ba′ show, room remains for contingency to intervene between A and B. We can say, for example, "all members of the yellow race are human", but we cannot say "all humans are members of the yellow race"; changing the universal into a particular judgement, we can say only "some humans are members of the yellow race". The skin colour that marks racial difference is a contingent mark of the concept of the human, and so long as this categorical contingency lies hidden within it, the only course open is to use the particular judgement, as a conversion "by accident".

[p. 27] Again, in mediate inference it is the third figure of the syllogism that yields a particular judgement as a new conclusion.

> (Darapti) All Ethiopians are black. All Ethiopians are human. Therefore some humans are black.
>
> (Felapton) No Ethiopians are white. All Ethiopians are human. Therefore some humans are not white.

Here the particular judgement in the conclusion shows that, for the general concept of the human, black (not white) skin is only a contingent mark, and that it happens to be in Ethiopians that the mark of being black (not white) forms part of the possible content of the concept of the human.

Categorical contingency, in short, must find its logical expression in the particular judgement. In other words, the contingency that belongs to the particular judgement [p. 28] [コマ 21] rests on the categorical contingency of contingent marks.

### 4. The contingent as isolated fact

We have shown that categorical contingency, as the contingency of contingent marks, underlies the contingency of synthetic and particular judgements. By considering the meaning of contingent marks further, we can now define the contingent as an isolated fact. The reason a contingent mark is placed, as non-essential, outside the identity of the concept lies in its isolation, and contingency therefore comes to mean, in general, the isolated fact that stands over against a system, and with it the individual thing.

Cohen[^c1s4-1] says of contingency that "a fact, even if its actuality has been proved, must be regarded as contingent so long as it has not yet been fitted into a general, or rather into the most general, connection" (Cohen, *Logik der reinen Erkenntnis*, 3. Aufl., S. 580). For the general concept of body held by mathematical natural science, for example, the organism [p. 29] is nothing other than an isolated fact. "Mathematical natural science, being limited to the mechanical, can neither determine the organic as organic nor even describe it. *The contingent* therefore stands for the organism. Organic natural forms are *contingent* in relation to the mechanical, not only relatively but also conceptually" (Cohen, *Kants Theorie der Erfahrung*, 3. Aufl., S. 708). Windelband[^c1s4-2] likewise regards the contingent as "the isolated fact" (*das vereinzelte Factum*) (Windelband, *Die Lehren vom Zufall*, S. 28). The meaning of this kind of contingency comes out clearly in the following example: "Even in the historical development of art collecting, the interest of art scholarship was quite secondary, and artistic and cultural-historical interests predominated. Leaving aside the periods ruled by the love of decoration, by curiosity or by religious feeling, what was collected first was chiefly the remains of ancient art. Only with the Renaissance did works of every period come to be collected, and as a result the need was felt to order the remains of each period systematically, and art-historical research at last arose. The famous collections of earlier times, however, because the history of their formation was so personal and *contingent*, were seldom [p. 30] [コマ 22] ordered and enlarged according to art-historical interest; that happened only with newly founded collections. The national museums of the various countries today are the result of centralisation, in which many famous collections were brought together in one place, formed the core, and from there developed step by step in a systematic way" (Kojima Kikuo, "The Problem of a National Museum", *Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, printed 18 March Shōwa 6).[^c1s4-3] The general concept here takes the form of "art scholarship" or "the art-historical system".

The notion of so-called "occasional crime"[^c1s4-4] works in much the same way, placing a certain kind of crime in the position of an isolated fact in relation to a general concept of a given personality. The following story from the *Liezi*[^c1s4-5] is almost the same kind of case:

> The followers of Zihua all came from hereditary houses. Dressed in white silk, they rode in carriages, strolled at their ease and looked about them with disdain. When they saw Shang Qiukai, old and weak, his face dark and weathered, his clothes and cap in disorder, there was not one who did not look down on him. Before long they were taking liberties with him, mocking and deceiving him, pushing and striking him; there was nothing they did not do. Shang Qiukai never showed anger, and the guests ran out of tricks and grew tired of their jokes. At last they climbed a high terrace with him, and one of them said idly before the crowd, "Whoever can throw himself down will have a hundred pieces of gold." All of them eagerly agreed. Shang Qiukai took it to be true and threw himself down first. His body was like a flying bird; he came to rest on the ground, and neither bone nor flesh was harmed. The Fan household took it for *chance* and did not yet think it strange. So they pointed to a deep pool at the bend of the river and said, "There is a precious pearl down there; dive and you can have it." Again Shang Qiukai followed their word and dived, and when he came out he had indeed found a pearl. Only then did the company begin to wonder, and Zihua had him placed among the retainers who eat meat and wear silk. Suddenly the Fan storehouse caught fire. Zihua said, "If you can go into the fire and bring out the brocade, I will reward you according to how much you fetch." Shang Qiukai went without hesitation, in and out of the fire; no ash clung to him and his body was not scorched. The Fan household now thought he [p. 31] possessed the Way, and together they apologised to him, saying, "We did not know you possessed the Way and we deceived you; we did not know you were a divine man and we insulted you. You may take us for fools, for the deaf, for the blind. May we dare to ask about your Way?" (*Liezi*, "The Yellow Emperor")[^c1s4-v1]

Zihua's guests in the Fan household had formed a general concept of Shang Qiukai as a man to be scorned, and so they took as chance an isolated fact that could not be subsumed under that concept. When similar facts recurred several times in his actions, they realised that it was no chance, discarded their earlier general concept, and in its place constituted, as an identity, the concept of a man who possesses the Way, a divine man.

The isolated fact can also come to mean the individual thing or the individual. Aristotle holds that "universals (τὰ καθόλου) belong by themselves, whereas accidents do not belong by themselves but are said only of particulars (τὰ καθ' ἕκαστα)" (Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, Δ 9, 1017b). Husserl says: "Individual existence of every kind is, speaking quite generally, *contingent*. ... But the sense of this contingency, which means factuality, is limited by the fact that it stands in a correlative relation to a necessity. This necessity does not mean [p. 32] [コマ 23] the mere factual subsistence of a valid rule ordering spatio-temporal facts; it has the character of essential necessity and so relates to essential universality. ... It belongs to the sense of everything contingent to have precisely an essence, and therefore an eidos to be grasped in its purity. ... Every tone, for example, has in itself an essence, purely understood as a moment to be seized from the individual tone, and at the highest level the universal essence tone in general, or rather the auditory in general" (Husserl, *Ideen zu einer reinen Phänomenologie*, S. 9). For Husserl, moreover, since the phenomenological domain is formed by the transcendental and the eidetic reductions, "in the phenomenological domain there is no contingency" (ibid., S. 288). Husserl's phenomenology is a science of essences grounded in the intuition of ideas, and its proper task consequently leaves no room for the contingent. Because categorical contingency means the individual thing or the individual, it also becomes clear why contingent attributes are so often called individual attributes, and why Schleiermacher sought to restrict the range of the synthetic judgement, as "judgement proper" (*eigentliches Urteil*), to individual facts (Schleiermacher, *Dialektik*, [p. 33] Ausg. v. Jonas, § 155, S. 88-89; cf. 405).

[^c1s4-1]: Tr. Hermann Cohen (1842-1918), founder of the Marburg school of neo-Kantianism.
[^c1s4-2]: Tr. Wilhelm Windelband (1848-1915), founder of the Baden school of neo-Kantianism; *Die Lehren vom Zufall* (1870) was his doctoral dissertation.
[^c1s4-3]: Tr. Kojima Kikuo, Japanese historian of Western art. Shōwa 6 is 1931.
[^c1s4-4]: Tr. The criminologist's *Gelegenheitsverbrechen*: crime arising from circumstance, set against crime arising from settled disposition.
[^c1s4-5]: Tr. The *Liezi*, a Daoist text traditionally attributed to Lie Yukou; the story of Shang Qiukai is in the "Yellow Emperor" chapter. Kuki quotes the classical Chinese.
[^c1s4-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 子華之門徒皆世族也、縞衣乘軒緩步闊視、顧見商丘開年老力弱、面目黎黑衣冠不檢、莫不眲之、既而狎侮欺詒、攩㧙挨抌、亡所不爲、商丘開常無慍容、而諸客之技單、憊於戲笑、遂與商丘開俱乘高臺、於衆中漫言曰、有能自投下者賞百金、衆皆競應、商丘開以爲信然、遂先投下、形若飛鳥揚於地、肌骨無䃣、范氏之黨以爲*偶然*、未詎怪也、因復指河曲之淫隈曰、彼中有寶珠、泳可得也、商丘開復從而泳之、既出、果得珠焉、衆昉同疑、子華昉令豫肉食衣帛之次、俄而范氏之藏大火、子華曰、若能入火取錦者、從所得多少賞若、商丘開往無難色、入火往還、埃不漫、身不焦、范氏之黨以爲有道、乃共謝之曰、吾不知子有道而誕子、吾不知子之神人辱子、子其愚我也、子其聾我也、子其盲我也、敢問其道 (列子黃帝). Our translation: "The followers of Zihua were all of hereditary families. In white silk they rode in carriages, walked slowly and looked about haughtily. Turning, they saw Shang Qiukai, old in years and weak in strength, his face dark, his clothes and cap untidy; there were none who did not look down on him. Then they treated him with familiarity and contempt, cheated and deceived him, pushed, struck and jostled him; there was nothing they did not do. Shang Qiukai always had no look of resentment, and the guests' tricks were exhausted and they were wearied of jesting and laughing. At length they climbed a high terrace together with Shang Qiukai, and one among the crowd said idly, 'Whoever can throw himself down will be rewarded with a hundred pieces of gold.' The crowd all competed to respond. Shang Qiukai took it to be true, and at length threw himself down first. His form was like a flying bird; he floated down to the ground, and his bones were unharmed. The Fan household took it to be *chance* and did not yet think it strange. Therefore they again pointed to a deep pool at the bend of the river and said, 'In there is a precious pearl; by diving it can be got.' Shang Qiukai again followed them and dived for it. When he came out, he had indeed got a pearl. The crowd then for the first time doubted together, and Zihua then for the first time had him placed in the rank of those who eat meat and wear silk. Shortly afterwards the Fan storehouse had a great fire. Zihua said, 'If you can enter the fire and take out the brocade, I will reward you according to how much you get.' Shang Qiukai went without a look of difficulty, entered the fire, went and returned; the ash did not soil him, and his body was not scorched. The Fan household thought he possessed the Way, and so together they apologised to him, saying, 'We did not know that you possessed the Way and we deceived you; we did not know that you were a divine man and we insulted you. You may take us for fools; you may take us for deaf; you may take us for blind. We dare to ask about your Way'" (*Liezi*, "Huangdi").

### 5. Exceptional contingency

In the previous section we considered how categorical contingency takes on the meaning of the isolated fact, the individual thing or the individual. Let us now consider the case in which the general concept means a law and the contingent means an exception. The constitutive content of a concept, insofar as it signifies a universal determination, has the value of a *law* for the individual representations to be subsumed under that concept, and consequently any possible content that runs counter to the constitutive content is seen as a possible *exception*.

Aristotle gives cold weather in the dog days of summer as an example of the accidental (Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, E 2, 1026b).[^c1s5-1] We also have, say, the concept of clover. Three leaves are found in clover "for the most part", or "almost always", and so we witness this as an identity and make it, as an essential mark, part of the constitutive content of the concept of clover. Strictly speaking it cannot be called a necessary mark, [p. 34] [コマ 24] but insofar as it is grasped as an identity we regard it as "quasi-necessary", and it thereby acquires the value of a law. Each individual clover depends in actuality on this quasi-universality of three leaves, and the determination "three-leaved" holds of each individual clover as a law. Four leaves, by contrast, can form part of the possible content of clover, but insofar as it lacks identity with the concept it remains only a contingent mark, and it is seen as a possible exception to the law. To give one more example: when a certain meteorologist's forecasts have the high success rate of ninety-nine per cent, "being right" becomes constitutive content and builds up the general concept of that meteorologist's forecasts. "Being right" thereby acquires the value of a law for each individual case, so that the possible content "being wrong" comes to be regarded as an exceptional contingency in relation to the law. In this way categorical contingency can take the form of exceptional contingency.

Exceptional contingency appears in the following passage: "I have seen that in a state of nature it must be a rare fact for some *chance* deviation of structure, such as a monstrosity, to be preserved, [p. 35] and that even if preserved at first, it must generally be lost by subsequent crossing with ordinary individuals" (Darwin, *On the Origin of Species*, trans. Koizumi Makoto, vol. 1, p. 266). In the next example, a certain fact is said not to be an exceptional contingency: "Labour disputes do not arise merely because workers harbour grievances about their working conditions, nor should they be started for that reason. They arise only when the workers believe they can win if they fight. Statistically, disputes are most frequent when a boom is about to turn into a depression, and when the economy has emerged from depression and taken its first step into a period of activity. In the former case employers, sensing trouble ahead before anyone else, try to lower working conditions, whilst the mass of workers, slow to register the subtle movements of the economy, are apt to misjudge the real situation and resist the employers' retrenchment. In the latter case, corporate profits are rising, prices are climbing and the cost of living is soaring, yet wages are slow to increase, so the workers lose patience and prepare to strike, whilst the employers, unsure how far the first shoots of recovery will grow, are reluctant to relax their retrenchment. The frequent labour disputes immediately after the Great War correspond to the former case, and the recent increase in disputes in America and in our country [p. 36] [コマ 25] is probably a good example of the latter. We should therefore not lightly dismiss the present labour disputes as *chance* occurrences. We should rather recognise that we are entering an era of labour disputes and prepare to prevent their ravages in advance" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 14 September Shōwa 9). The passage concerns the relation between the general concept of an era of labour disputes and the individual facts of particular disputes, and its claim is that, since this is an era of labour disputes, the present disputes are no exceptional contingency. A labour dispute arising in an era without labour disputes would be an exceptional contingency.

Categorical contingency, as a contingent mark, does not take part in the subsuming function of universal identity. Since that subsuming function operates through the schema "always" or "almost always", the categorical contingency it abstracts away has, as the negation of "always" and "almost always", the structure of "sometimes" or "rarely". It is above all in the case of "rarely" that the contingent takes on the meaning of an exception to a general law, and this is why some of the words that express chance are rooted in rarity. There is an old Japanese word for chance, *wakuraba ni*. A *wakuraba* [p. 37] is a leaf that has coloured like autumn foliage in summer and withered at the tips, something seen only "rarely". It is the law of summer leaves to be green, yet now and then one has turned colour, and for the green leaves of summer such colouring is a rare exception. The word *tamatama*, too, means chance and at the same time carries the sense of "rarely". *Tamatama* intensifies its meaning by repeating *tama*, and *tama* is said to have the sense of *tema* (手間). This *tama* has almost the same meaning as *mama*, that is, *ma-ma* (間間). The core meaning must therefore lie in the *ma* that *tama* and *mama* share. What is *ma*? *Ma* is 間, an interval, a spatial and temporal interval. An interval in turn comes to mean what exists only after an interval has been left, and so *mare*, "rare", is itself a contraction of *ma are* (間有れ) (see the dictionary *Genkai*). Since *ma* is what exists only rarely, *ma* also means chance. *Ma ni au* 〔"to be in time"〕 means to fit a chance occasion; *ma ga warui* 〔"to be awkward", "to be unlucky"〕 means that the chance one has met lacks fit; and *konna ma ni natta* 〔"it has come to this pass"〕 means that things have turned into this chance state of affairs. In short, *mama*, [p. 38] [コマ 26] *tama*, *tamatama* and *mare* all form a single family of words on the basis of *ma*. That *wakuraba ni* and *tamatama*, while meaning exceptional contingency in the sense of "rarely", became words for chance in general shows that categorical contingency is most easily grasped in the form of exceptional contingency.

What formal logic, in its theory of fallacies, calls the "fallacy of accident" (σόφισμα παρὰ τὸ συμβεβηκός, *fallacia ex accidente*) also concerns, for the most part, exceptional contingency. The so-called simple fallacy of accident applies a statement about a general concept even to exceptional, contingent content. An example would be to reason from the fact that a dream (*yume*, from *i-me*, "sleeping eye") is something seen while asleep and largely visual in content, to the conclusion that the dreams of the blind are also largely visual. The fallacy arises from applying a general statement about the general concept of dreaming to the exceptional contingency of the dreams of the blind, for it is clear from the diaries of blind people and other sources that for the blind a dream is not something seen but something heard, its content largely auditory. The converse fallacy of accident generalises directly from a truth about an exceptional contingency. An example would be to argue from the fact that "morphine is valuable as a medicine" that "since the main constituent of opium is morphine, [p. 39] opium is good for the health". The fallacy arises from failing to distinguish the exceptional, contingent case of a sick body in need of an anaesthetic or analgesic from the body in general.

We have considered exceptional contingency, and in sum, when a non-essential mark of a general concept is a contingent mark in the form of an exception, its non-essentiality, its contingency, stands out with particular force.

[^c1s5-1]: Tr. Kuki's 土用 (*doyō*) is the hottest period of the Japanese summer, standing here for Aristotle's dog days.

### 6. The reason for the existence of categorical contingency

In the *Nāgasena Sūtra* (那先比丘經),[^c1s6-1] Milinda puts the following question to Nāgasena: "People in the world all have heads, faces, eyes, bodies and four limbs complete. Why, then, are some long-lived and some short-lived, some often ill and some seldom ill, some poor and some rich, some honoured and some base, some handsome and some ugly, some trusted by others and some doubted, some bright and some dull? Why are they not the same?"[^c1s6-v1] (vol. 1). This is a philosophical question that holds within it human joy and suffering, yet in the end it is nothing other than a question about the contingency of the individual thing, about categorical contingency. It is a question about the contingent determinations that individual human beings possess in relation [p. 40] [コマ 27] to the general concept of the human. Nāgasena answered: "It is like the fruits that grow on the many trees: some are sour, some bitter, some pungent, some sweet."[^c1s6-v2] But this merely offers another example of categorical contingency.

A doubt of this kind about the existence of categorical contingency means, in general, a doubt about the existence of individual facts and individual things, and a doubt about the existence of individual things is the question why, besides genera and species, there exist individual things at all. In the end this is a question about existence itself. For if a given species contained no individual things within it, the species would itself become an individual thing and exist in relation to its genus; that genus in turn, by negating the particulars within it, would itself become an individual thing and exist in relation to a higher genus; and so we would ascend to the highest genus. To negate individual things and think only the existence of the highest genus is nothing other than to think an emptiness and an abstraction. We can, of course, conceive of a case in which, as the universal individuates itself, all its individual forms possess, on the basis of the universal's identity, a strict equality among themselves. In that case, however, the individual thing would become something like what Husserl calls ideal singularity [p. 41] (eidetic singularity), and could not be said to possess true individuality. In the actual world we must think that Leibniz's principle of the "identity of indiscernibles" holds without exception. Each monad, as a "metaphysical point", must occupy a position at a "mathematical point" and express the universe from its own standpoint, so that there are no two things in the universe that are perfectly alike, no two leaves perfectly alike, no two raindrops perfectly alike. That individual things show a differentiation going beyond ideal singularity must be an indispensable condition of the actuality of the actual. And since general concepts such as species and genus were originally constituted from individual things by the work of identifying abstraction, it is only to be expected that the contingent marks which abstraction placed outside the circle of identity should, as categorical contingency, speak in turn of the existence of individual things.

The doubt about the existence of categorical contingency further includes a doubt about the case in which a contingent mark, taking the form of an exception, threatens the general concept itself. To this we must answer that a general concept which admits exceptions is not fixed and static but, being generative and dynamic, [p. 42] [コマ 28] signifies rather a movement towards a general concept. We might say that a general concept of this kind possesses the universality of reflective judgement, a universality set as a task, and lacks the universality of determinant judgement, a universality already given.[^c1s6-2] Between the general concept and the individual thing there is movement. The individual thing is incommensurable with logic, and here lies the possibility of the exception. Hegel says: "On the surface of nature, so to speak, contingency has free play, and this should be acknowledged as such, without the demand (sometimes wrongly attributed to philosophy) to find in it something that could only be thus and not otherwise. ... It is entirely correct to say that the task of science, and more precisely of philosophy, is in general to come to know the necessity hidden under the semblance of contingency. This must not be understood, however, as if the contingent belonged merely to our subjective representation and therefore had simply to be eliminated in order to arrive at the truth. Scientific efforts that pursue this direction one-sidedly will hardly escape the justified reproach of being an empty game or a stiff pedantry" [p. 43] (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, S. 195). And on the relation between law and individual fact he says: "Law and the administration of justice contain contingency on one side, and the reason is that law is a general determination that must be applied to individual cases. To take a stand against this contingency would be to insist on an abstraction" (Hegel, *Grundlinien der Philosophie des Rechts*, hrsg. v. Lasson, 1911, S. 341). Hegel is speaking here of law and of the movement of contingency within its general determinations, but the same can be said of natural laws and individual chance events in general. Boutroux[^c1s6-3] states a well-known phrase on contingency within natural law: "Laws are the bed through which the torrent of facts passes. The facts follow that bed, but it was originally the facts that hollowed it out" (*Les lois sont le lit où passe le torrent des faits: ils l'ont creusé, bien qu'ils le suivent*) (Boutroux, *De la contingence des lois de la nature*, 9e éd., p. 39). Since every law consists in a relation of subsumption between a general concept and individual facts, it is only to be expected that, insofar as the general concept has the character of a universality of reflective judgement, set as a task, contingency in the form of the exception should accompany the law as its reverse side. [p. 44] [コマ 29]

[^c1s6-1]: Tr. The Chinese translation of the *Milindapañha*, the dialogue between the Indo-Greek king Menander (Milinda) and the Buddhist monk Nāgasena. Kuki quotes the classical Chinese.
[^c1s6-2]: Tr. Kuki draws on Kant's distinction between determinant (*bestimmende*) and reflective (*reflektierende*) judgement, and on the neo-Kantian contrast between what is given (*gegeben*) and what is set as a task (*aufgegeben*).
[^c1s6-3]: Tr. Émile Boutroux (1845-1921), French philosopher; *De la contingence des lois de la nature* (1874) was his doctoral thesis.
[^c1s6-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 世間人頭面目身體四支皆完具、何故有長命者、有短命者、有多病少病者、有貧者富者、有長者、有卑者、有端正者、有醜惡者、有爲人所信者、爲人所疑者、有明者、有闇者、何以故不同 (卷上). Our translation: "The people of the world have heads, faces, eyes, bodies and four limbs all complete. For what reason are there the long-lived and the short-lived, the much ill and the little ill, the poor and the rich, the honoured and the base, the handsome and the ugly, those trusted by others and those doubted by others, the bright and the dull? For what reason are they not the same?" (first fascicle).
[^c1s6-v2]: Tr. Kuki's text: 譬若衆樹木生菓、有酢者有苦者有辛者有甜者. Our translation: "It is like the many trees bearing fruit: there are the sour, there are the bitter, there are the pungent, there are the sweet."

### 7. From categorical contingency to hypothetical contingency

The doubt about the existence of categorical contingency shows that the problem must now be developed on a new horizon. If there is a chilly day in the dog days of summer, it is contingent for the general concept of the dog days. Yet that the temperature was low in the dog days of one particular year must have a cause, sunspots or something else. Again, in the particular judgement of perception "this clover is four-leaved", the connection between "this clover" and "four-leaved" has necessity. The connection of "clover" and "four-leaved" is contingent only when a general concept is being thought. As soon as the demonstrative "this" gives "clover" a determination, the relation between a single particular clover and four leaves is no longer contingent.
