That "this clover" is "four-leaved" must have some cause, whether its state of nourishment, the influence of the climate, the stimulus of an injury or something [p. 45] else. Everyone accepts that a snake with two heads on one body is an extremely rare and contingent existence. Yet biologists' experiments have shown that a two-headed snake can be produced artificially by surgery, and so it is thought that the two-headed snakes found in nature arise because the head was split in two at a very early stage. We regard the two-headed snake as contingent only insofar as we see it in relation to the general concept of the snake; that "this" snake has two heads must have some cause. When we regard those born with disabilities, or those with severe intellectual disability,[^c1s7-1] as contingent, it is likewise in relation to the general concept "human", while "this" disabled person and "that" person must each have embryological or other causes. When we regard a handsome or an ugly appearance as contingent, it is because we have set up the general concept "human" and witness the particular in relation to it; that "this" person is handsome and "that" person ugly must have some cause, in the way the germ cells of the two sexes combined, in the mother's health during pregnancy, or elsewhere. In the same way, racial difference by skin colour is contingent for the conceptual essence of the "human". But the relation between a particular race and its skin [p. 46] [コマ 30] colour is in no way contingent; that "this" race has a certain skin colour must have its cause in light, temperature and other conditions. Likewise, that the forecast of a meteorologist whose success rate reaches ninety-nine per cent should prove wrong is an exceptional contingency only in relation to the general concept of forecasts with a ninety-nine per cent success rate; in "this" particular case there must be some cause, a sudden change in the weather, say, or some agitation in the forecaster's mind.

In this way we move from the problem of conceptuality to the problem of groundedness, from the problem of categorical contingency to the problem of hypothetical contingency.

[^c1s7-1]: Tr. Kuki's 白痴 (*hakuchi*) is a period term, rendered here as "those with severe intellectual disability"; his 不具者 (*fugusha*) is rendered "those born with disabilities".

---

## Chapter 2. Hypothetical contingency (第二章 假說的偶然) [コマ 30-99]

### 1. Contingency of ground

[p. 47] [コマ 30] Leibniz called the law of contradiction and the law of sufficient reason the "two great principles" (Leibniz, *Opera philosophica*, ed. Erdmann, p. 515, 707), and for him the law of contradiction is in the end nothing other than the law of identity, as when he speaks of "the axiom of identity or, what is the same, the axiom of contradiction" (Ib., p. 138). Kant, too, did not distinguish the two (Kant, *Logik*, hrsg. v. Jäsche, S. 58). In what relation, then, do these two great principles, the "law of identity" and the "law of ground", stand to each other?[^c2s1-1] In the fragment "First Truths" (*Primae veritates*) Leibniz showed that groundedness can be reduced to identity. The predicate or consequent is always inherent in the subject or antecedent (*Semper praedicatum seu consequens inest subjecto seu antecedenti*), and inherence (*inesse*) is nothing other [p. 48] [コマ 31] than identity (*idem esse*), so that groundedness is, in the end, rooted in identity. The proof of any truth rests, after all, on the law of ground, that "nothing is without a ground". If the law of ground did not hold, there would be truths that could not be proved a priori, which is to say truths that could not be resolved into identity, and this is contrary to the nature of truth, since truth, whether explicitly or implicitly, is always identical (*Alioqui veritas daretur, quae non potest probari a priori, seu quae non resolveretur in identicas, quod est contra naturam veritatis, quae semper vel expresse vel implicite identica est*). The first truth, in other words, is nothing other than the proposition "A is A" (*Opuscules et fragments inédits de Leibniz*, éd. Couturat, p. 518-519). To borrow the concise formulation of Couturat,[^c2s1-2] "The law of identity says that all identical (analytic) propositions are true. The law of ground affirms that all true propositions are identical (analytic)" (Couturat, Sur la métaphysique de Leibniz, *Revue de Métaphysique et de Morale*, t. X. 1902, p. 8). Émile Meyerson[^c2s1-3] likewise points out that the law of ground is based on the law of identity: "This conviction of rationality, this conviction of necessary connection, has its origin solely in the reduction to the identity that exists between antecedent and consequent" [p. 49] (Émile Meyerson, *Du cheminement de la Pensée*, I. 1931, p. 55).

It follows that ground and consequent possess identity in the sense just described, and the relation between them is consequently necessary. Whatever does not stand in a relation of ground and consequent is, by contrast, contingent, and we can call this contingency of ground, as opposed to necessity of ground.[^c2s1-4] This contingency *of ground*, together with *causal* contingency and *teleological* contingency, which I explain later, I call by the general name of hypothetical contingency. The division of hypothetical contingency into these three rests on a division of the hypothetical relation into three, namely groundedness, causality and purposiveness. Schopenhauer divided the law of sufficient reason into four roots: the "principle of the sufficient reason of becoming" (*principium rationis sufficientis fiendi*), the "principle of the sufficient reason of knowing" (*principium rationis sufficientis cognoscendi*), the "principle of the sufficient reason of existence" (*principium rationis sufficientis essendi*) and the "principle of the sufficient reason of acting" (*principium rationis sufficientis agendi*). The principle of the sufficient reason of becoming corresponds to causality, which we shall treat later; the principle of the sufficient reason of knowing corresponds to the groundedness just described; and the principle of the sufficient reason of acting corresponds roughly to purposiveness, which I discuss later. What he calls the principle of the sufficient reason of existence refers to the ground of intuitive existence, that is, it is a principle concerning mathematical [p. 50] [コマ 32] ground (Schopenhauer, *Über die vierfache Wurzel des Satzes vom zureichenden Grunde*). Corresponding to these four divisions of the law of sufficient reason, Schopenhauer divides necessity into four kinds, physical, logical, mathematical and practical necessity (Schopenhauer, *Die Welt als Wille und Vorstellung*, Anhang. Kritik der Kantischen Philosophie. Sämtliche Werke. hrsg. v. Deussen, I. S. 549). Whether it is right to distinguish mathematical ground from logical ground, and so to set up a mathematical necessity alongside logical necessity, is one of the important problems of epistemology, but we cannot go deeply into it here. We have simply followed Lotze, who says that "the necessary, whose non-existence is impossible, is only what is conditioned as follows: (1) what is determined by a ground as consequent, (2) what is determined by a cause as effect, (3) what is determined by a purpose as means" (Lotze, *Mikrokosmus*, III, 1864, S. 551). We have distinguished the hypothetical relation into three, groundedness, causality and purposiveness, and, corresponding to necessity of ground, causal necessity and teleological necessity, have separated out three kinds of contingency: contingency of ground, causal contingency and teleological contingency.

[p. 51] Contingency of ground is of two kinds. In the first, the non-existence of a ground is witnessed negatively with respect to a single thing; in the second, the existence of some relation other than groundedness is witnessed positively between two or more things. The former may be called negative contingency of ground and the latter positive contingency of ground.

[^c2s1-1]: Tr. 理由律 (*riyūritsu*), the law of ground (or of reason); 充足理由律 is the law of sufficient reason. Kuki's 理由 (*riyū*) covers both "ground" and "reason"; it is rendered "ground" throughout, except in the established phrase "law of sufficient reason" and in Schopenhauer's Latin titles.
[^c2s1-2]: Tr. Louis Couturat (1868-1914), French logician, editor of Leibniz's unpublished papers.
[^c2s1-3]: Tr. Émile Meyerson (1859-1933), Polish-born French philosopher of science.
[^c2s1-4]: Tr. 理由的偶然 (*riyūteki gūzen*), literally "contingency pertaining to ground", is rendered "contingency of ground"; 理由的必然 is "necessity of ground".

### 2. Negative contingency of ground

Negative contingency of ground is the case in which the non-existence of a ground is witnessed negatively, and the concept of this kind of contingency appears in the following passage: "When I do not know what is true with sufficient clarity and distinctness, then if I refrain from passing judgement I am plainly acting rightly and am not mistaken. But if I either affirm or deny, I do not use my free will rightly. If I have turned towards the wrong side, I am certainly mistaken. If I have taken hold of the other side, I fall upon the truth *by chance*, but that does not mean [p. 52] [コマ 33] I am without fault. For it is clear by the natural light that the perception of the intellect must always precede the determination of the will" (Descartes, *Meditationes* IV. *Oeuvres*, éd. Adam & Tannery, VII. p. 59-60; cf. *Principia philosophiae*, I. § 44. *Oeuvres*, éd. Adam & Tannery, VIII. p. 21). Since such a person affirmed at will whilst lacking a clear and distinct ground sufficient to affirm the truth, he fell into the truth by chance, and contingency in this case is negative contingency of ground, contingency as the non-existence of a ground. The following passage is of the same kind: "That science finds the highest and most complete expression of its explanation in atomism is due to the very structure of science, and is not the result of a kind of *chance*. That science, as the labour of an understanding that knows nothing clearly and distinctly except number, masters the obscure and confused manifold of reality is possible only through number" (Arthur Hannequin,[^c2s2-1] *Essai critique sur l'hypothèse des atomes*, Paris, Alcan, p. 129). For this author, that is, there is a sufficient ground in the very structure of science for its arriving at the consequent we call atomism, and since science and atomism are bound together by necessity of ground, contingency as the non-existence of a ground cannot be found there. The same holds in the following case: "When the dialectical [p. 53] structure is kept without the least distortion, being and nothingness, which are relatively opposed to each other, become entirely equal in rank and standing, and we have no way of distinguishing precedence and subordination between them. We must then abandon the structure of the dialectic in its usual interpretation, in which being turns into nothingness and that nothingness into being, and must think that being and nothingness are both, equally and directly, manifested from within absolute being through its self-limitation or self-negation. For that reason, from the standpoint of the absolute, being and nothingness become mutually exchangeable, and to attach a before and an after to the arising of the two can only be put down to the *contingent* caprice of the individual who thinks it" (Takahashi Satomi,[^c2s2-2] *Zentai no tachiba*, p. 277). Logically, being and nothingness are entirely equal in rank and standing, and there is no ground for placing either one first and the other after. If we do place one first and the other after, we do so merely at will, without a necessary ground, and this is contingency of ground. Negative contingency of ground, in short, is a mental product that lacks a sufficient ground.

A mental product lacking a sufficient ground is, generally speaking, something irrational, and such irrational negative contingency of ground finds a place to exist, with comparative constancy, in dreams, in madness and in artistic production. The tendency by which ideas are posited in a dream is [p. 54] [コマ 34] nothing other than wholly contingent fancy. As Bergson also says, in dreams we encounter contingent events countless times. Memory rakes together fragments of every kind of recollection from here and there and offers them to the consciousness of the sleeper without making them consistent, and the sleeper seeks some meaning in that meaningless heap. Thinking that the inconsistency comes from gaps, he calls up recollections of various other kinds to fill them, but these too often appear with the same disorder, so that a further new explanation becomes necessary, and the same thing goes on without end. The instability of dreams rests, in other words, on the fact that sensation is not made to correspond exactly to recollection, so that room is left for mischief, and in dreams very different recollections of every kind come to fit one and the same sensation. In Uchida Hyakken's *Hyakkien zuihitsu*[^c2s2-3] we find the following: "The eighth of August, autumn begins. When I woke once at dawn, I left the shōji and the window wide open and went back to sleep. At the foot of the Denzūin[^c2s2-4] slope, a big yellow horse came walking towards me. Its body was absurdly long, and of its four legs the left hind one was short. No one was leading it, so I thought it dangerous. When I said to Kiikō,[^c2s2-5] who had come with me, [p. 55] 'It's dangerous, shall we run?', the horse turned towards us. At that moment I saw two men in black clothes on the far side of the horse. When the horse had gone off somewhere, I woke up. I heard a crow cawing nearby. It went on cawing for a long time in a strangely low voice." The sensory datum here is that "a crow went on cawing for a long time in a strangely low voice". This called up the recollection of the gloomy slope road at Denzūin, the recollection of a yellow of strangely low tone, the recollection of a horse with a long body, the recollection of a strange form in which only one of four legs is short, and the recollection of the *kurombō*[^c2s2-6] of the theatre, dressed in black like a crow. All these recollections chase the sensation around, each trying to come back to life within it, and there a contingent product such as a yellow horse arises, which comes limping towards us at the foot of the Denzūin slope. Different recollections, again, cling to the sensation together, or catch up with it in turn, one after another, so that just when we think there is a man in black clothes on the far side of the horse, the yellow horse in an instant turns into the man in black. The dreamer reasons within the dream, "No one was leading it, so I thought it dangerous", "It's dangerous, shall we run?", "When I said shall we run, the horse turned towards us", "The horse turned [p. 56] [コマ 35] towards us. At that moment", and so forms logical connections in an attempt to remove the absurdity of the dream, but in doing so makes it all the more senseless. The contingent caprice that a dream unfolds is almost beyond explanation (cf. Bergson, *L'Énergie spirituelle*, p. 91, 114).

The intellectual world of the madman is likewise a tangle of contingent caprice. Descartes describes how "they stubbornly insist that they are kings whilst in abject poverty, say they are clothed in purple whilst naked and will not listen, or else say that their head is earthenware, or believe themselves to be pumpkins or glass bottles" (Descartes, *Meditationes* I. *Oeuvres*, éd. Adam & Tannery, VII. p. 19). We may also cite the words of Shakespeare's mad King Lear as an example of contingency of ground:[^c2s2-7] "Let us have Regan anatomised. I would have it examined what on earth grows in that wretch's heart. Is there some particular cause that brews such a cruel heart? You, I take into my service as one of my hundred knights. Only I do not like your dress. You will say, I suppose, that it is the Persian fashion, but I would have it changed. Be quiet, quiet. Draw the curtain. So, so. Now, we shall finish supper [p. 57] in the morning" (Act III, Scene 6). The anatomy of a body and the examination of a cruel heart, nakedness and dress, the morning and supper: the positing of ideas here lacks a sufficient ground.

It is often said that art, whilst it approaches dream and madness on the one hand, differs from them on the other. In certain kinds of artistic production, however, negative contingency of ground appears to an extreme degree, and some works make us think of dream or madness itself. Book Sixteen of the *Man'yōshū*,[^c2s2-8] for example, contains two "poems to which the mind attaches nothing": "On my beloved's brow there grows / the sugoroku bull's / saddle, the sore upon it",[^c2s2-v1] and "The round stone my husband makes his loincloth: / on the mountains of Yoshino / icefish hang".[^c2s2-v2] These poems were made in response to an order issued by Prince Toneri,[^c2s2-9] "If there is anyone who makes a poem with nothing it rests on, I will reward him with coin and silk",[^c2s2-v3] and "with nothing it rests on" means nothing other than the non-existence of a ground. The "rikai" poem of the *Kakyō hyōshiki*,[^c2s2-10] "Kasuga mountain, a boat rowing over the peak, Yakushiji, the island of Awaji, the mouldboard of a plough",[^c2s2-v4] is of the same kind. Between one word and the next there is no logical connection at all, so that almost nothing positive can be found in it, and in the run of the words the non-existence of a ground is only witnessed negatively. Yet we still cannot deny that there is some art in it. Shakespeare's *A Midsummer Night's Dream* is an example of the symbolisation of the abstract [p. 58] [コマ 36] nonsense of chance, and at the same time a realistic depiction of the most concrete truth of human life. As Bottom the weaver confesses, it is "a dream wondrous beyond all bounds", "a dream that human wisdom can make nothing of", "a dream such as the eye of man has never heard, nor the ear seen, nor the hand tasted, nor the tongue thought, nor the heart passed on". "Whoever thinks to explain such a dream is a great ass", "a great fool". As "Bottom's Dream", "the bottom has gone right out of it" (It hath no bottom).[^c2s2-11] To "have no bottom" is to "have nothing it rests on", that is, the non-existence of a ground. On the "supramundane play and surreal laughter"[^c2s2-12] of this kind of "meaninglessness", that is, of "nonsense", Mr Takahashi Satomi sets out an interesting philosophical consideration (Tōhoku Imperial University, Faculty of Law and Letters, *Tenth Anniversary Philosophical Essays*, p. 53).

[^c2s2-1]: Tr. Arthur Hannequin (1856-1905), French philosopher of science.
[^c2s2-2]: Tr. Takahashi Satomi (1886-1964), Japanese philosopher at Tōhoku Imperial University. *Zentai no tachiba* means "The Standpoint of the Whole".
[^c2s2-3]: Tr. Uchida Hyakken (1889-1971), Japanese novelist and essayist; *Hyakkien zuihitsu* ("Hyakkien Essays") is a collection of his essays.
[^c2s2-4]: Tr. Denzūin, a Pure Land Buddhist temple in Koishikawa, Tokyo.
[^c2s2-5]: Tr. 奇異公 (*Kiikō*), a nickname for a companion; Kuki quotes it without comment, and the reading is not certain.
[^c2s2-6]: Tr. 黒坊 (*kurombō*), the black-clad stage assistants of the Japanese theatre, conventionally treated as invisible.
[^c2s2-7]: Tr. The Lear passage, and Bottom's speech below, are translated from Kuki's Japanese version. His Japanese gives Bottom a rustic dialect that the English does not reproduce.
[^c2s2-8]: Tr. The *Man'yōshū* is the eighth-century anthology of Japanese poetry. The two poems are nos. 3838-3839; *sugoroku* is a board game played with dice.
[^c2s2-9]: Tr. Prince Toneri (676-735), son of Emperor Tenmu.
[^c2s2-10]: Tr. The *Kakyō hyōshiki* is a treatise on poetics of 772 by Fujiwara no Hamanari. "Rikai" (離會, "separated meeting") is one of its categories of poem; Kuki gives the term without explanation.
[^c2s2-11]: Tr. Kuki writes 底 (*soko*, "bottom") with the reading ボトム (*botomu*), so that Bottom's name and the "bottom" of his dream coincide, as in the English.
[^c2s2-12]: Tr. 出世間的 (*shussekenteki*), "supramundane", a Buddhist term for what lies beyond the worldly.
[^c2s2-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 吾妹子が額(ぬか)に生ひたる雙六(すごろく)の牡牛(ことひのうし)の鞍の上の瘡(かさ). Our translation: "On my beloved's brow there grows / the sugoroku bull's / saddle, the sore upon it."
[^c2s2-v2]: Tr. Kuki's text: 吾背子が犢鼻(たふさき)にする圓石(つぶれいし)の吉野の山に氷魚(ひを)ぞさがれる. Our translation: "The round stone my husband makes his loincloth: / on the mountains of Yoshino / icefish hang."
[^c2s2-v3]: Tr. Kuki's text: もし由る所無き歌を作る人あらば、賜ふに錢帛を以てせむ. Our translation: "If there is anyone who makes a poem with nothing it rests on, I will reward him with coin and silk."
[^c2s2-v4]: Tr. Kuki's text: 春日山峰漕ぐ船の藥師寺淡路の島の犁(からすき)の耳(へら). Our translation: "Kasuga mountain, a boat rowing over the peak, Yakushiji, the island of Awaji, the mouldboard of a plough."

### 3. Positive contingency of ground

In positive contingency of ground, what is witnessed between two or more things is some other positive relation, one that is not a necessary relation [p. 59] through groundedness. Contingency of this kind often appears in relations of number. If we convert the so-called Archimedean value of π, the ratio of the circumference, 3 1/7, into a decimal, for example, we obtain the following recurring decimal:[^c2s3-1]

> 3.142857142857……

Here the repetend 142857 is repeated "again and again" (πάλιν καὶ πάλιν),[^c2s3-2] and in this case the numerical property belonging to the approximate value of the ratio of a circumference to its diameter exists in a relation of necessity of ground, without any relation whatever to the system of numeration. The part corresponding to the first to sixth decimal places is consequently conditioned with necessity by the property of the number, and the part corresponding to the seventh to twelfth decimal places is likewise conditioned with necessity. There is, however, the device of decimal numeration, adopted in order to express that approximate value in figures, and on the basis of this device 142857 and 142857 are positively witnessed as contingency. From the twelfth decimal place onwards the same relation is repeated without end. Let us now take this number 142857 and arrange its figures in order, in the direction in which the hands of a clock move, at the vertices of a hexagon inscribed in a circle on a disc. Doubled, the number becomes 285714; tripled, [p. 60] [コマ 37] 428571; quadrupled, 571428; multiplied by five, 714285; and multiplied by six, 857142. These numbers have the same figures in the same order as those on the disc. To obtain the double we need only take 2 as the starting point and go round clockwise; to obtain three, four, five and six times the number we take the starting point at 4, 5, 7 and 8 respectively and go round clockwise in the same way. This number 142857 becomes 999999 when multiplied by seven, and is consequently the integer part of the quotient of one million divided by seven. The necessary relation between 142857 and the numbers two, three, four, five and six times it is, after all, the relation between a number corresponding to a common divisor and its various multiples, and on the ground that the common divisor is a multiple of nine, the consequent follows that its various multiples are also multiples of nine. That the special relation described above exists in addition to this, however, rests on regarding each number as something ultimate and on thinking of a circular movement that returns from the figure at the right-hand end to the figure at the left-hand end. A coincidence of this kind, drawn out by circular movement, is a contingent relation entirely unconnected with the necessary relation between a number and its various multiples. There we find something unmoving and wholly at rest, which can be read, for instance, as "is not in the world" [p. 61] (428571),[^c2s3-3] something that does not change in the least when multiplied by two, three, four, five or six. It is, at any rate, a relation worthy of attention, and positive contingency exists where that relation is positively witnessed.

In name divination,[^c2s3-4] the fact that several names come to have a positive relation through the stroke count of their characters is likewise a positive contingency of ground, existing where there is no logical relation. Yukio (行雄), Hisanori (久徵), Teisuke (禎介), Takeo (武郎), Senji (宣治) and other such names have no logical relation whatever among themselves, yet they show a contingent relation in the so-called earth grade, that is, in the total stroke count of the given name being eighteen, and on this one of the foundations of name divination is built. The logical relation lies between the meaning a name expresses and the name itself, since the ground on which a given name arises lies in the meaning it expresses. From one ground the name Yukio follows as consequent, from another the name Hisanori, and between Yukio and Hisanori there is no necessary relation through groundedness at all. That both have a stroke count of eighteen is a positively witnessed contingency. A logical relation between one name and another must be a relation of meaning. Yukio, Tsugio, Mitsuo, [p. 62] [コマ 38] Yoshio and Yasuo, for example, share the character 雄,[^c2s3-5] which might be called a common divisor of meaning. These names must have arisen from some particular ground, a meaning such as "manly", for example, and in this case, since each of them is the consequent of the same ground, they stand in a logical and necessary relation to one another. The further feature they share, a total stroke count of eighteen, is, however, a contingency grasped positively, outside groundedness.

Positive contingency of ground is also strikingly apparent in the phonological relations of language, that is, in cases where a relation of sound exists positively where there is no logical relation through groundedness. Examples are the relation between *hachi* as "bowl" (鉢), "bee" (蜂) and "eight" (八), or the mutual relation of *senkō* as "incense stick" (線香), "selection" (詮衡), "specialisation" (專攻), "moving in secret" (潜行), "bright crimson" (鮮紅), "military merit" (戰功) and "an immortal" (仙公). Between these words there is no logical relation by which one, as ground, and another, as consequent, condition each other's positing. Nor do they have the logical relation of being various consequents arising from a common ground. What exists positively is only the relation of sound, and that is why we call the relation contingent. Contingency of this kind has a certain literary value in the forms of alliteration, end rhyme, the pivot word (*kakekotoba*), the pillow [p. 63] word (*makurakotoba*), the acrostic (*orikuri*) and the palindrome (*kaibun*)[^c2s3-6] (cf. *Iwanami kōza Nihon bungaku*, Kuki, "Nihon shi no ōin", p. 8, p. 119, p. 155).[^c2s3-7] To take pillow words first, in *momoshiki no, ōmiyadokoro* and *kusamakura, tabiyadori sesu*[^c2s3-v1] the positing of the pillow word and of the seven-syllable phrase that follows are mutually and necessarily conditioned by a logical relation of meaning.[^c2s3-8] In those that rest on alliteration, however, such as *inafune no, ina ni wa arazu* and *u no hana no, uki koto are ya*,[^c2s3-v2] and in those that rest on a pivot word, such as *tamadare no, o-chi no ōno no*, where *o* is both 緒 and 越, and *azusayumi, haru no yamabe o*, where *haru* is both "to string" (張) and "spring" (春),[^c2s3-v3] true groundedness is lacking between the pillow word and the following seven-syllable phrase, and there is only a contingent relation of sound.[^c2s3-9] The essence of the pivot word, further, is to split one sound into two different meanings and to grasp the contingent coincidence of sound that the two meanings share. As for end rhyme, in the line "East of the city of Luoyang, the peach and plum blossom" each word is necessarily conditioned by logical relation, and the same is true of the line "Flying here, flying there, into whose house does it fall?",[^c2s3-v4] yet between the two lines a contingent relation, having nothing to do with the groundedness of the words' meaning, is established through the phonological character that 花, "blossom", and 家, "house", both belong to the level tone and the 麻 rhyme.[^c2s3-10] The poem *okitsu tori (i) / kamo doku shima ni (i) / waga ineshi (i) / imo wa wasureji (i) / yo no kotogoto ni (i)*,[^c2s3-11][^c2s3-v5] by the contingent relation of a single rhyme held throughout, [p. 64] [コマ 39] forms a recurring circular movement repeated without end. In an acrostic such as *karagoromo / kitsutsu narenishi / tsuma shi areba / harubaru kinuru / tabi o shi zo omou*,[^c2s3-12][^c2s3-v6] again, a further new meaning is added, doubly, to the whole poem, which is already permeated by the groundedness of the meaning of its words, through a contingent relation of sound. A palindrome such as *nakakiyo no tōno neburi no mina mezame naminoribune no oto no yoki kana*[^c2s3-13][^c2s3-v7] is the same poem whether read from the beginning or from the end, and in producing through circular movement a special contingency over and above the logical and necessary relation, it has a character similar to that of the number 142857 cited earlier.

Mr Terada Torahiko, in his *Mangekyō*,[^c2s3-14] writes: "As I was taught English and German, step by step, I often came across words of the same meaning with sounds very like Japanese. Though I thought this was probably *chance*, it seemed rather difficult to prove that all of them were *chance* coincidences" (p. 296), and he gives such examples as beat and butu, flat and filattai, new and nii, fat and futo, easy and yasasi, clean and kilei, angry and ikari, anchor and ikari, tray and tarai, mattress and musiro. There are, of course, many cases in which what was at first thought to be a contingency of sound is resolved into necessity [p. 65]. *Kami* as "god" (神), "above" (上) and "hair" (髮), for example, are all venerable things situated towards the head, and the wolf, *ōkami*, is also a fearsome *kami*.[^c2s3-15] These are all consequents conditioned by a common ground, and among them there is a logical and necessary relation based on groundedness. It goes without saying that positive contingency of ground is not to be found in cases of this kind.

[^c2s3-1]: Tr. 3 1/7 is 22/7, the upper bound for π given by Archimedes.

[^c2s3-4]: Tr. 姓名判斷 (*seimei handan*), divination by the number of brush strokes in the characters of a name. The "earth grade" (地格, *chikaku*) is the total stroke count of the given name. The readings given for the names are the translator's and are not certain.
[^c2s3-5]: Tr. 雄 (*o*), "male".
[^c2s3-6]: Tr. The pivot word uses one sequence of sounds in two senses at once; the pillow word is a fixed epithet attached to a following word; the *orikuri* places the syllables of a word at the head of each line; the *kaibun* reads the same backwards as forwards.
[^c2s3-7]: Tr. "Nihon shi no ōin" ("Rhyme in Japanese Poetry") is Kuki's own study, in the Iwanami series of lectures on Japanese literature.
[^c2s3-8]: Tr. *Momoshiki no* ("of the hundred stones") qualifies *ōmiyadokoro*, "the great palace"; *kusamakura* ("grass pillow") qualifies *tabiyadori*, "lodging on a journey".
[^c2s3-9]: Tr. *Inafune* ("rice boat") leads into *ina*, "no"; *u no hana* (the deutzia flower) leads into *uki*, "sorrowful"; *tamadare* ("jewelled blind") goes with *o* in the sense of 緒, the cord on which the jewels are strung, whilst 越 gives the place name Ochi (越智); *azusayumi* ("catalpa bow") goes with *haru*, to string a bow.
[^c2s3-10]: Tr. The two lines are by the Tang poet Liu Xiyi.
[^c2s3-11]: Tr. A song from the *Kojiki*. Each line ends on the vowel *i*.
[^c2s3-12]: Tr. A poem by Ariwara no Narihira. Kuki marks the first syllable of each line, *ka*, *ki*, *tsu*, *ha*, *ta*, with emphasis circles; together they spell *kakitsubata*, the iris.
[^c2s3-13]: Tr. A traditional New Year verse.
[^c2s3-14]: Tr. Terada Torahiko (1878-1935), Japanese physicist and essayist; *Mangekyō* means "Kaleidoscope". The romanisations are given as Kuki prints them.
[^c2s3-15]: Tr. The word play turns on *ōkami*, "wolf", heard as *ō-kami*, "great *kami*".
[^c2s3-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 「百敷の、大宮どころ」「草枕、旅やどりせす」. Our translation: "of the hundred stones, the site of the great palace"; "grass for pillow, he takes lodging on the journey".
[^c2s3-v2]: Tr. Kuki's text: 「稻舟の、いなにはあらず」「うの花の、うきことあれや」. Our translation: "rice boat, it is not 'no'"; "deutzia flower, is there something sorrowful?"
[^c2s3-v3]: Tr. Kuki's text: 「玉垂の、を(緒、越)智の大野の」「梓弓、はる(張、春)の山べを」. Our translation: "jewelled blind, of the great moor of Ochi" (*o*, cord, crossing); "catalpa bow, the hillside in spring" (*haru*, stringing, spring).
[^c2s3-v4]: Tr. Kuki's text: 「洛陽城東桃李花」「飛來飛去落誰家」. Our translation: "East of the city of Luoyang, the peach and plum blossom; / flying here, flying there, into whose house does it fall?"
[^c2s3-v5]: Tr. Kuki's text: 「奧つ鳥(i)鴨着く島に(i)我が率寢し(i)妹は忘れじ(i)世のことごとに(i)」. Our translation: "The birds of the offing, / to the island where the wild ducks come, / the girl I lay with / I will not forget, / all the days of my life."
[^c2s3-v6]: Tr. Kuki's text: 「*か*らごろも*き*つつなれにし*つ*ましあれば*は*るばるきぬる*た*びをしぞおもふ」. Our translation: "I have a wife, familiar as a Chinese robe long worn, / and so I think of the journey that has brought me so far."
[^c2s3-v7]: Tr. Kuki's text: 「なかきよのとおのねぶりのみなめざめなみのりぶねのおとのよきかな」. Our translation: "In the long night, all wake from distant sleep; how pleasant the sound of the ship riding the waves."

### 4. Causality and purposiveness

Leibniz's so-called law of sufficient reason contains both the "ground of knowledge" and the "ground of reality",[^c2s4-1] and in this sense it also carries the meaning of the law of causality. This is clear from the words of his fifth letter to Clarke:[^c2s4-2] "This principle is the principle that requires a sufficient reason in order that a thing exist, an event occur, a truth arise" (Leibniz, *Opera philosophica*, ed. Erdmann, p. 778). There are, admittedly, theorists who hold that the distinction between the ground of knowledge (reason) and the ground of reality (cause) is a lasting acquisition for epistemology, [p. 66] [コマ 40] and against them Émile Meyerson[^c2s4-3] replies: "Perhaps that is so. But our claim is that the essence of reasoning consists in more or less consciously setting this distinction aside" (É. Meyerson, *Du Cheminement de la Pensée* I, 1931, p. 55). In any case, insofar as the law of ground is reduced to the law of identity, there should be no objection to thinking that the law of causality is reduced to the law of identity as well. As Meyerson puts it, "the law of causality is nothing other than the law of identity applied to the existence of things in time" (É. Meyerson, *Identité et Réalité*, 3. éd., p. 38). What we call the cause of some event is, that is, something identical that is found within another event. When water arises as the result of the combination of hydrogen and oxygen, this means that the elements, hydrogen and oxygen, maintain themselves identically within the compound. The causal relation consequently takes as its ideal the possibility of being expressed as an equation, and this is the meaning of "cause equals effect" (*causa aequat effectum*). Causality too, in other words, can be reduced to identity. And if the causal relation is nothing other than a relation of identity, the law of causality should possess the necessity that belongs to the law of identity. We think that if hydrogen [p. 67] and oxygen are combined they necessarily become water, so that a necessary relation exists between the combination of hydrogen and oxygen and the water. For hydrogen and oxygen maintain themselves identically within the water. There is something identical there, and consequently there is necessity there.

The relation of purpose and means can, further, be seen as a kind of causal relation in the broad sense. It is in this sense that we have the concept of the "final cause" (*causa finalis*) over against the "efficient cause" (*causa efficiens*). Aristotle, that is, regarded both "the beginning of motion" (ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς κινήσεως) and "that for the sake of which" (τὸ οὗ ἕνεκα) as "causes" (αἰτία) that answer the question "why" (διὰ τί) (Aristoteles, *Physica*, II, 3; *Metaphysica*, Δ, 2). Kant likewise speaks of "two kinds of causality", calling the causal relation in the narrow sense "real causal connection" and the relation of purpose and means "ideal causal connection" or "causal combination through concepts of reason" (Kant, *Kritik der Urteilskraft*, § 65). In the causal relation and in the relation of purpose and means, however, the relation runs in opposite directions. What comes later as result is thought first as purpose, and what comes first as cause is thought later as means. Because the purpose acts as a cause and produces the means as its result, what was originally result becomes cause, and [p. 68] [コマ 41] what was originally cause becomes result. Renouvier[^c2s4-4] says accordingly that "the means is a kind of result peculiar to the cause called a purpose" (Renouvier, *Traité de Logique générale*, 1912, II, p. 164), and so, to borrow the words of Hamelin,[^c2s4-5] purposiveness is "determination by the future" (Hamelin, *Essai sur les éléments principaux de la représentation*, 2e éd., p. 332). The future ought to lack actuality, and it can take on actuality and act as a cause only through the fact of the temporal anticipation of consciousness. Purposiveness is consequently, in the strict sense, a concept whose validity is confined to the range of consciousness. If we wish to acknowledge this concept as a constitutive principle in the natural world too, beyond the range of consciousness, there is probably no way of doing so except through the mediation of a concept of the unconscious understood as what Bergson calls "annulled consciousness" (*conscience annulée*) (Bergson, *L'Évolution créatrice*, 25 éd., p. 156). There is a way of thinking that starts from the fact that conscious acts are changed by habit into unconscious reflex movements, and that sees the natural world as the limit at which consciousness has become unconscious through habit. Habit is, so to speak, the differential continuity by which will passes over into nature. "The history of habit represents the return of freedom to nature, or rather the invasion of the domain of freedom by natural spontaneity," and "thus [p. 69] perhaps the rule of knowledge and foresight appears in the domain of nature," as Ravaisson[^c2s4-6] says (Ravaisson, *De l'habitude*, nouvelle éd. 1927, p. 62, 16). Kant explains that genius carries out artistic production half unconsciously, "as nature" (Kant, *Kritik der Urteilskraft*, § 46), and his theory of genius is precisely the bond that joins his aesthetics to his philosophy of nature. Whilst Kant permits the application of purposiveness in the natural world only as a regulative principle, he nonetheless hints at the concept that would have to be assumed if it were to be acknowledged as a constitutive principle, namely the concept of the unconscious. In any case, the relation of purpose and means is an inverted causal relation.

Now, the relation of purpose and means takes the form "for the sake of A, B must be done", and in doing so shows that a necessary relation holds between purpose and means. This necessity comes, in the end, from the causal relation. The relation of purpose and means, "for the sake of A, B must be done", presupposes the relation of cause and effect, "if B is done, A necessarily arises", and this is why necessity is present in it. In general, the realisation of a purpose requires the causal relation. The purpose has to be brought about as the result of some event, and for that the cause has to be taken up as means; and between cause and effect there is a necessary relation. [p. 70] [コマ 42] When a definite result is willed as purpose, it is therefore necessarily required that a definite cause be willed as means. We might, of course, say that the necessity of the causal relation and the necessity of the relation of purpose and means differ in a way that can be expressed by "must" (*Müssen*) and "ought" (*Sollen*), but both are alike in being necessity. When Aristotle, among the three meanings of necessity he lists, speaks of "that without which the good is not" (τὸ οὗ οὐκ ἄνευ τὸ εὖ) (Aristoteles, *Metaphysica*, Δ. 7. 1072b),[^c2s4-7] he means necessity from the teleological standpoint. And when Hegel says that "this pure interplay with itself is necessity unveiled, or posited", and that "the truth of necessity is therefore freedom", he too is speaking of the necessity that belongs to the relation of purpose and means (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, §§ 157, 158). Nicolai Hartmann[^c2s4-8] says the following of the necessity of the ought in the narrow sense, that is, of the ethical absolute ought: "In the essence of the ought there lie urging, tendency, compulsion. That compulsion holds even when the object of the compulsion is something that cannot be realised. Modally speaking, it can mean nothing other than the necessity of the object. And yet that necessity [p. 71] is one in which the possibility of the object's realisation is lacking. The peculiarity of such ethical necessity does not lie in its meaning, modally, something new of its own, for example, ultimately, a new Modality. The difference of ethical necessity from theoretical, ontological necessity lies solely in the point that this necessity, without regard to possibility, that is, without regard to the real conditions of the actuality of the object, posits that object as necessary" (N. Hartmann, *Logische und ontologische Wirklichkeit*, Kantstudien XX, S. 21). In short, causality and purposiveness both possess necessity on the basis of identity.

[^c2s4-1]: Tr. 認識理由 and 實在理由, the scholastic *ratio cognoscendi* (the ground by which something is known) and *ratio essendi* (the ground by which something is). In the parentheses that follow, Kuki glosses the first as 理由 (reason, ground) and the second as 原因 (cause).
[^c2s4-2]: Tr. Samuel Clarke (1675-1729), English theologian and Newtonian, whose correspondence with Leibniz (1715-1716) ended with Leibniz's fifth letter.
[^c2s4-3]: Tr. Émile Meyerson (1859-1933), French philosopher of science, who argued that scientific explanation seeks identity through time.
[^c2s4-4]: Tr. Charles Renouvier (1815-1903), French neo-critical philosopher.
[^c2s4-5]: Tr. Octave Hamelin (1856-1907), French idealist philosopher, Renouvier's follower.
[^c2s4-6]: Tr. Félix Ravaisson (1813-1900), French spiritualist philosopher, author of *De l'habitude* (1838).
[^c2s4-7]: Tr. The passage on the three senses of necessity is *Metaphysics* Λ 7, 1072b. The 1948 printing reads Δ.
[^c2s4-8]: Tr. Nicolai Hartmann (1882-1950), German philosopher of ontology and ethics.

### 5. Causal contingency and teleological contingency

Contingency, as we have seen, is the negation of necessity, and so over against causal necessity and teleological necessity there should be two contingencies, each the negation of one of them: *causal contingency* and *teleological contingency*. Causal contingency is the contingency that arises through the lack of causality, and teleological [p. 72] [コマ 43] contingency is the contingency that arises through the lack of purposiveness. I have already said that these two, taken together with the contingency of ground, are to be considered the three modes of hypothetical contingency (see pp. 49-50).

Among the Japanese words that express chance, *yukurinaku*, *hashinakumo* and *futo* each contain a negative word as one of their moments, and all of them are formed on the basis of a negation, whether of groundedness, of causality or of purposiveness.[^c2s5-1] *Yukurinaku* means "without *en*", that is, "without *en'yu*", and is therefore probably the negation of groundedness. The *hashi* of *hashinakumo* means a head or a beginning, the end of a thread, so that the word may be taken to negate causality, in the sense of "without cause". *Futo* may be written with characters chosen for their sound, but insofar as it can be thought to be related to *fui to*, there should be no objection to taking it as the negation of purposiveness. The Greek αὐτόματον likewise comes from αὐτό, "of itself", and μάτην, "without reason", and so arises on the same foundation as these Japanese words.[^c2s5-2] *Futo* may also be related to the onomatopoeic *futto*. *Futto*, *hyotto*, *hyokkori*, *pokkuri* and similar words suggest chance; *futto*, *hyotto* and *hyokkori* are probably based on the sound of breath or of wind, and *pokkuri* on the sound of something rising from under water to the surface.[^c2s5-3] [p. 73] As auditory symbols of contingency in the sense of hypothetical contingency, that is, of contingency as the negation of groundedness, causality and purposiveness, these words are extremely apt. If we wished, incidentally, to give a visual symbol of contingency, we could say with La Mettrie[^c2s5-4] "like those mushrooms that spring up from one day to the next" (*semblable à ces champignons qui paraissent d'un jour à l'autre*) (La Mettrie, *L'Homme machine*, Paris, 1865, p. 98). In Naruse Mukyoku's "Dialogue on Chance"[^c2s5-5] the metaphor of "a frog that jumped out from under the veranda" is used (*Dialogue on Chance*, pp. 433, 436). In Greece, when chance, that is, Tychē (τύχη), was worshipped as a goddess, she was given as her attributes a rolling "ball" or "wheel", or a fluttering "feather". The visual symbol of contingency as one of the modal categories is not, moreover, a matter that symbolic logic can regard with complete indifference. When I later choose )( as the sign of contingency, this is nothing other than a symbolisation of contingency as "the Encounter of two independent elements" (獨立なる二元の邂逅) (see p. 196).[^c2s5-6] C. I. Lewis[^c2s5-7] (C. I. Lewis, *A Survey of Symbolic Logic*, 1918, p. 293, 312) (see p. 193) calls the property common to apodicticity (see p. 198), that is, to impossibility (~) and necessity (~-), "strict implication" (*Strict Implication*), and the property common to assertoricity, that is, to truth ( ) and falsity (-), "material implication" (*Material Implication*); [p. 74] [コマ 44] and he calls the property common to problematicity, that is, to possibility (-~) and contingency (-~-), "consistency" (*Consistency*). To express "strict implication" he uses the sign ⥽, to express "material implication" the sign ⊃, and to express "consistency" the sign ○.[^c2s5-8] Consistency means the property by which A and B can stand together, and refers to the case in which it is possible that something is A and also possible that it is not A, that is, in which A is contingent. p ○ q expresses -~p (p is possible) or -~-p (it is possible that not p, which is to say that p is contingent).[^c2s5-9] That the relation between p and q is represented by ○ shows that whether things will turn out p or q is left open as a problem, undetermined. Whether the "ball" symbolised by ○ will roll towards p or towards q is wholly uncertain. Its character is entirely different from that of "material implication", expressed by the hammer shape ⊃, or of "strict implication", expressed by the anchor shape ⥽. That ○ has become in this way the symbol of problematicity in symbolic logic rests on exactly the same psychology as the Greeks' choice of the "ball" as an attribute of the goddess Tychē.

Of the three modes of hypothetical contingency, the contingency of ground concerns the so-called "ground of knowledge", [p. 75] and so belonged purely to the range of logic. Causal contingency and teleological contingency, by contrast, concern the "ground of reality", and so they leave the abstract range of logic behind and arise in the empirical domain of the philosophy of nature and the philosophy of mind. We can therefore call causal contingency and teleological contingency together *empirical contingency*.

In general, the mechanistic view of the world, in its thoroughgoing form, recognises nothing but causal necessity, and consequently leaves no room for the existence of causal contingency. It does, however, deny teleological necessity, and as a result it acknowledges teleological contingency. The world view of natural science represents this tendency. When the teleological view takes a thoroughgoing form, on the other hand, it tries to explain everything by teleological necessity alone, so that teleological contingency does not exist; in its place, as a result of the negation of causal necessity, causal contingency can exist. When Christian theology explains everything by the will of God, its recognition of the existence of miracles, which signify causal contingency, rests on this relation. In this sense causal necessity combines readily with teleological contingency, and teleological necessity combines readily with causal contingency. Paradoxical expressions such as "the necessity of chance" rest on the possibility of combinations of this kind. [p. 76] [コマ 45] When Plato speaks of "by chance (τύχη), out of necessity (ἀνάγκη)" (Platon, *Leges*, X, 889), when Leibniz speaks of "trying to attribute everything to the necessity of matter or to some chance" (Leibniz, *Discours de métaphysique*, § 19), and when Engels speaks of "the necessity immanent in chance" (*Dialectics of Nature*, vol. 2, Iwanami Bunko, p. 28), they refer to the combination of causal necessity and teleological contingency. When Thomas Aquinas says that "God willed that it should happen contingently" (Thomas Aquinas, *Summa theologiae* I. 19. 8c), or that "nothing is so contingent that it does not have in itself something necessary" (Ib., I. 86. 3c), he is thinking, by contrast, of the combination of teleological necessity and causal contingency. Thomas's so-called "law of necessity or contingency" (*lex necessitatis vel contingentiae*) advocates the combination of causal contingency as freedom with teleological necessity as providence. And when Lange[^c2s5-10] writes in his *History of Materialism* that "there is nothing else that contradicts itself so completely as chance and necessity, and nevertheless there is nothing else that is so often confused as the two" (Lange, *Geschichte des Materialismus*, I. S. 13), this too is because the combination of causal necessity with teleological contingency on the one side, and of teleological necessity with causal contingency [p. 77] on the other, so readily comes about. Let us provisionally call this way of combining "heterogeneous combination". Heterogeneous combination is not, however, the only possible way of combining. Causal necessity and teleological necessity may also combine to form a philosophy that emphasises necessity, and causal contingency and teleological contingency may combine to constitute a philosophy that stresses contingency. The Stoics taught a thoroughgoing determinism that starts from teleological necessity and arrives at causal necessity (see p. 292), and Epicurus advocated a univocal indeterminism that sets out from causal contingency and ends in teleological contingency (see pp. 115-116, 127). This way of combining can be called "homogeneous combination".

![Figure 1](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-01-p077.png)

**Figure 1.** Causal and teleological necessity and contingency: homogeneous combination runs vertically, heterogeneous combination horizontally (1948, p. 77). Labels: 因果的必然 causal necessity; 目的々偶然 teleological contingency; 目的々必然 teleological necessity; 因果的偶然 causal contingency; 必然性 necessity; 偶然性 contingency; 因果性 causality; 目的性 purposiveness. Corner labels are set right to left.

Let us express these relations by a figure like the one above 〔Figure 1〕.[^c2s5-11] In this figure, homogeneous combination (of causal necessity with teleological necessity, and of causal contingency with teleological contingency) [p. 78] [コマ 46] runs in the vertical direction, and heterogeneous combination (of causal necessity with teleological contingency, and of teleological necessity with causal contingency) runs in the horizontal direction.

In Greece, causal contingency and teleological contingency were originally both expressed by the one word τύχη. This is why Democritus, attacking causal contingency from the standpoint of the mechanistic view, and Plato, refuting teleological contingency from the standpoint of the teleological view, both use the same word, τύχη. When Democritus says that "human beings have made the idol of chance (τύχη) as an excuse for their own perplexity" (Diels, *Fragmente der Vorsokratiker* II. Demokritos Fr. 119), or that "chance (τύχη) is fond of giving, but cannot be relied upon; nature (φύσις), by contrast, is sure" (Ib., Fr. 176), the chance he means should be understood as causal contingency, opposed to nature, that is, opposed to causal necessity. When Plato, on the other hand, criticises the atomists by saying that "according to what they say, it seems that nature (φύσις) and chance (τύχη) made the greatest and most beautiful things, and art made the lesser" (Platon, *Leges*, X, 889), or "not through mind, nor through any god, nor through art, but through nature (φύσις) and chance (τύχη)" (Ib.), the chance he means [p. 79] is properly understood as teleological contingency in heterogeneous combination with the causal necessity called nature.

Leibniz distinguished, within chance, between *contingence* and *hasard* (Leibniz, *Opera philosophica*, ed. Erdmann, p. 763). Since *contingence* is treated as belonging to one group with freedom and spontaneity, there is no objection to regarding it as *causal contingency* in heterogeneous combination with teleological necessity; and since *hasard* is treated as of the same kind as compulsion and absolute necessity, it may be regarded as *teleological contingency* in heterogeneous combination with causal necessity. In modern times Boutroux,[^c2s5-12] following Leibniz, likewise calls causal contingency *contingence* and teleological contingency *hasard*. Where, for example, he takes the standpoint of the opponents of his own theory and raises the question whether causal contingency is not led into teleological contingency, he asks: "If the principle of *contingence* is not *hasard*, what in the world can it be?" (Boutroux, *De la contingence des lois de la nature*, 9e éd., p. 140). And in reply he says: "If *contingence* did not to some degree reign in the series of determining causes, *hasard* would reign in the series of final causes" (Ib., p. 143).

[p. 80] [コマ 47] This usage is not, however, generally followed. Hamelin, for example, calls causal contingency and teleological contingency taken together *hasard*, and then calls causal contingency "chance through the non-existence of causality" (*hasard par néant de causalité*) and teleological contingency "chance through the non-existence of purposiveness" (*hasard par néant de finalité*) (Hamelin, *Essai sur les éléments principaux de la représentation*, 2e éd., p. 324, 329). Bergson similarly calls both "the absence of efficient cause" (*absence de cause efficiente*) and "the absence of final cause" (*absence de cause finale*) *hasard* (Bergson, *L'Évolution créatrice*, 25 éd., p. 254-255). Windelband[^c2s5-13] too calls the two together *Zufall*, and uses the terms *kausaler Zufall* and *teleologischer Zufall* for each (Windelband, *Die Lehren vom Zufall*, S. 65).

The distinction between causal contingency and teleological contingency is very important, yet, as Bergson himself points out, it displays "a peculiar oscillation of the mind" (*le singulier ballottement de l'esprit*), passing from the one to the other (l. c. p. 255). Where the distinction between the two is not marked in language, it is therefore especially important to recognise it with great care. Lange, for example, says: "Against the teleologists, in order to escape the fetters of purpose, [p. 81] we assert the contingency of events. But as soon as the law of sufficient reason becomes the problem, we at once give up that same contingency again" (Lange, *Geschichte d. Materialismus*, I, S. 14). He speaks of "the same contingency" (*dieselbe Zufälligkeit*), but in fact it is not the same: the former is teleological contingency and the latter causal contingency. Jaspers,[^c2s5-14] to take another example, says: "Human beings seek to escape alternately from the one by means of the other. By the thought of necessity they try to escape from arbitrary chance, and by the thought of the possibility and opportunity of chance they try to escape from merciless necessity" (Jaspers, *Philosophie*, II, S. 217). What he means is that human beings try to escape from teleological contingency by the thought of teleological necessity, and from causal necessity by the thought of causal contingency. In other words, by the thought of the heterogeneous combination of teleological necessity with causal contingency, they try to escape from the threat of the heterogeneous combination of causal necessity with teleological contingency.

As I have already said, causal contingency arises through the negation of causality, and teleological contingency through the negation of purposiveness. In causal contingency there is the case in which, with regard to a single event, we witness *negatively* the non-existence of causality, and the case in which, between two or more events, [p. 82] [コマ 48] we witness *positively* the existence of a relation other than causality; in either case it is equally the negation of causality. Teleological contingency likewise includes the case in which, with regard to a single event, we grasp *negatively* the non-existence of purpose, and the case in which, between two or more events, we grasp *positively* the existence of a relation other than purpose, and both cases have in common the negation of purposiveness. There exist, in short, four kinds: negative causal contingency, positive causal contingency, negative teleological contingency and positive teleological contingency. If we add the two kinds already treated, negative contingency of ground and positive contingency of ground, six kinds of contingency are to be considered within hypothetical contingency.

![Figure 2](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-02-p082.png)

**Figure 2.** The six kinds of hypothetical contingency (1948, p. 82). Labels: 假說的偶然 hypothetical contingency; 理由的偶然 contingency of ground; 經驗的偶然 empirical contingency; 目的々偶然 teleological contingency; 因果的偶然 causal contingency; 積極的 positive; 消極的 negative.

[p. 83] In elucidating contingency, we try to proceed step by step from the subjective towards the objective. We began by treating categorical contingency, which has its basis in the logical structure of the general concept and its marks, and then treated the contingency of ground, which likewise belongs to the purely logical dimension. Now that we leave the logical domain and turn to the elucidation of empirical contingency, the natural order must be to begin with teleological contingency, which is comparatively subjective, and to move on afterwards to causal contingency.

Encounter is 邂逅 (*kaikō*), the word of Kuki's definition of contingency (p. 148). 遭遇 (*sōgū*), which he also uses, and which glosses Cournot's *rencontre* (pp. 10, 179), is "encounter" in lower case (see the Translator's Note); 二元 (*nigen*) is rendered "two elements".

Kuki's diagonal labels read 因果性 (causality) from causal necessity to causal contingency and 目的性 (purposiveness) from teleological contingency to teleological necessity; the side labels are 必然性 (necessity) and 偶然性 (contingency).
