### 6. Negative teleological contingency

Negative teleological contingency grasps the non-existence of purpose negatively, and it divides further into two cases: contingency as *purposelessness* and contingency as *counter-purposiveness*. Contingency as purposelessness is the case in which purposiveness is simply negated. Contingency as counter-purposiveness is the case in which the purpose that ought to be realised is affirmed, and at the same time its non-realisation is witnessed in a particular instance.

[p. 84] [コマ 49] To the first case belongs, for example, the assertion of teleological contingency for the universe as a whole, as the other half of a mechanistic determinism. Here the idea of teleological contingency, in heterogeneous combination with causal necessity, is applied across the whole of existence. We find it, for instance, in La Mettrie's *L'Homme machine*: "Man has perhaps been thrown *by chance* onto some point or other of the surface of the earth. ... Now the pantheist asks a question. Is chance, then, so great a geometer that it varies at its own pleasure, in this way, the many works of which chance is thought to be the author, and yet such variety cannot prevent them from attaining the same end? ... This is certainly all the most favourable things that can be said for the existence of a God. ... But the atheist can oppose to it things equally strong and absolutely contrary. For if we listen further to what the natural scientists say, they will say this. The same cause that, in the hands of the chemist, through various chance mixtures, made the first mirror, made, in the hands of nature, clear water, and this serves merely a nameless shepherdess. ... That mirrors and water are not made so that people can reflect their figures in them [p. 85] is no different from all other bodies with smooth surfaces that have the same property. The eye is in fact a kind of wall mirror in which the soul can view the figures of objects just as they are presented by those bodies, but it has not been proved that this organ was in fact made specially for this viewing, nor has it been proved that it was specially placed in the eye socket. ... That the eye sees is only because it is organised as it now is and placed in such a place as it now is, and once we suppose, for the production and development of the human body, the same laws of motion that nature follows, it cannot be that this marvellous organ should have another organisation and be placed in another place" (La Mettrie, *L'Homme machine*, trans. Sugi Toshio, Iwanami Bunko, pp. 100-104).[^c2s6-1] What is called chance here is the first case of negative teleological contingency: it means contingency as purposelessness, and simply negates purposiveness. The same meaning of contingency appears in the following two examples, though their standpoint is, of course, the opposite of La Mettrie's, one that denies teleological contingency. Nishida[^c2s6-2] writes: "To think of something like a soul of plants, as Fechner does,[^c2s6-3] may be fanciful. But unless we think, in some sense, [p. 86] [コマ 50] of something conscious, for example something like an unconscious consciousness, we can only think of a living thing as a *chance* combination of physical forces, and cannot think of anything such as its own existence" (Nishida Kitarō, *Fundamental Problems of Philosophy*, p. 134). And Nagai Hisomu[^c2s6-4] writes: "The skin in every part is something that does not develop sufficiently until about three months after birth have passed, but the tactile sense of the skin and mucous membrane in the region of the lips alone is an exception, and already shows its function one day after birth. That is, as soon as an infant is born it searches for the mother's breast, which is to supply the sole nourishment of life, and thereby, in the place important for maintaining its own life, the sense of touch shows an early and excellent development. This is truly not *chance*" (Nagai Hisomu, *The Boundary between Biology and Philosophy*, p. 231).

The second case is negative teleological contingency in the sense of contingency as counter-purposiveness, that is, the case in which a fact contrary to a purpose that ought to be realised is witnessed. A person with severe intellectual disability is one example.[^c2s6-5] If the existence of thinking activity signifies, for a human being, a purposiveness that ought to be realised, then such a person, in whom this means the non-existence of that thinking activity, comes to be thought of as something contingent. The phenomenon of a flower becoming double can likewise be seen as teleological contingency. If the function of the flower is taken to be the governing of the plant's reproduction, then the change of stamens into petals [p. 87] runs contrary to that purpose, and so in botany the double flower is regarded as a malformation, that is, as a contingent phenomenon. Again, if having three leaves is regarded as one of the purposes that clover ought to realise, the four-leaved clover lacks the realisation of that purpose and is therefore something contingent. For a snake with one body, realising one head is purposive, and so a snake with two heads on one body is a counter-purposive, which is to say a contingent, existence. Aristotle called this kind of teleological contingency "contrary to nature" (παρὰ φύσιν) (Aristoteles, *Physica*, II, 6, 197b), and Hegel attributed it to "the impotence of nature" (*Ohnmacht der Natur*) (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 250). Behind the declaration that something is contrary to nature stands the teleological view that "nature makes nothing without reason (μάτην)" (Aristoteles, *De caelo*, I. 4, 271a). And to judge nature impotent, one must already have imposed on nature, as the purpose for the completion of the particular, the type understood as a conceptual determination.

This kind of "contrary to nature" sometimes coincides with what, within categorical contingency, we called in particular exceptional contingency (see Chapter 1, section 5). This second mode of negative teleological contingency arises when the general concept in categoricity is regarded as a purpose and the realisation of that purpose is demanded. [p. 88] [コマ 51] Plato's Idea, as "the Idea of the Good", is valid in the character of an ought, and it is for this reason that actuality, in presenting exceptions "contrary to nature", exposes its own "impotence". Categoricity and purposiveness stand, after all, in an inseparable inner relation. That the general concept can be seen as something valid in itself as a purpose is clear in the light of its universality as a universality of reflective judgement, a universality set as a task. Boutroux too says: "The logical order, that is, the operation that subordinates facts to concepts, perhaps conceals the spontaneous activity of an inner reason or of final causes. The concept would be no more than the logical sign of the final cause. Thus individuals would have their reason for existence in the species" (Boutroux, *De la contingence des lois de la nature*, 9 éd., p. 168).

[^c2s6-1]: Tr. Kuki quotes La Mettrie in the Japanese translation by Sugi Toshio (杉捷夫), a scholar of French literature; the passage is translated here from Kuki's Japanese.
[^c2s6-2]: Tr. Nishida Kitarō (1870-1945), founder of the Kyoto School and Kuki's senior colleague at Kyoto Imperial University. *Fundamental Problems of Philosophy* renders 哲學の根本問題 (*Tetsugaku no konpon mondai*, 1933).
[^c2s6-3]: Tr. Gustav Theodor Fechner (1801-1887), German physicist and founder of psychophysics, who attributed a soul to plants in *Nanna, oder über das Seelenleben der Pflanzen* (1848).
[^c2s6-4]: Tr. Nagai Hisomu (1876-1957), Japanese physiologist at Tokyo Imperial University. *The Boundary between Biology and Philosophy* renders 生物學と哲學との境 (*Seibutsugaku to tetsugaku to no sakai*).
[^c2s6-5]: Tr. Kuki's 白痴 (*hakuchi*) is a period term, rendered here, as in Chapter 1, by "severe intellectual disability".

### 7. Positive teleological contingency

Positive teleological contingency is the case in which we positively witness, between two or more events, the existence of some relation other than purpose.[^c2s7-1] A case of this kind would be digging [p. 89] a hole in order to plant a tree and finding treasure coming up out of the ground. Aristotle gives this example in Book V of the *Metaphysics* (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Δ 30, 1025a), and Boethius later commented on it (Boethius, De interpretatione, Ed. II. lib. III. Migne p. 491, 506), and ever since it has often been cited as an example of chance.[^c2s7-2] Planting the tree was the purpose, and obtaining treasure was not included in that purpose, which is why we call the finding of the treasure chance. On the one side there is the series of actions in which the gardener digs the ground and plants the tree, and on the other the series of actions in which a thief, or someone of the kind, hid treasure in the ground, and between these two series, each independent of the other, some positive relation other than purposiveness has been established. Or again, we say that Brand, the seventeenth-century chemist, discovered phosphorus by chance when, trying in the manner of alchemy to obtain a substance that would turn silver into gold, he evaporated urine, heated the residue strongly and obtained as a result an element that gives off light.[^c2s7-3] Here too we speak of chance because obtaining phosphorus was not included in the purpose. In this case, too, there is on the one side a series of actions carrying out an alchemical design and on the other a chemical series in which phosphorus was liberated from phosphate, and between these two series a relation of another kind, one that is not purposiveness, has been positively established.

[p. 90] [コマ 52] In general, the greater part of what is called chance in everyday life is this positive teleological contingency. Let me give two or three examples. "On the fifteenth of December, *by chance*, Major-General Kondratenko, the enemy's commander of the land-front defence, was killed in action at the North Fort of East Jiguanshan. Kondratenko was, it seems, not only excellent in the tactics of fortress defence but also enjoyed very great trust, and it had long been rumoured that because Kondratenko was in charge the garrison could fight with confidence. We did not know whether or not he had come to the North Fort of East Jiguanshan on the fifteenth of December, but our twenty-eight-centimetre shell *by chance* struck there and he was killed" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 4 March 1935).[^c2s7-4] Our shells had the purpose of striking the enemy, but they did not take Major-General Kondratenko in particular as their target. That the shell was fired towards the North Fort of East Jiguanshan and that the major-general came to that fort on that day are two independent series, and this is why his death in action is called chance. A second example: "Mr Masuda Jirō, president of Shōwa Electric Power, was summoned to the Tokyo District Court on the morning of the twenty-sixth and, after a lengthy examination by Prosecutor Kanazawa, was further subjected to detention questioning by Preliminary Judge Yamaguchi, and at six in the afternoon, on a charge of official corruption, he was committed to Ichigaya Prison under compulsory measures prior to [p. 91] indictment. It is reported that he was first examined as a witness in the Shōden scandal, which the Metropolitan Police Board and the Public Prosecutor's Office had for some time been investigating, by Prosecutor Nakajima, in charge of the Shōden case, and that in the course of this examination his deep involvement in the decorations-buying affair was *by chance* uncovered by the same prosecutor, so that he was sent on anew to the examination room of Prosecutor Kanazawa, in charge of the decorations-buying affair" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 27 September 1929).[^c2s7-5] The prosecutor who first examined him had as his sole purpose the investigation of the Shōden case, and the discovery concerning the decorations-buying affair, falling wholly outside that purpose, is consequently called chance. The investigation of the Shōden case on the one side and the investigation of the decorations-buying affair on the other formed two entirely independent teleological series, and between these two series a new positive relation was established that neither had aimed at in any way. To give one more example: on 10 September 1934 the entertainment broadcasts in the radio programme of the Tokyo Broadcasting Station were all relayed from the Osaka Broadcasting Station, and that day's newspaper discussed this as "this programme born of chance". The paper first states the facts: "Today, beginning with the afternoon 'Solo Song and Orchestral Music', and including the evening 'Gidayū' and 'Rakugo', the entertainment broadcasts are all relayed from Osaka, so that it takes the form of 'AK literary and arts department closed today'. The AK authorities say that this programme was arranged entirely *by chance*, [p. 92] [コマ 53] but that since the programme arrangement committee decided it thus, nothing can be done about it."[^c2s7-6] Next it explains how this came about: "Today's entertainment broadcasting is, for AK, a programme arrangement wholly without precedent. Why did such an irregular thing come about? Since the reorganisation of the Broadcasting Corporation, the programmes of each station have been decided, a month's worth at a time and half a month in advance, at a programme arrangement meeting held once a month. When today's programme was decided, the gidayū from eight in the evening was scheduled as a relay from the Bunrakuza, and since the broadcasting time it required was uncertain, the next entertainment item was entrusted to BK. Further, since light music was wanted for the afternoon, 'Solo Song and Orchestral Music' was put in, and this, it is said, *by chance* originated from BK. The arrangement committee says, in other words, that it does consider the originating station, but because it considers chiefly the type of broadcast, this result came about. Voices of criticism have nonetheless been raised that this is a blunder on the part of the programme arrangement meeting." The complaint, that is, was that "for a regional station it might be another matter, but this is as if AK had entirely lost face as the parent station", and the secretary-general of the programme arrangement meeting excuses himself as follows: "That the entertainment all ended up originating from BK was entirely a result of *chance*, and of course we wish to take care [p. 93] that such a thing does not happen again" (*Tōkyō Asahi Shinbun*, 10 September 1934). According to this explanation, that the evening entertainment broadcasts, "Gidayū" and "Rakugo", both came from BK was a matter entrusted to BK because the broadcasting time they required was uncertain, and there is no chance whatever in it. What is called chance is that the afternoon entertainment broadcast, "Solo Song and Orchestral Music", also came from BK. Why is this chance? Because, with regard to the afternoon entertainment broadcast, the arrangement committee had taken only the type of broadcast into the series of purpose, and adopting something that originated from BK was not included in the purpose. The teleological series that decided that the evening entertainment programme should come from BK and the teleological series that decided that the afternoon entertainment programme should be "Solo Song and Orchestral Music" were decisions of one and the same arrangement committee, yet they constituted two independent series, and for this reason an unexpected chance manifested itself.

[^c2s7-1]: Tr. Kuki writes 目的々 for 目的的, "teleological".
[^c2s7-2]: Tr. Boethius (c. 480-524), Roman philosopher and translator of Aristotle; the reference is to his second commentary on Aristotle's *De interpretatione*.
[^c2s7-3]: Tr. Hennig Brand (c. 1630-c. 1710), Hamburg alchemist, who isolated phosphorus in 1669. Kuki's 意圖 is rendered "design" to keep it apart from 故意, "intention".
[^c2s7-4]: Tr. Roman Kondratenko (1857-1904), Russian general in command of the land defences of Port Arthur, killed by a Japanese 28 cm howitzer shell during the siege in December 1904. East Jiguanshan (Japanese Higashi-keikanzan) was one of the fortified hills of Port Arthur. Kuki's newspaper dates are given in the Shōwa era; they are converted here.
[^c2s7-5]: Tr. The Shōden scandal (昭電疑獄) concerns Shōwa Electric Power. Kuki writes 買勳事件, "decorations-buying affair"; the 1929 scandal over the sale of state honours is usually called 賣勳事件.
[^c2s7-6]: Tr. AK and BK are the call-sign abbreviations of the Tokyo (JOAK) and Osaka (JOBK) stations of the Japan Broadcasting Corporation. *Gidayū* is the narrative chanting of the bunraku puppet theatre; *rakugo* is comic storytelling; the Bunrakuza was the puppet theatre in Osaka.

### 8. Purpose without purpose

What, then, do we positively witness in positive teleological contingency? [p. 94] [コマ 54] It is something that was not set up as a purpose in the case concerned, yet is nonetheless of a kind that could have been a purpose. In positive teleological contingency in particular, a kind of "purpose without purpose" usually casts a strong shadow. To borrow Bergson's words, it looks as though a "good genius" (un bon génie) or an "evil genius" (un mauvais génie) were at work (Bergson, L'Évolution créatrice, 25 éd., p. 254; Les deux sources de la morale et de la religion, 7 éd., p. 152-157).[^c2s8-1] The death in action of Major-General Kondratenko seems, to our army, like a purpose aimed at by a good genius, and to the Russian army like a purpose aimed at by an evil genius. In the example of the broadcast programmes, likewise, what happened can be thought of as the purposive act of an evil genius from the side of the Tokyo Broadcasting Station and as the purposive act of a good genius from the side of the Osaka Broadcasting Station. Of course, in negative teleological contingency too, insofar as the non-existence of a purpose is asserted, there is some "purpose without purpose", and it is over against this that the condition of being "without purpose", the non-existence of purpose, is specifically asserted. In that case, however, the whole emphasis falls on grasping the non-existence of purpose itself, and so long as this is so, teleological contingency appears in the form of negativity. In positive teleological contingency, by contrast, it is the existence of "purpose without purpose" that is positively emphasised [p. 95] and witnessed. In other words, within the two-membered structure "purposeless / purpose", negative teleological contingency lays particular emphasis on the first member, whilst positive teleological contingency places its stress rather on the second.[^c2s8-2]

Consider the following newspaper report: "Yamada Isuzu, star of the First Film Company, was invited by the captain of the *Isuzu* of the Invincible Fleet to visit the cruiser *Isuzu*, then in port at Osaka, on the nineteenth, and was moved by the hospitality she received from Captain Yamada and the young officers. On the twentieth she listened over the radio to the gallant scene of Captain Yamada of the *Isuzu* commanding the First Fleet's landing force at the naval review on the Osaka Castle East parade ground. That afternoon she sent Captain Yamada of the *Isuzu* a letter of thanks for his hospitality together with a signed copy of the commemorative photograph taken with him on board, and she has decided to hang an enlargement of this photograph in her home, so as long to commemorate this curious tie" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, Kyoto edition, 21 October 1934).[^c2s8-3] In this case the motive for appointing Captain Yamada to command the cruiser *Isuzu* was in no way aimed at combining Yamada with Isuzu. Yet the combination of Yamada and Isuzu produced the very name of the film actress Yamada Isuzu. This is no more than a teleological contingency, but it has an all too striking "purpose without purpose". Had [p. 96] [コマ 55] there been no actress called Yamada Isuzu, the contingency that Captain Yamada commands the *Isuzu* would have had almost no resonance at all. The existence of the actress Yamada Isuzu supplied, as it were, a sounding box, and the contingency gave forth a powerful sound. We can say, then, that the essence of positive teleological contingency lies in grasping "purpose without purpose" with a positive emphasis.

As a result of this emphasis on "purpose without purpose", chance comes to take on the appearance of not being chance, and so takes on an intentional structure of self-negation. "With the rise of the question of a silver loan for the purpose of relieving the price of silver in America, and with a faction at home responding to it, the situation reached a point at which nothing could be done about the wavering of the plan to reform the currency system through the import of silver. It would not necessarily be arbitrary to see the invitation, at Mr Rajchman's urging, of Mr Salter, head of the economic and financial section, and Mr Haas, head of the communications section, as arising from a deep concern to prevent a split in the Nanjing government and to keep the loan plan unified. That the fall of Mr Hu Hanmin, a sympathiser of the silver loan, came two days after Mr Haas entered the capital and two days before Mr Salter arrived was an event *by chance and yet not by chance*" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 12 March 1931).[^c2s8-4] In Mushanokōji Saneatsu's *His Sister* [p. 97] there is likewise the line, "I have a feeling that my going to your place that day *was not by chance*" (Act III).[^c2s8-5] Expressions such as "by chance and yet not by chance" or "it cannot be thought chance" are forms in which the second member of "purposeless / purpose" is emphasised so strongly that the first has almost lost its resonance.

The psychologist Henri Piéron, in his essay "Essay on chance: the psychology of a concept" (Henri Piéron, Essai sur le hasard: la psychologie d'un concept), lays particular stress on this aspect of contingency.[^c2s8-6] According to Piéron, if we are walking by a lake and there is a boat there, we do not especially call this chance. Only if at that moment we had a strong wish to go out rowing do we call it a "happy chance". Again, if a cat crosses our path as we walk along the road, we do not especially treat this as chance. But if a cyclist, at the very moment of swerving to avoid a car, runs into a cat and kills it, then, and only then, does he curse the "unhappy chance" that put the cat in the road. Piéron accordingly holds that "subjective interest" (intérêt subjectif) forms an important moment in chance, and he emphasises the "subjective side" (côté subjectif) or "human element" (élément humain) of the phenomenon of chance, a view of interest as the theory of a psychologist [p. 98] [コマ 56] (Revue de Métaphysique et de Morale, 1902, p. 688-691).

Also belonging to the psychological interpretation of chance is the idea that chance is that in which a minute difference in the cause brings about a large difference in the effect. Renan, for example, says that chance is "what does not have a moral cause proportioned to the effect" (ce qui n'a pas de cause morale proportionnée à l'effet) (Ernest Renan, L'Avenir de la Science, p. 24), and Poincaré too says of chance that "the difference in the cause is imperceptible, and the difference in the effect is for me of very great importance" (La différence dans la cause est imperceptible, et la différence dans l'effet est pour moi de la plus haute importance) (H. Poincaré, Science et Méthode, p. 71).[^c2s8-7] When we judge an effect to be large in comparison with its cause, the standard of that evaluation lies mainly in purposiveness, and consequently the so-called "grave effect" often turns out to be nothing other than a "purpose without purpose". This is clear from the examples of grave effects that the two give: Renan cites "Gustavus Adolphus struck by a cannonball at Lützen" (l. c. p. 494) and Poincaré "a terrible catastrophe" (l. c. p. 68).[^c2s8-8] It is for the same reason that Renan specifies a "moral" cause and Poincaré says "for me".

### 9. Intention and chance

[p. 99] When, in positive teleological contingency, the emphasis on the second member of "purposeless / purpose" reaches an extreme, there also arise cases in which, as a practical matter, it is hard to tell whether something is *chance* or *intention*. "The preliminary Anglo-Japanese negotiations between Ambassador Matsudaira and the British Prime Minister, Mr MacDonald, held a third meeting on the twenty-eighth, but . . . meanwhile, in America, in the preliminary Japanese-American negotiations being conducted between Mr Stimson and Ambassador Debuchi, Japan's ambassador to the United States, Mr Stimson too, like the British Prime Minister, has presented Ambassador Debuchi in writing with a proposed solution as his private plan, and, whether by *intention* or by *chance*, he appears, as Britain did, to ask for an explicit statement of the number of ships in place of Japan's demand for seventy per cent" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 2 December 1929).[^c2s9-1] In Natsume Sōseki's *Kokoro* we likewise find the words: "The mistress very seldom went out. Even on the rare occasions when she left the house, she never went off leaving the young lady and me alone together. Whether that too was *chance* or *intention*, I do not know" (Part III, 14) [p. 100] [コマ 57].[^c2s9-2]

In general, the distinction between intention and chance has decisive significance in the constitution of a crime, since it is what makes clear where legal responsibility lies. In the "Announcement to Kang" chapter of the *Book of Documents* there are these words: "The king said: Oh, Feng! Be reverent and clear in your punishments. If a man has committed a small crime which is no mischance but is carried through to the end, doing of himself what is unlawful, and doing it so, then though his crime is small he must be put to death. If a man has committed a great crime which is not carried through to the end but is only mischance and misfortune, happening by chance, and he has fully declared his guilt, then he is not to be put to death."[^c2s9-3][^c2s9-v1] Here 適 (*tamatama*), that is, chance, stands opposed to 終, that is, carrying something out with intention, and it is nothing other than teleological contingency. The contingency that becomes a problem for the law in particular is positive teleological contingency. What fully constitutes a crime is intention, in the sense of the will to the crime, that is, criminal intent. Article 38 of the Criminal Code states that "an act done without the intention of committing a crime is not punished", and so constitutes crime in the eminent sense only within the domain of teleological necessity.[^c2s9-4] So-called "negligence" is positive teleological contingency. One explanation of negligence runs: "Negligence has inattention as its core. In this respect it must be distinguished from the fact of chance (*force majeure*)" (Makino Eiichi, *Revised Japanese Criminal Law*, p. 178).[^c2s9-5] The concept of chance, however, is a higher concept that can include within its extension both a certain kind of negligence and *force majeure*, and there is no reason why chance must be interpreted in an especially narrow sense and identified [p. 101] with *force majeure*. The difference between negligence and *force majeure* is that in the former the chance could have been removed, whilst in the latter it could not. Seen quantitatively, the contingency in negligence is small and the contingency in *force majeure* is large, so that the difference between them lies in a difference in the degree of contingency (see pp. 212-213). Suppose a hunter's bullet misses the deer and hits a woodcutter nearby. There is, of course, no *force majeure* here, but there is inattention, and negligence is constituted precisely where, through inattention, the facts that make up the crime went unrecognised. Yet even if this is not *force majeure*, we cannot say that it is not chance. The teleological necessity of the hunter's activity of will connects the hunter with the deer, and that the bullet unexpectedly struck the woodcutter constitutes, with respect to that teleological necessity, a positive teleological contingency. The proviso to the first paragraph of Article 38 of the Criminal Code, "Provided that this does not apply where the law has special provisions", lays down that negligence is punished in negligent homicide and injury and in other cases, and it does so because, faced with an unfortunate chance, it emphasises that the chance could have been removed, that is, that the quantity of contingency was minute, and so holds the agent responsible for inattention. The cases in which positive teleological contingency means *force majeure*, and consequently has [p. 102] [コマ 58] no relation whatever to crime, are cases of the following kind: "Where a chance causal connection has been formed because a chance circumstance that could not be foreseen has newly been added, the result cannot, in criminal law, be attributed to the activity of will in question. For example, where a person who has suffered a slight wound is crushed to death by a structure brought down in a storm while being carried to hospital for treatment, or is burned to death in a fire while in hospital, it cannot be recognised that there is a causal relation in criminal law between the act of injuring and the death by burning" (Motoji Shinkuma, *Japanese Criminal Law: General Part*, 43rd ed., p. 306).[^c2s9-6] If A has intentionally inflicted violence and injury on B, and B is then crushed or burned to death in a natural disaster while being carried to hospital or while in hospital, this constitutes, with respect to the teleological necessity of A's criminal activity of will, a positive teleological contingency in the form of a "concurring chance circumstance". "The concurrence of chance circumstances" and "negligence" either have no relation at all to crime or are merely punished by exception, as so-called crimes of negligence, and this is because it is only through positive teleological contingency that the question of whether a crime has been constituted arises at all, and then only barely. By contrast, where a crime is willed on the horizon of teleological necessity but its completion is prevented by a chance obstacle, it is natural that it should, as an "attempt frustrated by an obstacle", in principle be treated as equivalent [p. 103] to the completed offence. "When the completion of a crime has not been accomplished because of a chance circumstance, the court has the power, according to the facts and the manner of the act, to apply the sanction laid down for the completed offence" ("Italian" draft of 1921, Article 16) (Makino Eiichi, *Revised Japanese Criminal Law*, pp. 277-278).[^c2s9-7] An event that happens to act as an obstacle is clearly a positive teleological contingency with respect to the teleological necessity of the criminal will, and for this reason crimes of this kind do not necessarily receive a reduced punishment.

That the chance which becomes a problem for the law is positive teleological contingency is clear also from the theory of the crimes of gambling and lotteries. Article 185 of the Criminal Code punishes "a person who has engaged in gaming or betting with property on a chance winning or losing", and "the reason for punishing acts of gambling and lotteries is that, by letting the speculative spirit run unchecked in public morals, they paralyse sound economic thinking and, at worst, threaten to obstruct the functioning of the national economy. The reason such acts are deemed unlawful is that, whereas the gain and loss of property ought in general to have a proper cause, taking a mere chance circumstance as that cause runs counter to the idea of justice in economic society" [p. 104] [コマ 59] (Ono Seiichirō, *Lectures on Criminal Law*, p. 460).[^c2s9-8] It is insofar as gambling and lotteries constitute a positive teleological contingency with respect to the teleological necessity of economic activity of will that the law forbids them, as harmful to the functioning of the national economy. The concept of "chance winning or losing" can be understood at its root only through the concept of disjunctive contingency, which I discuss later (see Chapter 3, section 8), but within the domain of the law's concern we may safely say that the concept of positive teleological contingency suffices. Positive teleological contingency, in short, is the dimension to which chance in the legal sense belongs, and through its "purpose without purpose" it is sometimes brought into an outward proximity with "intention".
