If a man has a small crime which is not a mischance but is carried through to the end, doing of himself what is unlawful, and doing it so, then though his crime is small he must be put to death. If a man has a great crime which is not carried through to the end but is only a mischance and misfortune, happening by chance, and he has fully declared his guilt, then he is not to be put to death."

### 10. Automaton and tychē

We have seen that, through the mediation of "purpose without purpose", positive teleological contingency can take on a form so close to "intention" that the two are hard to tell apart. It is from this point that Aristotle's distinction between *automaton* (αὐτόματον) and *tychē* (τύχη) can also be understood.[^c2s10-1]

[p. 105] Plato did not yet draw this distinction, and he uses "by *tychē*" (τύχῃ) and "by *automaton*" (ἀπὸ τοῦ αὐτομάτου) in exactly the same sense. In the *Protagoras*, for example, a virtue such as justice comes "neither by nature nor by chance" (οὐ φύσει οὐδ' ἀπὸ τοῦ αὐτομάτου): it comes from education, and whoever possesses it has acquired it by effort. Defects such as ugliness, smallness or weakness, on the other hand, together with the merits opposite to them, he treats as coming "by nature or by chance" (φύσει ἢ τύχῃ) (Platon, Protagoras, 323). Nature and chance appear here twice as a pair, and where he says "by chance" Plato writes "by *automaton*" on the one occasion and "by *tychē*" on the other, which is to say that he uses the two in exactly the same sense.

Aristotle, by contrast, drew the distinction between *automaton* and *tychē* in the *Physics*. On his account, what the two have in common is that an event belonging to the domain of purposiveness occurs without having as its purpose what it in fact brings about, its cause (an efficient cause or a final cause) lying elsewhere. In other words, both are [p. 106] [コマ 60] the "cause by *symbebēkos*" (αἰτία κατὰ συμβεβηκός) of an event in the domain of purposiveness.[^c2s10-2] The "cause by *symbebēkos*" refers to a mere "purpose without purpose", one that has no necessary relation whatever to the true system of cause and effect, or of purpose and means; it refers, that is, to the mere "purpose without purpose" of the event that was in fact brought about. In relation to the true causal or teleological necessity at work in the case, this "purpose without purpose" has the contingency that a contingent attribute has in relation to an essence, the contingency of the *symbebēkos* (see p. 16), and this is why it is called the "cause by *symbebēkos*". The cause of a statue, for example, is the sculptor, whilst its "cause by *symbebēkos*" is a white man or a musician. Being a white man or a musician has, in relation to the sculptor as essential cause, no more than the contingency of a contingent attribute: the sculptor happened to belong to the white race, or happened to have a talent for music, and the white man or the musician is for this reason the statue's "cause by *symbebēkos*". In short, *automaton* and *tychē* alike, as the "cause by *symbebēkos*" of an event in the domain of purposiveness, possess no more than a mere "purpose without purpose", and what they share is the negation of teleological necessity. Both, that is, are teleological contingency (Aristoteles, Physica, [p. 107] II. 5).

Where, then, do the two differ? *Tychē* refers to the case in which a contingent event concerns an agent able to act by "design" (προαίρεσις), whilst *automaton* is a concept that holds also where this is not so.[^c2s10-3] In extension, *automaton* is the superordinate concept that includes *tychē*: *tychē* is always *automaton*, but *automaton* is not necessarily *tychē*. Consequently *automaton* is found in animals and in inanimate things as well, whereas *tychē* is limited to agents able to design purposive action. *Tychē*, in other words, is tied to "action" (πρᾶξις) through the mediation of two concepts, "good fortune" (εὐτυχία) and "happiness" (εὐδαιμονία), and so is made to belong to the practical domain, which is why inanimate things, animals and small children, having no design, know nothing of *tychē* (l. c. II. 6). This is to say that with *tychē* the question whether there was "intention" can arise in practice, whilst with *automaton* this question, by its very essence, does not arise.

The example of *tychē* that Aristotle gives in the *Physics* is the following. A went to a certain place for some purpose other than collecting money, and there, by chance, he was able to collect it. [p. 108] [コマ 61] Collecting the money was not included in the purpose for which A went to that place, and collecting the money is consequently the "cause by *symbebēkos*", that is, the "purpose without purpose". Since this example of *tychē* concerns an agent able to act by design, the question of intention or chance can arise in it, and we can also conceive of a case in which A went to that place "intentionally", in order to collect the money.

Four examples of *automaton* are given. In the first, a tripod falls down but lands standing on its legs, so that it can be sat on; in the second, a stone falls and strikes a man; in the third, a horse comes this way and is thereby saved. That the tripod fell from a high place and came to stand on its three legs is mere causal necessity, and its becoming something one can sit on cannot be the purpose of its fall. Likewise, that the stone fell to a certain fixed place is mere causal necessity, and its striking a man cannot be the purpose of its falling. And again, that the horse came this way is mere causal necessity, and its being saved cannot be the purpose of its coming this way. [p. 109] Since the tripod, the stone and the horse cannot act by design, these are examples of *automaton*; and since, by the nature of a tripod, a stone or a horse, a purpose is for them absolutely impossible, these are cases in which there is no possibility whatever that the "purpose without purpose" might be understood as "intention". These three examples are also examples of positive causal contingency, which is discussed later, but that will be taken up in its place (see pp. 139-140).

The fourth example of *automaton* is the case in which something occurs contrary to nature (παρὰ φύσιν). This example first assumes, in accordance with the teleological view, that teleological necessity governs the natural world, and it is then the case in which, as a phenomenon contrary to nature, a purpose fails to be realised. It is what we treated earlier as the case of "counter-purpose" within negative teleological contingency (see p. 87). As I have already said, even in negative teleological contingency some "purpose without purpose" lies latent wherever the non-existence of a purpose is asserted (see pp. 94-95), so that negative and positive contingency are, in the end, hard to separate sharply. This fourth example also concerns a purpose of nature and does not concern the practical human being who can act by design. That anything should "intentionally" have taken counter-purpose as its purpose is unthinkable, [p. 110] [コマ 62] and this is what makes *automaton* *automaton*. Compared with the first three examples, however, the fourth differs entirely in the way the negated purpose exists. In the first three, the purposes negated are "in order to sit", "in order to strike a man" and "in order to be saved", and such purposes occupy a position outside (ἔξω) the causal series. In the fourth, the purpose negated is one that ought to be realised by nature, and such a purpose occupies a position within (ἐντός) the natural causal series. In the first three examples, something that ought not to exist is asserted not to exist; in the fourth, something that ought to exist is asserted not to exist, and by this counter-purposiveness is pointed out. All four, however, are examples of *automaton*, insofar as there is no possibility that they were designed "intentionally".

On the difference between *automaton* and *tychē*, there is also an example given by Alexander of Aphrodisias, famous as a commentator on Aristotle.[^c2s10-4] When a horse that has run away happens to meet its master, this is *automaton* for the horse and *tychē* for the master. The horse lacks [p. 111] the possibility that its "purpose without purpose" might be replaced by "intention", whereas the master has it. Whether the possibility of "intention" exists or not must be the chief point in distinguishing *tychē* from *automaton*. And insofar as *tychē* and *automaton* both concern purposiveness, whether or not that purposiveness can be posited, by design, within the domain of practice must be a difference of considerable importance.

Let me add a word on how *automaton* and *tychē* have been translated. Latin assigned *casus* to *automaton* and *fortuna* to *tychē*, and also rendered *automaton* literally as *per se vanum*, where *per se* translates αὐτό ("of itself") and *vanum* translates μάτην ("without reason"). According to Aristotle, *tychē* is called "good fortune" (εὐτυχία) when its result is a great good and "misfortune" (δυστυχία) when it is a great evil (Aristoteles, Physica, II. 5, 197a), and since what "good fortune" (*secunda fortuna*) and "misfortune" (*adversa fortuna*) have in common is "fortune" (*fortuna*), Latin translated *tychē* as *fortuna*.

The modern languages do not agree, and there are broadly three different approaches. (1) The first carries the Latin *casus* and *fortuna* over into a modern language as they stand. Hamelin is an example,[^c2s10-5] translating *automaton* [p. 112] [コマ 63] as *hasard* and *tychē* as *fortune* (Aristote Physique II, Traduction et Commentaire par O. Hamelin), and Wicksteed and Cornford likewise translate *automaton* as *chance* and *tychē* as *fortune* or *luck* (Aristotle The Physics, with an English Translation by P. H. Wicksteed and F. M. Cornford).[^c2s10-6] *Hasard* and *chance* both share their etymology with *casus*. (2) The second translates directly from the Greek into a modern language. Milhaud translates *automaton* as *spontané* and *tychē* as *hasard* (G. Milhaud, Le hasard chez Aristote et chez Cournot, Études sur la Pensée Scientifique, pp. 137-158),[^c2s10-7] and Gomperz likewise translates *automaton* as *das Spontane* and *tychē* as *Zufall* (Gomperz, Griechische Denker, III, Aristoteles und seine Nachfolger, Kap. X. Von Zufall und Notwendigkeit).[^c2s10-8] Hardie and Gaye do the same, translating *automaton* as *spontaneity* and *tychē* as *chance* (Aristotle Physica translated into English by R. P. Hardie and R. K. Gaye).[^c2s10-9] We should note that the terms are reversed between the case in which the Latin translation was carried into a modern language and the case in which a modern translation was made directly from the Greek: in the former, *hasard*, *Zufall* and *chance* are assigned to *automaton*, in the latter to *tychē*. Translation directly from the Greek [p. 113] is on the whole the better. Yet in translating *automaton*, to place the weight on αὐτό ("of itself") and render it "spontaneity", in effect leaving μάτην ("without reason") out of account, is inappropriate, for insofar as contingency appears clearly in the word μάτην, it ought not to be slighted. (3) The third translates the Greek strictly and literally. Prantl translates *tychē* as *Zufall* and *automaton* as *das grundlos von selbst Eintretende* (Aristoteles' Acht Bücher Physik. Griechisch und Deutsch herausgegeben von C. Prantl, 1854).[^c2s10-10] Since this rendering of *automaton* translates strictly and literally, just as they stand, the several moments contained in the original word, it must be called the most appropriate translation so far as the philosophical content of the word is concerned, the elegance or clumsiness of the translation being a separate question. Prantl himself states the need to translate literally in this way (Ib., S. 483-484), and I have already noted that Latin too had the literal rendering *per se vanum*. Through literal translation, the half of the meaning that was slighted when *automaton* was rendered "spontaneity" regains its rights.

[^c2s10-1]: Tr. *Automaton* (αὐτόματον), "what happens of itself", spontaneous chance; *tychē* (τύχη), luck or fortune. Aristotle discusses both in *Physics* II.4-6.
[^c2s10-2]: Tr. *Symbebēkos* (συμβεβηκός), "accident" (Latin *accidens*), introduced in Chapter 1, section 1. αἰτία κατὰ συμβεβηκός is the cause *per accidens*.
[^c2s10-3]: Tr. προαίρεσις, deliberate choice. Kuki's 意圖 is rendered "design", as in section 7, to keep it apart from 故意, "intention".
[^c2s10-4]: Tr. Alexander of Aphrodisias (fl. c. 200 CE), Peripatetic philosopher and the most influential ancient commentator on Aristotle.
[^c2s10-5]: Tr. Octave Hamelin (1856-1907), French idealist philosopher.
[^c2s10-6]: Tr. Philip H. Wicksteed (1844-1927) and Francis M. Cornford (1874-1943), translators of the *Physics* for the Loeb Classical Library (1929-1934).
[^c2s10-7]: Tr. Gaston Milhaud (1858-1918), French philosopher and historian of science, whose essay Kuki also cites in the Introduction.
[^c2s10-8]: Tr. Theodor Gomperz (1832-1912), Austrian classical philologist, author of *Griechische Denker* (*Greek Thinkers*).
[^c2s10-9]: Tr. R. P. Hardie and R. K. Gaye, translators of the *Physics* for the Oxford translation of Aristotle (1930).
[^c2s10-10]: Tr. Carl Prantl (1820-1888), German classical philologist and historian of logic. *Das grundlos von selbst Eintretende*: "what occurs of itself without ground".

### 11. From teleological contingency to causal contingency

[p. 114] [コマ 64] So far I have discussed teleological contingency. Yet contingency of this kind on the teleological horizon, seen from the standpoint of causal relation in the narrow sense, must also be considered to have arisen as the effect of some cause, and so to possess causal necessity. To take first negative teleological contingency as "purposelessness", the idea that human beings were thrown by chance onto the surface of the earth is nothing other than one half of the mechanistic view, according to which human beings were produced necessarily by the laws of motion. To take negative teleological contingency as "counter-purpose", severe intellectual disability must have some cause in the tissue of the cerebrum, and above all in the hereditary material of its cells,[^c2s11-1] and a flower's becoming double must have a determinate cause in the stimulating action of parasitic fungi or insects and in the flower's reaction to it. As for positive teleological contingency, that someone dug the ground and found treasure has its cause in the inertia of the object that lay at rest in the opaque earth, and in the mechanical action of the hoe together with the human force applied to it. [p. 115] That phosphorus was obtained by evaporating urine and heating it strongly was caused, in the same way, by the phosphate contained in the urine as an end product of physiological combustion, together with the chemical action applied to it. And that the investigation of the Shōden case happened to become connected with the decorations-buying affair must have had its root cause in the personal character and the social circumstances of the person under examination.[^c2s11-2] In this way teleological contingency, taking the same direction as categorical contingency, is reduced to causal necessity, or at least to the problem of causality in the narrow sense.

[^c2s11-1]: Tr. 白痴 (*hakuchi*), a period term, rendered as in Chapter 1, section 7.
[^c2s11-2]: Tr. The treasure, the phosphorus and the Shōden case are the examples of section 7.

### 12. Negative causal contingency

On the horizon of causality, the problem of causal contingency remains, and I shall first discuss negative causal contingency, in which the non-existence of causal necessity is negatively witnessed. Epicurus, who adopted atomism, thought that the original motion of the atoms was a vertical motion from above downwards, but he taught that there are atoms which, without any cause (ἀναιτίως) and of themselves (*sponte*), deviate very slightly from the vertical line [p. 116] [コマ 65]. Lucretius called this slight departure from the vertical "inclination" (*clinamen*): "The mind itself is not, in each of its actions, constrained by an inner necessity, as though bound, to endure and suffer. This is owing to the *minute* (*exiguum*) *inclination* (*clinamen*) of the primary bodies, determined neither in space nor in time"[^c2s12-v1] (Lucretius, De rerum natura II. 289-293). This Epicurean indeterminism was advanced against the determinism of the Stoics, and on the problem of determinism it also stands directly opposed to the atomism of Democritus. The *clinamen* is indeed the type of negative causal contingency. Causal contingency in this sense is nothing other than *spontaneity*, and, as the word "without a master" (ἀδέσποτος) shows (Diogenes Laertios, X. 133), it also comes to mean *freedom*.

That negative causal contingency can mean *freedom* is a point we should note. "This right to be able to enjoy *contingency* unhindered within certain fixed conditions has hitherto been called *personal freedom*" (Marx and Engels, "Die deutsche Ideologie", trans. Miki Kiyoshi, Iwanami Bunko, p. 116).[^c2s12-1] Again: "The dialectical materialism of scientific socialism takes the natural-scientific methodology that excludes every teleological standpoint [p. 117] as the sole principle of concept formation, ignores the typicality of the historical concepts of the human sciences, and seeks to put the construction of concepts of order in place of understanding by types. It consequently cannot escape taking away the room in which the universal of reflective judgement can arise, the universal that makes possible teleology as the regulative principle of the logic of dialectic; it blocks the place where *freedom* or *contingency* should be admitted, and seeks to construct history solely by the logic of an entirely necessary dialectic. The world of dialectical movement that arises in this way is in fact nothing other than a kind of nature, its particular falls into a particular that is merely an instance of a concept of law, and it can no longer demand to be, with a unique individuality, an object of historical knowledge" (Tanabe Hajime, "Hegel's Philosophy and Dialectic", pp. 383-384).[^c2s12-2] And again: "That dialectic turns spiritual life into logical necessity is something rightly done only under the priority of actuality; conversely, it does not determine the whole of actuality exhaustively, reaching even into a future that has not yet occurred, and so there must necessarily be *contingency* relative to dialectic there. The positive content that appears as what fills this place, left over negatively, is *freedom*" (ibid., p. 278).

When causal contingency means indeterminate spontaneity, Japanese expresses it with the word *onozukara*, [p. 118] [コマ 66] and when it is joined with teleological necessity and takes on the meaning of freedom, Japanese uses the word *mizukara*.[^c2s12-3] "Now I love my lonely dwelling, my hut of a single room, *of myself* (*mizukara*). When *on occasion* (*onozukara*) I go out to the capital I am ashamed to have become a beggar, yet when I return and stay here I pity others for running after the dust of the world" (*Hōjōki*).[^c2s12-4] The word *onozukara* can also mean "nature", and in that case, on the contrary, it can stand opposed to causal contingency. Insofar as it then also means teleological contingency, however, we should think of it as expressing a union of nature and chance. In "My View of Life" (p. 242), in the special issue of *Risō* on "The Philosophy of the View of Life", I used the word *onozukara* in this sense in particular, that is, in the sense of "nature" standing opposed to causal contingency.[^c2s12-5] "Even without deliberately learning and imitating, the things that catch the ear and the eye of anyone with a little talent must *naturally* (*jinen ni*) be many. And so she dashes off *mana*, and in letters between women, where it is out of place, she crams them in until more than half the page is filled; how distasteful, and one wishes she were gentler in this respect. Though in her heart she may not mean it so, it comes *of itself* (*onozukara*) to be read out in a stiff voice, and seems affected" (*Genji monogatari*, Hahakigi).[^c2s12-6] That in this passage *jinen ni* and *onozukara* have exactly the same meaning [p. 119] rests on the same Ground as Plato's treating nature and chance as of one kind (see p. 78). In short, the indeterminate spontaneity of *onozukara*, as causal contingency, turns through heterogeneous combination in the direction of teleological necessity and becomes the freedom of *mizukara*, and conversely runs through homogeneous combination in the direction of teleological contingency and becomes *jinen*, that is, nature.[^c2s12-7] Freedom and nature thus stand in direct opposition to one another. "If at that time the seed of Korean ginseng is sought out by your order, it can be procured *freely*, in any quantity. If ten or twenty thousand seeds are sown each year and this is continued for ten years, the seeds sown first will bear fruit from the fourth or fifth year onwards, so that within ten years they can be cultivated step by step. Then, when the seed has become plentiful, if it is sown here and there in the mountains and left, Korean ginseng will in time grow *naturally* in the mountains, just as Japanese ginseng does now" (*Hiraga Gennai zenshū*, published by the Hiraga Gennai Sensei Kenshōkai, vol. 1, pp. 194-195).[^c2s12-8]

::: scheme
Causal contingency\
= *indeterminate spontaneity*\
(*onozukara*)

- [(result of heterogeneous combination)]{.tag}\
  teleological necessity = *freedom* (*mizukara*)
- [(result of homogeneous combination)]{.tag}\
  teleological contingency = *nature* (*jinen*)
:::

Hegel's distinction between freedom (*Freiheit*) and arbitrariness (*Willkür*) likewise sets up, on the one side, *mizukara*, in which teleological necessity is most strongly emphasised, as "freedom", and on the other calls "arbitrariness" the result of the homogeneous combination of the causal contingency [p. 120] [コマ 67] of *onozukara* with the teleological contingency of *jinen*.[^c2s12-9] According to Hegel, the will holds the contingent within itself in the form of arbitrariness, merely as a sublated moment. Arbitrariness is the will in the form of contingency, and freedom is the will in the form of necessity. When people speak of the freedom of the will, they often have in mind mere arbitrariness. Arbitrariness, as the capacity to determine oneself to one thing or another, is of course an essential moment of free will, yet it is not freedom itself, only formal freedom. The truly free will holds the causal contingency of arbitrariness within itself as sublated, and is clearly conscious of its content as its own, as teleological necessity. The will that remains at the stage of arbitrariness, by contrast, even when it has determined itself to what is true and right in content, has not escaped the contentless emptiness of having been able, had it so pleased, to determine itself otherwise. Hegel thus opposes the teleological necessity of freedom as *mizukara* to the causal contingency of arbitrariness as *onozukara*, and he goes further, pointing out that arbitrariness contains a contradiction in that it has teleological contingency, and so [p. 121] sharpens its opposition to freedom. In arbitrariness, form and content fall apart. Arbitrariness contradicts itself in that it has causal contingency in its form, whilst in its content it has teleological contingency and, as the other side of this, causal necessity. Insofar as the content of arbitrariness is given, it is not grounded by the will itself (*mizukara*) within the will; it is grounded of itself (*onozukara*) in external circumstances. With regard to such content, freedom arises only in the form of choice, and such formal freedom is merely a freedom one imagines to be freedom. For if we analyse it in detail, we find that the cause of the will's determining itself precisely to B and not to A does not lie in the self; the cause of its self-determination lies in the externality of the circumstances that form the basis of the content the will found as given. Analysed in detail, the *onozukara* of arbitrariness as causal contingency proves, insofar as it is joined with teleological contingency, to be no more than the causal necessity of *jinen*. Because what was thought to be causal contingency is seen, as the result of analysis, to be causal necessity, arbitrariness contains a contradiction. Hegel's view is that arbitrariness, at the lower stage of the will, must be developed into freedom at the higher stage (Hegel, En-[p. 122] [コマ 68]cyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 145, Zusatz, S. 194).

We can divide the concept of freedom into two, negative freedom and positive freedom. Negative freedom then corresponds to indeterminacy and spontaneity as causal contingency, and positive freedom to what Hegel calls freedom, as teleological necessity. According to Nicolai Hartmann's study of Modality, negative freedom is the possibility of acting one way or the other, the predominance of possibility over necessity.[^c2s12-10] The ought, however, which is the essence of morality, is the predominance of necessity over possibility, so that activity conditioned by the ought cannot be called free in the negative sense; it can be called free in the positive sense. Negative freedom is "freedom of possibility" (Freiheit der Möglichkeit), that is, "freedom from necessity" (Freiheit „von" der Notwendigkeit), whilst positive freedom, as "freedom from possibility" (Freiheit „von" der Möglichkeit), must be "freedom of necessity" (Freiheit der Notwendigkeit). Hartmann holds that the freedom of necessity is necessity gushing forth freely, and so arrives at the paradoxical concept of "free necessity" (freie Notwendigkeit) (see N. Hartmann, Logische und ontologische Wirklichkeit, [p. 123] Kantstudien XX, S. 23-24; N. Hartmann, Ethik, 1926, S. 587-589). The "free necessity" of which Hartmann speaks is positive freedom as teleological necessity, whilst causal contingency, as indeterminate spontaneity, constitutes the concept of negative freedom.

In Eastern thought, further, there is a marked tendency to see freedom and nature as fused into one, without setting them in disjoined opposition. It may be possible to regard this as a stage in which the teleological necessity of *mizukara* and the causal necessity of *jinen ni* are sublated in the spontaneity of *onozukara*. An interesting problem lies here, but this is not the place to discuss it. On this point, Mr Abe Yoshishige's article "On Nature", published in *Risō* no. 17, is rich in suggestion.[^c2s12-11]

I have now briefly considered the relation of causal contingency, as indeterminate spontaneity, to freedom in the positive sense on the one hand and to nature on the other. It cannot be denied that, as a result, there is a tendency in the domain of the philosophy of spirit to replace the concept of causal contingency with the concept of teleological necessity. Teleological necessity, however, makes no sense unless at its origin it depends on causal contingency. Causal contingency must be the starting point of teleological [p. 124] [コマ 69] necessity. In other words, negative freedom as indeterminacy and spontaneity constitutes the indispensable condition of positive freedom as determinacy and necessity. More than this, if positive freedom as teleological necessity is understood as "liberation from obstacles", then, from the standpoint of the process by which the consciousness of freedom arises, positive freedom comes on the contrary to have the negative meaning, and negative freedom, as absolute spontaneity, even comes to have the positive meaning. Mr Amano Teiyū's "Personality and Freedom" makes this point clear:[^c2s12-12] "The empirical process by which we come to have the consciousness of freedom begins from the negative side. Only where there are obstacles do we become conscious of freedom. If there were no obstacles at all, the consciousness of freedom could not arise. This is why freedom is understood as liberation from obstacles. ...... And since in human beings action is thought to be based on the will, the obstacles that bind may be many and various, yet what is bound always comes down to the will. A human being is free when the will is not bound. Here the meaning of freedom turns to the positive side, namely that a person can either will an action or not will it. [p. 125] But this does not mean that the meaning of freedom changes, for the negative meaning too has the positive meaning as its Ground. When liberation from obstacles is said to be freedom, it is not the liberated state that is freedom; freedom is the possibility of activity, which through liberation has become able either to submit to the obstacles or not to submit" (Iwanami Lectures on Philosophy, Amano Teiyū, "Personality and Freedom", p. 4. See also p. 38. In the same work, personal freedom is discussed with philosophical passion in connection with "the comprehension of the intelligible world".)

[^c2s12-1]: Tr. Miki Kiyoshi (1897-1945), Japanese philosopher, translator of *The German Ideology* for the Iwanami Bunko series.
[^c2s12-2]: Tr. Tanabe Hajime (1885-1962), philosopher of the Kyoto School. The book is *Hēgeru tetsugaku to benshōhō* (ヘーゲル哲學と辯證法).
[^c2s12-3]: Tr. *Onozukara*, "of itself", and *mizukara*, "by oneself", can both be written 自ら. *Onozukara* also carries the sense "on occasion, by chance", as in the *Hōjōki* passage. 自然 can be read *shizen* or *jinen*, "nature" or "naturally".
[^c2s12-4]: Tr. The *Hōjōki* (方丈記, *An Account of My Hut*) is Kamo no Chōmei's essay of 1212.
[^c2s12-5]: Tr. Kuki's own essay 「我が人生觀」 (*Waga jinseikan*), in the special issue 「人生觀の哲學」 of the journal *Risō* (理想).
[^c2s12-6]: Tr. "Hahakigi" ("The Broom Tree") is the second chapter of the *Tale of Genji*. *Mana* are Chinese characters, whose use in letters was thought unbecoming in women.
[^c2s12-7]: Tr. 異種結合 and 同種結合 are rendered "heterogeneous combination" and "homogeneous combination".
[^c2s12-8]: Tr. Hiraga Gennai (1728-1780), Edo-period naturalist, inventor and writer. The quotation is in epistolary *sōrōbun*.
[^c2s12-9]: Tr. 恣意 renders Hegel's *Willkür*, here "arbitrariness".
[^c2s12-10]: Tr. Nicolai Hartmann (1882-1950), German philosopher of ontology and ethics. 當爲, "the ought", renders *Sollen*.
[^c2s12-11]: Tr. Abe Yoshishige (1883-1966), Japanese philosopher and educator.
[^c2s12-12]: Tr. Amano Teiyū (1884-1980), Japanese philosopher in the Kantian tradition. The essay appeared in the Iwanami series *Iwanami kōza tetsugaku* (Iwanami Lectures on Philosophy).

Such is the important role of causal contingency in the domain of the philosophy of spirit. In natural science, on the other hand, it cannot be denied that the general tendency is to try by every means to exclude the concept of causal contingency by means of the concept of causal necessity. Even when concepts such as chance error, chance generation and chance variation[^c2s12-13] arise, they are soon conquered by causal necessity. These concepts, too, arose in connection with causal contingency: chance error in relation to the cause of error in the measurement of quantities, chance generation in relation to the cause of the generation of organisms, and chance variation in relation to the cause of changes in the hereditary material. In each case, that is, one sought to witness contingency in some sense. [p. 126] [コマ 70] In each case, however, natural science replaces contingency with necessity: for chance error, by determining something that conforms to the causal law as the quantity to be measured; for chance generation, by bringing the boundary between inorganic and organic matter closer by way of chemical synthesis; and for chance variation, by necessitating the causes of variation experimentally, using radiation and other means. We may well say that the ambition of natural science lies precisely here. If natural science makes causal necessity its ultimate idea, and the philosophy of spirit postulates causal contingency as an indispensable condition of teleological necessity, it is only natural that a grave philosophical problem should unfold around the concept of causal contingency.

Whether causal contingency exists in the strict sense is one of the ultimate problems for natural scientists and philosophers alike. Among those who affirm its existence is, for example, Boutroux.[^c2s12-14] According to Boutroux, the causal law, as something abstract, can serve as a practical maxim of science, but in the concrete real world it does not apply strictly. All measurement is merely approximate, and to attain absolute precision is [p. 127] in principle impossible. Experimental verification comes down in the end to compressing the value of the measurable element of phenomena between limits brought as close together as possible. What we see is, so to speak, only the container in which the thing has been put, and not the thing itself. A *minute indeterminacy*, too minute to fall within the range of validity of our crude methods of measurement, can therefore be immanent in phenomena, and this is precisely causal contingency (*contingence*) as the non-existence of causal necessity. In this way Boutroux sought to give the "philosophy of freedom" advocated by Maine de Biran and Ravaisson[^c2s12-15] a foundation in the concept of contingency. Moreover, whereas in the indeterminism of Epicurus causal contingency forms a homogeneous combination with teleological contingency, in Boutroux causal contingency forms a heterogeneous combination with teleological necessity. Epicurus acknowledged the existence of the gods, but he thought that these gods dwell in the void between the worlds (μετακόσμια, *intermundia*), rest content in self-sufficient bliss, and do not interfere in any way with the world or with human beings. Boutroux's so-called "contingency of the laws of nature", by contrast, depends in the end on the teleological necessity of the universe (Boutroux, *De la contingence des lois de la nature*, 9e éd., pp. 155-157). Boutroux speaks of "necessity in the form of duty", that is, of "the end conceived as necessary", [p. 128] [コマ 71] and says that in God "freedom is infinite" whilst there is at the same time "practical necessity". He expounds God's "unceasing providence" and "particular providence", and says that "the contingency shown by the hierarchical organisation of the various forms and general laws of the universe is explained by this doctrine of the freedom of God". The quotation from Aristotle's *Parts of Animals* that he placed at the head of his book as a motto, "Here too there are gods",[^c2s12-16] indicates that his standpoint points to the teleological necessity lurking behind causal contingency, and at the same time hints that his thought is close to Thomas Aquinas's concept of the "law of necessity or of contingency" and to Malebranche's occasionalism.[^c2s12-17] Émile Borel,[^c2s12-18] too, affirms causal contingency in his book *Le Hasard*. His claim concerning physical phenomena is, on the one hand, that "the necessity of a certain total phenomenon is not incompatible with the freedom of the partial phenomena", and, on the other, that "even a determinism thought to be absolute with regard to the partial phenomena does not permit the total phenomenon to be foreseen with absolute rigour". "Absolute determinism is in any case difficult to support. For, however small the interference of freedom within the universe may be, between the universe in which such interference exists [p. 129] and the universe from which it is excluded an abyss lies" (Émile Borel, *Le Hasard*, 1920, pp. 290-295).

There will be not a few, however, who say with Poincaré[^c2s12-19] that "we have become absolute determinists" (H. Poincaré, *Science et Méthode*, p. 65). When a cone is stood on its apex, the direction in which it falls certainly seems to be a matter of chance. But for the direction of its fall to be determined there must necessarily be some cause. If the cone lacks symmetry even very slightly, that is, it inclines, however slightly, to one side or the other, and however slight that inclination may be, it determines the direction in which the cone falls. Even if the symmetry were perfect, an extremely minute vibration would incline it by some seconds of arc, and that slight inclination would determine the direction of its fall. In short, "a *minute* cause that escapes our notice, that we do not recognise, determines a considerable effect that we cannot fail to see, and then we say that the effect happened by chance" (ib., p. 68). Poincaré drew a sharp distinction, in the Kantian manner, between determinism and freedom, and sought to harmonise the domain of science with the domain of morality. "Just as it is impossible for a human being, [p. 130] [コマ 72] when doing science, not to reason as a determinist," he says, "so it is impossible for a human being, when acting, not to act as a free human being" (H. Poincaré, *Dernières Pensées*, Paris, Flammarion 1926, p. 246).

The point at issue between indeterminism and determinism concerning causality lies in whether we acknowledge a minute indeterminacy within determinacy or a minute determinacy within indeterminacy. The problem comes down, that is, to the single point of the "minute"; it is concentrated on the single point of Lucretius's *exiguum*. And so long as the minute indeterminacy that Boutroux and Borel assume cannot be measured by human beings, this may be, as Poincaré says, a problem "difficult to resolve clearly" (H. Poincaré, *La Valeur de la Science*, p. 248).

What attitude, then, does recent natural science take towards this problem? Let me quote here the words of a natural scientist: "The principle of what is usually called the causal law, that is, of the necessary succession of events, has been regarded as an absolutely necessary category for the establishment of natural science. For if no such necessary relation existed among events, we would not have been able to set up there any fixed law or principle whatever. [p. 131] However, the development of physics gradually came to doubt the absolute validity even of this category. First, in thermal phenomena the contingency of molecular motion came to be acknowledged, and accordingly it was made clear that the laws of thermodynamics, for example, are merely something that appears as the result of a transition from a state of contingent distribution of this molecular motion to another state of greater probability, and are by no means of absolute necessity, but permit many contingencies contrary to them. However, in this case the motion of each individual molecule was still regarded, as to its laws, as following necessary mechanical principles, and thermodynamically contingency results because, instead of seeing individual molecules 'microscopically', we see their great aggregate 'macroscopically', that is, statistically. According to the results of the recent development of the quantum theory of the electrons within the atom, however, it has been made clear that the law determining such a state of the electron (the wave function of the electron) is not likewise given necessarily, but can be expressed only with a certain contingent probability. This must truly be called an astonishing new fact. [p. 132] [コマ 73] It is a matter concerning the ultimate elements of matter. Moreover, that it can be determined only with contingency overturns at its root the old conception of necessary causality with respect to the basic principles of matter. We could, of course, by comparison with the example of molecular motion in thermal phenomena, interpret that in this case too some necessary law is hidden behind the contingency of the state of the electron, and that we merely observe its statistical results. However, the so-called necessary law behind it is, like the aforementioned form or orbit of the electron, in the end nothing other than a certain imagining, and goes beyond the domain of actual experience. Accordingly, we could there, with entirely equal right, assume the negation of necessary law and imagine, for example, something like the free will of the electron itself. For what goes beyond the domain of experience is already not an object of natural-scientific research" (Ishihara Jun, "The Supra-materialist Tendency of Modern Natural Science", *Shisō*, no. 100, commemorative special issue, pp. 84-85).[^c2s12-20] And: "When we consider that what is discussed here is the most basic phenomenon of physics, we cannot but realise how deeply contingency lies at the very root of natural science" (Ishihara Jun, *Outline of Natural Science*, p. 88).

[p. 133] As this quotation shows, the concept of negative causal contingency has recently come to be affirmed as so-called quantum-mechanical contingency, on the basis of the uncertainty principle, and natural science has come to show a tendency to give the advantage to the indeterminism that asserts a minute indeterminacy. Theorists who stress how far-reaching its philosophical outlook is have also gradually grown in number (cf. Ed. Le Roy, "Ce que la Microphysique apporte ou suggère à la Philosophie", *Revue de Métaphysique et de Morale*, 1935, esp. pp. 345-355).[^c2s12-21] Yet not every influential natural scientist necessarily accepts the uncertainty principle, and it should also remain somewhat doubtful how far it is compatible with the essence of natural-scientific thinking itself (cf. É. Meyerson, *Du cheminement de la pensée* I, pp. 63-64, III, pp. 763-765, 768-769).[^c2s12-22] There are also theorists who claim that these problems belong to the sphere of a "*quantum metaphysics*" (*métamicrophysique*, ultra-microphysics),[^c2s12-23] distinct from the domain of the former *metaphysics* (*métaphysique*, ultra-physics) (G. Bachelard, "Noumène et Microphysique", *Recherches philosophiques* I, pp. 55-65),[^c2s12-24] so that on philosophical ground the state of affairs still lacks clarity. Those who philosophise should perhaps not overrate the value of so-called quantum-mechanical contingency itself. Since the state of affairs is [p. 134] [コマ 74] as it is, we shall for the time being leave open the question of whether negative causal contingency exists in the so-called "domain of objects". Does no contingency, then, exist concerning causality in some other sense? Here, over against negative causal contingency, we have the concept of positive causal contingency.

[^c2s12-13]: Tr. 偶然誤差, 偶然發生, 偶然變異: chance (random) error, chance generation, chance variation. "Chance generation" renders 偶然發生, in the context of the origin of organisms.
[^c2s12-14]: Tr. Émile Boutroux (1845-1921), French philosopher, author of *De la contingence des lois de la nature* (1874).
[^c2s12-15]: Tr. Maine de Biran (1766-1824) and Félix Ravaisson (1813-1900), French philosophers of will, habit and spontaneity.
[^c2s12-16]: Tr. Aristotle, *Parts of Animals* I.5, where the saying is attributed to Heraclitus.
[^c2s12-17]: Tr. Nicolas Malebranche (1638-1715), French philosopher; occasionalism holds that God is the only true cause and that created things are merely the occasions of God's action.
[^c2s12-18]: Tr. Émile Borel (1871-1956), French mathematician, a founder of measure theory and modern probability.
[^c2s12-19]: Tr. Henri Poincaré (1854-1912), French mathematician, physicist and philosopher of science.
[^c2s12-20]: Tr. Ishihara Jun (1881-1947), Japanese theoretical physicist. *Shisō* (思想, "Thought") is a philosophy journal published by Iwanami. 超唯物的 is rendered "supra-materialist".
[^c2s12-21]: Tr. Édouard Le Roy (1870-1954), French philosopher and mathematician, successor to Bergson at the Collège de France.
[^c2s12-22]: Tr. Émile Meyerson (1859-1933), French philosopher of science.
[^c2s12-23]: Tr. Kuki's 量子上學 (*ryōshijōgaku*) is coined on the model of 形而上學 (*keijijōgaku*), metaphysics, literally the study of what lies "above form", replacing "form" with "quantum". Kuki glosses both terms in French: *métamicrophysique* as 超微物理學, "ultra-microphysics", and *métaphysique* as 超物理學, "ultra-physics".
[^c2s12-24]: Tr. Gaston Bachelard (1884-1962), French philosopher of science.
[^c2s12-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 精神自身は各々の行動にあたつて内的必然性によつて恰かも縛られて忍辱し受難するが如くに拘束されてゐるものではない。これは原體の有する、空間的にも時間的にも決定されてゐない*微小の*(exiguum)*傾き*(clinamen)のお蔭である (Lucretius, De rerum natura II. 289-293). Our translation: "The mind itself is not, in each of its actions, constrained as if bound by an inner necessity to endure and suffer. This is owing to the *minute* (*exiguum*) *inclination* (*clinamen*), determined neither spatially nor temporally, that the primary bodies have."

### 13. Positive causal contingency

Positive causal contingency is, as we have already said, the case in which we witness positively the existence of a relation other than causality between two or more events. If a tile falls from a roof and bursts a rubber balloon that was rolling under the eaves, for example, we think it chance, and if a volcano erupts during a solar eclipse, we call that chance too. The tile, owing to some cause, such as a loss of adhesion through the decay of the roof or its being dislodged by the force of the wind, followed the law of falling and came down at a certain place. The rubber balloon, with the slight impulse it first received, the elasticity of the rubber, its spherical shape and [p. 135] the slope and unevenness of the ground as causes, followed the laws of motion and rolled to a certain place. What we call chance is that two events belonging to different causal series were placed, at a certain place, in a certain positive relation. In the same way, the volcano erupted because the tension of underground hot water turning into steam reached a certain degree, and it did so according to the law of pressure, whilst the sun, because it was obscured by the moon, followed the simplest laws of astronomy and presented the phenomenon of darkness. The one causal series and the other are wholly independent. No causal relation can be recognised between them; the two series are not bound by necessity. The contingency in this case, too, lies in a non-necessary mutual relation between one causal series and another.

For the similar relation that holds among many events, we may consider the following example: "Let us consider a white ball. Just as a machine moves according to necessary laws, the ball too would roll on a smooth flat board according to laws known to us. If we roll countless such white balls, in great numbers, on one flat board, various spotted patterns of white balls that were not at all foreseen must surely appear on the board. Whatever pattern we find in each individual case, [p. 136] [コマ 75] all is chance" (Ishihara Jun, "God Loves Chance", magazine *Serupan*, June 1935 issue, p. 7).[^c2s13-1] Even where positive contingency is a relation among many events, however, it still has at its basis, as its prototype, the relation between two events. Contingency of this kind is very important. As Stuart Mill[^c2s13-2] also says: "It is not correct to say that some phenomenon was produced by chance. But one can say that two or more phenomena were combined by chance, that they merely exist simultaneously by chance or that one follows the other. That is, one can say so in the sense that they are not related in any way by causality" ("It is incorrect to say that any phenomenon is produced by chance; but we may say that two or more phenomena are conjoined by chance, that they co-exist or succeed one another only by chance; meaning that they are in no way related through causation") (J. Stuart Mill, *A System of Logic*, III, ch. XVII, § 2, New Impression 1925, p. 345).

This positive causal contingency grounds both categorical contingency and teleological contingency in their principal forms. That a certain clover is four-leaved was seen as a categorical contingency, [p. 137] but it has a causal necessity, such as the stimulus of an injury, and this causal necessity in turn probably has its Ground in positive causal contingency. That the leaf of a certain clover was injured at the early stage of its morphogenesis, that is, must rest on a positive causal contingency, such as a strong wind's happening to drive sand against that very part. Again, when a weather forecaster whose success rate is ninety per cent or more happens to make a wrong forecast, this is a categorical contingency, but it had as its causal necessity such facts as a sudden change in the weather or some agitation in the forecaster's mind. Yet such causal necessity, too, presupposes some positive causal contingency at its root. We can think, for example, that a positive causal contingency, such as air current A and air current B happening to collide, or to pass each other, on a line of discontinuity, formed the basis that brought on the sudden change in the weather. And at the root of the forecaster's agitation there lies hidden a positive causal contingency in which the act of making the forecast, on the one hand, and an event liable to cause agitation, on the other, are combined by chance: "Just the other day something like that happened; a child had a fever, but [p. 138] [コマ 76] *as ill luck would have it*, the forecast begins at eight in the evening. Just as it was about to begin, it reached *forty-three degrees*, and there was a fear that it might be diphtheria" (Fujiwara Sakuhei, *Talks on Astronomy and Meteorology*, p. 58).[^c2s13-3] In short, positive causal contingency grounds categorical contingency.

Further, since categorical contingency became negative teleological contingency when the general concept was seen as a purpose to be realised (see pp. 87-88), the present examples of the four-leaved clover and the mistaken forecast can also be seen as examples of negative teleological contingency, and it follows that behind negative teleological contingency, too, we cannot but think some positive causal contingency. In positive teleological contingency this is clearer still. If, in digging the ground to plant a tree, we unexpectedly turned up treasure, this, we said, is positive teleological contingency: since the sole purpose lay in planting the tree, the finding of the treasure is teleological contingency. Now, as we have said before, purposiveness is the reverse of causality. It first aims at the result that comes later, as purpose, and then chooses the cause that precedes that result, as means, so that the relation of purpose and means presupposes the relation of cause and effect. What causal relation, then, exists in this case in the shadow of purposiveness? On the one hand, the [p. 139] mechanical action of A's spade was the cause of the treasure's being buried in the ground. On the other hand, the mechanical action of B's spade was the cause of the earth's being dug over. There were two independent causal series, and these two causal series were placed by chance in a single relation. In short, positive causal contingency lies hidden at the root of positive teleological contingency.

Here, however, there is a phenomenon worth noting. We said earlier that teleological contingency is accompanied by a "purpose without purpose", and that in negative teleological contingency the first member of that phrase, "without purpose", is emphasised, whilst in positive teleological contingency the second member, "purpose", is emphasised.[^c2s13-4] This character of teleological contingency is reflected in positive causal contingency too, so that positive causal contingency also comes to have a kind of imagined "purpose without purpose". When the roof tile fell and just hit the rubber balloon, we feel as though some mischievous purpose were hidden in it; when the volcano's eruption and the solar eclipse occurred at the same time, we feel as though some mysterious purpose lay behind them. These feelings, however, are nothing more than the projection, through a kind of empathy,[^c2s13-5] of the "purpose without purpose" of teleological contingency onto positive causal contingency. And [p. 140] [コマ 77] from this standpoint we can also assert that the distinction between Aristotle's *automaton* and *tychē*[^c2s13-6] is the distinction between positive causal contingency and positive teleological contingency (see p. 109).

[^c2s13-1]: Tr. *Serupan* (セルパン, from the French *serpent*) was a Japanese general-interest monthly published by Daiichi Shobō.
[^c2s13-2]: Tr. John Stuart Mill (1806-1873), English philosopher and logician. Kuki gives the passage first in Japanese and then in Mill's English.
[^c2s13-3]: Tr. Fujiwara Sakuhei (1884-1950), Japanese meteorologist.
[^c2s13-4]: Tr. Kuki's terms are 上肢 and 下肢, "upper member" and "lower member": in the vertical Japanese phrase 目的なき目的, "without purpose" (目的なき) stands above "purpose" (目的).
[^c2s13-5]: Tr. 感情移入, the standard Japanese rendering of *Einfühlung*.
[^c2s13-6]: Tr. *Automaton* (αὐτόματον), spontaneous chance; *tychē* (τύχη), luck or fortune. See section 10.

### 14. Compound contingency

Contingencies of different kinds sometimes combine to constitute a single compound contingency. The case of someone on the way to gamble who unexpectedly finds a four-leaved clover by the roadside, for example, is a compound contingency. The four-leaved clover is itself a categorical contingency, and in addition, that a person going to gamble should happen to find on the way a four-leaved clover, which is regarded as a sign of good luck, is a positive teleological contingency. We have said that positive causal contingency lies hidden at the root both of categorical contingency and of teleological contingency, but here, further, categorical contingency and positive teleological contingency appear in a compound state. To give one more example, a newspaper (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, Kyoto edition, 19 April 1935) carried a report that, because a car with the number 1564 had run someone over, "the driver [p. 141] went pale and applied for a change of number", and this, too, is compound contingency. Between 1564 and "murder" (*hitogoroshi*) there holds, through a relation of sound, a positive contingency of ground,[^c2s14-1] and that a car bearing such an "uncanny" number happened to run someone over is a positive teleological contingency. Contingency is emphasised, that is, because a positive contingency of ground and a positive teleological contingency appear in a compound state.

We should note, further, that the basis of the compoundness of compound contingency is itself formed by contingency. In the example of the clover, between the four-leaved clover, which is a categorical contingency, and the positive teleological contingency of finding it, there lies a further positive contingency, namely that the four-leaved clover is a symbol of good luck and that the finder was on the way to gamble, hoping for luck, and this forms the basis of the compoundness. In the example of the car, likewise, between the positive contingency of ground given by the relation of sound and the positive teleological contingency of unexpectedly running someone over, there lies the positive contingency that the sound of the car's number corresponds to murder and that the chance the car met with was a killing, and it is only this [p. 142] [コマ 78] that makes it a compound contingency in the true sense. I shall discuss the compoundness of successive contingency later (see pp. 159 ff.).

[^c2s14-1]: Tr. The numerals 1, 5, 6, 4 can be read *hi(to)*, *go*, *ro(ku)*, *shi*, which sounds as *hitogoroshi* (人殺し), "murder", "killing".

### 15. The general character of positive hypothetical contingency

We have now considered each of the three kinds of hypothetical contingency by dividing it into negative and positive contingency. Negative contingency is the case in which the lack of a hypothetical antecedent (ground, cause or purpose) is grasped negatively with regard to a single event; positive contingency is the case in which a relation other than a hypothetical relation is witnessed positively between two or more events. Since in negative contingency the lack of an antecedent is merely grasped with regard to a single event, it can in this sense be called *absolute contingency*, and since in positive contingency it is the relation among two or more events that is judged to be contingent, it can be called *relative contingency*. In short, positive contingency, whether positive contingency of ground, positive teleological contingency or positive causal contingency, possesses [p. 143] both positivity and relativity.

What we must note here, however, is that every negative absolute contingency has, in the end, a positive relative contingency at its root. Relative contingency in general has a negative aspect, the lack of a hypothetical relation, and a positive aspect, the holding of some relation other than a hypothetical one. So-called negative contingency is the case in which only the negative aspect is witnessed, whilst positive contingency is the case in which we go further and witness the positive aspect along with it. Yet since a contingency wholly lacking the positive aspect cannot in reality exist, there must be some positivity present even when the positive aspect is not consciously grasped. That positivity may be of a kind to be witnessed in some other dimension, but in any case, unless some positivity is witnessed, we cannot speak of true contingency. In this sense we can say that all contingency is positive relative contingency.

Positive contingency of ground, for example, lies hidden at the root of negative contingency of ground. Earlier [p. 144] [コマ 79] we gave as an example of negative contingency the case of "falling by chance into the truth" without any ground. In that case the non-existence of a ground is witnessed negatively, but insofar as we say that one "falls into the truth", some relativity must lie hidden there. On the one side, that is, there is the series of necessary relations of ground and consequent that we call the knowledge of truth; on the other, there is the series of necessary relations of the free will that affirms or denies; and insofar as a non-necessary relative relation holds between the two series, truth emerges as a positive contingency. At the root of the negative contingency of lacking a ground of knowledge for the truth that is reached, there consequently lies the positive contingency that the series of the will's decisions, though lacking a ground of knowledge, fell into the series of the knowledge of truth. Lacking a ground of knowledge, that series of decisions could just as well have fallen into a logical series in which error holds. The positive existence we call truth is something that suddenly floated up where two independent series were placed in a contingent relation. That positive contingency lies hidden at the root of negative teleological contingency, too, I have already discussed in another connection, with regard to the case of "counter-purpose" (see p. 138). When a purpose that ought to be realised is [p. 145] not realised, that is, a purpose that in most cases is realised fails, in that case in particular, to be realised, and for this we could not but think some positive causal contingency on the reverse side. Even in the case of negative teleological contingency as "purposelessness", insofar as we assert the negation of purposiveness negatively, some "purpose without purpose" must be witnessed on the reverse side, even if unconsciously, and it is with regard to that purpose without purpose that purposiveness must be denied. If we claim, for example, that the eye is not made for the purpose of looking at objects, just as clear water is not made for the purpose of reflecting a person's figure, then some purpose without purpose in the structure and function of the eye is grasped positively, and only then is purposiveness refused with regard to it. And as to where that purpose without purpose comes from, we must think that it rests on a contingent relative relation among two or more causal series. Negative teleological contingency, then, even when it is mere "purposelessness", conceals some positive relative contingency at its root, just as it does in the case of "counter-purpose". The same holds, finally, for negative causal contingency, as becomes clear if we consider such concepts as chance error, [p. 146] [コマ 80] chance generation and chance variation. In chance error, unpredictable changes in the atmosphere or sudden minute changes in the instruments make up a positive relative contingency. In chance generation, it is assumed that, for living things to arise from non-living things, some positive causal contingency was established between matter and the state of its environment at a certain period in the history of the earth's formation. In chance variation, too, the changes occurring in the hereditary material acknowledge the existence of some positive relative contingency between the organism on the one side and temperature, light and so on on the other. And where negative causal contingency means spontaneity in the strict sense, we must think at its root at least a relative relation of positive contingency of ground between the non-actual side and the actual side.

In short, at the root of the negative absolute contingency of a single event there lies latent a positive relative contingency among two or more events. Schopenhauer, too, says that "the contingent is always only relative" (*Das Zufällige ist immer nur relativ*) (Schopenhauer, *Sämtliche Werke*, hrsg. v. Deussen, I, S. 550). And the positivity and relativity of positive relative contingency take on, as a dynamic relativity, the meaning of encounter or Encounter, of *sōgū* (遭遇) or *kaikō* (邂逅).[^c2s15-1] [p. 147] Cournot,[^c2s15-2] too, defines chance as "an encounter between facts each rationally independent of the other" (*une rencontre entre des faits rationnellement indépendants les uns des autres*) (Cournot, *Traité de l'enchaînement des idées fondamentales dans les sciences et dans l'histoire*, nouvelle éd. 1922, p. 67), and again as "a concurrence of two orders of rational facts independent of each other" (*concours de deux ordres de faits rationnels, indépendants l'un de l'autre*) (Cournot, *Matérialisme, Vitalisme, Rationalisme*, p. 227). This definition resembles Mill's, cited earlier (see p. 136), but whereas Mill's definition applies only to empirical chance, Cournot also takes into consideration chance in the purely logical domain. Cournot, too, at first had only empirical chance in view, and gave the definition "events brought about by the combination or encounter of other events belonging to series independent of each other" (*Les événements amenés par la combinaison ou la rencontre d'autres événements qui appartiennent à des séries indépendantes les unes des autres*) (Cournot, *Essai sur les fondements de nos connaissances*, nouvelle éd. 1912, p. 39), but he later found its range of application too narrow and revised it. The revised definition includes not only teleological [p. 148] [コマ 81] and causal chance but chance of ground as well, and it is apt. In this lies the superiority of Cournot's definition.

In any case, chance is defined as encounter or Encounter, as *sōgū* (遭遇) or *kaikō* (邂逅). The character 偶 in *gūzen* (偶然) carries the meaning of a pair, of facing, of union. It has the same sense as 遇, "to meet", and means meeting. An even number (*gūsū*, 偶數) is a number based on one and one meeting and becoming two, and the 偶 of *gūzen* is the 偶 of *gūza* (偶坐), sitting face to face, and of *haigū* (配偶), a spouse. The core meaning of contingency is the Encounter of A and B that negates the necessity of the law of identity, "A is A". We can, I think, define contingency as "the Encounter of two independent elements" (獨立なる二元の邂逅). Aristotle, too, sometimes uses the word "Encounter" (σύμπτωμα, *symptōma*) in place of chance (Aristoteles, *Phys.* II, 8, 199a; *De caelo* II, 8, 289b; *Rhet.* I, 9, 1367b, etc.). And, as Schopenhauer also pointed out (Schopenhauer, *Sämtliche Werke*, hrsg. v. Deussen, I, S. 550, III, S. 196), σύμ-πτωμα, συμ-βεβηκός, *con-tingens*, *ac-cidens* and *Zu-fall* all set out clearly, through their prefixes, the contact of two elements.[^c2s15-3] *Tychē* (τύχη) comes from *tynchanein* (τυγχάνειν), "to hit". "Chance" comes from *cadentia* and *hasard* from *casus*, so that both have their source in *cadere*, and *cadere*, "to fall", has the structure of *in......cadere*, "to fall onto ......", as when "a stone falls onto the head of a passer-by"; [p. 149] like *Zu-fall*, it makes sense only through relativity. I have already noted that Descartes, too, uses the expression "to fall by chance into the truth" (*casu incidam in veritatem*) (see p. 51). This *in-cidere*, the source of the word "incident", comes, needless to say, from *in-cadere*. That an apple *fell* by chance into Newton's field of vision, and that, when the Eitai Bridge collapsed, Miyokichi *fell* by chance into the boat of Shinsuke of Chijimiya,[^c2s15-4] must be said to carry a symbolic significance. *Fortuitus*, likewise, rests like *fortuna* on *fors* and comes from *ferre*, and this *ferre*, "to bring", also shows the structure of *in......ferre*, "to bring to ......", and so expresses relativity. Chance is also called *tekizen* (適然), and 適 is glossed as "to correspond is called 適", so that 適 clearly speaks of relativity.[^c2s15-5] Other Japanese words for chance, such as *yukiatari-battari*, *meguriawase*, *shiawase* and *magure-atari*, likewise hint at relative positive contingency.[^c2s15-6]

That contingency takes a marked form in the Encounter of two elements, A and B, was recognised alike by Plato in the *Symposium* and by Schopenhauer in "The Fate of the Individual".[^c2s15-7] Consider the legend in the *Kojiki*[^c2s15-8] in which the two gods Izanagi [p. 150] [コマ 82] and Izanami, saying "You go round from the right and *meet* me, I will go round from the left and *meet* you", "went round the heavenly pillar and *met*". Whether or not this was a rite performed since ancient times in India is a separate question; it is a primitive event extremely rich in symbolism. Genji and Utsusemi, too, "*met by chance*" (*wakuraba ni yuki-au*) at the Ōsaka barrier.[^c2s15-9] Or again: "I asked K whether he had gone out together with the young lady. K answered that it was not so. He explained that he had *met* them *by chance* at Masago-chō and so they had come back together" (Natsume Sōseki, *Kokoro*).[^c2s15-10] In short, as the general character of positive hypothetical contingency, and more broadly as the character of contingency itself in general, we witness the meaning structure of the *Encounter* of *two independent elements*.

[^c2s15-1]: Tr. Both words are rendered "encounter". 遭遇 (*sōgū*) is a meeting that befalls one; 邂逅 (*kaikō*) is a chance meeting, often of persons.
[^c2s15-2]: Tr. Antoine-Augustin Cournot (1801-1877), French mathematician, economist and philosopher of chance.
[^c2s15-3]: Tr. The Greek and Latin prefixes *sym-*, *con-*, *ad-* (*ac-*) mean "with", "together", "to"; German *zu* means "to". *Symptōma* is "a falling together", *symbebēkos* "what goes along with", *contingens* "what touches upon", *accidens* "what falls to", *Zufall* "what falls to".
[^c2s15-4]: Tr. An episode from kabuki drama: when the Eitai Bridge in Edo collapsed (in 1807), the geisha Miyokichi falls into the boat of Shinsuke, a cloth dealer of the Chijimiya.
[^c2s15-5]: Tr. 適然 (*tekizen*) is literally "fitting so"; 適 means to fit or suit. The gloss Kuki cites, 相當曰適, is in classical Chinese form.
[^c2s15-6]: Tr. *Yukiatari-battari*: hit-or-miss, happening upon things as they come; *meguriawase*: a coming round together, fortune; *shiawase*: fortune, happiness, literally a doing or fitting together; *magure-atari*: a fluke, a lucky hit.
[^c2s15-7]: Tr. In the *Symposium*, Aristophanes tells of human beings split in two and seeking their other halves. Schopenhauer's essay is "Transzendente Spekulation über die anscheinende Absichtlichkeit im Schicksale des Einzelnen" (1851).
[^c2s15-8]: Tr. *Kojiki* (古事記, "Record of Ancient Matters", 712), the oldest Japanese chronicle. Izanagi and Izanami are the creator pair; their circling of the heavenly pillar precedes their union.
[^c2s15-9]: Tr. In the "Sekiya" ("At the Pass") chapter of *The Tale of Genji*, Genji meets Utsusemi by chance at the Ōsaka barrier. *Wakuraba ni*, an old word for "by chance", is discussed in Chapter 1, section 5.
[^c2s15-10]: Tr. Natsume Sōseki (1867-1916), novelist; *Kokoro* (1914). K is a character in the novel's third part; Masago-chō is a district of Hongō in Tokyo.

*Section 16 begins partway down p. 150 (right page of コマ 82). Section 19 begins at the top of p. 172, so this part ends at the foot of p. 171.*

### 16. The Book of Changes and chance

The guiding principle of the *Book of Changes* (*Zhouyi*)[^c2s16-1] also lies in contingency as Encounter (邂逅). The dualism of yin and yang is its essence throughout, and the unexpected Encounter of line A with line B is the foundation of the entire *Changes*. The line (爻) means [p. 151] crossing (交), that is, it means nothing other than Encounter.[^c2s16-2] The *Changes* is exchange (交易). When the *Xici zhuan*[^c2s16-3] says, "The sages had the means to see the movements under heaven and to observe their meeting and interpenetration .... therefore these are called lines",[^c2s16-v1] it is stating that the *Changes* observes, in its concreteness, the contingent crossing of two elements (二元), as a dynamic relativity, within the changes and movements of heaven, earth and human life. "The two modes give birth to the four images"[^c2s16-v6] means nothing other than the four kinds of contingent Encounter: yang line with yang line (old yang ⚌), yin line with yin line (old yin ⚏), yang line with yin line (lesser yang ⚎) and yin line with yang line (lesser yin ⚍).[^c2s16-4] Plato, besides recognising ⚌ and ⚏, recognised the Encounter of ⚊ and ⚋ as arising from the *o-me* (ἀνδρόγυνος),[^c2s16-5] and so describes three ways of Encounter in all (Platon, Symposium, 191-192), but he does not go on to distinguish ⚎ from ⚍. That the *Changes* starts from the possibility of four combinations shows a rigour sufficient for the Encounter of two elements to serve, as guiding principle, as the foundation of the whole system. "The four images give birth to the eight trigrams"[^c2s16-v7] refers to the eight kinds of chance, Qian, Kun, Zhen, Xun, Kan, Li, Gen and Dui,[^c2s16-6] that arise when the four images in turn Encounter either a yang line or a yin line. The eight trigrams then cross with the eight trigrams by chance and give birth to the sixty-four hexagrams. In the light of the fact that the complete hexagram (大成の卦), formed as Encounter is piled upon Encounter from the first line to the sixth, is called the "encountered hexagram" (遇卦),[^c2s16-7] we can see how basic a meaning relative contingency has here.

In determining good and ill fortune (吉凶), [p. 152] [コマ 83] it is these relative contingencies that carry decisive meaning: which line, yin or yang, chances to fall into each line position of a hexagram, which line comes round to meet which in the two corresponding trigrams, inner and outer, and above all what lands squarely on the "centre" (中) of a trigram, its middle place. The structure of the *Changes* is in every respect extremely simple, as is characteristic of the work of genius. The *Changes* is "simple change" (簡易), that is, it seeks to explain individual phenomena through the combination of the unchanging principles of yin and yang. That the *Changes* is "the unchanging" (不易) rests on the unchangeability and necessity of the abstract rule by which yin and yang combine. Insofar as the question is how the two elements, yin and yang, combine to produce individual concrete realities, the *Changes* is "change" (變易). And the life that lies hidden deep at the root of both the unchanging and change, and that makes the *Changes* breathe as the *Changes*, is "exchange" (交易).[^c2s16-8] The *Tuan zhuan*[^c2s16-9] says, "When heaven and earth do not interact, the ten thousand things do not flourish"[^c2s16-v8]. The ambition expressed in "The *Changes* is commensurate with heaven and earth, and so can comprehend and order the way of heaven and earth"[^c2s16-v2] brings contingency with it as its consequence.

The *Zhouyi* is clearly conscious of its own structure as positive relative contingency in the hexagrams Xian, Heng and Weiji, and above all in Shihe and Gou.[^c2s16-10] Shihe, with Zhen below and Li above, has the figure ䷔, and to understand it we must begin from its resemblance to the hexagram Yi. Yi, with Zhen below and Gen above, has the figure ䷚, [p. 153] that is, the shape of jaws moving up and down to bite. Shihe differs from Yi in that a hard yang line has come by chance into the fourth place. The *Tuan zhuan* says, "When there is something between the jaws, it is called Shihe"[^c2s16-v9], and goes on: "Biting through, and so success; the firm and the yielding are divided; movement and brightness; thunder and lightning unite and become manifest"[^c2s16-v10]. "Biting together", "the firm and the yielding are divided" and "thunder and lightning unite" are all determinations grounded in the contingent Encounter of Zhen and Li. "Biting together" rests on the visual impression of ䷔. "The firm and the yielding are divided" because Zhen is a yang trigram and so firm, whilst Li is a yin trigram and so yielding. "Thunder and lightning unite" because Zhen is thunder and Li is fire, and the fire is understood as lightning. The commentary also says, "The yielding attains the centre and moves upward"[^c2s16-v11]. This "yielding" is the yin line in the fifth place, and it moves smoothly upward because it has landed squarely on the centre, the middle place, of the outer trigram. When it says "not in its proper place" (不當位), it means that the fifth place is a yang position and yet a yin line has come there by chance. The line statements say, "Six in the third place: biting dried meat, one meets poison"[^c2s16-v12],[^c2s16-11] and biting into meat and striking poison interprets the contingency that a yin line happens to have come into the third place, although that place is a yang position. "Nine in the fourth place: biting dried meat on the bone, one gets a metal arrow"[^c2s16-v13] means that on biting into dried meat on the bone one found an arrowhead left in the bone. This chance interprets the contingency of a hard yang line having come into the fourth place, [p. 154] [コマ 84] and the interpretation is endorsed by the fact that a yang line stands in a yin position and also, of itself, by the impression that the figure ䷔ makes on the eye. The hexagram Shihe places its whole weight on the contingency of the nine in the fourth place. Likewise, "Six in the fifth place: biting dried meat, one gets yellow gold"[^c2s16-v14] interprets the contingency that a yin line has come into the fifth place, which is a yang position and the centre of the trigram Li.

Next, Gou, with Xun below and Qian above, has the form ䷫. The *Tuan zhuan* says, "Gou is encounter; the yielding encounters the firm"[^c2s16-v15]. Since "to meet without expecting it is called encounter"[^c2s16-v16], in the hexagram Gou a single yin line encounters pure yang by chance, one yielding line happening upon (*kaikō*, 邂逅) five firm ones. Gou (姤) is none other than the 遘 of Encounter, *kaikō* (邂逅).[^c2s16-12] The meaning of Encounter, however, is not confined to the hexagram Gou. The Encounter of two elements is the essence of every hexagram and the foundation of the *Changes* as a whole. This must be the sense of the *Tuan zhuan*'s "Heaven and earth encounter each other, and all kinds of things become manifest",[^c2s16-v3] and the same holds for the *Xici zhuan*'s "Heaven and earth intermingle and the ten thousand things are transformed and refined; male and female join their essences and the ten thousand things are transformed and born".[^c2s16-v4] The so-called corresponding lines (應爻)[^c2s16-13] are likewise simply one manifestation of "heaven and earth encounter each other", "heaven and earth intermingle" and "male and female join their essences". The contingent relation between the third line and the top line, for instance, is a correspondence of this kind. In the hexagram Kui[^c2s16-14] we read: "Nine at the top: opposed and alone. One sees a pig covered in mud, a cartload of ghosts. First one draws the bow, afterwards one unstrings it. No robber, a suitor for marriage. Going on, one meets rain, and then good fortune".[^c2s16-v5] [p. 155] Kui, with Dui below and Li above, takes the figure ䷥. The nine at the top and the six in the third place came round to each other by chance in the first place, and because their meeting is chance the nine at the top harbours a vague doubt towards the six in the third place. On its way from the top to the third place it happens upon the trigram Kan ☵, and Kan is water, that is, rain. "Going on, one meets rain, and then good fortune" means that it is good fortune for the nine at the top to meet rain by chance on its way to the six in the third place, for rain washes even doubt away and dissolves it: "the many doubts vanish"[^c2s16-v17]. This correspondence is only one example, but whatever case we take, the *Zhouyi* in its concrete structure shows throughout the structure of positive relative contingency as Encounter. The Encounter of the two elements, yin and yang, must be the whole life of the *Zhouyi*.

[^c2s16-2]: Tr. Kuki glosses 爻 (*yao*, a line of a hexagram) by the graphically similar 交 (*jiao*, crossing, intercourse, exchange). 交易 below is "exchange" in this sense.
[^c2s16-3]: Tr. The *Xici zhuan* (繫辭傳), the "Commentary on the Appended Phrases", one of the commentaries attached to the *Changes*.

[^c2s16-6]: Tr. The eight trigrams: Qian (乾, heaven), Kun (坤, earth), Zhen (震, thunder), Xun (巽, wind), Kan (坎, water), Li (離, fire), Gen (艮, mountain), Dui (兌, lake).

[^c2s16-9]: Tr. The *Tuan zhuan* (彖傳), the "Commentary on the Judgements" of the hexagrams.
