### 17. Simultaneous contingency and successive contingency

Among the forms of positive relative contingency, the positive contingency of ground belongs purely to the domain of logic and so has nothing to do with temporal determination. The other two, the empirical forms of positive contingency, are correlations that lack causality and purposiveness, and for them, consequently, the temporal moment has decisive meaning. [p. 156] [コマ 85] This is why chance is called *toki no hazumi*, "a bounce of *time*".[^c2s17-1] When the *Xici zhuan* says, "The six lines intermingle; they are only the things of their *time*",[^c2s17-v1] it too is saying that the contingency with which the six lines come together depends on time. In the Old Testament book of Ecclesiastes we likewise read: "Again I turned myself about and looked under the sun: the swift do not win the race, the strong do not win the battle, the wise do not obtain food, the quick-witted do not obtain treasure, the knowledgeable do not obtain favour. All that befalls human beings is a thing of *time*, a thing of *chance*. And human beings do not know their time. As fish are caught in the evil net and birds in the snare, so the people of the world too, when the *time* of misfortune comes upon them unforeseen, are caught by that misfortune" (chapter 9, verses 11-12). And in Natsume Sōseki's *Kōjin*:[^c2s17-2] "When I began to write this letter, your brother was sleeping soundly. Now, as I finish it, he is again sleeping soundly. It strikes me as strange that I began writing *by chance* at a *time* when your brother was asleep and finish writing *by chance* at a *time* when he is asleep. Somewhere in me I have the feeling that if he never woke from this sleep, how happy he would be" (*Kōjin*, "Jinrō", 52). Nor should we think it strange that the *Kojiki*,[^c2s17-3] when it comes to expressing contingency, has no more than the word "time". For example: "So, having been driven out, he descended to Torikami on the upper reaches of the Hi in the land [p. 157] of Izumo. At this very *time*, chopsticks came floating down that river. Susanoo, thinking there must be people upstream, went up in search of them, and there were an old man and an old woman, the two of them, weeping with a young girl set between them" (vol. 1). "Then Haya-Susanoo drew the ten-fist sword that he wore at his side and cut the serpent to pieces, and the river Hi turned to blood as it flowed. Now, at the *time* he cut its middle tail, the edge of his sword broke. Thinking this strange, he pierced and split the tail with the point of his sword and looked, and there was the great sword *tsumugari*. He took this great sword and, thinking it a wondrous thing, reported it to Amaterasu" (vol. 1). "So at the *time* he arrived at the village of Kohata, he met a beautiful maiden at the crossroads. The Emperor asked the maiden, 'Whose child are you?', and she answered, 'I am the daughter of Wani no Hifure no Ōmi, and my name is Miyanushi Yakawaehime'" (vol. 2).

Empirical positive contingency, standing on the horizon of time, has *simultaneity* or *succession* as its constitutive determination. This is why Mill[^c2s17-4] spoke of things that "coexist, or one follows the other, by chance" (see p. 236). The three examples from the *Kojiki* are all simultaneous contingency. There is also, however, successive contingency. That a white fish leapt into the boat when King Wu of Zhou reached Mengjin and crossed [p. 158] [コマ 86] the river, and that a sea bass leapt into the boat when Kiyomori was at sea on his way to Kumano, together make up a successive contingency.[^c2s17-5] Satomi Ton[^c2s17-6] has a short story entitled "An Unfortunate Chance". A nervous woman throws a glass bottle out of a train window just as the train is coming onto a bridge over a busy street, and she thinks she hears a baby cry out in the street below. A month or two later, when she has gone to the public bath, the glass of the skylight in the ceiling breaks and the shards very nearly fall on her naked body. From then on, the story goes, her worry that the bottle she threw from the train window might have injured someone grows until she goes mad. That she threw a glass bottle from the train window and that the glass window in the ceiling broke together make up a successive contingency. The correspondence (符合) between dream and reality is also a successive contingency: one night we dream of someone we had never thought of at all, and the next day that person comes to visit, and this is nothing other than a successive contingency.

Insofar as the meaning of positive relative contingency lies, as dynamic relativity, in encounter and Encounter (*sōgū kaikō*, 遭遇邂逅), however, the meaning of contingency shows itself more clearly in simultaneity than in succession. [p. 159] Moreover, successive contingency always forms a compound whose basis is simultaneous contingency. Taken on its own, that a white fish leapt into King Wu's boat is already a contingency: between the course of King Wu's boat and the leap of the white fish there is a simultaneous contingency. It is precisely because it was a contingency that the *Shiji* records, "King Wu bent down, took it, and offered it in sacrifice" (武王俯取以祭). Likewise, that a sea bass leapt into Kiyomori's boat is already in itself a contingency, or, in the words of the *Heike monogatari*, "the blessing of the Gongen" (權現の御利生).[^c2s17-7] A successive contingency is constituted where these two simultaneous contingencies become its basis and are further placed in relation to each other. In the case of the woman who went mad, too, that the train was just coming onto the bridge over a crowded street at the moment she threw the bottle from the window is by itself already an "unfortunate chance", since a minute or two sooner or later it would not have happened. That the glass window in the ceiling broke just when she was at the public bath is also an "unfortunate chance". And it was where these two simultaneous "unfortunate chances" became the basis and formed a further compound that there arose the single successive "unfortunate chance" that drove her to madness. In the dream example, too, to dream of someone one had never thought of at all is a teleological contingency for the dreamer. [p. 160] [コマ 87] That someone one had never thought of should come to visit is, in turn, a teleological contingency for the master of the house. These two simultaneous contingencies become the basis, and a single successive contingency is constituted in the form of a correspondence between dream and reality.

Let us make the role of simultaneous contingency in the constitution of successive contingency clearer still. The two or more simultaneous contingencies that are the constitutive moments of a successive contingency must show a marked similarity in the character of the events. Successive contingency in fact takes the form of something like a mere repetition of one and the same simultaneous contingency, and it is in the repeated offering of a single simultaneous contingency that the particular impressive force of successive contingency lies. In the examples just given, the single contingency "a fish leapt into the boat of a ruler or a general" was repeated twice with identity. Likewise the single contingency "glass was broken in a dangerous place" was repeated twice with identity, and the single contingency "the appearance of an unexpected person A" was repeated twice, once in dream and once in reality. Naruse Mukyoku's "Dialogue on Chance"[^c2s17-8] gives an example of the same kind. He met an actress by chance at Arashiyama in Kyoto. That actress's friend, also an actress, turned out [p. 161] to be an actress he had once got to know by chance at a dance hall. Then, shortly afterwards and again by chance, a certain book came up, and the man to whom that book is dedicated turned out to be the author of a book entitled *The Actress* (Naruse Mukyoku, *Gūzen mondō* 444-450). Here the single contingency "running into an actress" is repeated three times with identity.

When the simultaneous contingencies that are the constitutive moments of a successive contingency have, in themselves, a fairly strong impressive force, the successive contingency does not need many repetitions to be constituted, and the examples above show that two or three suffice. When, however, the constitutive simultaneous contingencies have in themselves no more than a weak impressive force, the successive contingency needs many repetitions before it is constituted. Consider the great earthquakes of the last ten years or so.[^c2s17-9] The Kantō earthquake fell on 1 September, the Tajima earthquake on 23 May, the Tango earthquake on 7 March and the Izu earthquake on 26 November. Nine and one, five, two and three, three and seven, and one, one, two and six all add up to ten. That the Kantō earthquake occurred on 1 September, that is, on a day whose month and day add up to ten, is [p. 162] [コマ 88] certainly a simultaneous contingency, but as a single isolated fact it hardly deserves notice. When, on the other hand, the single contingency "an earthquake occurs on a date whose month and day add up to ten" is repeated several times with identity, a successive contingency of fairly strong impressive force is constituted. Again, suppose that someone sets out on a journey on the fourth of the month, that when he boards the train it leaves from platform no. 4, that the carriage he boards happens to be carriage no. 4, that his fellow passengers number four in all, and that the porter's number is also 4. In this case "person A runs into the number four" is a contingency of weak impressive force, but where that single simultaneous contingency is repeated in succession several times, a contingency is constituted that embodies sufficient impressive force. In cases like this, then, where a single simultaneous contingency has only weak force and becomes forceful as a successive contingency only when it has been repeated several times, it cannot be denied that the successiveness of contingency acquires a relatively independent meaning. Even so, the indispensable condition for a successive contingency to be constituted remains that each of its individual events be a simultaneous contingency. If all the factors lacked any contingency [p. 163] of their own, no successive contingency could be constituted. In the present example, if the person had deliberately chosen the number four on every occasion, there would, it goes without saying, no longer be any room for a successive contingency to arise.

In that a single thing is repeated several times with identity, contingency of this kind is, to let Proust, the author of *In Search of Lost Time*, describe it, something of which one can say: "They exist simultaneously in the present and the past, not actual but real, not abstract but ideal .... A moment freed from the order of time creates again in us the human being freed from the order of time, and makes us feel that moment" (Marcel Proust, *Le Temps retrouvé*, II, p. 16). Successive contingency of this kind may be said to be the contingency that we gave earlier as examples of positive contingency of ground (see pp. 59-63), the recurring decimal 142857, 142857 ..., personal names whose characters have eighteen, eighteen, eighteen ... strokes, or a rhyme held throughout on i, i, i, i, appearing within time. Fujitsubo, Utsusemi, Yūgao, the Rokujō Lady, Murasaki, Suetsumuhana, Gen no Naishi, Oborozukiyo no Naishi, Aoi, Hanachirusato, the Akashi Lady, the Saigū Consort, Asagao and Tamakazura are all, for Hikaru Genji, "happy chances" repeated with identity.[^c2s17-10] The twelve [p. 164] [コマ 89] initials V, Aⁿ, Aᵈ, M, Mⁱ, Aˡ, Aⁱⁿᵉ, Aᵖᵍ, Mᵈᵉ, C, G, A that Stendhal wrote with his stick in the sand on the shore of Lake Albano are all, for him, "unhappy chances" repeated with identity.[^c2s17-11] We may also, without difficulty, think of recurrent metaphysical time, such as transmigration, as a successive contingency constituted when a single simultaneous contingency is repeated an infinite number of times with identity, "again and again" (πάλιν καὶ πάλιν) (see Kuki, "Metaphysical Time", in the collection of essays for Dr Tomonaga's sixtieth birthday).[^c2s17-12] Successive contingency is in fact recurrent contingency. Through the identity that repetition carries, moreover, contingency of this kind takes on the mode of "the necessity of chance", and it opens a prospect that presses close to the concept of fate.

Cournot[^c2s17-13] gives the following example of chance (Cournot, *Traité de l'enchaînement des idées fondamentales dans les sciences et dans l'histoire*, nouvelle éd., 1922, p. 67-68). I want to weigh a pebble that my foot happened to strike, but I have only a kilogram balance to hand. Weighing it, I find that it lies between three and four kilos, and I write the figure 3 on a slip of paper. I buy a hectogram balance, weigh the part that the kilogram balance could not measure, and it comes to 3, so I write 3 to the right of the first 3. There is clearly no [p. 165] necessary connection of any kind between the figure first obtained and the figure obtained now, for there can be no connection between, on one side, the cause that gave this pebble the weight it has at the moment I weigh it and, on the other, the reason that led the French legislators to fix the kilogram as the unit of weight and to divide it decimally. In fixing the figure for hectograms, then, I do not expect to find any particular one among the ten figures of decimal numeration. If I obtain the figure 3 again, that is certainly a strange combination, but there is no necessary link in it and the encounter is merely contingent. I can go on to fix the figure for decagrams, and then for grams, decigrams and centigrams, and finally for milligrams, and so obtain a row of seven figures, but I do not expect to encounter any regularity in it. If I were to obtain the figure 3 all seven times, then precisely that extremely rare case would have occurred whose possibility is only once in 60,480 times. It would be like putting 60,480 different Chinese characters all jumbled together [p. 166] [コマ 90] in a single jar and happening to pull out the very character one wanted. It is a very strange case, but since there can be no connection between the weight of this pebble picked up by the roadside and the establishment of the units of weights and measures and of decimal graduation, however strange it is, it is chance. In Cournot's example, the combination of seven 3s is in itself a positive contingency of ground, but for the person weighing it appears within time as a successive contingency, three, three, three, three, three, three, three. Cournot then gives a case in which this same contingency can be necessity just as it stands. This time my foot has not stumbled on a pebble; instead a vessel filled with mercury has come into my hands. Even if the mercury weighs the same strange amount as the pebble, a completely different conclusion is drawn. Arithmetic tells us that one third, converted into a decimal, becomes the recurring decimal 0.3333333 ..., and so I conclude that this mercury was one third of 10 kilograms of mercury. Presumably some physicist somewhere weighed 10 kilograms of mercury with great exactness and divided it into three equal parts for some comparative experiment, and what he set aside as one of these [p. 167] must have come into my hands. The combination of seven 3s then becomes necessary. Successive contingency, that is, recurrent contingency, carries in this way the possibility of reversing into necessity.

Bergson,[^c2s17-14] in his book *Laughter*, discusses the comic quality of repetition. "Suppose I meet in the street one day a friend I have not seen for a long time. There is nothing comic in that situation at all. But if on that same day I meet him again, and then a third and a fourth time, in the end the two of us will laugh together at the 'coincidence' (符合)" (Bergson, *Le rire*, p. 91). This is the cheerful laughter of divine wisdom at the play of successive contingency. Yet is it true that running into a friend in the street, quite unexpectedly, "has nothing comic about it at all"? Is there not already a "coincidence" in it, and some degree of comic quality? It must be that the slight comic quality accompanying a simultaneous contingency has grown in geometric progression through successive repetition.

In the above, I think, we have made clear the relation between simultaneous and successive contingency, and in particular that simultaneous contingency has a basic meaning and takes part in the constitution of successive contingency. On the basis of this [p. 168] [コマ 91] point we could also go on to discuss the presentness that is the temporal character of contingency, but I shall not take that problem up here and leave it until later (see chapter 3, section 9).

[^c2s17-1]: Tr. 時のはずみ, literally "a bounce (rebound, impetus) of time", the ordinary Japanese phrase for something that happens on the spur of the moment.

[^c2s17-8]: Tr. Naruse Mukyoku, scholar of German literature at Kyoto Imperial University.

### 18. Contingency and spatio-temporal determination

We have made clear that the principal moment of positive relative contingency in the empirical world is simultaneity, and simultaneity, by its very essence, points to spatiality. In other words, contingency in the eminent sense lies at the point where two or more causal series intersect, and it is constituted "here and now" (*hic et nunc*),[^c2s18-1] that is, it is determined to a spatial "here" and a temporal "now". In the passages from the *Kojiki* quoted earlier, too, temporal determination coexists with spatial determination: spatial determinations such as "Torikami on the upper reaches of the Hi in the land of Izumo" or "the village of Kohata" cannot be dispensed with if empirical contingency is to be constituted.

The role of spatio-temporal determination in empirical positive contingency can also be seen easily from the structure of so-called directional divination.[^c2s18-2] To judge good and ill fortune by direction is nothing other than to try to understand the good and ill fortune one is to encounter by reducing it [p. 169] to the contingency of direction. In this case, however, the concrete content of a direction is not the direction itself. It is direction as determined by time. It is moving towards a certain space at a certain time that is judged good or ill for a certain particular person. Consequently, even where moving house in a certain direction has been judged unlucky, one need only change the time, and the concrete content of the direction changes with it, so that one may move in the same direction without harm. So long as the time is not changed, one is required to choose, at that same time, a direction with a different content. Directional divination, in short, constructs positive contingency purely intentionally, by means of space and time, and then mobilises every conceivable good and ill fortune to bring about the fulfilment of meaning (意味充實)[^c2s18-3] of this positive contingency constructed a priori. It is said, for example, that when Prince Itō Hirobumi moved in early January of Meiji 41 〔1908〕 from the Sōrōkaku at Ōiso to the Onshikan at Ōmori, he moved in a direction of great misfortune, and that this brought about his killing at Harbin the following year.[^c2s18-4] The reasoning runs as follows. Prince Itō was born in the old ninth month of Tenpō 12 〔1841〕. Tenpō 12 is a *kanoto-ushi* year, whose year star is Six White, and the old ninth month of an *ushi* year has Six White as its month star.[^c2s18-5] Prince Itō's natal star is therefore Six [p. 170] [コマ 92] White by both year star and month star. Now, January of Meiji 41 still belongs to the old twelfth month of the previous year, so that it is Meiji 40 that is in question. Meiji 40 is a year with Three Jade in the central palace; it is also a *hinoto-hitsuji* year, and the old twelfth month of a *hitsuji* year is a month with Three Jade in the central palace. Ōmori lies north-east of Ōiso, that is, in the direction Gen, and in a year of Three Jade and a month of Three Jade, Six White has come to Gen. By moving house towards the Six White of the year star and the Six White of the month star, Prince Itō committed the great misfortune of *honmeisatsu*. Gen, moreover, lies diametrically opposite the direction (south-west) of *hitsuji*, the branch of that year, and so is the direction of great misfortune called *saiha*. In short, by moving house in this direction in January of Meiji 41, that is, in the old twelfth month belonging to Meiji 40, Prince Itō, whose natal star is Six White, committed *honmeisatsu* and *saiha* together. Moving house in the same year and month, he would have been safe had he chosen any direction other than Gen. And since the concrete character of the direction Gen is determined by time, he could have moved to the same Gen without touching great misfortune had he only changed the time of the move. The contradiction in principle of directional divination, and its basic delusion, lie in its attempt to construct primordial fact a priori; yet insofar as it regards space and time as the sole determinants of empirical positive contingency, [p. 171] it may also be said to touch on one side of the truth.

The decisive meaning of spatiality and temporality in empirical positive contingency can be clearly recognised in the following case as well: "As driver Kosaka Masao and his assistant Ri Fukuhō were carrying the liquor merchant Kawamura Hiroshi in car Hyōgo 4252 towards Higashi-Naniwa, at the *instant* they were about to cross the Hanshin railway level crossing (*about 130 metres west of Amagasaki stop*), a two-car up train, nos. 307 and 361 (driver Yamada Tetsuzō), happened to come charging along and crashed into the side of the car. The car was wrecked, driver Kosaka and assistant Ri were killed outright, the one with his forehead and the other with the back of his head crushed, and the passenger Kawamura was injured in the knee joint of his left leg" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 15 May 1930).[^c2s18-6] With a difference of one metre or of one second, this unfortunate chance need not have occurred. This is why Aristotle, speaking of contingency, says "at some place and at some time" (καὶ ποὺ καὶ ποτέ)[^c2s18-7] (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Δ. 30, 1025a 22).

[^c2s18-1]: Tr. Latin, "here and now".
[^c2s18-2]: Tr. 方位判斷 (*hōi handan*), divination by the direction of a move or journey reckoned against the calendar, part of the Nine Star (九星) system of Japanese calendrical lore.
[^c2s18-3]: Tr. 意味充實, the Japanese rendering of the phenomenological term "fulfilment of meaning" (*Bedeutungserfüllung*).
[^c2s18-4]: Tr. Itō Hirobumi (1841-1909), first prime minister of Japan, was assassinated at Harbin on 26 October 1909. The Sōrōkaku was his villa at Ōiso; the Onshikan was his residence at Ōmori.
[^c2s18-5]: Tr. Nine Star terms: the year star (年家) and month star (月家) are the stars governing a year and a month; Six White (六白) and Three Jade (三碧) are two of the nine stars, and "in the central palace" (中宮) names the star occupying the centre of the chart; the natal star (本命) is the star of one's birth; *honmeisatsu* (本命殺) is the misfortune of moving towards one's own natal star; *saiha* (歲破, "year breaker") is the direction opposite the year's branch. *Kanoto-ushi* and *hinoto-hitsuji* are years of the sexagenary cycle; Gen (艮) is the north-east.
[^c2s18-6]: Tr. Hanshin is the Osaka-Kobe electric railway. The readings of the names 李馥法 (Ri Fukuhō) and 川村廣 (Kawamura Hiroshi) are not certain.
[^c2s18-7]: Tr. Greek, "both somewhere and at some time".

### 19. History and chance

[p. 172] [コマ 93] What we call irrationality and contingency within history rest on the concrete determination of time and space. As Poincaré[^c2s19-1] says, that King John Lackland[^c2s19-2] once passed by this spot is, for Carlyle the historian,[^c2s19-3] a precious reality that he would not exchange for all the theories in the world, whilst for Bacon the natural scientist[^c2s19-4] it is a matter of no consequence, since John Lackland will never pass this way again (Poincaré, *La Science et l'Hypothèse*, p. 168). Natural science concerns itself only with the rationality and necessity of law, or else with its probability. For history, by contrast, the irrationality and contingency of mere fact possess a life that cannot be thrown away. Even with something like a storm, natural science seeks some lawlike necessity or probability, taking into account as causes the distribution of land and sea, changes in weather, temperature and air pressure, and so on, so that if these definite conditions are fulfilled, the same storm phenomenon is produced any number of times, necessarily or with the greatest [p. 173] probability. Even where the centre of a storm always lies at a fixed point and its occurrence is confined to a fixed season, the concern of natural science lies in the abstract periodicity of the storm phenomenon. The necessity and universality of the law must be made clear in the abstract, and it is on this basis that the future can be predicted and defensive installations planned. Saying probability instead of necessity makes no difference whatever to the idea. For history, on the other hand, what is in question is the storm that arose in such-and-such a year and month, at such-and-such a place, in its once-only character and in its full concreteness. That a storm arose near Takashima in Hizen on the first day of the intercalary seventh month of Kōan 4[^c2s19-5] is, for history, a matter of concern in its full concreteness. At the time of the Mongol invasion the Retired Emperor Kameyama presented a votive prayer to the Ise Shrine and sought to take the national crisis upon his own person,[^c2s19-6] and Hōjō Tokimune showed his firm resolve by beheading the Yuan envoys at Tatsunokuchi.[^c2s19-7] That a storm arose near Takashima on that day, and that as a result an enemy army of several tens of thousands perished in the sea, is a chance that history singles out for special mention as "the divine wind of Ise". The irrationality of history, in other words, rests on the once-only determination of space and time, and it is against the background of this concrete determination that contingency appears. "By chance, on the day of a great festival in the town, among [p. 174] [コマ 94] the crowds gathered there, at the moment when the two of us first met, we were at once, just as we were, bound together most closely; and since then nothing has been as close to the two of us as we were to each other" (Montaigne, *Essays*, I, trans. Sekine Hideo, pp. 347-348).[^c2s19-8] What may be seen as the extreme limit of historical irrationality is the following case: "The man called Murakoshi Sanjūrō is the man who happened to be passing just in front when the famous Akechi Mitsuhide was stabbed with a bamboo spear. ...... Although he was resigned to being human refuse with nothing whatever to do with history, because he was by chance the bystander of a certain event he unexpectedly came to be noticed by history" (Tatsuno Yutaka, *Riyan*, p. 201).[^c2s19-9]

Hegel, on the one hand, presupposes as a truth that an ultimate purpose rules in history, that there is reason in history, and under that presupposition he says that "philosophical contemplation has no intention other than to eliminate the contingent" (Hegel, *Vorlesungen über die Philosophie der Weltgeschichte*, hrsg. v. Lasson, I, S. 5). On the other hand, however, he also recognises the right of chance: "With regard to spirit and its activity too, we must guard against being led astray by the well-meant effort of rational cognition into pointing out as necessary phenomena that have the character of contingency, [p. 175] or into trying to construct them, as it is called, a priori. Language, for example, is so to speak the body of thought, and yet in language chance undoubtedly plays a marked role. The same holds for law, art and other forms" (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 145, Zusatz, S. 195). And again: "It is reason itself that acknowledges that contingency, contradiction and semblance have their own sphere and right, though a restricted sphere and right" (Hegel, *Grundlinien der Philosophie des Rechts*, hrsg. v. Lasson, S. 173). According to Cournot,[^c2s19-10] the distinction between history and science, between the historical element and the scientific element, is more essential than Bacon thought. The distinction does not depend on the fact that two different faculties, memory and reason, exist in the human mind. Even if human beings had never used memory to write history, nor reason to do science, the course of phenomena would still contain, on the one hand, constant laws, so that systematic coherence is possible, and on the other, room for the products of chance, that is, for the products of the contingent conjunction of various causal series independent of one another. The concept of chance, in short, has its foundation in nature [p. 176] [コマ 95] itself, and does not depend solely on the weakness of the human mind. The same holds for the distinction between the historical datum and the theoretical datum. An intelligence able to trace the series of phases through which the solar system has passed much further back than we can would still encounter, as we do, primitive, capricious, contingent facts, and would have to acknowledge them under the name of the historical datum. The historical datum, that is, means the result of the contingent concurrence of causes that acted in a still more distant past (Cournot, *Essai sur les fondements de nos connaissances*, Chap. XX, *Du contraste de l'histoire et de la science*, nouvelle édition, p. 460).

[^c2s19-1]: Tr. Henri Poincaré (1854-1912), French mathematician and philosopher of science. Section 19 begins at the top of p. 172 (right page of コマ 93).
[^c2s19-2]: Tr. King John of England (r. 1199-1216), known as John Lackland.
[^c2s19-3]: Tr. Thomas Carlyle (1795-1881), Scottish historian and essayist.
[^c2s19-4]: Tr. Francis Bacon (1561-1626), English philosopher and advocate of the inductive natural sciences.
[^c2s19-5]: Tr. The date falls in 1281, during the second Mongol invasion of Japan. Takashima is an island off Hizen province in northern Kyushu.
[^c2s19-6]: Tr. Kameyama (1249-1305), retired emperor at the time of the invasions. The Ise Shrine is the principal shrine of the imperial ancestral deity Amaterasu.
[^c2s19-7]: Tr. Hōjō Tokimune (1251-1284), regent of the Kamakura shogunate. Tatsunokuchi was an execution ground near Kamakura.
[^c2s19-8]: Tr. Montaigne is describing his first meeting with Étienne de La Boétie, in the essay on friendship. Sekine Hideo (1895-1987) was a Japanese translator of Montaigne.
[^c2s19-9]: Tr. Akechi Mitsuhide (1528-1582), the general who overthrew Oda Nobunaga, was killed by peasants with a bamboo spear whilst fleeing after his defeat. Tatsuno Yutaka (1888-1964) was a scholar of French literature and essayist.
[^c2s19-10]: Tr. Antoine-Augustin Cournot (1801-1877), French mathematician, economist and philosopher of chance.

### 20. The objectivity of contingency

Chance is what brushes past us in "this place" and winks at us in "this moment". What Hegel calls the "indifferent" (*gleichgültig*) (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 250) is here sublated into a fleeting concern,[^c2s20-1] and contingency, in this sense, is nothing other than the character of an apex of concern at [p. 177] the point where indifference intersects with indifference. What structure, then, and what kind of existence does this apex, this point of intersection, possess?

Poincaré takes "the complexity of causes" (Poincaré, *Science et Méthode*, pp. 73-76) to be the principal meaning of contingency because he assumes that the point of intersection can be completely explained by the causal series that converge upon it. The same holds when Spinoza says that "a thing is called contingent only because of *the imperfection of our knowledge* (*defectus nostrae cognitionis*), and for no other reason" (Spinoza, *Ethica*, I. 33. Schol. 1), when Hume ascribes chance to "*our ignorance* (our ignorance) of the real causes of some event" (Hume, *An Enquiry concerning Human Understanding*, VI), and when Laplace[^c2s20-2] regards chance as "the expression of the *ignorance* in which we are of the true causes (*l'expression de l'ignorance*)" (Laplace, *Essai philosophique sur les probabilités*, I, Paris, Gauthier-Villars, 1921, p. 2). All of them presuppose that the point of intersection is in reality no point of intersection of indifference with indifference, being one brought about by concern, and they presuppose a complete knowledge of that point. In this way they arrived at the theory that chance is merely something subjective, nothing other than a confession of insufficient knowledge.

[p. 178] [コマ 96] Against this, however, there are two points that we must consider. First, it is of course true that a point of intersection which looks like a point of intersection of indifference with indifference has sometimes in reality been brought about by causal concern. But should we conclude from this that every point of intersection depends in advance on concern, in the way that all the knots in all the threads of a cast net converge on the single point at its crown?[^c2s20-3] Can we really argue so casually that everything called chance is two or more causes acting *together* and necessarily producing the point of intersection, or that there is no chance whatever so long as we know the *connection* among the causes of phenomena? When the special case, in which what was taken for chance in fact arose from a necessary conjunction of causes, is casually generalised into the inference that all chance is the product of the necessary conjunction of causes, is it not precisely here that we find an insufficiency of knowledge, or an immaturity of thought? We must consider that there can also be cases in which wholly independent series cross at a point of intersection. Cournot asks whether the word chance corresponds to an idea with a density of its own, whether that [p. 179] idea has an object outside us and consequences that we cannot escape, or whether it is rather no more than a "breath of the voice" (*flatus vocis*),[^c2s20-4] a deception to dress up our ignorance of the true causes. His answer is "no": "No, the word chance is not something unrelated to external reality. The word chance expresses an idea that is manifested within observable phenomena and has an efficacy that is taken into account in the governance of the world. This idea is an idea based on reason. ...... It is, that is, the idea of the actual independence (*indépendance actuelle*) and of the contingent encounter (*rencontre accidentelle*) of various chains or various series of causes" (Cournot, *Matérialisme, Vitalisme, Rationalisme*, p. 222). Suppose that a meteorite falls in a white-hot state and makes a powder magazine explode. Chance then has a share in the explosion of the powder magazine, since there is no connection whatever between the causes that determine the direction in which the meteorite advances towards the earth and its falling at a certain definite point on the earth's surface, on the one hand, and the cause that determined that explosives should be stored at exactly the point where the meteorite would one day fall, on the other. We may equally suppose that, in place of a powder magazine, the meteorite fell on a natural solfatara[^c2s20-5] or an oil source [p. 180] [コマ 97] and set it alight. There is no connection whatever between the work by which nature brought solfataras and oil sources into existence and the forces that determined the direction of the meteorite's advance and the point at which it touches the earth (Ib., pp. 47-48). Chance, in short, is nothing other than the conjunction between systems of causes and facts, each unfolding independently in its own series. Where an intelligence greater than the human differs from us on this point is merely that it makes fewer errors than human beings do, or that it never errs at all. It is simply that such an intelligence never thinks of series which in truth influence each other as independent, nor, on the contrary, imagines a linkage between causes which are in truth independent, and that it recognises with strict precision what, in the course of phenomena, belongs to the domain of necessity and what belongs to the domain of chance. Chance is by no means something subjective. Chance has objectivity. "In this sense it is true to say that chance governs the world, or rather that chance has a part, and a notable part, in the governance of the world" [p. 181] (*Il est vrai de dire en ce sens que le hasard gouverne le monde, ou plutôt qu'il a une part, et une part notable, dans le gouvernement du monde.*) (Cournot, *Essai sur les fondements de nos connaissances*, nouvelle édition, p. 47). We have thought of chance as the apex at which indifference intersects with indifference. To acknowledge the indifference of chance in the strict sense is, consequently, the ground on which we admit the objectivity of chance.

Second, even if we suppose that a point of intersection has been brought about by the concern of causality, we must bear in mind that a complete explanation and an adequate knowledge of that point of intersection are merely an idea, for the causal series that determine a point of intersection are in fact innumerable. When Aristotle says that the contingent attributes of an individual are "unlimited" (ἄπειρος)[^c2s20-6] (Met. E. 2, 1026b; Phys. II. 5), that the contingent cause is "indeterminate" (ἀόριστος) (Met. Δ. 30, 1024b; Phys. II. 5)[^c2s20-m1], and that there is no science of the contingent (Met. E. 2), he does so on the basis of the innumerability of the causal series that converge on the point of intersection and, connected with this, the innumerability of the points of intersection themselves. When Leibniz speaks, concerning contingent truths, of "detail without bounds" (*détail sans bornes*) and of "the infinite" (*infinité*) (Leibniz, *Monadologie*, 36), it is for the same [p. 182] [コマ 98] reason. The complete explanation of a single point of intersection would probably presuppose the complete explanation of an infinite number of points of intersection, and the complete explanation of an infinite number of points of intersection must be a total emptiness in which not a single point of intersection remains. To say that the contingency of points of intersection depends wholly on the subject is, in other words, to subjectivise everything, and we must then arrive at the conclusion that nothing any longer has objectivity.

[^c2s20-1]: Tr. Kuki sets 關心 (*kanshin*, "concern", whose first character means relation or involvement) against 無關心 (*mukanshin*), which renders Hegel's *gleichgültig* and is translated "indifference" throughout this section.
[^c2s20-2]: Tr. Pierre-Simon Laplace (1749-1827), French mathematician and author of the classical theory of probability.
[^c2s20-3]: Tr. A cast net (投網, *toami*) is gathered by its threads to a single top point (Kuki's 龍頭, *ryūzu*) from which it is thrown and hauled.
[^c2s20-4]: Tr. The phrase used in medieval nominalism for a universal regarded as a mere word.
[^c2s20-5]: Tr. A volcanic vent emitting sulphurous gases (Kuki's 硫氣孔).
[^c2s20-6]: Tr. *Apeiros*, "unlimited, without bound"; *aoristos*, below, "indeterminate, undefined".
[^c2s20-m1]: Tr. Kuki's reference is as printed. Book Δ, chapter 30, on the accidental, is at 1025a (1025a14-34); 1024b falls within Δ 29.

### 21. From hypothetical contingency to disjunctive contingency

Let us now take up the standpoint opposite to the one we have held so far, and try to think about the formation of the point of intersection while excluding chance as far as we can. Where A and B intersect at point C, for example where air current A and air current B rub against each other at a point on a line of discontinuity, or where man A and man B meet at a certain spot in the street, let us suppose that A has A′ as its cause and A′ has A″, that B has B′ as its cause and B′ has B″, and that A″ and B″ have S as their common cause. In that case [p. 183] the Encounter (邂逅) of A and B cannot be called chance in the strict sense. Yet S itself in turn means the point of intersection of M and N, and there is room for chance there. The causal series M M′ M″, which includes M, and the causal series N N′ N″, which includes N, can in turn be thought to have a further common cause, T. Yet T too means the point of intersection of one causal series with another, and there is room for chance there as well, whilst the two series can again be thought to have a common cause. In this way we go back to x. What, then, is this x? Whilst assuming throughout, within the domain of experience, the rule of necessity, we have pursued x as an idea "without end". When we have been able to grasp that idea beyond the "infinite", however, we must recognise that it is "primal contingency". As Schelling[^c2s21-1] says, "of it one can only say that it *is*, and not that it *necessarily is*" (*man kann von ihm nur sagen, dass es Ist, nicht, dass es notwendig Ist*); it is "the oldest primal contingency" (*der älteste Urzufall*) (Schelling, *Sämmtliche Werke*, 1856-1857, II. 1. S. 464; II. 2. S. 153).

[p. 184] [コマ 99] Nāgasena, whom we met earlier,[^c2s21-2] put a question back to Milinda: "Why are these trees not the same?"[^c2s21-v1] Milinda answered the monk, and answered himself, by saying, "What is not the same is so because the original plantings each differ."[^c2s21-v2] Yet in this answer the contingency of the trees has merely been transferred, by the law of causality, to the contingency of the seedlings. Again, when the *Madhyama Āgama* (fascicle 44, the *Parrot Sutra*) says, "Know that this deed has such a retribution",[^c2s21-v3] and when the *Tattvasiddhi* (fascicle 10, chapter on the causes of deeds) says, "The ten thousand things arise from deeds as cause",[^c2s21-3][^c2s21-v4] it looks as though contingency has been given a causal explanation, but in reality contingency has merely been extended, unresolved, infinitely back as far as "primal contingency". The problem is thus transferred from the empirical domain of hypothetical contingency to the metaphysical domain of disjunctive contingency.

[^c2s21-1]: Tr. F. W. J. Schelling (1775-1854), German idealist philosopher; *Urzufall* is the term of his late philosophy.
[^c2s21-2]: Tr. See Chapter 1, section 6, on the *Nāgasena Sūtra* (那先比丘經), the Chinese version of the dialogue between the monk Nāgasena and King Milinda.
[^c2s21-3]: Tr. The *Madhyama Āgama* (中阿含經) is the Chinese "middle-length" collection of early Buddhist discourses; the *Tattvasiddhi* (成實論) is an Indian Buddhist treatise surviving in Chinese. "Deed" renders 業, karma; "the ten thousand things" is the Chinese idiom for all things.
[^c2s21-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 此等樹木何故不同. Our translation: "Why are these trees not the same?"
[^c2s21-v2]: Tr. Kuki's text: 不同者本栽各異. Our translation: "What is not the same is so because the original plantings each differ."
[^c2s21-v3]: Tr. Kuki's text: 當知此業有如是報也 (中阿含經, 卷四四, 鸚鵡經). Our translation: "Know that this deed has such a retribution."
[^c2s21-v4]: Tr. Kuki's text: 萬物從業因生 (成實論, 卷一〇, 明業因品). Our translation: "The ten thousand things arise from deeds as cause."

## Chapter 3. Disjunctive contingency (第三章 離接的偶然) [コマ 99-166]

### 1. The meaning of disjunctive contingency

[p. 185] [コマ 99] Disjunctive contingency concerns the relation between whole and part. The whole, by the very character of being a whole, possesses absolute identity: insofar as it is determined as something complete, the whole is self-identical through and through, and necessity therefore accompanies its existence. The part, by contrast, lacks absolute self-identity by the very character of being a part. Insofar as it is a part it presupposes other parts, which is to say that it carries within itself the character of being able to be this part or that part, and it is in this that the contingency of the part lies. And since each part, while enveloped within the whole, stands in a disjunctive relation to the other parts, contingency of this kind is called disjunctive [p. 186] [コマ 100] contingency.

A triangle, for example, is an acute triangle, a right triangle or an obtuse triangle. So long as no other triangle can be conceived, the triangle is regarded as extensionally complete, as one whole, and the acute triangle has contingency in relation to the triangle as a whole in that, instead of being an acute triangle, it could just as well be a right or an obtuse triangle. Water, likewise, is liquid, solid or gas. So long as no other state can be thought, water viewed extensionally can be considered a whole that contains parts, and water as liquid has contingency in relation to water as a whole in that, instead of being liquid, it could just as well be solid or gas. The part must, of course, have a sufficient ground for its own existence. Yet instead of that part existing by that ground, it is also *possible* that another part should exist by another ground, and it is here that disjunctive contingency lies.

The Japanese word *tamatama* is sometimes written with the character 儻, and 儻 is glossed as "a word of what may be so", that is, as meaning "or" or "perhaps".[^c3s1-1] The contingency the character 儻 signifies must therefore be the contingency of the case "A, or B, or C, or D", in other words disjunctive contingency. [p. 187] Take the poem "In my heart I do not forget you, yet the days when *tamatama* I do not see you are many, and the months have passed"[^c3s1-v1] (Man'yō, book 4).[^c3s1-2] Here the year as a whole contains three hundred and sixty-five days as its parts. Suppose they met on a certain day of a certain month. That day corresponds to one of the three hundred and sixty-five parts, and so it carries the possibility of having been another day, another part: it could have been day A, or day B, or day C, or day D. Yet it was, *tamatama*, this particular day of this particular month, and that it was so has contingency in relation to the year as a whole. This contingency is nothing other than disjunctive contingency.

Let us give a few more examples of disjunctive contingency. "Though *wakuraba ni* I am human, though I too have been made like other men, on my shoulders I throw only rags, a sleeveless jacket of cloth without cotton padding, hanging in tatters like sea-pine, ...... Is it so helpless as this, the way of the world?"[^c3s1-v2] (Man'yō, book 5).[^c3s1-3] That *wakuraba ni* here means disjunctive contingency becomes clear if we compare it with a poem such as "If only I can be merry in this world, in the life to come I would gladly become an insect or a bird"[^c3s1-v3] (Man'yō, book 3).[^c3s1-4] Or again: "This nembutsu gate is spoken solely for those people who, though they are companions who should think of the life to come again and again with no other mind, turn to pointless mistaken causes, practise miscellaneous practices without regard to the time or to themselves, and, though this time they were *tamatama* born into the human realm so hard to receive, throw away the vow of Amida so hard to meet, return again to the old home of the three evil paths, turn through the wheel of birth and death, and do not know the sorrow of passing [p. 188] [コマ 101] many hundreds of thousands of kalpas" (Wago tōroku, Nenbutsu taii).[^c3s1-5] This too, like the previous example, is a disjunctive contingency concerning the whole of life, a whole whose disjuncts are insect, bird, human and so on; within the disjunctive relation of either insect, or bird, or human, that the disjunct human was posited is disjunctive contingency.

A further example: "To begin with, the teachings of the Buddha's whole lifetime are divided into exoteric schools and esoteric schools, Great Vehicle and Lesser Vehicle, provisional teachings and real teachings, the treatise masters and the commentators, sections and eight schools, and their doctrines run on in ten thousand differences. Some expound the purport that all dharmas are empty, some make clear the meaning that all dharmas are the true aspect, some set up the doctrine of the five distinct natures, some discuss the principle that all have Buddha-nature; each school contends for the ultimate and supreme doctrine, and each argues for its school of the most profound and correct doctrine. All these are true words of the sutras and treatises. Moreover, they are the golden words of the Tathāgata. Sometimes he preached them adjusting to the capacities of his hearers, sometimes he taught them reflecting on the times. Which is shallow and which deep? It is hard to discern right and wrong in either. That too is teaching, this too is teaching; do not hold to one-sided attachment against each other. ...... All alike are paths of liberation from birth and death. Yet now those who study that envy this, and those who recite this disparage [p. 189] that. The dull are easily confused by this, and those of shallow talent find it hard to discern. If one *tamatama* goes to one dharma and tries to accumulate merit, the disputes of the various schools at once come upon one in turn. If one thinks to range widely over all the teachings and discuss their doctrines, the life of one lifetime soon draws to its close" (Shūi Wago tōroku, Tozanjō).[^c3s1-6] Here we have the disjunctive contingency that each of the eight schools, that is, each of eight disjuncts, has in relation to Buddhism as a whole.

Again: "With which embryo cell (A or a) the two kinds of pollen cell (A and a) combine depends entirely on *chance*" (Mendel, *Zasshu shokubutsu no kenkyū*, trans. Koizumi Makoto, Iwanami Bunko, p. 74).[^c3s1-7] This is the disjunctive contingency that characterises each of the four disjuncts A|A, A|a, a|A and a|a. And again: "Buffon first of all emphasised that the probability that the angle between the orbital planes of the various planets and the plane of the ecliptic should naturally, entirely by *chance*, lie within seven and a half degrees (that is, one twenty-fourth of the greatest possible inclination of 180 degrees) is extremely small. This had already been pointed out earlier by Bernoulli. For one planet the probability of turning out so by *chance* is only one in twenty-four. Accordingly the probability that the five planets known at that time should all be so is 24⁻⁵, that is, the small figure of about one in eight million" (Arrhenius, *Shiteki ni mitaru kagakuteki uchūkan no hensen*, trans. Terada Torahiko, Iwanami [p. 190] [コマ 102] Bunko, pp. 138-139).[^c3s1-8] Contingency here, if we speak of one planet, is the disjunctive contingency that each of twenty-four disjuncts has in relation to the whole; if we speak of five planets, it is the disjunctive contingency that each of roughly eight million disjuncts, twenty-four to the fifth power, has in relation to the whole.

These examples will have made it clear that each part within a whole, as a merely possible disjunct, constitutes disjunctive contingency in relation to the necessity of the whole.

[^c3s1-1]: Tr. 儻 (Chinese *tang*) means "if" or "perchance"; the gloss Kuki cites, 或然之詞, is in classical Chinese lexical form. On *tamatama* see Chapter 1, section 5.
[^c3s1-2]: Tr. The *Man'yōshū* (萬葉集), the eighth-century anthology of Japanese poetry. The poem uses 儻 for *tamatama*.
[^c3s1-3]: Tr. From Yamanoue no Okura's "Dialogue on Poverty" (貧窮問答歌), *Man'yōshū*, book 5. *Wakuraba ni*, an old word for "by chance", is discussed in Chapter 1, section 5. Kuki glosses 作 as *nare*, "become", so the line means "I too was born a human being as others are". "Sea-pine" is *miru*, a seaweed.
[^c3s1-4]: Tr. From Ōtomo no Tabito's poems in praise of sake, *Man'yōshū*, book 3.
[^c3s1-5]: Tr. The *Wago tōroku* (和語燈錄) is a collection of the Japanese-language writings of Hōnen (1133-1212), founder of the Pure Land (Jōdo) school; *Nenbutsu taii* (念佛大意) is one of them. The text as printed has 僻胤, read here as 僻因 (*hekiin*), "mistaken cause". The three evil paths are the realms of hell, hungry ghosts and animals; a kalpa is an aeon.
[^c3s1-6]: Tr. The *Shūi Wago tōroku* (拾遺和語燈錄) is the supplementary collection to the *Wago tōroku*; *Tozanjō* (登山狀) is a letter addressed to the monks of Mount Hiei. The "eight schools" are the established schools of Nara and Heian Buddhism; "the five distinct natures" (五性各別) is a Hossō doctrine, against which "all have Buddha-nature" (悉有佛性) stands.
[^c3s1-7]: Tr. Gregor Mendel (1822-1884); *Zasshu shokubutsu no kenkyū* (雜種植物の研究) is the Japanese translation of his *Versuche über Pflanzen-Hybriden* (1866). Kuki writes each combination of pollen cell and embryo cell as a pair divided by a stroke.
[^c3s1-8]: Tr. Svante Arrhenius (1859-1927), Swedish chemist; Terada Torahiko (1878-1935), Japanese physicist and essayist. Georges-Louis Leclerc, comte de Buffon (1707-1788), French naturalist.
[^c3s1-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 心には忘れぬものを儻(たまたま)も見ざる日數多(まね)く月ぞ經にける (萬葉、卷四). Our translation: "In my heart I do not forget you, yet the days when *tamatama* (儻) I do not see you are many, and the months have passed."
[^c3s1-v2]: Tr. Kuki's text: *わくらば*に人とはあるを、人並に吾も作(なれ)るを、綿も無き布肩衣の、海松のごとわわけさがれる、襤褸(かがふ)のみ肩に打ち懸け、……斯くばかり術なきものか、世の中の道 (萬葉、卷五). Our translation: "Though *wakuraba ni* I am human, though I too have been made like other men, on my shoulders I throw only rags, a sleeveless jacket of cloth without cotton padding, hanging in tatters like sea-pine, ...... Is it so helpless as this, the way of the world?"
[^c3s1-v3]: Tr. Kuki's text: この世にし樂しくあらば來む生(よ)には蟲に鳥にも吾はなりなむ (萬葉、卷三). Our translation: "If only I can be merry in this world, in the life to come I would gladly become an insect or a bird."

### 2. Modality in general

In disjunctive contingency, plainly, the problem of contingency touches deeply on the problem of possibility. The positing of one *possible* disjunct is contingent, and because it is contingent the positing of another is also *possible*. Up to now we have treated the contingent as the opposite concept of the necessary; what relation, then, does the contingent bear to the possible? Before investigating that relation, let us first consider Modality in general, and in considering Modality [p. 191] we cannot but take Aristotle's classical treatment as our point of departure (Aristoteles, De Interpretatione 12, 13; Metaphysica Δ 5, 12).

Over against actuality or present existence we think non-actuality or non-existence. The actual is what actually is, and the non-actual is what actually is not. "What actually is" can be divided into "what is not able not to be" and "what is able not to be", which is to say that actuality contains "the necessary" (ἀναγκαῖον, *necessarium*) and "the contingent" (ἐνδεχόμενον, *contingens*). "What actually is not", likewise, can be divided into "what is able to be" and "what is not able to be", so that non-actuality contains "the possible" (δυνατόν, *possibile*) and "the impossible" (ἀδύνατον, *impossibile*).[^c3s2-1] In determining the four Modalities of the necessary, the contingent, the possible and the impossible in this way, however, we have provisionally done so from the standpoint of possibility, taking "is able to ......" and "is not able to ......" as our standard. The four Modalities ought to be determinable from the standpoint of any one of them, and so let us set them out in turn.

(1) Determination from the standpoint of *possibility*

> Possible = to be is possible (δυνατὸν εἶναι, *possibile esse*)
> [p. 192] [コマ 103] Impossible = to be is not possible (οὐ δυνατὸν εἶναι, *non possibile esse*)
> Contingent = not to be is possible (δυνατὸν μὴ εἶναι, *possibile non esse*)
> Necessary = not to be is not possible (οὐ δυνατὸν μὴ εἶναι, *non possibile non esse*)

(2) Determination from the standpoint of *impossibility*

> Impossible = to be is impossible (ἀδύνατον εἶναι, *impossibile esse*)
> Possible = to be is not impossible (οὐκ ἀδύνατον εἶναι, *non impossibile esse*)
> Necessary = not to be is impossible (ἀδύνατον μὴ εἶναι, *impossibile non esse*)
> Contingent = not to be is not impossible (οὐκ ἀδύνατον μὴ εἶναι, *non impossibile non esse*)

(3) Determination from the standpoint of *necessity*

> Necessary = to be is necessary (ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι, *necesse esse*)
> Contingent = to be is not necessary (οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι, *non necesse esse*)
> Impossible = not to be is necessary (ἀναγκαῖον μὴ εἶναι, *necesse non esse*)
> Possible = not to be is not necessary (οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον μὴ εἶναι, *non necesse non esse*)

(4) Determination from the standpoint of *contingency*

> Contingent = to be is contingent (ἐνδεχόμενον εἶναι, *contingens esse*)
> [p. 193] Necessary = to be is not contingent (οὐκ ἐνδεχόμενον εἶναι, *non contingens esse*)
> Possible = not to be is contingent (ἐνδεχόμενον μὴ εἶναι, *contingens non esse*)
> Impossible = not to be is not contingent (οὐκ ἐνδεχόμενον μὴ εἶναι, *non contingens non esse*)

Trendelenburg, for example, determines Modality from the first standpoint, that of *possibility* (Trendelenburg, Logische Untersuchungen II, 3 Aufl. S. 177-227). Lewis determines it from the second, that of *impossibility* (C. I. Lewis, *A survey of symbolic logic*, 1918, p. 292).[^c3s2-2] In his symbolic logic ~ is the sign of impossibility and - the sign of simple negation, and expressed with these signs Modality takes the following form:

> ~p ...... p is impossible
> -~p ...... p is not impossible (that is, p is possible)
> ~-p ...... that p is not, is impossible (that is, p is necessary)
> -~-p ...... that p is not, is not impossible (that is, p is not necessary)

Oskar Becker, by contrast (Oskar Becker, Zur Logik der Modalitäten, Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. [p. 194] [コマ 104] phän. F. XI. S. 510), holds that the connection of the whole is clearer, and more positive, when viewed from the third standpoint, that of *necessity*, than from the standpoint of impossibility.[^c3s2-3] We need only put •-p in place of ~p, • being the sign of necessity; Becker uses a dot-and-stroke notation (*Punkt-Strich-Notation*) that works with • and -:

> •p ...... p is necessary
> -•p ...... p is not necessary
> •-p ...... that p is not, is necessary (that is, p is impossible)
> -•-p ...... that p is not, is not necessary (that is, p is possible)

The main difference between Lewis and Becker concerns which modality, by carrying three signs, has come to occupy the position of the complex term. In Lewis it is -~-p (that p is not, is not impossible = p is not necessary = p is contingent) that carries three signs; in Becker it is -•-p (that p is not, is not necessary = p is possible). The importance of this will become clear later (see pp. 226, 231). Both Lewis and Becker, moreover, add the two cases

> [p. 195] p ...... p is true
> -p ...... p is false

which correspond, from our ontological standpoint, to "actuality" and "non-actuality", and they take all of these together as the whole of Modality.

That in fact no one attempts to view modality from the fourth standpoint, that of *contingency*, is probably due to the non-logical character of contingency itself. Yet for a logic rooted in life it should not be impossible to reduce, to some degree, the distance between the domain of logic and the immediacy of experience, and determining the other Modalities from the standpoint of contingency is something we often find in ordinary usage. "For critical philosophy to be completed as a system, ...... teleology is required as the division that makes clear the foundation of purposiveness. That what answers this requirement is the *Critique of Judgement* of 1790 needs no saying anew. That this so-called third Critique, as the crown of the system of critical philosophy that unifies and completes Kant's world-view, has given great influence both to the Romantic philosophy immediately after Kant and to the idealist philosophy of the present day is *not by chance*" (Tanabe Hajime, *Kanto no mokuteki ron*, pp. 2-3 [p. 196] [コマ 105]).[^c3s2-4] Or again: "That he, who, trying to experience as far as he could the unfree condition of the blind, sometimes seemed almost to envy the blind, became in later years truly blind, is in fact because such an attitude of mind since his boyhood had its influence, and when one thinks of it, it is *not by chance*" (Tanizaki Jun'ichirō, *Shunkinshō*, p. 27).[^c3s2-5] In both passages "is necessary" can be put in place of "is not by chance", and so they determine necessity from the standpoint of contingency. When we say "That there are *no* factories in this neighbourhood *is by chance*", we mean "It is also *possible* that there should be factories", and so we determine possibility from the standpoint of contingency. And when we say "Since facilities for scholarly research institutes are insufficient in our country, it is *not by chance* that the problem of unemployment among the intellectual class is *not* solved", we mean "That it should be solved is *impossible*", and so we determine impossibility from the standpoint of contingency.

If we now provisionally use )( as the sign of contingency (see p. 73), its relations to the other modalities are as follows:[^c3s2-6]

> )(p ...... p is contingent
> [p. 197] -)(p ...... p is not contingent (that is, p is necessary)
> )(-p ...... that p is not, is contingent (that is, p is possible)
> -)(-p ...... that p is not, is not contingent (that is, p is impossible)

If to these four we add the two cases

> p
> -p

we obtain six kinds of Modality.

Now contingency negates the necessity of existence, and necessity negates the contingency of existence, so that necessity and contingency stand in contradictory opposition. Impossibility negates the possibility of existence, and possibility negates the impossibility of existence, so that possibility and impossibility likewise stand in contradictory opposition. On the other hand, impossibility asserts the necessity of non-existence, and necessity asserts the impossibility of non-existence, and in this sense necessity and impossibility can be considered similar; the feature they share is called "apodicticity" (*apodictica*). Contingency, again, affirms the possibility of non-existence [p. 198] [コマ 106], and possibility affirms the contingency of non-existence, so that possibility and contingency can be considered similar; the feature they share is called "problematicity" (*problematica*). And the horizon of actuality and non-actuality, of present existence and non-existence, is called "assertoricity" (*assertoria*), over against apodicticity and problematicity. In the usual Japanese terms for the modal distinctions among judgements, *problematica* has one customary rendering and *assertoria* and *apodictica* several, but for these three modalities we choose the words "problematic", "assertoric" and "apodictic".[^c3s2-7] Represented as a figure, the foregoing appears as above 〔Figure 3〕.[^c3s2-8]

![Figure 3](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-03-p198.png)

**Figure 3.** The first system: the modalities on the horizons of actuality and non-actuality, with apodicticity, problematicity and assertoricity (1948, p. 198). Labels: 必然 necessary; 偶然 contingent; 可能 possible; 不可能 impossible; 現實 actuality; 非現實 non-actuality; 確證性 apodicticity; 問題性 problematicity; 言明性 assertoricity. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

[^c3s2-1]: Tr. "Present existence" renders 現存在, and "non-existence" 非存在. "What actually is" and "what actually is not" render 現に有るもの and 現に無いもの; in the formulas below, "to be" and "not to be" render 有ること and 無いこと.
[^c3s2-2]: Tr. C. I. Lewis (1883-1964), American logician, a founder of modern modal logic. In the 1918 *Survey* he writes ~ for impossibility and - for negation.
[^c3s2-3]: Tr. Oskar Becker (1889-1964), German philosopher and student of Husserl; the article appeared in Husserl's *Jahrbuch für Philosophie und phänomenologische Forschung*.
[^c3s2-4]: Tr. Tanabe Hajime (1885-1962), philosopher of the Kyoto school; *Kanto no mokuteki ron* (カントの目的論), "Kant's Teleology".
[^c3s2-5]: Tr. Tanizaki Jun'ichirō (1886-1965), novelist; *Shunkinshō* (春琴抄, 1933), "A Portrait of Shunkin". The man who blinds himself is the servant Sasuke.
[^c3s2-6]: Tr. In Chapter 2, section 5, Kuki introduces )( as a sign for contingency as the encounter of two independent elements.
[^c3s2-7]: Tr. Kuki lists the usual renderings: for *problematica* 蓋然的 ("probable"); for *assertoria* 實然的 or 正然的 ("actually so", "rightly so"); for *apodictica* 確然的, 必當然的, 必然的, 成然的 ("certainly so", "necessarily so"). His own terms are 問題的 (*mondaiteki*), 言明的 (*genmeiteki*) and 確證的 (*kakushōteki*), built on "problem", "statement" and "confirmation".
[^c3s2-8]: Tr. The figure, printed at the head of p. 198, is a square. Corners: top left necessary (必然), top right contingent (偶然), bottom left possible (可能), bottom right impossible (不可能). The top side is labelled actuality (現實), the bottom side non-actuality (非現實). A dotted diagonal joining necessary and impossible is labelled apodicticity (確證性); a dotted diagonal joining contingent and possible is labelled problematicity (問題性); a dotted vertical line through the centre is labelled assertoricity (言明性).

### 3. Two systems of Modality

The foregoing has shown, as a matter of course, that there are two different principles by which a system of Modality can be [p. 199] constructed. The first principle of construction lies in the negation of the *predicate*: with regard to one and the same subject, the predicate is affirmed or denied. We first place "existence" as the subject. To affirm existence as possible is *possibility* in the narrow sense, and to deny existence as possible is *impossibility* in the narrow sense. We then place "non-existence" as the subject and affirm or deny it as possible. To affirm non-existence as possible is *contingency*, and to deny non-existence as possible is *necessity*. Following this first method of construction, the system of Modality presents the combinations (1) actuality and non-actuality, (2) possibility and impossibility, (3) necessity and contingency, and we may think of the opposition of actuality and non-actuality as the case in which "existence" is placed as subject and the predicate affirms or denies it directly.

The second principle of construction lies in the negation of the *subject*: one and the same predicate is posited of mutually contradictory subjects. We first place existence and non-existence as subjects and "affirm" them as possible. To affirm existence as possible is *possibility* in the narrow sense, and to affirm non-existence as possible is *contingency*. We then again place existence and non-existence as subjects, and this time "deny" them as possible. To deny existence as possible is *impossibility*, and [p. 200] [コマ 107] to deny non-existence as possible is *necessity*. Following this second method of construction, the system of Modality shows the combinations (1) actuality and non-actuality, (2) possibility and contingency, (3) necessity and impossibility, and the opposition of actuality and non-actuality may here be thought of as the case in which existence and non-existence are placed as subjects and are directly "affirmed".

We might also think of the formation of the two systems in the following way. Let us first set up the possibility of existence and the necessity of existence, and consider for each of them two kinds of opposite. The possibility of existence has two opposites: (A) "that existence is not possible", that is, the impossibility of existence, and (B) "that non-existence is possible", that is, the contingency of existence. The necessity of existence likewise has two opposites: (A) "that existence is not necessary", that is, the contingency of existence, and (B) "that non-existence is necessary", that is, the impossibility of existence. The first system of Modality takes the opposites of kind A and so forms the pairs possibility and impossibility, necessity and contingency; the second system takes the opposites of kind B and forms the pairs possibility and contingency, necessity and impossibility. Each system then adds the pair actuality and non-actuality.

[p. 201] Let us compare the two systems. In the first system the two members of each combination are viewed from the point of view of their difference, so that each pair is nothing other than a *contradictory opposition*,[^c3s3-1] and the law of contradiction and the law of excluded middle are both valid. Nothing can be actual and at the same time non-actual, and everything must be either actual or non-actual. On the horizon of actuality, again, nothing can be necessary and at the same time contingent, and everything must be either necessary or contingent. And on the horizon of non-actuality, in the same way, nothing can be possible and at the same time impossible, and everything must be either possible or impossible.

In the second system, by contrast, each pair is viewed from the point of view of similarity, each combination being formed on the basis of a point of agreement, whether assertoricity, problematicity or apodicticity. With regard to necessity and impossibility, the law of excluded middle is not valid whilst the law of contradiction is, so that the two stand in the relation of so-called *contrary opposition*. There is no requirement, after all, that something be either necessary or impossible, since the problematic can lie between them as a third, which is to say that the law of excluded middle does not hold; and here lies the limit of apodicticity. Yet [p. 202] [コマ 108] since nothing can be necessary and at the same time impossible, the law of contradiction does hold. With regard to possibility and contingency, on the other hand, the law of contradiction is not valid whilst the law of excluded middle is, so that the two stand in the relation of so-called *subcontrary opposition*. We cannot say that something is unable to be possible and contingent at the same time. Possibility and contingency can hold together, since what presently exists by chance is able to presently exist, and that the possible does not become actual but remains within the non-actual is merely a matter of chance. Between possibility and contingency, in short, the law of contradiction does not hold. If, however, we extend the standpoint of problematicity, and think of necessity as the limit of possibility and of impossibility as the limit of contingency, we can say that everything must be either possible or contingent, and in this sense the law of excluded middle holds. As for the combination of actuality and non-actuality, it was viewed in the first system from the side of difference, as contradictory opposition, whilst in the second system we may take it to be viewed especially from the side of similarity, as assertoricity.

There is no need to take it up directly as a problem here yet, but we may also note that the relation between necessity and possibility, and the relation between impossibility and contingency, can both be seen as standing in the relation of so-called *subalternation* [p. 203], since, as I have just said, necessity is thought of as the limit of possibility and impossibility as the limit of contingency (see pp. 220-221). Let us represent these relations in the figure on the left 〔Figure 4〕.[^c3s3-2]

![Figure 4](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-04-p203.png)

**Figure 4.** The square of opposition of the four modalities (1948, p. 203). Labels: 必然 necessary; 不可能 impossible; 可能 possible; 偶然 contingent; 反對 contrary; 小反對 subcontrary; 大小 subalternation; 矛盾 contradictory; (現實) (actuality); (非現實) (non-actuality). Horizontal labels are set right to left.

If we speak of contingency alone, in the first system "contingency and necessity" stand opposed in contradictory opposition, whilst in the second system "contingency and possibility" construct a relation of subcontrariety on the common ground of problematicity. We may say, then, that to see contingency and necessity as a pair is a static and abstract standpoint, and to see contingency and possibility as a pair a dynamic and concrete one.

Kant's categories of Modality correspond to the first system just described, but they also show a connection with the second. In the "table of categories" Kant divided Modality into (1) possibility and impossibility, (2) present existence and non-existence (that is, actuality and non-actuality) and (3) necessity and contingency, and he attempted to apply this division in the chapter on "the postulates of empirical thought in general" as well.[^c3s3-3] The table of categories, however, is derived from the division [p. 204] [コマ 109] of judgements in formal logic. Kant discovered (1) possibility from the problematic judgement, (2) present existence from the assertoric judgement and (3) necessity from the apodictic judgement, and he then assigned to each the concept of its contradictory opposite to make a pair, setting impossibility beside possibility, non-existence beside present existence and contingency beside necessity. Deriving possibility, present existence and necessity from the table of judgements, as a first stage, and assigning to each its contradictory opposite, as a second stage, were done from separate standpoints. Had Kant kept consistently to the principle of division given by the table of judgements, possibility and contingency would have formed one set, as sharing the problematicity of the problematic judgement, and necessity and impossibility another, as sharing the apodicticity of the apodictic judgement. In its ultimate and decisive form, then, Kant's table of categories belongs to the first system of Modality, but in the genetic process at its point of departure it shows a connection with the second.

The second system also appears in Leibniz: "Truths of reasoning (*raisonnement*) are *necessary*, and their opposite is *impossible*. And truths of fact (*fait*) are *contingent*, and their opposite is *possible*" (Leibniz, Opera philosophica, ed. Erd- [p. 205] mann, p. 707).[^c3s3-4] Leibniz grasped necessity and impossibility in the apodicticity of reasoning, and understood contingency and possibility in the problematicity of fact. Hegel, too, thinks broadly in accordance with this second system. "Possibility and contingency are the two moments of actuality" (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 145), and pure impossibility, as the impossibility of impossibility, reverses into necessity (Ib, S. 193, Anm. 1).

There is, in fact, a marked difference of standpoint according to whether actuality is viewed statically or dynamically. Viewed statically, actuality has the stillness of assertion and does not move. In terms of the figure of Modality given earlier on p. 198 〔Figure 3〕, the vertical line of assertoricity joining actuality and non-actuality is then taken as the starting point. Within actuality, that whose non-existence is impossible is necessity and that whose non-existence is possible is contingency, so that necessity and contingency are thought of as opposed; within non-actuality, that whose existence is possible is what is called possibility and that whose existence is impossible is what is called impossibility, so that possibility and impossibility are placed in a relation of opposition. This is the standpoint of the first system, and from such a standpoint everything is, so to speak, static and decided.

[p. 206] [コマ 110] If, by contrast, we stand at the standpoint of the second system and view actuality dynamically, actuality is thought of as problematic, no longer as assertoric. Actuality has thickness; it is pregnant with problems, and these problems must be unfolded. In the earlier figure, accordingly, we take as our starting point the problematicity that joins possibility and contingency, assertoricity having lost its own self-evidence and turned into problematicity. Contingency, insofar as it means the possibility of non-existence, is something that occupies a position in existence whilst being rooted in non-existence. Possibility, insofar as it means the possibility of existence, is something that occupies a position in non-existence whilst being directed towards existence. And actuality, insofar as it is constituted by possibility and contingency, contains within its thickness the two moments of existence and non-existence, and constantly unfolds problems. According to Hegel, possibility is the inner and contingency the outer, and actuality is nothing other than "the identity of the inner and the outer". Possibility, as inner actuality, is essentiality; contingency, as outer actuality, is the immediate. When abstract possibility acts on immediate actuality and immediate actuality acts on abstract possibility, in other words when the inner makes an immediate self-transposition into the outer and the outer into the inner, [p. 207] real possibility arises and unfolds into necessity. Necessity is nothing other than "unfolded actuality" (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, §§ 142-148; Wissenschaft der Logik, hrsg. v. Lasson, II. S. 169-184).[^c3s3-5]

From the standpoint that views actuality dynamically in this way and considers the development of Modality, the figure on p. 198 〔Figure 3〕 should probably be altered as shown on the left 〔Figure 5〕.[^c3s3-6]

![Figure 5](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-05-p207.png)

**Figure 5.** The second system: the square of Figure 3 turned through forty-five degrees on the line of problematicity (1948, p. 207). Labels: 必然 necessary; 可能 possible; 偶然 contingent; 不可能 impossible; 現實 actuality; 確證性 apodicticity; 問題性 problematicity. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

To obtain this figure we need only rotate the whole of the earlier figure through forty-five degrees about the straight line of problematicity that joins possibility and contingency. "Impossibility" is drawn with dotted lines because it is seen as negative necessity. If the earlier figure reflects the first system of Modality, this figure arises on the basis of the second.

[^c3s3-1]: Tr. Kuki uses the terms of the traditional square of opposition: 矛盾對當, contradictory opposition; 反對對當, contrary opposition; 小反對對當, subcontrary opposition; 大小對當, subalternation.
[^c3s3-2]: Tr. The figure, on p. 203, is a square of opposition. Corners: top left necessary (必然), top right impossible (不可能), bottom left possible (可能), bottom right contingent (偶然). The top side is labelled contrary (反對), the bottom side subcontrary (小反對), and each vertical side subalternation (大小). The diagonal joining necessary and contingent is labelled contradiction (矛盾) with "(actuality)" (現實); the diagonal joining impossible and possible is labelled contradiction with "(non-actuality)" (非現實).
[^c3s3-3]: Tr. In the *Critique of Pure Reason* the categories of modality are *Möglichkeit/Unmöglichkeit*, *Dasein/Nichtsein* and *Notwendigkeit/Zufälligkeit*; "present existence" renders Kuki's 現存在 for *Dasein*. The "Postulates of Empirical Thought in General" are in the Analytic of Principles.
[^c3s3-4]: Tr. The passage is Leibniz, *Monadology*, § 33, in J. E. Erdmann's edition of the *Opera philosophica* (1840).
[^c3s3-5]: Tr. Kuki follows Hegel's terms: *Möglichkeit*, *Zufälligkeit*, *Wirklichkeit*, the inner (*das Innere*) and the outer (*das Äußere*), real possibility (*reale Möglichkeit*, Kuki's 現實的可能性). "Moment" renders 契機.
