[^c3s3-6]: Tr. The figure, on p. 207, is the earlier square standing on one corner: necessary (必然) at the top, possible (可能) at the left, contingent (偶然) at the right, impossible (不可能) at the bottom. A solid horizontal line joining possible and contingent is labelled problematicity (問題性). The upper triangle, in solid lines, is labelled actuality (現實), and an arrow labelled apodicticity (確證性) rises from the horizontal line towards necessary. The two lower sides, running to impossible, are dotted.

(delete the line and the blank line after it)

### 4. The relation of similarity between contingency and possibility

[p. 208] [コマ 111] When contingency and possibility are seen over against the apodictic completeness of necessity, they cannot help being regarded as problematic and incomplete modalities of existence,[^c3s4-1] and it is on this point that contingency and possibility are often witnessed as extremely similar to each other. That existence is possible means at the same time that non-existence is possible as well, since what distinguishes the merely possible in existence from the necessary in existence lies precisely in the fact that its non-existence, too, is possible. This is why possibility and contingency are so often taken to be one and the same.

Aristotle, too, whilst on the one hand distinguishing the possible from the contingent, on the other hand tries to see the two as one and the same. He says that "what is capable of being cut or of walking is also capable of not walking or of not being cut", and that "the *possibility of being* and the *possibility of not being* are the same" (Aristoteles, De interpretatione, 12, 21b), [p. 209] and he goes on to say that "from the *possibility* of being follows the *contingency* of being, and the latter can be converted with the former" (τῷ μὲν γὰρ δυνατὸν εἶναι τὸ ἐνδέχεσθαι εἶναι, καὶ τοῦτο ἐκείνῳ ἀντιστρέφει) (Ib., 13, 22a).[^c3s4-2] He also says: "Therefore the *possible* means, in one sense, as was just said, that whose being false is *not necessary*; in another sense it means the true (*what is*); and in another sense it means what is true and *contingently is*" (τὸ μὲν οὖν δυνατὸν ἕνα μὲν τρόπον, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, τὸ μὴ ἐξ ἀνάγκης ψεῦδος σημαίνει, ἕνα δὲ τὸ ἀληθὲς [εἶναι], ἕνα δὲ τὸ ἐνδεχόμενον ἀληθὲς εἶναι) (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Δ 12, 1019b). Possibility is here determined in three ways: (1) from the standpoint of necessity, as "what is not necessary"; (2) from the standpoint of actuality, as "what is"; and (3) from the standpoint of contingency, as "what contingently is". The passage is extremely rich in implication, but we should note that it identifies the possible with the contingent.

Boethius[^c3s4-3] assigned *possibile* to δυνατόν (the possible) and *contingens* to ἐνδεχόμενον (the contingent), yet he said that "being contingent and being possible mean one and the same thing, and there is no difference whatever between them" (*contingens esse et possibile esse idem significat, nec quidquam discrepat*). If we insist, he added, [p. 210] [コマ 112] on stating a difference, it is merely a formal one, namely that the negative form of the one has the word *impossibile*, whereas there is no word *incontingens* to serve as the negative form of the other (Boethius, De interpretatione, Ed. II. lib. V. Migne p. 582-583). Abelard[^c3s4-4] likewise said that "the possible and the contingent mean entirely the same thing" (*possibile quidem et contingens idem prorsus sonant*) (Abélard, Ouvrages inédits, publ. par Cousin, Dialectica, p. 265).

Spinoza, too, thought of the contingent and the possible as standing extremely close to each other. In explaining the contingent he says:

> Now I shall briefly explain how we should understand the contingent, but before that I wish to explain how we should understand the *necessary* and the *impossible*. A thing is called *necessary* either in relation to its own essence or in relation to its cause, for the existence of a thing follows necessarily either from its own essence and definition or from a given efficient cause. For the same reason a thing is called *impossible* either because its essence or definition involves a contradiction, or because there exists no external cause determined to produce such a thing. A thing is called contingent, by contrast, only because of the imperfection of our knowledge, and for no other reason. [p. 211] For a thing of which we do not know whether its essence involves a contradiction, or of which we know for certain that its essence involves no contradiction but about whose existence we can assert nothing with certainty, because the order of its causes is hidden from us, can never seem to us either necessary or impossible, and for that reason we call it *contingent* (*contingens*) or *possible* (*possibilis*). (Spinoza, Ethica, I, 33, Schol. I)

It is not, however, that Spinoza drew no distinction at all between the contingent and the possible, and he expresses their relation as follows: "When we keep in view only the essence of individual things, I call them *contingent* insofar as we find nothing that necessarily posits their existence or necessarily excludes it. When we keep in view the causes by which individual things must be produced, I call the same individual things *possible* insofar as we do not know whether these causes are determined to produce them. In the Scholium 1 to Proposition 33 of the first part I did not distinguish between the *possible* and the *contingent*, because there was no need there to distinguish them strictly" (Ib., IV, def. 3, 4).

Nicolai Hartmann, MacColl and others can also be regarded as taking the standpoint that identifies the possible with the contingent, but I shall discuss this later (see pp. 224-227) [p. 212] [コマ 113].[^c3s4-5] In short, what we have considered so far is the standpoint that sees the contingent and the possible in a relation of similarity, as sharing the character of problematicity.

[^c3s4-1]: Tr. 確證的 (*kakushōteki*, apodictic) and 問題的 (*mondaiteki*, problematic) are Kant's modal terms for judgements; see the glossary (apodicticity, problematicity).
[^c3s4-2]: Tr. Δυνατόν (*dynaton*): the possible. Ἐνδεχόμενον (*endechomenon*), from ἐνδέχεσθαι (*endechesthai*), "to admit": what admits of being otherwise, the contingent.
[^c3s4-3]: Tr. Boethius (c. 480-524), Roman philosopher and translator of Aristotle's logical works; the reference is to his second commentary on *De interpretatione*, in Migne's *Patrologia Latina*.
[^c3s4-4]: Tr. Peter Abelard (1079-1142), French logician and theologian; the *Ouvrages inédits d'Abélard* were edited by Victor Cousin.
[^c3s4-5]: Tr. Nicolai Hartmann (1882-1950), German philosopher; Hugh MacColl (1837-1909), Scottish logician and an early modal logician.

### 5. The relation of proximity between contingency and impossibility

We have seen the relation of similarity between contingency and possibility. On the other hand, it is extremely important to turn our attention to the relation of opposition between contingency and possibility, or, in other words, to the relation of proximity between contingency and impossibility. Let us take necessity and impossibility as the starting points for this consideration. Possibility is born through the negation of impossibility, and from the single point at which it is born it grows step by step, until the limit of its increase coincides with necessity. The negation of necessity, in turn, gives birth to contingency, which, taking the single point of its birth as its beginning, increases step by step, until the limit of its increase coincides with impossibility. Here the oppositional relation between possibility and contingency, and the relation of proximity between contingency and impossibility, become clear. As possibility increases, contingency decreases, and as contingency increases, [p. 213] possibility decreases. The extreme of the increase of possibility coincides with the extreme of the decrease of contingency, and this is necessity; the extreme of the increase of contingency coincides with the extreme of the decrease of possibility, and this is impossibility. What has just been said can be represented by the following figure 〔Figure 6〕.[^c3s5-1]

![Figure 6](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-06-p213.png)

**Figure 6.** The increase and decrease of possibility and contingency between necessity and impossibility (1948, p. 213). Labels: 必然 necessity; 不可能 impossibility; 可能 possibility; 偶然 contingency.

From this we can understand the special position held by the exceptional contingency treated in Chapter 1, section 5. Exceptional contingency is a contingency in which the mark is non-essential and the content of possibility is at its smallest, and it is consequently the most conspicuous kind of contingency. What is regarded as an essential mark, by contrast, lies at the extreme of the increase of possibility, approximating to necessity, and as the constitutive content of the concept it has the value of a law. That clover has three leaves means that it possesses an essential mark of this kind. Strictly speaking, being three-leaved cannot be called necessary for the concept of clover, but as the limit of the increase of possibility it is quasi-necessary. That a clover has four leaves, on the other hand, [p. 214] [コマ 114] is an exception, a contingency containing extremely little possibility, one that grazes the domain of the impossible, and this is why the four-leaved clover stands out as something especially contingent.

Meinong[^c3s5-2] too says: "It deserves attention that a chance is said to be the more remarkable, not the larger the possibility it contains, but the smaller. There is of course some contingency in two colleagues running into each other in their office, but we think it a special chance when they run into each other in a town far from where the office is. The possibility in that case is far smaller than in the former" (Meinong, Über Möglichkeit und Wahrscheinlichkeit, 1915, S. 243). When the *Book of Changes* says "biting dried meat, one meets poison" (噬腊肉、遇毒),[^c3s5-3] the possibility that the meat, being old and dried, has gone bad and harbours poison is fairly large. There is some contingency in having eaten meat and been poisoned, but it is no great chance, because the possibility is large. When it says, on the other hand, "biting dried meat on the bone, one gets a metal arrow" (噬乾胏、得金矢), it is of course not impossible for a metal arrowhead to be lodged in dried meat on the bone, if the animal was shot with a bow in the hunt, but if it were a golden arrow, [p. 215] say, the possibility would be extremely small, and the contingency, in return, extremely large.

I once boarded a Tōkaidō line train from Kyoto to Tokyo and went into the dining car a little later than the appointed time. Only one seat was left empty, so I took it. The man next to me spoke to me, and when I looked, it was my younger brother-in-law, who lives in Miyazaki Prefecture. In that moment I was struck by the strangeness of it. When I thought about it, however, it was the day before the first-anniversary memorial service for my father-in-law in Tokyo, and the train was the limited express *Tsubame*.[^c3s5-4] That the two of us should take a train on that day, and that we should both board the *Tsubame*, had a fairly large possibility. The contingency lay rather in the fact that, although there were several dinner sittings, we had both, without arranging it, chosen the same one, and that, although the dining car was full, the only empty seat was the one next to my brother-in-law. As I came to see that a fairly large possibility was contained in the encounter, its contingency gradually diminished, and I ceased to find it so very strange. Had it not been an express train, or had we had no common business in Tokyo, the contingency would have been greater in proportion as the possibility was smaller.

Again, immediately after the World War I came to know S, a Russian in exile in Japan and a young man who played the piano well, [p. 216] [コマ 115] and we were on fairly close terms. He once appeared as accompanist for Russian dancing at a theatre in Kamakura. After he left for Harbin to join the fighting under a White general,[^c3s5-5] however, there was no word of him at all, and I thought he might have been killed in battle. Some years later, when I was staying in Freiburg in Germany, I went with someone who was being treated for neuralgia to the electrotherapy room of the university hospital, and I was not a little surprised to find that the assistant there was S. After wandering from place to place he had finally come to Freiburg, studied medicine, and was now an assistant at the university. My first impression was of a great chance, but this too had a fairly large possibility, and when I considered it carefully the contingency diminished. The language in which I had spoken with him in Japan was German. He deeply regretted having had to break off his studies because of the revolution, and he had said that, although Japan had a distinctive culture of its own, he missed the culture of Europe. Given these circumstances there was good reason for him to have gone to Germany and completed his studies there, and our meeting again in Germany therefore contained a good deal of possibility. The contingency hung on the fact that it happened, without anyone's arranging it, in a small South German town such as Freiburg, [p. 217] and that he happened to be the assistant in the electrotherapy room. Had it been, say, that the boy who brought my breakfast when I spent a night at a hotel in some remote place on a journey through Africa turned out to be S, the contingency would have been greater, just as the possibility would have been smaller.

The next example comes from Wilhelm von Scholz's *Der Zufall*, which I quote from Naruse Mukyoku's "Dialogue on Chance":[^c3s5-6]

> This is said to be a true story about a certain person. In the spring of 1914 this person's wife, then living in the Schwarzwald, took photographs of their eldest son, who was turning four; it was, it seems, a small six-exposure film. She needed a minor operation and went to Strassburg, intending to have the photographs developed there, but for some reason her return was suddenly brought forward, so she named the photographer's shop to a Russian woman student she knew, asked her to see to the developing, and left. The war began soon afterwards, she had no further chance to see the student, and in the end she never received the photographer's receipt. A few days before the outbreak of war her husband, who had gone to Strassburg, called in at the shop and explained that, though he had no receipt, he was so-and-so and had left a film to be developed, [p. 218] [コマ 116] but the photographer would not hand the film over on any account, and his wife, it seems, was bitterly disappointed. Things stayed like that until 1916, when the wife bought a film, in Frankfurt, I think, took it with her to a spa called Soden, and there photographed their daughter, then a year and a half old. When she had it developed, every picture turned out to be double-exposed. Thinking that could not be, she held the film up to the light and looked closely, and to her astonishment the figure of her eldest son appeared, hazy, like a dream, against the snow of the Schwarzwald. Overlapping it could be seen the naked figure of his curly-haired little sister sunbathing; brother and sister had become one in the film.

The explanation offered for this chance is that "in the confusion of the war the first photographer sold the film, mixed up with others, to the next photographer, or perhaps a third party came in between". Yet the great chance lies in this, that "going to the shop without a second thought (a step later and someone else might have bought it), she came by that very film just at the right moment" (Naruse Mukyoku, Dialogue on Chance, pp. 451-452, Wilhelm von Scholz, Der Zufall, 1924, S. 21-24). The possibility contained in this chance is extremely small, almost [p. 219] equal to impossibility, and the contingency is consequently extremely great.

That chance is sometimes understood as touching impossibility appears in the following words as well: "Mr Huth,[^c3s5-7] in his book *Consanguineous Marriage*, included a long quotation from a Belgian, who reported that he had bred very closely related domestic rabbits over a great many generations without seeing the least harmful result. The account was published in a most reputable journal, the bulletin of the Royal Academy of Belgium, yet I could not help doubting it. If it was not the result of some kind of *chance*, I could not see the reason for it, and from my own experience of breeding animals I considered such a thing *impossible*" (Francis Darwin, *Charles Darwin's Autobiography, His Religious Views and Reminiscences*, trans. Koizumi Makoto, Iwanami Bunko, p. 107).[^c3s5-8] And when it is said "*by chance* born into the human realm, *so hard to obtain*" (*Wago tōroku*, "Nenbutsu taii"),[^c3s5-9] chance is again seen as a possibility close to impossibility.

We might also think that Kant perceived the proximity of chance and impossibility. In connection with the table of categories, Kant points out that in every class the third category is always a synthesis of the first and the second, and he gives as one example that "necessity is nothing other than existence given through possibility itself" [p. 220] [コマ 117] (Kant, Critique of Pure Reason, B. 111).[^c3s5-10] Now the contradictory opposite of necessity is contingency, and the contradictory opposite of possibility is impossibility, so that if we apply Kant's claim to this case as well, we can say that "contingency is nothing other than existence (non-existence) given through impossibility itself". In any case, we should keep a close watch on the fact that contingency, in its opposition to possibility, approaches impossibility without limit.

[^c3s5-1]: Tr. In Kuki's figure a circle is divided by an S-shaped curve running from the top point, labelled necessity, to the bottom point, labelled impossibility. The left half is labelled possibility, with an arrow curving up towards necessity; the right half is labelled contingency, with an arrow curving down towards impossibility.
[^c3s5-2]: Tr. Alexius Meinong (1853-1920), Austrian philosopher of the Graz school.
[^c3s5-3]: Tr. Both lines come from hexagram 21 of the *Book of Changes*, 噬嗑 (*shihe*, "Biting Through"). 腊肉 is dried meat; 乾胏 is dried meat on the bone. 金矢 is usually read as a metal or bronze arrow; Kuki takes up the reading "golden".
[^c3s5-4]: Tr. The *Tsubame* ("Swallow") was the limited express on the Tōkaidō line, introduced in 1930. The first-anniversary memorial (一周忌, *isshūki*) is a Buddhist service held a year after a death.
[^c3s5-5]: Tr. 白系 (*hakkei*): "White", that is, anti-Bolshevik, Russian.
[^c3s5-6]: Tr. Wilhelm von Scholz (1874-1969), German writer; *Der Zufall: Eine Vorform des Schicksals* (1924). Naruse Mukyoku, Japanese Germanist; his "Dialogue on Chance" (偶然問答) is cited in Chapter 2, section 5.
[^c3s5-7]: Tr. Kuki's ハス (*Hasu*), rendered as Huth; see the editor's notes.
[^c3s5-8]: Tr. Kuki quotes the Japanese translation by Koizumi Makoto of Francis Darwin's edition of his father's autobiography.
[^c3s5-9]: Tr. Hōnen (1133-1212), founder of the Pure Land (Jōdo) school in Japan; the *Wago tōroku* (和語燈錄) collects his writings in Japanese. The human realm is "hard to obtain" because birth as a human being is rare among the realms of rebirth.
[^c3s5-10]: Tr. In Kant's table the categories of modality are possibility and impossibility, existence and non-existence, necessity and contingency; necessity, the third, is the synthesis of the first two.

### 6. The third system of Modality

If, on the basis of the emphasis on the relation of opposition between contingency and possibility, we stress the proximity of contingency to impossibility and the proximity of possibility to necessity, a new way of constructing the system of Modality can be devised. The combination (1) actual and non-actual, (2) necessary and possible, (3) impossible and contingent must be the third system of Modality. What underlies this system is the concept of the limit: necessity is thought as the limit [p. 221] that possibility can approach without end, and impossibility as the limit that contingency can approach without end. Consequently, as I said earlier (see pp. 202-203), we may say that the relation between the necessary and the possible, and the relation between the impossible and the contingent, form what is called the relation of subalternation.[^c3s6-1]

The thought of Nicolai Hartmann (N. Hartmann, Logische und ontologische Wirklichkeit, Kantstudien. XX. S. 1-28) can also be understood from this standpoint. In Hartmann's view, Kant placed the Modalities in the order possibility, actuality, necessity because, from the standpoint of the logic of knowledge, he took the degree of certainty of knowledge as his standard. If, from the standpoint of the logic of existence, the degree of the object's existence is taken as the standard, the order must instead be possibility, necessity, actuality. Epistemologically, actuality is superior to possibility in certainty and inferior to necessity, and it therefore stands between the two. Ontologically, however, actuality must be thought as the synthesis of possibility and necessity, and it therefore takes the last position, higher than both. Actuality and non-actuality on the one side, and necessity and possibility on the other, belong, moreover, to entirely different modal dimensions.

[p. 222] [コマ 118] What matters for our problem is that Hartmann thought of possibility and necessity as a pair. Possibility and necessity stand in a special relation of opposition. The lack of possibility directly means a kind of necessity, since impossibility is nothing other than negative necessity (*negative Notwendigkeit*), and the lack of necessity directly means negative possibility (*negative Möglichkeit*), since non-necessity (*Nicht-Notwendigkeit*) is nothing other than the possibility of non-existence (*Möglichkeit des Nichtseins*). Possibility and necessity, then, are not simply each the opposite of the other. The contradictory opposite of possibility (impossibility) falls directly under the superordinate concept of necessity, and the contradictory opposite of necessity (non-necessity) falls directly under the superordinate concept of possibility, and this is evidence that possibility and necessity stand in a close relation of proximity. What, then, is their original correlation? It is that possibility as a whole coincides entirely with necessity, and that this coincidence is actuality. To put it in detail: actuality is constituted from a number of conditions, each of which is necessary for actuality and cannot be lacking. Taken separately, however, each condition means no more than the possibility [p. 223] of the conditioned, and does not yet mean its actuality. Only when the conditions are all fulfilled and work together does the conditioned become actual. At that point the conditioned is not merely possible but necessary as well; it can no longer not exist, and it is absolutely required by the whole of its conditions. This *coming together* (*Zusammentreffen*) of possibility and necessity in the conditions of a concrete form is what constitutes the actuality of that form. Possibility is nothing other than the modal expression for what is called a condition, as a constitutive moment, and necessity refers to the whole of the conditions. Hartmann points out that this relation between possibility and necessity had already appeared in Aristotle's correlation of potentiality (δύναμις) and actuality (ἐνέργεια),[^c3s6-2] where the coming together of potentialities necessarily results in actuality.

To establish what we are calling the third system of Modality, however, we must, besides setting up the correlation of possibility and necessity, also set up the correlation of contingency and impossibility. Hartmann has not done this, and I [p. 224] [コマ 119] think this is a great defect in his account. Hartmann grounds the correlation of possibility and necessity indirectly, through the contradictory concept of each: the contradictory opposite of possibility is a kind of necessity, and the contradictory opposite of necessity is a kind of possibility, and in this, he held, lies the close relation between the two. Alongside the correlation of possibility and necessity, therefore, he ought naturally to have expounded the correlation between their contradictory opposites. The contradictory opposite of possibility is impossibility, and the contradictory opposite of necessity is non-necessity, that is, contingency. Hartmann sets up no special correlation of contingency and impossibility, however, because for him contingency, or non-necessity, is nothing other than possibility. Non-necessity is the possibility of non-existence; and since, in the modality of possibility, the existence and the non-existence of one and the same thing do not exclude each other but constitute two coexisting possibilities, non-necessity, he says, is directly a negative possibility and at the same time indirectly a positive one. For this reason Hartmann does not acknowledge non-necessity as an independent modality. As a result he sets up, on the one side, the three modalities of necessity, possibility and impossibility, and on the other side the two modalities of actuality and non-actuality, so that as a whole [p. 225] he posits five basic modalities. In the domain of the first oppositional series, possibility occupies the middle, because in existential value impossibility is the smallest and necessity the greatest. On the other side, the two modalities form an oppositional domain of their own, in which non-actuality is the smallest in existential value and actuality the greatest, and in this domain there is no middle term. Between the one domain and the other, two further points about existential value are to be noted. The first is that non-actuality, being less negative than impossibility and more negative than possibility, must be located modally between the two. The second concerns the relative positions of actuality and necessity. There are, as we see in mathematical connections, necessities that lack actuality, whereas actuality, as the synthesis of possibility and necessity, always contains possibility and necessity within itself, sublated as moments, and actuality must therefore be placed above necessity. According to Hartmann, then, the modalities stand in the ascending order of rank shown here 〔Figure 7〕.[^c3s6-3]

![Figure 7](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-07-p225.png)

**Figure 7.** Hartmann's ascending order of the modalities (1948, p. 225). Labels: 現實性 actuality; 非現實性 non-actuality; 必然性 necessity; 可能性 possibility; 不可能性 impossibility. The labels are set right to left.

[p. 226] [コマ 120] As I have already said, it is a serious defect that contingency has no place in this table of modalities. Hartmann holds, as noted above, that non-necessity is directly a negative possibility and at the same time indirectly a positive one, and that it therefore does not form a modality independent of possibility. This, however, is a prejudice that comes from giving formal logic too much weight, and it is shared by all the theorists who identify contingency with possibility. Lewis[^c3s6-4] (see p. 194), too, regards non-necessity (-~-p), which takes three signs, as a "complex truth-value", and does not count it among the five truth-values (*five truth-values*) of what he calls the "system of strict implication" (*system of strict implication*). This too is because he stands entirely within the standpoint of formal logic. Lewis acknowledges on the one hand that -~-p is "irreducible" and "a distinct and recognizable idea", yet on the other hand excludes it as "seldom needed in logic or in mathematics" (C. I. Lewis, A Survey of Symbolic Logic, 1918, p. 292). MacColl does the same. He acknowledges only [p. 227] five basic modalities, "true", "false", "certain", "impossible" and "variable", which he writes τ, ι, ε, η and θ respectively, and he takes the last, θ or "variable", to contain both "not certain" and "not impossible" (H. MacColl, Symbolic Logic and its applications. 1906).[^c3s6-5]

It is, after all, a standing teaching of formal logic that the proposition "it is possible that A is B" is compatible with the proposition "it is possible that A is not B", and it may well be natural that formal logic draws no distinction between the possible and the contingent. In the logic of existence, however, possible existence and possible non-existence are by no means the same. That is to say, the possibility that "existence is possible", on the horizon of non-existence, and the non-necessity that "non-existence is possible", on the horizon of existence, are by no means one and the same. The possibility that "existence is possible" has an existential-logical structure that unfolds towards the necessity that "existence is necessary". The non-necessity that "non-existence is possible", by contrast, shows an existential-logical structure that retreats towards the impossibility that "non-existence is necessary". Non-necessity is no doubt a negative possibility, yet the concept of "contingency" exists precisely because there is something in it to be positively distinguished from possibility. The [p. 228] [コマ 121] possible and the contingent do not both stand in a relation of subalternation to some neutral limit. The possible stands in a relation of subalternation to the necessary in particular, and the contingent to the impossible in particular. Since this relation can be properly grasped only in the logic of existence, it may be unreasonable to look for the difference between the possible and the contingent within formal logic itself. Had the world of formal logic been everything, the concept of contingency might perhaps never have arisen. Actuality, however, demanded that the concept of contingency be born, even against the neutral claim of formal logic. Just as, in actuality, the time before sunrise and the time after sunset are not the same, so in the logic of existence possibility and contingency are not the same.

That Hartmann, though he takes the standpoint of ontology, refused to acknowledge the modality of contingency on formal-logical arguments must have some further Ground besides, and Hartmann does in fact reject contingency from the standpoint of ontology itself. For him chance is "the logical place of ignorance" (*locus logicus ignorantiae*), where, in the causal and the teleological case alike, knowledge of the necessary connection is not reached. [p. 229] It expresses no more than the logical fact that knowledge of actuality can exist without knowledge of necessity, and it does not lead to the conclusion that there is an actuality containing no necessity. Hartmann thus takes his stand on "the Kantian world-picture, governed without remainder theoretically by causal connection and practically by purpose", and confines the meaning of contingency to a result of insufficient knowledge. Against indeterminism, which understands chance in a strictly ontological sense, Hartmann objects that it atomises the events of the world and breaks a unified stream down into unconnected single occurrences, and he points out that a chance that recognises no condition in another, having its condition in itself, postulates the concept of "self-cause" (*causa sui*). Yet even supposing that every event in the universe is strictly conditioned with necessity, we have already considered that the idea of "primal contingency" stands, at an infinite remove, as the beginning of the conditions, and we cannot but recognise in primal contingency a kind of self-cause.[^c3s6-6] Nor is this all. Can the *coming together* (*Zusammentreffen*) of conditions, which Hartmann himself acknowledges, really be understood through the concept of necessity alone? [p. 230] [コマ 122] Is it not here that the concept of chance, which Hegel too acknowledged as "external actuality", is required? Even if everything could be understood as necessary through the category of reciprocal action, so long as the absolute is thought as the whole of all events, we are led back to the concept of primal contingency, or self-cause, as the chance standing over against the sum of necessity. And if we take into account the concept of disjunctive contingency, the theme of this chapter, it will become clear how untenable it is to reject the concept of contingency. That Hartmann, in the correlation of contingency and impossibility, acknowledged only the one term, impossibility, as a Modality, and identified the other term, contingency, with the possible, so denying it modal independence, must be judged a marked defect from the standpoint of ontology.

On this point Oskar Becker[^c3s6-7] (Oskar Becker, Zur Logik der Modalitäten, Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. phän. F. XI) seems to think more closely in keeping with the matter itself. Becker starts from the way of thinking of the first system of Modality, passes through the second system, and presses on towards the third. Since he treats the problem of Modality purely formally, from the standpoint of symbolic logic, he has of course not fully elucidated the concept of the contingent. He does, however, firmly oppose Lewis's [p. 231] refusal to regard non-necessity, -~-p, as one of the basic forms. He reduces its signs from three to two, writing it -•p, and under the name "non-necessary" or "possibly false" he treats the contingent as a basic modality, so that he sets up six irreducible Modalities (*sechs irreduktible Modalitäten*). Becker expresses the mutual relations of the Modalities with six abbreviations: N (*notwendig*, necessary), W (*wahr*, true), F (*falsch*, false), U (*unmöglich*, impossible), M (*möglich*, possible = *nicht unmöglich*, not impossible) and M′ (*möglicherweise falsch*, possibly false = *nicht notwendig*, not necessary). He gives the following combinations as dividing up the whole domain of Modality:[^c3s6-8]

> (1) W and W̄ = F
>
> (2) N and N̄ = M′
>
> (3) U and Ū = M

These correspond to the combination (1) actual and non-actual, (2) necessary and contingent, (3) impossible and possible, [p. 232] [コマ 123] and so they make up the first system of Modality. The relation is also shown clearly in the following figure 〔Figure 8〕.

![Figure 8](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-08-p232.png)

**Figure 8.** Becker's six modalities on a single line between + and - (1948, p. 232). Key: Becker's letters, no Japanese: N notwendig (necessary); W wahr (true); M möglich (possible); M′ möglicherweise falsch (possibly false); F falsch (false); U unmöglich (impossible).

Becker also holds, however, that N (•p) and U (•-p) form symmetrical modalities (*symmetrischer Modus*) through a change of sign, as do M (-•-p) and M′ (-•p), and W (p) and F (-p). If, in this figure, we take WF as the central axis and consider the symmetry to the left and right, the standpoint of the second system appears; and if we take the modalities above the horizontal line as one set and those below it as another, the standpoint of the third system appears. Becker further uses the figure below 〔Figure 9〕 to clarify these relations. From the standpoint of the second system he sets "the logic of necessity" against "the logic of possibility", grasping N and U, on the one hand, in their similarity as apodictic, and on the other hand grasping the common domain (*das gemeinsame Gebiet*) of M×M′ as problematic. This domain is "indeterminate" (*unbestimmt*), "neither necessary nor impossible" [p. 233] (*weder notwendig noch unmöglich*). From the standpoint of the third system, again, he grasps on the one hand the relation of proximity

> N < W < M

and on the other the relation of proximity

> U < F < M′

and with regard to positivity and negativity he sees the series of the Modalities as follows:

> +NWM     M′FU-

![Figure 9](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-09-p233.png)

**Figure 9.** Becker's figure of the positive and negative modalities (1948, p. 233). Key: Letters as in Figure 8.

This grasps the necessary, the actual and the possible, on the one side, in their positivity, and the impossible, the non-actual and the contingent, on the other, in their negativity.[^c3s6-9] These are excellent considerations on Becker's part. It is regrettable, however, that because he kept throughout to the standpoint of formal logic he did not sufficiently elucidate the contingent as contingent. Although he insisted on six *irreducible* Modalities, he still regarded the contingent as "possibly false", a kind of possibility, and the mere fact that he used the abbreviation M′ against M is enough to show the trace of this thinking.

[p. 234] [コマ 124] In short, the core of the third system of Modality lies in witnessing, on the one hand, the correlation of subalternation between the necessary and the possible and, on the other, the correlation of subalternation between the impossible and the contingent. Necessity, as the limit of possibility, is sometimes called "super-possibility", and in the same way we could call impossibility, as the limit of contingency, "super-contingency". The standpoint of the third system can be put in the form of a figure 〔Figure 10〕.[^c3s6-10]

![Figure 10](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-10-p234.png)

**Figure 10.** The third system: necessity as circumference, impossibility as tangent, contingency at the point of tangency S (1948, p. 234). Labels: 必然性(可能性) necessity (possibility); 偶然性 contingency; 不可能性 impossibility, its characters split along the tangent, 不可 towards T and 能性 beyond S.

In this figure the circumference represents *necessity* and the arc represents *possibility*; the tangent ST represents *impossibility*, and the point of tangency S represents *contingency*. As the producing point of the curve, S sets out from itself, advances in the direction of the arrow, and comes to rest when it has finished producing the whole circumference. The circumference and the point S, grasped in the state of rest, represent *actuality*; the arc and the tangent ST, thought in the state of motion, represent *non-actuality*. [p. 235] When the arc representing possibility increases in the direction of the arrow, its limit is the circumference representing necessity. When the arc decreases, on the other hand, S′ moves against the direction of the arrow and approaches S without end, and the line SS′ approaches the line ST without end, until, at the limit, SS′ coincides with ST and S′ with S, and it is then that S emerges as contingency. In other words, contingency is S, the point at which the tangent ST, representing impossibility, touches the curve representing possibility. Contingency, having its position on the straight line of impossibility, is something close to nothingness; and just as the negation of the circumference is the point, contingency is the negation of necessity. Again, just as an infinite number of points of tangency other than S can be thought on the circumference, contingency is no more than one disjunct within the totality of disjunctive possibility. Yet contingency, once it becomes aware that it is itself the producing point, is the force of actuality that sets out from infinitesimal possibility, fills out the curve continuously, and can finally unfold possibility as far as the circumference of necessity. Contingency is the driving force that brings possibility to awareness, leading it from the virgin possibility of what is possible by chance to the maternal possibility of what is always possible. Contingency merely fastens, frailly, an existence at its extreme point to a single point of actuality, but as the productive principle of reality it has the passion to bear the whole of productive activity.

[p. 236] [コマ 125] The same state of affairs can also be expressed in the following figure 〔Figure 11〕.[^c3s6-11]

![Figure 11](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-11-p236.png)

**Figure 11.** Contingency as the apex of the triangle of possibility, on the line of impossibility (1948, p. 236). Labels: 必然性 necessity (on AB); 可能性 possibility; 不可能性 impossibility (on A′B′, split either side of the apex); 偶然性 contingency (at the apex). Horizontal labels are set right to left.

Contingency is a point within the straight line representing impossibility and, at the same time, the apex of the triangle representing possibility. Contingency is nihility, and it is reality as well.[^c3s6-12] The apex, as nihility that is at once reality, is the force that bears, as producing point, the existence of the whole triangle. The base of the triangle represents necessity, in its state of completion as the end of developmental production. Contingency, itself an infinitesimal impossibility, seizes infinitesimal possibility at the perilous extreme point, gives "I" to "Thou" and receives "Thou" into "I",[^c3s6-13] conceives possibility within possibility, and finally coincides with necessity. Necessity and impossibility are represented by horizontal lines of the same length, AB and A′B′, in order to symbolise the apodicticity common to both. Contingency and possibility, by contrast, issue from a single point and coincide with the two points of AB only at their limit, in order to symbolise the problematicity common to contingency and possibility. The single [p. 237] point of contingency and the line of necessity are drawn thick and black to represent the actuality common to both, and possibility and impossibility are drawn with dotted lines to express the non-actuality common to both. Actuality is the character common to necessity, as "developed actuality", and to contingency, as actuality newly given birth. Just as a child at birth lets out its first cry, actuality declares itself in contingency with a loud cry. It is a marked character of contingency that it has actuality in spite of its nihility. Possibility, on the other hand, though it lies within the non-actual, yearns for the passage to actuality on the strength of its rightful claim to reality. What makes impossibility, eternally dead in the non-actual and in nihility, leap towards actuality is the supernatural power that contingency possesses.[^c3s6-14]

[^c3s6-1]: Tr. 大小對當 (*daishō taitō*): the relation of subalterns in the traditional square of opposition, in which the universal implies the particular of the same quality.
[^c3s6-2]: Tr. Δύναμις (*dynamis*): potentiality, capacity; ἐνέργεια (*energeia*): actuality, being-at-work.
[^c3s6-3]: Tr. In Kuki's figure an upward arrow divides the two columns, and each modality is set at its level by short rules on the opposite side.
[^c3s6-4]: Tr. Clarence Irving Lewis (1883-1964), American logician, whose system of strict implication founded modern modal logic. In Lewis's notation ~ is "impossible" and - is negation.
[^c3s6-5]: Tr. On MacColl, see section 4.
[^c3s6-6]: Tr. Primal contingency (原始偶然, *Urzufall*): see the glossary.
[^c3s6-7]: Tr. Oskar Becker (1889-1964), German philosopher and logician, a student of Husserl; his essay appeared in Husserl's *Jahrbuch für Philosophie und phänomenologische Forschung*.
[^c3s6-8]: Tr. A bar over a letter (W̄, N̄, Ū) marks its negation.
[^c3s6-9]: Tr. In Kuki's figure a rectangle, with + at its left and - at its right, is divided by three vertical lines. N stands in the leftmost compartment, W at the first line, F at the third, U in the rightmost compartment. A brace above, labelled M, runs from the left edge to the line at F; a brace below, labelled M′, runs from the line at W to the right edge. The scan in Figure 9 shows the exact extent of the braces.
[^c3s6-10]: Tr. Kuki's figure: a circle, labelled inside "necessity (possibility)", with arrows running round it anticlockwise. A straight line touches the circle at the point S, lower right, and runs up to the right to T; the characters of "impossibility" are written along it on either side of S, and "contingency" is written at S. A second straight line runs from S through the point S′, higher on the circle, and dotted lines fan out from S between SS′ and ST.
[^c3s6-11]: Tr. Kuki's figure is an inverted triangle. Its upper side AB is a thick line labelled necessity; its other two sides are dotted and meet at a lower apex, marked with a black dot and labelled contingency; possibility is written inside. A dotted horizontal line A′B′ runs through the apex, with the characters of "impossibility" written on either side of it.
[^c3s6-12]: Tr. 虛無 (*kyomu*) and 虛無性 (*kyomusei*) are rendered "nihility", to keep them apart from 無 (*mu*), "nothingness". 實在 / 實在性 (*jitsuzai* / *jitsuzaisei*): reality. In 虛無卽實在, "nihility that is at once reality", 卽 (*soku*) means "is at once", "qua".
[^c3s6-13]: Tr. 我 (*ware*) and 汝 (*nanji*): "I" and "Thou".
[^c3s6-14]: Tr. 神通力 (*jinzūriki*): in Buddhist usage, the supernormal powers (Sanskrit *abhijñā*) attained through meditation.

### 7. Summary of the three systems

Looking back over the preceding discussion, let us summarise the distinctive features of the first, second and third systems of Modality.

[p. 238] [コマ 126] In the *first* system, necessity and contingency are regarded as a pair of modalities standing in contradictory opposition on the horizon of actuality, and possibility and impossibility as a pair standing in contradictory opposition on the horizon of non-actuality.[^c3s7-1] In the *second* system, necessity and impossibility, although they constitute a contrary opposition, are regarded as a pair of modalities because they stand in a relation of similarity in their character of apodicticity, and possibility and contingency, although they constitute a subcontrary opposition, are regarded as a pair because they stand in a relation of similarity in their character of problematicity. In the *third* system, necessity and possibility are regarded as a pair of modalities standing, in the dimension of reality, in the relation of proximity that belongs to subalternation, and contingency and impossibility as a pair standing in the same relation of proximity in the dimension of nihility.[^c3s7-2]

The three systems can be represented in the following figures 〔Figures 12 to 14〕, with these abbreviations and signs:[^c3s7-3]

- R: actuality (*Realitas*)
- R′: non-actuality (*Non-Realitas*)
- N: necessity (*Necessitas*)
- [p. 239] C: contingency (*Contingentia*)
- P: possibility (*Possibilitas*)
- I: impossibility (*Impossibilitas*)
- `+`: reality = being
- `-`: nihility = nothingness

![Figure 12](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-12-p239.png)

**Figure 12.** (I)[^c3s7-4] The first system of modality (1948, p. 239). Key: Letters and signs, no Japanese: R actuality; R′ non-actuality; N necessity; C contingency; P possibility; I impossibility; + reality = being; - nihility = nothingness.

![Figure 13](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-13-p239.png)

**Figure 13.** (II)[^c3s7-5] The second system of modality (1948, p. 239). Key: Letters and signs as in Figure 12.

![Figure 14](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-14-p239.png)

**Figure 14.** (III)[^c3s7-6] The third system of modality (1948, p. 239). Key: Letters and signs as in Figure 12.

Let me briefly explain these figures. (I) *The first system.* What lies above and what lies below the horizontal line stand in contradictory opposition: the upper part represents reality (`+`) and the lower part nihility (`-`), whilst the left side represents *actuality* (R) and the right side *non-actuality* (R′). *Necessity* (N) and [p. 240] [コマ 127] *contingency* (C) stand in contradictory opposition on the side of actuality, necessity belonging to the domain of reality and contingency to the domain of nihility. *Possibility* (P) and *impossibility* (I) stand in contradictory opposition on the side of non-actuality, possibility belonging to the domain of reality and impossibility to the domain of nihility.

(II) *The second system.* Here what lies above and below the horizontal line represents *actuality* (R) and *non-actuality* (R′), and the relation of contradictory opposition appears in the opposition of left and right. Within the range of actuality, however, the left side represents reality (`+`) and the right side nihility (`-`), whereas within the range of non-actuality the reverse holds, the left side representing nihility and the right side reality. *Necessity* (N) and *impossibility* (I) form a contrary opposition, and *contingency* (C) and *possibility* (P) a subcontrary opposition; the large circle of contrary opposition represents apodicticity, and the small circle of subcontrary opposition represents problematicity.

(III) *The third system.* Measured from the centre of the circle, the left side represents reality (`+`) and the right side nihility (`-`), whilst the part above the horizontal line represents *actuality* (R) and the part below it *non-actuality* (R′). The large circle of *necessity* (N) subsumes the small circle of *possibility* (P), the two forming a subalternation in the domain of reality, and the large circle of *impossibility* (I) subsumes the small circle of *contingency* (C), the two forming a subalternation in the domain of nihility. Since necessity and contingency form a contradictory opposition within the range of actuality, the large circle of necessity and the small circle of contingency are drawn in dotted lines in the range of non-actuality, [p. 241] that is, in the part below the horizontal line, to show that they do not belong there. In the same way, since impossibility and possibility form a contradictory opposition within the range of non-actuality, the large circle of impossibility and the small circle of possibility are drawn in dotted lines in the range of actuality, that is, in the part above the horizontal line, to show that they do not belong there. This concludes the explanation of the figures.

If, further, we speak of contingency alone and of its relations in the three systems, then in the first system contingency is regarded as the actual that contradicts necessity, in the second system as something problematic that resembles possibility, and in the third system as something of nihility, on the point of being subsumed by impossibility. Shown in a figure on the model of the one on p. 198 〔Figure 3〕,[^c3s7-7] this relation of contingency takes the following form 〔Figure 15〕:[^c3s7-8]

![Figure 15](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-15-p241.png)

**Figure 15.** Contingency in the three systems of modality (1948, p. 241). Labels: 偶然 contingent; 必然(第一體系) necessary (first system); 可能(第二體系) possible (second system); 不可能(第三體系) impossible (third system); 現實性(矛盾) actuality (contradictory); 問題性(小反對) problematicity (subcontrary); 虛無性(大小) nihility (subalternation). Horizontal labels are set right to left.

The three systems of Modality, moreover, each view the modalities from a different standpoint, and every one of them has [p. 242] [コマ 128] its own distinctive features. They are by no means of such a nature that one should be taken up and the others discarded. The structure of contingency, too, is grasped completely only when it is witnessed synthetically from these three points of view, and the cooperation of the three systems must be emphasised.

[^c3s7-1]: Tr. 矛盾對當 (contradictory opposition), 反對々當 (contrary opposition), 小反對々當 (subcontrary opposition) and 大小對當 (subalternation) are the four relations of the traditional square of opposition.
[^c3s7-2]: Tr. 實在性 is rendered "reality" and 虛無性 "nihility"; in the legend below Kuki equates them with 有 and 無, being and nothingness. "Nihility" keeps 虛無性 apart from 無, which is "nothingness" throughout. 現實性 remains "actuality", although Kuki's Latin for it is *Realitas*.
[^c3s7-3]: Tr. Kuki writes "the figures on the left", which in the vertical setting means those that follow. On p. 239 the legend and the three circle figures stand one above another, with the explanation in the lowest columns of the page.
[^c3s7-4]: Tr. Figure (I): a large circle divided by a horizontal diameter, with R at its left end and R′ at its right, `+` above the diameter and `-` below. Inside it are two equal circles, side by side and touching at the centre; the left one holds N above the diameter and C below, the right one P above and I below.
[^c3s7-5]: Tr. Figure (II): a large circle divided by a horizontal diameter, R above and R′ below. Inside it a large circle on the left (N above the diameter, I below) and a small circle on the right (C above, P below) touch at a point on the diameter to the right of centre. Straight lines run from that point to the top of the left circle, marked `+`, and its bottom, marked `-`, and to the top of the small circle, marked `-`, and its bottom, marked `+`.
[^c3s7-6]: Tr. Figure (III): a large circle divided by a horizontal diameter, with `+` at its left end and `-` at its right, R above and R′ below. On the left a large circle (N) contains a small circle (P); on the right a large circle (I) contains a small circle (C); all four touch the centre. The upper arcs of N and C and the lower arcs of P and I are solid; the lower arcs of N and C and the upper arcs of P and I are dotted.
[^c3s7-7]: Tr. The square figure on p. 198 closes section 2.
[^c3s7-8]: Tr. Kuki writes "as above": on p. 241 the figure stands above the closing columns of this paragraph. Three lines run from a point at the upper right marked 偶然 (contingent): leftwards to 必然 (necessary), labelled 現實性 (actuality) with 矛盾 (contradictory); straight down to 不可能 (impossible), labelled 虛無性 (nihility) with 大小 (subaltern); and diagonally down to the left to 可能 (possible), labelled 問題性 (problematicity) with 小反對 (subcontrary). Each end point carries its system in parentheses. The horizontal labels are set right to left.

### 8. The play of chance and the concept of probability

Up to this point we have considered contingency mainly on the basis of its relation to possibility or impossibility. A consideration of this kind means standing behind a given actuality or existence and glimpsing, at its starting point, the direction in which it develops, and for that, actuality or existence must be seen from the disjunctive standpoint. In the form "A is either B, or C, or D", that is, disjunctive possibility, and with it impossibility, is made the background of actuality.

Let us consider the case in which, taking in the disjunctive possibility that belongs to the faces of a die, we engage in the *play* of chance. The reason that a particular face shown by a die is in general taken to have the character of contingency is that it is thought against the background of the possibility of six [p. 243] [コマ 128] faces, which is to say that we think it could equally have been any of the other five. Probability theory, certainly, will seek some constancy here and try to exclude chance. Yet what is this so-called exclusion of chance? What is probability?[^c3s8-1] The probability of the occurrence of an event is the ratio of the number of cases favourable to its occurrence to the number of all possible cases; in other words, probability is the quotient obtained by dividing the number of favourable cases by the number of all possible cases. What probability theory determines with regard to the play of dice is consequently no more than the quantitative relation between all the possible cases in which a particular face of the die appears or does not appear and the "favourable" contingent cases in which that face appears. There is a difference here between a die that is perfectly homogeneous and symmetrical and one whose perfection is somewhat lacking, but if we assume it to be perfect, the law of probability that is set up as a priori probability says only that each face of the die has a probability of 1/6 of appearing, that is, only that appearing once in six throws is probable. This theoretical quantitative relation, moreover, is ideally valid in practice only for the sum of innumerable cases, [p. 244] [コマ 129] and if the number of throws is small the results may even be extremely lopsided. Suppose, again, that after experimenting with the die over, say, ten thousand throws, we found that the probability of the one is 9/60 and the probability of the six, on the opposite face, is 11/60. This is what is called empirical probability. Empirical probability in this case has more objective value than the earlier a priori probability of 1/6, but in the end it is of the same nature. Whether the probability is a priori or empirical, the law of probability holds on what is called the macroscopic horizon, and consequently, on the microscopic horizon, the contingent variability of which face comes up in each case still strictly remains.

Let us now consider a little further the meaning of *probability*, the basic concept of probability theory. It is usual to divide probability into two kinds, philosophical (qualitative) probability and mathematical (quantitative) probability. What falls short of certainty is called probability, and of a probability we say that it is greater or smaller. If we think that greater and smaller are quantitative matters, then all probability is in essence quantitative probability. There are, however, cases that permit strict quantitative determination and cases that do not, and the case that does not permit [p. 245] strict quantitative determination is called philosophical or qualitative probability, whilst the case that permits it is called mathematical or quantitative probability (see W. Wundt, *Logik*, I, 4. Aufl., S. 419-422; Cournot, *Théorie des Chances et des Probabilités*, 1843, pp. 427-428, 437-440; R. Berthelot, *Un romantisme utilitaire*, I, 1911, pp. 311-312).[^c3s8-2] Philosophical probability shows itself most clearly in inference by analogy and in inductive inference, whereas mathematical probability is the theme of probability theory.

The concept of probability, further, contains within it the moment of an approach to certainty, as is clear from the fact that inference by analogy and inductive inference draw near to certainty from probability. In keeping with this character, probability in the narrow sense is a concept applied only to cases greater than half of certainty, and consequently, for cases smaller than half of certainty, the concept of improbability arises. Kant's definition of probability is of this narrow kind. According to Kant, "by probability is to be understood an acknowledgement on insufficient Grounds; in that case, however, the ratio of the insufficient Grounds to the sufficient Grounds is greater than the ratio of the Grounds of the opposite to the sufficient Grounds", and it follows that "probability is greater than half of certainty (*mehr, als die Hälfte der Gewissheit*)" (Kant, [p. 246] [コマ 130] *Logik*, hrsg. v. Jäsche, 3. Aufl., S. 90-91). Kant further argued, on the ground that "only the mathematician can determine the ratio of insufficient Grounds to sufficient Grounds" (ibid.), that the concept of probability properly ought to belong to the domain of mathematics. Probability in the domain of mathematics, the probability of which probability theory speaks, is probability in the broad sense, valid for the whole that comprehends both probability in the narrow sense and improbability.

According to Laplace,[^c3s8-3] "the ratio of the number of favourable cases to the number of all possible cases is the measure of probability, and probability is nothing other than a fraction whose numerator is the number of favourable cases and whose denominator is the number of all possible cases" (Laplace, *Essai philosophique sur les Probabilités*, Paris, Gauthier-Villars, 1921, p. 6). In Cournot's formulation, probability is "the ratio of the number of chance occasions favourable to the event to the total number of chance occasions" (Cournot, l. c., p. 24). Probability, in short, insofar as its essence is a quantitative relation, is nothing other than probability as a quantity of probability.[^c3s8-4] Mises[^c3s8-5] defines probability as follows: "The limiting value of the relative frequency with which a given mark appears (with the proviso that this limiting value must be insensitive to place selection) is called the probability of the appearance of this mark within the collective under consideration" [p. 247] (Richard von Mises, *Wahrscheinlichkeit, Statistik und Wahrheit*, 1928, S. 29). Relative frequency (*relative Häufigkeit*) corresponds to the fraction of which Laplace speaks, but Mises, from the standpoint of empirical or statistical probability, makes the determination of the limiting value of relative frequency his main aim. That the limiting value is insensitive to place selection (*gegen Stellenauswahl unempfindlich*) means that the limiting value is in no way affected by place selection, place selection being the singling out of only a certain place, or a certain part, of a series. As an example of a case in which the limiting value is affected by place selection, he gives a highway on which a small milestone stands every hundred metres and a large milestone every thousand metres. If we walk along this highway for a long distance, we find that the limiting value of the relative frequency with which we come upon a large milestone in the series of milestones is 1/10. If, however, we make a selection within the series of milestones and decide, for example, to attend only to every other small milestone, the relative frequency of the large milestones is no longer 1/10 but changes to 1/5. In this case, that is, when by free selection a part of the elements is singled out from the whole and attention is paid to that part alone, the limiting value of the relative [p. 248] [コマ 131] frequency is affected. With a die this never happens. Even if we throw a die and take account only of every other throw, the frequency does not change, provided we go on for a sufficiently long time. The collective that probability theory takes as its problem must be one that satisfies the requirement that the limiting value of the frequency remains unchanged even when, by arbitrary place selection, only a part of the elements is singled out from the whole and attended to. A change in the limiting value of the frequency comes about because there is lawfulness in the series, and the absence of change comes about because there is irregularity (*Regellosigkeit*) in the series. Probability theory determines the degree of probability by discovering a determinate quantitative relation with regard to irregularity of this kind.

The quantity of probability, after all, is in essence a fraction, and so it comes to be smaller than 1 and greater than 0; its maximum, 1, means certainty or necessity, and its minimum, 0, means impossibility. When Kant, regarding what makes something probable, says that the ratio of the insufficient Grounds to the sufficient Grounds is greater than the ratio of the Grounds of the opposite to the sufficient Grounds, and again that probability is greater than half of certainty, this is because he thinks of 1/2 [p. 249] and above as probability, and consequently, as we have already said, what lies below 1/2 can be called improbability. Probability of 0.5 and above, of this kind, is probability in the narrow sense, but the probability with which probability theory deals is probability in the broad sense, valid for the whole range between 1 and 0. And since probability occupies a position midway between necessity and impossibility, its relation to possibility and contingency must also be considered. Possibility and contingency stand, so to speak, back to back at any degree whatever of probability in the broad sense, possibility facing towards 1 and contingency facing towards 0.

![Figure 16](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-16-p249.png){overlays="no"}

**Figure 16.** Probability between necessity (1) and impossibility (0), with the relations of opposition (1948, p. 249). Labels: 必然 necessary; 不可能 impossible; (可能) (possible); (偶然) (contingent); 矛盾 contradictory; 反對 contrary; 小反對 subcontrary; 大小 subalternation; (狹義の)蓋然 probable (in the narrow sense); 非蓋然 improbable; 蓋然 probable. Horizontal labels are set right to left.[^c3s8-6]

Let us represent these relations by the figure above 〔Figure 16〕. The relations of opposition themselves have already been shown in a figure on p. 203 〔Figure 4〕. In the figure, for convenience, possibility and contingency, standing in subcontrary opposition, are placed below 0.5, but it goes without saying that they can be located at any point between 1 and 0.

[p. 250] [コマ 132] Probability theory discovers laws of probability on the macroscopic horizon with regard to irregularity, and yet it cannot exclude that irregularity, that is, contingent variability, from individual cases on the microscopic horizon. It is here that the radical powerlessness of probability theory lies, with regard to the philosophical posing of the problem of contingency.

Of course, seen microscopically, the face that the die shows in each individual case will presumably be determined necessarily by physical properties: the die itself, the manner of throwing, the resistance of the air, the surface onto which it is thrown, and so on. Contingency nonetheless lies in the fact that, from the ultimate standpoint, we can think that another causal series of necessity could also have been taken, that we can think that the causal series now given as actuality does not necessarily possess absoluteness; it lies, in other words, in our thinking that there is a discrepancy between possibility and actuality. Poincaré[^c3s8-7] says the following about the play of roulette: "Let us divide a disc into a hundred sectors, colour them alternately red and black, and imagine a needle spinning on a pivot. If the needle stops on a red sector, we win; if not, we lose. Clearly everything depends on the first push we give the needle. Suppose that the needle turns ten or twenty times; according to whether we pushed it strongly or weakly at first, [p. 251] it stops sooner or later. A difference of only a thousandth or a two-thousandth in the force of the first push is enough for the needle to stop either on a black sector or on the next red one. These are slight differences that the muscular sense cannot perceive and that even more delicate instruments could not measure. How the needle we have just set in motion will move, consequently, cannot be foreseen. That is precisely why, with pounding hearts, we wait and leave everything to chance" (Poincaré, *Science et Méthode*, pp. 70-71). What makes the contingent contingent at all is that, when the needle has stopped on red, we think that it could also have stopped on black, and when it has stopped on black, that it could also have stopped on red. Yet we do not necessarily think so for the reason Poincaré gives. It is not only for the subjective reason that a slight difference in the force of the first push cannot be measured even by a delicate instrument, and that how the needle will move cannot therefore be foreseen, since contingency does not have its Ground solely in a lack of knowledge of the facts. The true contingency that sets the player's heart pounding lies, rather, in the force of the first push itself: in the fact that the determinate strength given as fact does not necessarily possess absolute necessity, that we think it could also have been some other strength, [p. 252] [コマ 133] that is, in the fact that no contradiction whatever arises if we think of it as some other strength.

Let us take horse racing as a further concrete example. That horse racing is one kind of what is called "winning and losing by chance"[^c3s8-8] cannot be disputed. Of course, it cannot be said that there is no tendency to try to exclude chance from horse racing. According to Kikuchi Kan's "My Philosophy of Betting Tickets" (carried in *Hanashi*, published by Bungei Shunjū-sha, May issue, Shōwa 10),[^c3s8-9] "A dividend that is the result of good judgement brings great satisfaction, whether the amount is large or small. The dividend from a *fluke*, even if it is two hundred yen, is *speculative*, and is nothing a proper betting-ticket fan should count to his credit"; "Even a long shot of a hundred and twenty or thirty yen may be worth two hundred yen in credit, and even a dividend of two hundred yen may be worth nothing in credit. Taking two hundred yen on a new horse by a *fluke* is not much different from simply *picking up money*"; and "There are people who bet without even knowing what a thoroughbred is, which is to be lamented. To bet without the least study of the horses' bloodlines, records and so on is to be a *gambler*." The writer appears to be opposed to enjoying horse racing as a play of chance. "Examine carefully the results of each race at the same meeting, and thanks to that you will be able to take one or two big long shots [p. 253]." He tries, that is, to set up laws of probability by means of statistics and to exclude chance as far as possible from winning and losing itself, and this attitude agrees with his attempt to exclude chance from the size of the dividend: "The size of the dividend on a *place* bet depends mostly on how the other favourites cross the line, and in that respect it is *more contingent*. One had better aim for a big long shot on a win bet."[^c3s8-10] When the writer takes up, on the one hand, the standpoint of the "judge of form" and, on the other, that of the betting-ticket buyer who expects a profit, it is natural that he should seek to exclude chance from horse racing. Yet will he succeed? At the end he says: "In buying betting tickets, winning is extremely hard. To enjoy one's betting at a sacrifice that does not strain one's means, this would be the proper way of the racing fan. Not only has no racing fan ever built a storehouse, but it is said that anyone who goes in and out of the racecourse for two or three years running must be a person of very considerable means. ... Buying betting tickets is a pastime. It is a squandering of money. If you truly wish to make money, you had better apply yourself to a proper family trade." This presumably tells us that the macroscopic character of the law of probability does not succeed in removing microscopic chance. In spite of his emphasis on "judgement", consequently, and in spite of his rejection of "gamblers", he acknowledges that at least one half of "the charm of horse racing" still lies in the play of chance, in "winning and losing by chance" [p. 254] [コマ 134].

What, then, is the character of the chance immanent in horse racing? As in the case of the die, we might take microscopic chance to be something that holds only on the macroscopic horizon, and so say that on the microscopic horizon the order in which the horses finish is causally determined throughout. Contingency as a practical problem would then dissolve into something subjective, dependent on the incompleteness of our knowledge. Contingency of that kind differs in degree, subjectively, according to how much the spectators know, and it merely constitutes, in connection with the whole mental mechanism, the object of a psychological study of contingency. And yet, independently of such subjective chance, there exists, philosophically, an objective contingency. When horse A has taken first place, horse A's first place does not necessarily possess absolute necessity, and so long as it involves no logical contradiction to think of the case in which horse A came second or third, contingency lies in the fact of horse A's first place. If causal determination existed in every detail, and if, let us suppose, it were all known, then the order of finishing would also be known entirely in advance, and in that case horse racing would presumably not take place as a course in time. [p. 255] Even so, though the contingency that should first be seen at the finishing line is lost, contingency still exists at the starting line. When the horses have lined up at the start, both the order of finishing and the margins are already decided by the line-up itself, so that there is no need to run the race at all; the finishing line has come to coincide with the starting line, and the duration of time between the two has been completely eliminated. And yet the contingency of disjunctive contingency still exists, in the fact that each determination in that case could also have been another determination.

Disjunctive contingency of this kind is not infrequently despised as empty contingency or empty possibility. According to Hegel, possibility of this kind is a mere abstraction over against concrete actuality, and so long as a thing does not contradict itself, everything becomes possible. This form of non-contradiction is a form that can be given to any content whatever, provided only that the content is separated from its concrete connection, and consequently even the most irrational things come to be thought possible. Just as a stone thrown into the air falls back, it is possible that the moon will fall to the earth tonight; it is possible that the King of Turkey will convert to Christianity, become a Catholic priest [p. 256] [コマ 135] and become Pope of Rome.[^c3s8-11] Words such as "such and such is possible", taken merely formally, are shallow and empty: "Especially in philosophy, there should be no talk of saying that such and such is 'possible', or that 'something else too' is possible, or, as people often say, that such and such is 'thinkable'" (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 143, S. 191-192; *Wissenschaft der Logik*, hrsg. v. Lasson, II, S. 171). We can, however, also think the opposite. Without completely cutting the content off from its concrete connection, as Hegel describes, we can think disjunctive possibility, and with it disjunctive contingency, while preserving a certain moderate concreteness. Even without imagining the King of Turkey becoming Pope, it is not necessarily irrational to think of the case in which Ibrahim Pasha, besieging Missolonghi, happened to be struck by a cannonball and fell, the Turkish army raised the siege, and Byron did not die in a foreign land.[^c3s8-12] Conversely, where irrationality arises because we have stopped halfway, without fully leaving the concrete behind, we can dissolve the irrationality by leaving the concrete still further behind and going over into the abstract. That the moon will fall to the earth tonight is very markedly encumbered by the concreteness of time and space. If we think instead of the case in which, in the formation of the universe, the moon [p. 257] was never made, this is not necessarily irrational, and even if we think of the case in which the earth too was never made and man never arose, it cannot be called irrational. Astronomy teaches that the moon is too large in relation to the earth and is, as the satellite of a planet, something of an exception, and it teaches also that our solar system, in which the star did not become a multiple star and the planets revolve around a single sun as their centre, is an exception to the law of the formation of fixed stars. Behind the theory of evolution's account of the evolution from animals to man, too, there must be the thought of something like the possibility that the evolution to man did not take place and that the anthropoid apes formed the summit of biological development. And when philosophy undertakes a consideration of "nothingness", it must presuppose the disjunctive relation given by the law of excluded middle, or else some supra-logical possibility of thought.

Husserl[^c3s8-13] too says: "The cognitive acts that ground experience posit the actual individually; they posit it as existing in space and time, as something that is at this position in time and has this duration and a determinate actual content, and that actual content, according to its essence, could equally have been at any other position in time. They posit it, again, as something that is at this place in a determinate physical form [p. 258] [コマ 136], and that same actuality, according to its essence, could equally have been at any place whatever and in any form whatever. Likewise that actuality, even if in fact it does not change, could have changed, or could have changed in a way other than the way in which it in fact changed. ... According to its essence it could also have been something else. Some determinate natural law may well hold, and according to that natural law, if such and such an actual state of affairs in fact exists, such and such a determinate consequence must in fact exist. But such a law merely expresses a factual determination, which could itself have run entirely otherwise; and that determination, as something belonging in advance to the essence of the objects of possible experience, already presupposes that the objects it governs are, seen in themselves, contingent" (Husserl, *Ideen zu einer reinen Phänomenologie*, S. 8-9). Benno Erdmann[^c3s8-14] says likewise: "Actuality has become the state it is in necessarily, according to causal connection. This necessity, however, was by no means anywhere the only possible [p. 259] one" (Benno Erdmann, *Logik*, 3. Aufl., S. 488). And August Faust[^c3s8-15] says: "If the concrete filling-out of the content of all intra-worldly existence is still to be understood conceptually in any way, it becomes clear that an extremely contentless abstract concept such as *possibilitas logica* (logical possibility) is precisely what is indispensable" (August Faust, *Der Möglichkeitsgedanke*, II, S. 59).

From the ultimate standpoint, which admits logical possibility and with it disjunctive contingency, it is contingent that this clover has four leaves and not three, and contingent that Mount Asama is a volcano and neither a fault-block mountain nor a fold mountain.[^c3s8-16] It is also contingent that Toyotomi Hideyoshi was born in Nakamura in Owari, and not in Kyōto, or Ōsaka, or anywhere else.[^c3s8-17] Leibniz, stating that innumerable worlds were possible, holds that there could have been a world in which Sextus Tarquinius was born in Thrace and became the happiest of men, the object of the veneration of the whole people, a world in which he was born in Corinth and grew old in mediocrity, loved by his fellow citizens, a world in which he was born in Rome, violated another man's wife and became an exile, and besides these innumerable worlds containing Sextuses of every kind and in innumerable fashions (*Sextus de toute espèce, et d'une infinité de fa-* [p. 260] [コマ 137] *çons*) (Leibniz, *Théodicée*, III, §§ 414-416, *Opera philos.*, ed. Erdmann, pp. 622-624).[^c3s8-18] We could in fact have been American, or French, or Ethiopian, or Indian, or Chinese, or of any other nationality, and that we are Japanese is contingent. We could also have been insects, or birds, or beasts, and that we are human, neither insect nor bird nor beast, is contingent. The *Zō agon gyō* (fascicle 15) gives a skilful figure for the contingency of being born human.[^c3s8-19] A blind turtle of immeasurable lifespan, hidden in the great ocean, puts its head above the water once in a hundred years, and a floating log with a single hole in it drifts on the sea, east and west as the wind takes it; to be born human, the sutra says, is like this blind turtle, as it raises its head, happening upon the hole in the log.
