If we think of one disjunct of disjunctive possibility as thrown out like the face of a die, we can find a profound meaning in the thought of the Sāṃkhya-Yoga doctrine, or of the philosophy of the Vedānta school, that the unattached and free Brahman creates and transforms 〔the world〕 for the sake of *play*.[^c3s8-20] Heraclitus too said: "Time is a child at *play*, playing at *shōgi*" (Diels, *Die Fragmente der Vorsokratiker* I, Herakleitos Fr. 52).[^c3s8-21]

[p. 261] Contingency of this kind, in short, is the relation that an actuality has to possibility as a whole, and accordingly to impossibility as well, when one disjunct of disjunctive possibility has been posited before our eyes as actuality. And in the speed of the transition by which disjunctive possibility, pushing through the opposition of impossibility, slides into actuality, we feel the streamlined form of the play of chance.[^c3s8-22]

[^c3s8-1]: Tr. Kuki uses two words that English renders as "probability": 確率 (*kakuritsu*), the mathematical measure (here, and in "probability theory", 確率論), and 蓋然性 (*gaizensei*), probability as a modal concept (the section title and the next paragraph onwards). Both are given as "probability"; the literal version marks the Japanese where they need to be told apart.
[^c3s8-2]: Tr. Wilhelm Wundt (1832-1920), German psychologist and philosopher. René Berthelot (1872-1960), French philosopher; *Un romantisme utilitaire* is a study of pragmatism.
[^c3s8-3]: Tr. Pierre-Simon Laplace (1749-1827), French mathematician and astronomer.
[^c3s8-4]: Tr. Here the two terms meet: probability (蓋然性) is probability (確率) as a "quantity of probability" (蓋然量, *gaizenryō*).
[^c3s8-5]: Tr. Richard von Mises (1883-1953), Austrian-born mathematician, author of the frequency theory of probability. Kuki's 集合 (*shūgō*), "collective", renders Mises's *Kollektiv*; 箇所選擇 (*kasho sentaku*), "place selection", renders *Stellenauswahl*.
[^c3s8-6]: Tr. The labels are those of the square of opposition: 矛盾, contradictory; 反對, contrary; 小反對, subcontrary; 大小 (literally "greater-lesser"), subaltern. Kuki's 對當關係 (*taitō kankei*) is rendered "relations of opposition".
[^c3s8-7]: Tr. Henri Poincaré (1854-1912), French mathematician and philosopher of science. *Science et Méthode* appeared in 1908.
[^c3s8-8]: Tr. 偶然の輸贏 (*gūzen no shuei*): 輸贏 is losing and winning, a classical term for a contest or wager.
[^c3s8-9]: Tr. Kikuchi Kan (1888-1948), novelist and dramatist, founder of the publisher Bungei Shunjū-sha and a racehorse owner. Shōwa 10 is 1935. Italics in his quotations mark Kuki's emphasis.
[^c3s8-10]: Tr. "Place" renders プラッセ (*purasse*), from the French *placé*, a bet that the horse finishes in the first places; the "win bet" is 單勝 (*tanshō*). A "long shot" (穴, *ana*) is an outsider that pays a large dividend.
[^c3s8-11]: Tr. Hegel's example (*Encyclopaedia*, § 143, Zusatz) is that the Sultan might become Pope; Kuki's トルコ王 is "King of Turkey".
[^c3s8-12]: Tr. Ibrahim Pasha (1789-1848), Egyptian commander who fought for the Ottomans against the Greek insurgents. Lord Byron (1788-1824) died of fever at Missolonghi in April 1824. Ibrahim's forces took part in the later siege of 1825-1826.
[^c3s8-13]: Tr. Edmund Husserl (1859-1938). *Ideen zu einer reinen Phänomenologie und phänomenologischen Philosophie* appeared in 1913.
[^c3s8-14]: Tr. Benno Erdmann (1851-1921), German philosopher and logician.
[^c3s8-15]: Tr. August Faust (1895-1945), German philosopher. *Der Möglichkeitsgedanke* is a two-volume history of the concept of possibility.
[^c3s8-16]: Tr. Mount Asama (浅間山), an active volcano on the border of Nagano and Gunma prefectures.
[^c3s8-17]: Tr. Toyotomi Hideyoshi (1537-1598), the warlord who unified Japan; Nakamura in Owari province lies in present-day Nagoya.
[^c3s8-18]: Tr. Sextus Tarquinius, son of the last king of Rome, whose rape of Lucretia led to the fall of the monarchy. In the closing myth of the *Théodicée* the goddess Pallas shows Theodorus the possible worlds and their many Sextuses.
[^c3s8-19]: Tr. 雜阿含經 (*Zō agon gyō*), the Chinese translation of the *Saṃyuktāgama*, a collection of the Buddha's discourses. The blind turtle and the floating log is a standard Buddhist figure for the rarity of a human birth.
[^c3s8-20]: Tr. Sāṃkhya (數論) and Yoga (瑜伽) are paired schools of Indian philosophy; Vedānta (吠檀多) is the school founded on the Upaniṣads. Brahman (梵) is the absolute. The *play* (遊戲) of the creator corresponds to the Sanskrit *līlā*.
[^c3s8-21]: Tr. The fragment reads αἰὼν παῖς ἐστι παίζων, πεσσεύων. Kuki renders αἰών as 時, "time", and the board game πεσσοί as *shōgi* (將棋), Japanese chess.
[^c3s8-22]: Tr. 流線型 (*ryūsenkei*), "streamline", the aerodynamic form of 1930s design.

### 9. The temporal character of contingency

Oskar Becker[^c3s9-1] has also noted that the logic of Modality stands in a deep relation to temporality (Oskar Becker, Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. phän. F. XI, S. 539), and for us it is of particular importance to elucidate the temporal character of contingency. I have already discussed the temporal determination of contingency, and in particular the simultaneity and successiveness of hypothetical contingency (see Chapter 2, section 17). But what special character, in general, does contingency possess on the temporal horizon, over against the other modalities? By considering the relation between contingency and the other modalities on the horizon of time, [p. 262] [コマ 138] we can make the structure of contingency clearer still.

Let us take possibility first. Through the schema "in advance", possibility has the temporal character of the *future*. Among the philosophers who stress the connection between possibility and the future is Heidegger (see Iwanami Kōza Tetsugaku, Kuki, "The Philosophy of Existence", pp. 54-55, 59-77).[^c3s9-2] The central point of Heidegger's philosophy is the idea of care as projection (Entwurf) and of resoluteness as running ahead (Vorlaufen).[^c3s9-3] Projection means throwing possibility, as possibility, in advance ahead of oneself, and the way in which Existenz is its possibility as possibility is care in the mode of projection. Running ahead, in turn, is running ahead into possibility, and in running ahead possibility must be understood as possibility, formed as possibility and held out as possibility; to anticipate possibility as possibility, running ahead into it in advance, is what makes resolve a running ahead. And insofar as it is grounded in the existence of care itself that care is originarily modalised, through anticipatory resoluteness, in the form of understanding projection, the principal moment of care must be "being ahead of itself" (Sich-vorweg-sein). Once the ontological meaning of care is interpreted as temporality, the principal moment of temporality accordingly lies in the future as well. The primary phenomenon of temporality [p. 263] is the future. Time has its starting point in the future, and its direction runs, on the basis of the "future" (Zu-kunft) as a "coming towards" (zu-kommen) oneself from the future, towards the past that has been. It is the self-futurity of possibility that makes possible the running-ahead character of resolve, and possibility is "futural" because its temporal character is "of the future".

Necessity, on the other hand, has the *past* as its temporal character, through the schema "already". Aristotle called the necessary essence "what it was to be" (τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι).[^c3s9-4] The necessary essence, that is, is something more than what simply "is" (ἐστί); in order to be actually, it is something that already "was" (ἦν). To ask about essential necessity, then, it is not enough to ask "what is it?" (τί ἐστι); the question must also include "what was it?" (τί ἦν). Necessity has continuance from the past as its temporal moment. Plato's Idea is the type of essential necessity, and to grasp the Idea we must look back across the infinite past and recollect (ἀνάμνησις)[^c3s9-5] the intuition of a former life. Because the Idea is the necessary, it already existed in the far past too, [p. 264] [コマ 139] in its unchanging form, and to recollect what had been forgotten and return to the far past is the only way of witnessing the necessary. The same holds for "In the beginning was the Word" (ἐν ἀρχῇ ἦν ὁ λόγος) (Gospel of John 1:1).[^c3s9-6] The necessity of the Logos is thought to have been present already at the beginning of all things and to remain unchanged even now. The temporal character of necessity is clearly of the past. Nor is the past character of necessity lost when we take the standpoint that sees necessity as unfolded actuality. Someone may object that if necessity is the unfolding of actuality, necessity must surely occupy a place in the future. Yet if necessity is the unfolding of actuality, then, precisely because it is that unfolding, necessity as unfolded actuality must carry with it a recollection of the past. Just as possibility always has a prospect onto the future, necessity must always have a retrospect onto the past. All unfolding, in other words, unfolds from future possibility towards past necessity. We call possibility futural because, in the form not yet unfolded, the unfolding in the future is anticipated "in advance"; we call necessity past because, in the form "already" unfolded, the course of unfolding in the past [p. 265] is looked back upon.

Whereas the temporality of possibility is the future and that of necessity is the past, the temporality of contingency is the *present*, whose schema is the "now". In general, the possible of the future passes through the actual into the necessary of the past. The possible, from great possibility down to the infinitesimal possibility that borders on impossibility, becomes actual through the possibility of the possible, and the actual unfolds into the necessary. Contingency in the broad sense is, in general, the case in which the possible *meets* the plane of actuality, so that even what has great possibility takes on, to a greater or lesser degree, the character of contingency insofar as it meets that plane. Hegel too says that "the contingent is the actual that is at the same time determined as merely possible" (Hegel, Wissenschaft der Logik, hrsg. v. Lasson, II, S. 173). Contingency in the eminent sense, moreover, is nothing other than the case in which the smallest possibility, or impossibility, meets the plane of actuality. And insofar as actuality means, temporally, the present, the temporality of contingency must also be the present. The "now" in the preface to the *Kyōgyōshinshō*, "hard to meet, *now* I have been able to meet it",[^c3s9-7] is the temporal schema of contingency. Contingency is, in general, created in presentness. Contingency in the eminent sense, as the limit of the diminution of future possibility, [p. 266] [コマ 140] gushes forth suddenly from the nothingness of an impossibility that has no future, passing through the non-existent single point of the present.

There is no objection to thinking that contingency's being an Encounter in the present means the "falling" of presence, as "being alongside" (Sein-bei), nor to thinking of its movement as "falling off".[^c3s9-8] It goes without saying, however, that to construe "chance" (Zufall) as "falling" (Verfall) or "falling off" (Abfall) is permissible only on the condition that every axiological point of view is excluded from the words "falling" and "falling off".

Primal contingency, further, may be thought to bear the eternal past, as the "oldest" contingency (see p. 283),[^c3s9-9] and it may also be thought to possess futurity, as what is pregnant with every contingency still to come. Yet when primal contingency is recollected in pastness it has been modalised into necessity, and when it is anticipated in futurity it has been modalised into possibility. What makes primal contingency contingency must lie in the presentness by which it is contingent in the given instant of the "now". What is pursued in pastness is not contingency, [p. 267] and what is expected in futurity is not contingency either; contingency must be something triggered in presentness alone. I noted briefly earlier that successive contingency has its basis in simultaneous contingency, and that from this point too the temporal character of contingency can be determined as the present (see pp. 167-168).[^c3s9-10] Even apart from that structural fact, however, we can grasp the presentness of contingency by taking successive contingency entirely by itself. If, deceived by the word "succession", we represent something enduring in successive contingency, it is already no longer contingency, and turns into something belonging to the necessary or the possible. What makes successive contingency contingency must depend on its present character, by which it coincides, in the given instant of the "now", with what was "already" given. The present in which contingency comes about is the present that "passes in a point" (in puncto praeterit) (Augustinus, Confessiones XI, § 28), a present equal to nothingness. And where contingency comes about precariously in a present equal to nothingness, we feel the nihilistic character[^c3s9-11] that contingency shares with impossibility.

As Becker also points out (l. c. S. 539), actuality is the "direct mode" (modus rectus), whereas possibility, which takes its steps towards actuality, and necessity, which advances its steps from actuality, are both the "oblique mode" (modus obli- [p. 268] [コマ 141] quus). Brentano,[^c3s9-12] in his theory of time, made the present the "direct mode" and the future and the past the "oblique mode" (F. Brentano, Psychologie vom empirischen Standpunkt, hrsg. v. Kraus, II, S. 225). Presentness, as actuality, is to be "looked at straight on", whereas the futurity of possibility and the pastness of necessity can be brought into the field of vision only when one who stands in the actuality of the present "looks obliquely" to the right and to the left. The temporal priority of contingency follows from this. If we intuit states of affairs bodily and originarily, contingency is neither experienced as the negation of necessity nor understood in connection with possibility. To look straight at contingency as the actual in the present is the original, primary and primal fact, and then, secondarily, we can think of looking obliquely at the futural possible as a tendency towards the future, and at the past necessary as continuance from the past. In other words, possibility and necessity, by their very temporal character, can be witnessed only obliquely, and the only modality that can be looked at straight on is contingency, present at a single point. That contingency is determined as the negation of necessity, or as the correlate of possibility, belongs to the domain of logic, which has already departed from the immediacy of experience. In the immediacy [p. 269] of experience, contingency, as the mode of looking straight on, as the direct mode, holds temporal priority insofar as it has its position in the present. It is, moreover, nothing other than the pulse of the eternal present as the instant.

On the basis of this consideration, the temporal characters of the possible, the necessary and the contingent can be represented by the figure above 〔Figure 17〕.[^c3s9-13]

![Figure 17](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-17-p269.png)

**Figure 17.** The temporal characters of the modalities: necessity in the past, possibility in the future, contingency in the present (1948, p. 269). Labels: 過去 past; 未來 future; 現在 present; 現實 actuality (現 above the axis, 實 below); 必然 necessity; 可能 possibility; 偶然(不可能) contingency (impossibility). Horizontal labels are set right to left.

In grasping presentness as the temporal character of contingency, I think we have at the same time made clear the close relation between contingency and *actuality*. In the first system of modality, contingency was determined as standing in contradictory opposition to necessity on the static horizon of *actuality*. In the second system, the *actual* was problematised dynamically, and contingency and possibility were witnessed in the relation of subcontrary opposition, as constituting the actuality of problematic actuality. In the third system, contingency was grasped in the dimension of nihility, as standing with impossibility in the relation of proximity that belongs to subalternation, and yet, for all that, [p. 270] [コマ 142] it was understood as proclaiming its own actuality as the birth of the *actual* (see p. 237).[^c3s9-14] Meinong[^c3s9-15] too points out that, in relation to necessity, it is an indispensable condition of contingency that it should be more than the negation of necessity, in that "despite the lack of necessity, factuality is nonetheless extant" (Meinong, Über Möglichkeit und Wahrscheinlichkeit, 1915, S. 240). In relation to possibility, as Hegel says, contingency is nothing other than "immediate actuality" (unmittelbare Wirklichkeit). Contingency is precisely "a determinate being" (ein Dasein), "an existence" (eine Existenz), "an immediate" (ein Unmittelbares) (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 146 u. Zusatz).[^c3s9-16] Contingency, in short, is the actuality of the actual as the merely actual. As Schelling says, the contingent is that of which "one can only say that it Is" (nur sagen, dass es Ist), "what happens in the eminent sense" (das Geschehene κατ' ἐξοχήν)[^c3s9-17] (Schelling, Sämmtliche Werke, 1856-1857, II, 1, S. 464, II, 2, S. 153). And presentness, as the temporal character of contingency, is also in truth grounded in the actuality of contingency as the merely actual.

[^c3s9-3]: Tr. Kuki's terms for Heidegger's: 投企 (*tōki*), *Entwurf*, projection; 先驅 (*senku*), *Vorlaufen*, running ahead; 決意性 (*ketsuisei*), *Entschlossenheit*, resoluteness; 關心 (*kanshin*), *Sorge*, care; 會得 (*etoku*), *Verstehen*, understanding. 實存 (*jitsuzon*) is left as *Existenz* to keep it apart from 存在 (*sonzai*), existence.
[^c3s9-4]: Tr. Aristotle's phrase for essence, literally "the what it was to be". Kuki renders it 何で在るべく在つたかのもの.
[^c3s9-5]: Tr. *Anamnēsis*, Plato's recollection of what the soul knew before birth.
[^c3s9-6]: Tr. Kuki glosses 太初 with the reading はじめ (*hajime*), "beginning".
[^c3s9-7]: Tr. The *Kyōgyōshinshō* (教行信證) is the principal work of Shinran (1173-1263), founder of the Jōdo Shin school of Pure Land Buddhism. The line, 遇ひがたくしていま遇ふことを得たり, is from its general preface.
[^c3s9-8]: Tr. *Sein-bei* (being alongside) and *Verfallen* (falling) are Heidegger's terms in *Being and Time*. Kuki plays on *Zufall* (chance), *Verfall* (falling, decline) and *Abfall* (falling off, apostasy), all built on *Fall*.
[^c3s9-9]: Tr. Primal contingency (原始偶然, *Urzufall*) is treated in section 13. The page reference is as printed; p. 283 of this printing falls in section 11.
[^c3s9-10]: Tr. The text reads 同時的偶然た基礎を有つ, apparently a misprint for 同時的偶然に基礎を有つ. The pages cited are in Chapter 2, section 17.
[^c3s9-11]: Tr. 虛無的 (*kyomuteki*), nihilistic in the modal sense: belonging to the side of nihility (虛無性), which Kuki sets against reality (實在性) in his systems of modality. It is kept apart from 無 (*mu*), nothingness.
[^c3s9-12]: Tr. Franz Brentano (1838-1917), Austrian philosopher and psychologist. *Modus rectus* and *modus obliquus* are his direct and oblique modes of presentation. Kuki's 正視 (*seishi*) and 斜視 (*shashi*) are "looking straight on" and "looking askance".

A horizontal axis runs from 過去 (past) on the left to 未來 (future) on the right, crossed by a vertical line labelled 現 above and 實 below (現實, the actual). The point of crossing is labelled 現在 (present) and 偶然 (不可能), contingent (impossible). A straight line rises through it from lower left to upper right; the hatched triangle above the axis on the right is 可能 (possible), the hatched triangle below the axis on the left is 必然 (necessary), each closed by a dotted line to the vertical. A short arrow runs from the origin towards the necessary.
[^c3s9-14]: Tr. The three systems of modality are set out in sections 3 to 7. Contradictory, subcontrary and subalternation are relations on the logical square of opposition (矛盾對當, 小反對對當, 大小對當).
[^c3s9-15]: Tr. Alexius Meinong (1853-1920), Austrian philosopher, founder of the theory of objects.
[^c3s9-16]: Tr. *Dasein* in Hegel is usually rendered "determinate being"; Kuki gives 現存在 (*genzonzai*). 實存 (*jitsuzon*) renders *Existenz*.
[^c3s9-17]: Tr. κατ' ἐξοχήν, "par excellence".

### 10. Contingency and the emotion of wonder

[p. 271] In connection with the temporal character of contingency, we can understand its affective equivalent,[^c3s10-1] and we may begin by considering the affective equivalents of possibility and necessity. Possibility, on the basis of its temporal character, futurity, has the feeling of *anxiety* as its equivalent, and this is also why Heidegger's philosophy of possibility takes anxiety to be the fundamental-ontological state-of-mind.[^c3s10-2] Necessity, on the basis of its temporal character, pastness, has the feeling of *calm*. When the possibility of the future passes over into the necessity of the past, the tense feeling of anxiety changes into the relaxed feeling of calm. Impossibility, as negative necessity, is nothing other than the negative half of necessity. The feeling of anxiety that belongs to possibility takes the form either of the pleasure of *hope* or of the displeasure of *worry*, depending on the nature of the event that is possible in the future. When possibility passes over affirmatively into necessity, hope relaxes into the feeling of *satisfaction* and worry into the feeling of *gloom*; and when possibility turns negatively into negative necessity, [p. 272] [コマ 143] that is, into impossibility, hope relaxes into its opposite, the feeling of *disappointment*, and worry into its opposite, the feeling of *relief*. Hope and worry, the affective equivalents of possibility, are both feelings that carry the tension of anxiety, whereas relief, satisfaction, disappointment and gloom, the affective equivalents of necessity (positive and negative), are all feelings of calm in some state of relaxation. The tension of feeling, in other words, is grounded in the futurity of possibility, and its relaxation in the pastness of necessity.

What, then, is the feeling that answers to contingency? As the existence of words such as *kigū* and *kien* shows,[^c3s10-3] the affective equivalent of contingency is the emotion of *wonder*. Necessity has the subdued feeling of calm because the problem has "already" been solved with analytic clarity. Contingency, by contrast, arouses the excited feeling of wonder because the problem is thrown out "before our eyes" still unsolved. The emotion of wonder is grounded in presentness, the temporal character of contingency. In short, the necessary, because of the decided apodicticity of its past, has only the static, weak feelings of relaxation and subsidence, whereas the possible and the contingent, because of their problematicity, bring with them the dynamic, strong feelings of tension and excitement. And the chief difference between the tense feeling of anxiety that belongs to the possible [p. 273] and the excited feeling of wonder that belongs to the contingent is that the former concerns the future and the latter the present. Possibility is the modality in which nothingness awaits being in the future; contingency is the modality in which being, embracing the present, beholds nothingness.

Let us represent the modalities and their affective equivalents in the figure above 〔Figure 18〕.[^c3s10-4] Impossibility is regarded here as negative necessity and drawn in dotted lines, and the dotted part may be imagined as folded back upwards, so that the vertex "impossible" coincides with the vertex "necessary".

![Figure 18](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-18-p273.png)

**Figure 18.** The modalities and their affective equivalents (1948, p. 273). Labels: 必然 necessary; 可能 possible; 偶然 contingent; 不可能 impossible; 平穩 calm; 不安 anxiety; 驚異 wonder; 緊張 tension; 興奮 excitement; 弛緩 relaxation; 沈靜 subsidence; 靜的 static; 動的 dynamic. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

The emotion of wonder is what Aristotle calls θαυμάζειν and Descartes *admiration*.[^c3s10-5] Descartes defines wonder as follows: "Wonder is a sudden astonishment of the mind, which makes it attentively consider things that seem to it rare and extraordinary [p. 274] [コマ 144]" (Descartes, Les passions de l'âme, Art. 70, Oeuvres, éd. Adam & Tannery, XI, p. 380); and "wonder is the first of all the passions" (Ib., Art. 53, Adam & Tannery, XI, p. 373). Wonder, the affective equivalent of contingency, is the metaphysical emotion that we harbour, at the present instant at which one of the possible disjuncts has been posited, towards the absolute ground of that positing. When Plato in the *Symposium* says that if A "meets by chance" (*kaikō*, 邂逅; ἐντύχῃ) B, "they are struck with wonder" (θαυμαστὰ ἐκπλήττονται) (Platon, Symposium, 192),[^c3s10-6] this is a notable example of θαυμάζειν as philosophical wonder. Philosophy is born, in truth, from wonder at chance. And ontological feeling can be seen as unfolding from the wonder of contingency, through the anxiety of possibility, to the calm of necessity.

This wonder ought, moreover, to accompany the positing of any disjunct whatever, but it appears especially markedly when the positing feels as though it were a teleological positing, that is, when there is a "purpose without purpose". A certain politician who had once organised a new party, speaking when he returned to his original party, said: "After we had resolved on the merger, a *tsujiura*[^c3s10-7] came out. It said, 'Others are others; come home soon, says the old man.'[^c3s10-v1] It is a truly *strange* thing." [p. 275] And a certain senior colleague, who had called him a runaway son, said: "I was *strangely* moved by a *strange* *tsujiura* like that" (Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun, 7 July, Shōwa 4).[^c3s10-8] Again, under the title "A Strange Tie in Court", a newspaper once carried the report that "the hearings of two lawsuits of closely similar content, which had arisen for widows over their late husbands' children after the husbands' deaths, were, *strangely*, held on the same day in the same court (the Ōsaka Court of Appeal, Court No. 2)" (Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun, November, Shōwa 4). What is called "strange" or "odd" here[^c3s10-9] is nothing other than wonder at the "purpose without purpose" of chance.

We should note, further, that wonder at chance from the human standpoint can become the comic of chance from the divine standpoint.[^c3s10-10] Since the difference of standpoint is decided mainly by the relative magnitudes of subject and object, it is usual that, faced with a chance of small existential significance, the subject, being relatively large, feels the comic of divine intelligence (see pp. 58, 167), whereas, faced with a chance of large existential significance, the subject becomes relatively small and feels wonder as a human feeling. Something like the comic of a pun is nothing other than the laughter of intelligence at a positive contingency of ground of extremely small existential significance.[^c3s10-11] The laughter that laughs at a chance of large existential significance must be the clear laughter of the overman Za- [p. 276] [コマ 145] rathustra,[^c3s10-12] and it must laugh at the pettiness of a humanity that wonders. The comic of chance, in short, is a feeling that accompanies the divine self-reflection that takes the feeling of wonder as its object.

[^c3s10-1]: Tr. 感情當價 (*kanjō tōka*), literally "feeling-equivalent": the feeling that answers to a given modality. 感情 (*kanjō*) is rendered "feeling" and 情緒 (*jōcho*) "emotion".
[^c3s10-2]: Tr. 情態性 (*jōtaisei*), Heidegger's *Befindlichkeit*. In *Being and Time* anxiety (*Angst*) is the basic state-of-mind of Dasein.
[^c3s10-3]: Tr. 奇遇 (*kigū*), a strange meeting, and 奇緣 (*kien*), a strange tie or affinity.
[^c3s10-4]: Tr. In the printing the figure stands at the top of p. 273, above the paragraph on Aristotle and Descartes. It is a rhombus: 必然 (necessary) at the top under a circled 平穩 (calm); 可能 (possible) at the left beside a circled 不安 (anxiety) and the label 緊張 (tension); 偶然 (contingent) at the right beside a circled 驚異 (wonder) and the label 興奮 (excitement); 不可能 (impossible) at the bottom. The upper sides are solid and the lower sides dotted. The vertical from 必然 is labelled 靜的 (static) and the horizontal from 可能 to 偶然 動的 (dynamic). Two arrows rise from 緊張 and 興奮 to the labels 弛緩 (relaxation) and 沈靜 (subsidence) above 平穩. The arrows are drawn only approximately here.
[^c3s10-5]: Tr. θαυμάζειν (*thaumazein*), to wonder. *Admiration* is the first passion in Descartes' *Les passions de l'âme* (1649), in the older French sense of wonder.
[^c3s10-6]: Tr. From Aristophanes' speech, on the halves of the divided human beings who find one another.
[^c3s10-7]: Tr. 辻占 (*tsujiura*), a fortune slip, often a verse, of the kind sold wrapped in sweets.
[^c3s10-8]: Tr. Shōwa 4 is 1929.
[^c3s10-9]: Tr. 奇 (*ki*) and 妙 (*myō*). Kuki's emphasis falls on 妙 in the two quotations from the politicians and on 奇しくも (*kushikumo*, "strangely") in the newspaper report, all rendered "strange".
[^c3s10-10]: Tr. 可笑味 (*okashimi*), the comic, the laughable.
[^c3s10-11]: Tr. Puns are discussed as positive contingency of ground (理由的積極的偶然) in Chapter 2, section 3.
[^c3s10-12]: Tr. The laughing Zarathustra of Nietzsche's *Thus Spoke Zarathustra*.
[^c3s10-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 人は人、早く歸へれと親爺いひ. Our translation: "Others are others; come home soon, says the old man."

### 11. Chance and art

That literature values chance is grounded mainly in the emotion of wonder. Aristotle too said in the *Poetics* that "what is to be wondered at is delightful" (Aristoteles, de poetica 24, 1460a; Rhetorica, 3, 2, 1404b).[^c3s11-1] Literature, that is, poetry in the broad sense, has contingency as an important moment on both of its sides, content and form. As to content, contingency forms the backbone of the whole in, for example, the *Asagao nikki*,[^c3s11-2] where there is more than a single chance, the whole being nothing other than a set of variations on chance as its main theme. The Nō play *Semimaru*,[^c3s11-3] again, lives as a Nō play precisely because the mere fact that Semimaru and his elder sister meet by chance at the Ōsaka barrier carries sufficient dramatic effect. That Miyuki, who encounters (*kaikō*, 邂逅) by chance [p. 277] Komazawa, a traveller, at the inn at Shimada, has wept her eyes out and become blind, and that Semimaru is a blind man, symbolise, like the blindness of the tortoise of immeasurable lifespan that meets the hole in the floating log,[^c3s11-4] nothing other than the coincidental and blind character of chance that is expressed in such phrases as "chance coincidence" and "blind chance".[^c3s11-5] Because the "purpose without purpose" of chance is blind, it gives birth to tragedy and to comedy. This is why Novalis[^c3s11-6] too says: "Everything poetic must be fairy-tale-like. The poet worships chance" (Novalis Schriften, hrsg. v. Minor, III, S. 4). In our own country, too, Mr Nakagawa Yoichi[^c3s11-7] has recently put forward the claim that "against the necessitarianism of Marxism, unless we place the Ground of our thinking in the theory of chance, our literature will wither and die". "Truth without the marvellous[^c3s11-8] is no more than an empty dream. That is, the realism of today must be something that pursues the marvellous of truth, the truth of the marvellous, that stands on the theory of chance. ... I once said, 'The realism of the present is to pursue to the end the *marvellous* that *truth* has.' But these words change at the same time into the claim of romanticism. For this proposition becomes romanticism if one stresses the marvellous, and becomes realism if one stresses truth. [p. 278] [コマ 146] But to speak more precisely, today these two isms have come to have to be strongly bound together in the theory of chance. ... Thus has not literature now reached a period of transformation? It seems meaningless, whatever its concern, political, rhetorical or human, so long as, sunk in stillness, it does not have the will to unravel the yarn-ball of chance. ... We must judge our literature once again by the quantity of chance events, choose its material by the truth of chance, and make the power of imagination that lies in human life beat its wings once again. I firmly believe that what will revive the literature of today depends solely on the transformation of this basic thinking" (Tōkyō Asahi Shinbun, 9-11 February, Shōwa 10, Nakagawa Yoichi, "The Yarn-Ball of Chance"; see Shinchō, July issue, Shōwa 10, Nakagawa Yoichi, "On the Literature of Chance").[^c3s11-9]

As to form, I have already set out at length, as an example of positive contingency of ground, how contingency as phonetic coincidence is brought to life artistically in literature (see Chapter 2, section 3). It is also an interesting fact that the legends about the origin of rhyme have their centre of gravity in chance coincidence. According to a Persian legend, a king called Behram dearly loved a serving-woman called Dilara- [p. 279] m.[^c3s11-10] Whenever the king spoke with her he ended his words with "Dilaram", and she in reply always ended hers with "Great lord Behram", and thus, it is said, the art of rhyme arose. The chance coincidence that the king's name and the serving-woman's name shared the same end-rhyme became the basis from which the art form of rhyme arose. Paul Valéry speaks of the "twin smiles" (sourires jumeaux) that exist between one word and another (Valéry, Aurore),[^c3s11-11] comparing the phonetic coincidence between word and word to the contingent relation between twins. Defining poetry from the formal point of view, Valéry further calls it "a pure system of the chances (fortunes) of language" (Valéry, Variété, p. 159),[^c3s11-12] and he speaks of the "philosophical beauty" (Ib., p. 67) that belongs to rhyme. Oskar Becker, again, says that "transience" (Fragilität) and "fragility" (Zerbrechlichkeit) are basic characteristics of the aesthetic (Oskar Becker, Von der Hinfälligkeit des Schönen und der Abenteuerlichkeit des Künstlers. Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. phän. F. Husserl-Festschrift, S. 27),[^c3s11-13] and there is nothing so much at the very edge, so transient and so fragile, as chance. Here lies, once more, the beauty of chance. To take contingency into the form of poetry, as a glance exchanged between sound and sound, a passing-by between word and word, means to symbolise the pulse of life in poetry. [p. 280] [コマ 147] And that the meaning of contingency latent in the belief in *kotodama*[^c3s11-14] is actualised as a transient, fragile art form must speak of the richness of the power of poetry. In short, the notable meaning that contingency has in the content and form of literature lies mainly in metaphysical wonder and in the "philosophical beauty" that accompanies it.

Let me add a word, further, on the inner relation that art in general has to contingency. It can be considered from two points: first, the structural character of art itself is contingent; second, art likes to take chance as its object-content. As to the first point, it cannot be denied that science and morality[^c3s11-15] have contingency as a driving force, both at their point of departure and in the process of their unfolding, and yet, in spite of this, they pursue, in possibility, the necessity of the whole as an infinite task. On the possible path of necessary connection, science and morality never know their thirst quenched. "The noble man thereby makes himself strong without ceasing" (*Book of Changes*, Xiang commentary),[^c3s11-16] or "Only by being active and never resting even for a moment is one a man"[^c3s11-v1] (Goethe, *Faust*, Part I, 1759):[^c3s11-17] this is the domain of science and morality. Art, by contrast, fulfils and completes itself partially and in isolation. [p. 281] A work of art, as a completed state, is cut off from connection with anything else in a so-called "microcosmic structure", and it is intuited independently as a whole. Herein lies what is contingent in the structural character of art itself; art, so to speak, has contingency as its final form. Whereas science and morality are "futural" cultural forms, as possibility that pursues necessity without end, art is a "present" cultural form that contemplates itself in the contingency before it. To call out to the "moment" of the present, "Stay, for you are so beautiful"[^c3s11-v2] (Goethe, *Faust*, Part I, 1700; Part II, 11582), or to sigh "the maidens' figures, let me keep them a while"[^c3s11-v3] (Archbishop Henjō),[^c3s11-18] is the realm of art. Freedom in art, again, is freedom *from* all necessity. In art, absolute spontaneity appears suddenly and vanishes abruptly, and there lies the contingency of what is called inspiration and adventure. The genius is one who, reflecting the contingency of primal contingency, opens wide eyes of wonder at their own making. "This impulse, which develops in the direction of the density, intensity and depth of feeling, radiates each artistic individual, through the influence it exerts on the instinct of individuation, in response to the developmental process of feeling. Consequently, for one who stands on this impulse, [p. 282] [コマ 148] artistic creation is rather *chance*" (Abe Jirō, *World Culture and Japanese Culture*, pp. 88-89).[^c3s11-19] That ink painting and colourless film force on the viewer the contingency of total colour-blindness, and that radio drama forces on the listener the contingency of the blind, are all, if we trace them to their root, grounded in the isolated and contingent structural character of art itself.

The second point, that art likes to take chance as its object-content, is grounded, I think, in the fact that chance is accompanied by a feeling of life. Contingency in the world of living things is typical, and even meteorological contingency is compared to living things (Fujiwara Sakuhei, *Weather and Human Life*, pp. 32-33).[^c3s11-20] The contingency of natural phenomena is what is hard to foresee and cannot be caught by law. In it individuality and freedom appear; the licence of life and the play of caprice appear. That life, that play, is beautiful, and wonder at its lively deviation moves us. Lipps[^c3s11-21] too says that "the contingent, as the free, richly varied play of natural forces not fitted into law, is beautiful" (Theodor Lipps, Ästhetik, I, 2 Aufl. S. 205). We might also consider the important place that contingency occupies in the appreciation of Eastern ceramics. In the making of ceramics, the fire in the kiln takes part in the making while keeping a certain degree of independence from the maker's [p. 283] intention. Distortions arise from this in the shape, and flavour seeps out in the colour, and so-called *yōhen*[^c3s11-22] is nothing other than contingency as artistic beauty and natural beauty. Among contemporary French painters there are some who leave to chance the choice of the objects to be painted in a still life: on their way, hit or miss, they add to the canvas, each time, whatever catches their eye. To take the contingency of life into the efficient cause of artistic making, one must have considerable conviction about the meaning that chance has in life and in art, and about their connection. Mr Watsuji Tetsurō[^c3s11-23] has pointed out the meaning of contingency in the making of *renku*:[^c3s11-24] "In *renku* each verse has an independent world. And yet between them there is a subtle connection, and while one world unfolds into another world, it also has a unity as a whole. Since this unfolding between verse and verse is usually carried out by different authors, the unity that one author's power of imagination has is deliberately thrown away, and the direction of the unfolding is left rather to 'chance'. Consequently the unity as a whole is the product of 'chance', but moreover, for that very reason, the whole becomes on the contrary richer, and gives rise to turns that could not be expected of a single author [p. 284] [コマ 149]" (Watsuji Tetsurō, *Fūdo*, p. 325). The fact that stringed instruments have a flavour that keyboard instruments lack is probably also grounded mainly in contingent variability. On the relation between chance and the life of art, let me quote the words of an artist himself. "People feel great wonder at chance things. ... Especially for us painters, who are always in direct contact with nature, this wonder of chance is precisely good fortune. Whatever misfortune it may be, one comes to feel like accepting it, on one side, with enjoyment. That painters are mostly people who leave things as they are, or are capricious, is because they dislike expecting things. ... Only, depending on one's way of thinking, all everyday things may perhaps be chance. Life changes even according to whether or not one is receptive to chance" (Kawaguchi Kigai, "Chance", Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun, 30 April, Shōwa 8).[^c3s11-25] I said that the inner relation of art to contingency is, first, that the structural character of art itself is contingent, and second, that art loves contingent things as its object-content; but, as the passage just quoted shows, the two points probably have deep roots in common in the human existentiality of the artist.

[^c3s11-1]: Tr. *Poetics* 24, 1460a: τὸ δὲ θαυμαστὸν ἡδύ, "the marvellous is pleasant".
[^c3s11-2]: Tr. *Asagao nikki* ("Morning Glory Diary"), the story, staged in puppet theatre and kabuki, of Miyuki and Komazawa Jirōzaemon, lovers repeatedly parted and brought together by chance.
[^c3s11-3]: Tr. A Nō play attributed to Zeami. The blind prince Semimaru, abandoned at the Ōsaka barrier (逢坂の關), is found there by his deranged elder sister Sakagami.
[^c3s11-4]: Tr. The Buddhist parable of the blind tortoise that rises once in a hundred years and puts its head through the single hole of a log drifting on the ocean, quoted by Kuki from the 雜阿含經 (*Saṃyuktāgama*) in section 8, as an image of the rarity of human birth.
[^c3s11-5]: Tr. 偶然の暗合 (*gūzen no angō*) and 盲目的偶然 (*mōmokuteki gūzen*). 暗合 is an unplanned coincidence, literally a "dark fit".
[^c3s11-6]: Tr. Novalis, pen name of Friedrich von Hardenberg (1772-1801), German Romantic poet and philosopher.
[^c3s11-7]: Tr. Nakagawa Yoichi (1897-1994), Japanese novelist associated with the New Sensationalist school (新感覺派).
[^c3s11-8]: Tr. 不思議 (*fushigi*), the mysterious or marvellous; a different word from Kuki's 驚異, wonder.
[^c3s11-9]: Tr. Shōwa 10 is 1935. *Shinchō* is a literary monthly. "Yarn-ball" renders 毛毬 (*kemari*), a ball of wound thread.
[^c3s11-10]: Tr. Kuki's ベエラム and ディレラム; the legend concerns the Sasanian king Bahrām Gūr and Dilārām. The point of the story is that the two names rhyme.
[^c3s11-11]: Tr. "Aurore", the opening poem of Valéry's *Charmes* (1922).
[^c3s11-12]: Tr. Kuki writes 偶然(運), "chance (fortune)", giving 運 (*un*, luck, fortune) as an alternative reading of the French.
[^c3s11-13]: Tr. The Husserl Festschrift appeared in 1929 as a supplementary volume of the *Jahrbuch*. 果無さ (*hakanasa*) is transience, evanescence; 懷れ易さ is printed with 懷 for 壞 (*kowareyasusa*, breakability). *Fragilität* is Kuki's spelling.
[^c3s11-14]: Tr. 言靈 (*kotodama*), the spirit or power dwelling in words, an old Japanese belief.
[^c3s11-15]: Tr. 學問 (*gakumon*), learning or science in the broad sense of *Wissenschaft*.
[^c3s11-16]: Tr. 君子以自彊不息, from the Xiang (象傳) commentary on the first hexagram, Qian. Kuki quotes the classical Chinese.
[^c3s11-17]: Tr. *Faust* I, 1759: "Nur rastlos betätigt sich der Mann." Line 1700 is "Verweile doch! du bist so schön!"; line 11582 is its echo in Part II.
[^c3s11-18]: Tr. Henjō (816-890), Buddhist prelate and one of the Six Poetic Immortals. The line closes his poem in the *Kokinshū* (also no. 12 of the *Hyakunin isshu*), asking the winds to close the cloud paths so that the dancing maidens cannot return to heaven.
[^c3s11-19]: Tr. Abe Jirō (1883-1959), Japanese philosopher and critic. 世界文化と日本文化.
[^c3s11-20]: Tr. Fujiwara Sakuhei (1884-1950), Japanese meteorologist. 氣象と人生.
[^c3s11-21]: Tr. Theodor Lipps (1851-1914), German philosopher and psychologist, theorist of empathy (*Einfühlung*).
[^c3s11-22]: Tr. 窯變 (*yōhen*), kiln transformation: unplanned changes of colour and glaze produced by the fire.
[^c3s11-23]: Tr. Watsuji Tetsurō (1889-1960), Japanese philosopher and ethicist; *Fūdo* (風土, 1935) is his study of climate and culture.
[^c3s11-24]: Tr. 連句 (*renku*), linked verse composed in turn by several poets, each verse joining to the one before.
[^c3s11-25]: Tr. Kawaguchi Kigai (1892-1966), Japanese painter in the Western style. Shōwa 8 is 1933.
[^c3s11-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 活動して暫くも休まずにゐてこそ男兒だ (ゲーテ、ファウスト、第一部一七五九). Our translation: "Only by being active and not resting even for a moment is one a man."
[^c3s11-v2]: Tr. Kuki's text: 止まれ、お前はいかにも美しいから (ゲーテ、ファウスト第一部一七〇〇、第二部一一五八二). Our translation: "Stay, for you are so beautiful."
[^c3s11-v3]: Tr. Kuki's text: 乙女の姿しばしとどめん (僧正遍昭). Our translation: "the maidens' figures, let me keep them a while."

*Section 12 begins at the top of p. 285 (left page of コマ 149). Section 13 begins at the top of p. 300 (right page of コマ 157). Section 14 begins partway down p. 313, where this part ends.*

### 12. Chance and fate

[p. 285] [コマ 149] Metaphysical wonder at contingency sometimes takes the form of wonder at *fate*. When contingency carries a core meaning for the existentiality of the human being, a meaning that engages the whole person, it is called fate, and contingency as fate, through its heterogeneous combination with *necessity*, displays the structure of "the necessary-contingent",[^c3s12-1] confronting the whole existence of the human being sternly and with transcendent power.

The structure of the necessary-contingent can be seen in Heraclitus's idea of fate (εἱμαρμένη, *fatum*).[^c3s12-2] He stresses necessity when he says that "in any case it is *fated* (ἔστι εἱμαρμένα)" (Diels, *Fragmente der Vorsokratiker*, I. Herakleitos, Fr. 137), and at the same time he stresses the contingency of the world when he says that "the most beautiful world is a heap of rubbish piled up *at random* (εἰκῇ)" (Ib., Fr. 124). Kant, in his *Lectures on Metaphysics*, holds that what is absurd among the connections of things is blind necessity (*die blinde Notwendigkeit*), that is, fate [p. 286] [コマ 150] (*Schicksal*), and blind chance (*das blinde Ungefähr*), that is, chance (*Zufall*); the former, he says, is "what is grounded neither in the essence of the matter nor in other causes", the latter "what is contingent in every respect" (*Kant's Vorlesungen über die Metaphysik*, 2 Aufl. hrsg. v. Schmidt, S. 52 f.). In the *Critique of Pure Reason*, too, he declares two propositions to be a priori laws of nature: that "nothing happens through blind chance", that is, "in the world there is no chance" (*in mundo non datur casus*), and that "no necessity in nature is blind", that is, "there is no fate" (*non datur fatum*). He holds that both belong to the dynamical principles, though the former, as a consequence of the principle of causality, is to be understood according to the categories of relation, whilst the latter, which adds the concept of necessity to the determination of causality, is to be interpreted according to the categories of modality. Moreover, when Kant says here that "there is no chance" or that "there is no fate", he is saying only that there is none "in the world", and this "world" is nothing other than the so-called "world of appearances", or "nature", that is the object of mathematical natural science. As he puts it, "these principles agree in that they permit nothing that would bring interruption and hindrance to the understanding and to the continuous connection of all appearances, that is, to the unity of the concepts of the understanding, [p. 287] in *empirical synthesis*" (see Kant, *Critique of Pure Reason*, B. 280-282). Kant appears to understand fate as necessity and to set it over against contingency, but this holds only at the level of the words, for in fact he sees a deep inner connection between the two. Contingency on the one side, and necessity as fate on the other, agree in their blindness, and blind means "that by which one can see nothing" (*das, wodurch man nichts sehen kann*) (*Kant's Vorlesungen über die Metaphysik*. 2 Auf. hrsg. v. Schmidt, S. 52). Blind contingency, contingent in every respect, and blind necessity, grounded neither in the essence of the matter nor in other causes, are in the end one and the same. For Kant too, in short, the concept of fate is seen in the structure of the necessary-contingent.

Schopenhauer, in his essay on the fate of the individual,[^c3s12-3] sets out the necessary-contingent connection of contingency as fate: "However purely contingent the course of things may appear, it is nevertheless at bottom not contingent. Rather, all these chances themselves, τὰ εἰκῇ φερόμενα (what is brought about at random), are embraced by one deeply hidden necessity, εἱμαρ- [p. 288] [コマ 151] μένη (fate), and chance itself is a mere instrument of that necessity" (Schopenhauer, *Sämtliche Werke* hrsg. v. Deussen, IV. S. 228). "At the root of this *mundus phaenomenon* (world of appearances), ruled by chance, there will lie, wholly and everywhere, a *mundus intelligibilis* (intelligible world) that governs chance itself" (Ib., 232). The concept of fate is "the deep-lying root-unity of necessity and contingency" (Ib., 235), "the final unity of necessity and contingency" (Ib.). The same sense finds expression in the words "If *by chance* you obtain practice and faith, rejoice far back in the *conditions of past lives*"[^c3s12-v1] (*Kyōgyōshinshō*, Preface),[^c3s12-4] and the fatalism and the acausalism of the six teachers likewise share a point of agreement that is hard to tell apart,[^c3s12-5] since to say "all is made by former destiny"[^c3s12-v2] and to say "all is without cause and without condition"[^c3s12-v3] come in the end to the same thing (*Madhyama Āgama*, fascicle 3, *Du jing*). It is because such an inner relation holds between chance and fate that Wilhelm von Scholz gives his book *Chance* the alternative title *A Preliminary Form of Fate* (*Eine Vorform des Schicksals*).[^c3s12-6] In Kunikida Doppo's "The Fatalist",[^c3s12-7] when A says "I am not a fatalist", B is made to press him with "Then are you a believer in chance?", and this retort, which is no more than irony, presupposes that fate and chance are ultimately one and the same. In Mushanokōji [p. 289] Saneatsu's "The Man Who Plays Go with Fate"[^c3s12-8] we find the words "Fate is something of which one cannot tell where it is lying in wait", and here one might just as well say "chance" in place of "fate". I have already mentioned in passing that successive contingency, as recurrent contingency, shows the combination of contingency and necessity and in some cases presses close to the concept of fate (see p. 264).

Among those who grasped the connection between chance and fate most deeply is Schelling. For Schelling, primal contingency is "unprethinkable destiny" (*unvordenkliches Verhängnis*).[^c3s12-9] It is unprethinkable because consciousness can neither think nor recollect what came before it, and it is destiny because the will, seeing a result it did not intend, is struck with wonder and believes that, though it now stands in actuality, it is the same as when it was still in possibility. What is primal contingency for consciousness in pure substantiality is, in other words, fate for actual consciousness. Actual consciousness is not itself conscious of the act through which it came to be, since by that act it became something wholly other and was cut off from its former state, and to recollect a former state there must be identity between the one who recollects and what is recollected. That a sleepwalker cannot, on waking, recollect what he did in his sleep is because [p. 290] [コマ 152] the person asleep and the person awake are different persons. Through primal contingency consciousness is thenceforth subject to an "inescapable fate" (*unabwendliches Schicksal*), yet for consciousness that has now become actual and has separated from itself, that primal contingency sinks necessarily into a bottomless depth. Primal contingency is the "first-born fortune" (*Fortuna primigenia*)[^c3s12-10] from which every other contingency springs (Schelling, *Sämmtliche Werke* II, 2, 1857, S. 153-154).

Kant says that "there are usurped concepts (*usurpirte Begriffe*), such as fortune (*Glück*) and fate (*Schicksal*), which circulate with almost general indulgence but are sometimes required to answer the question 'what is the right?', and then people fall into no small perplexity over their deduction" (*Critique of Pure Reason*, B. 117).[^c3s12-11] If a "subjective deduction" can be made, however, the problem of an "objective deduction" does not arise; if the subjective reality of these concepts can be shown, there is no need "to prove their objective reality" (Ib.). Even if they are "usurped concepts", that is to say, it is enough that they are borrowed from the humanity of the human being.

On Spengler's view, fate belongs to the ultimate lived experience of the individual soul and of a culture as a whole, [p. 291] and can be communicated only through religion and art, not through concepts and proofs.[^c3s12-12] What grasps what fate is, is lived experience and not scientific experience, intuition and not calculation; what comprehends it is depth (*Tiefe*) and not intellect (*Geist*), an organic logic and not the logic of the inorganic. Fate is the word for an inner certainty that cannot be described (*Schicksal ist das Wort für eine nicht zu beschreibende innere Gewissheit*) (Spengler, *Der Untergang des Abendlandes*, I. 1924, S. 152, 153, 180). It may well be impossible, as Spengler says, to describe clearly what fate is, but I think we can make clear, to a certain degree, how the concept of fate arose, what relations it keeps with other, similar concepts, and what moments it contains within itself. The concept of fate probably arose in cases where teleological contingency overawes the whole existence of the human being. Teleological contingency readily enters into heterogeneous combination with causal necessity, and causal necessity in turn, through homogeneous combination, combines with teleological necessity; this "necessity-contingency" complex, having acquired a transcendence of actuality through the enormity of the matter itself, is nothing [p. 292] [コマ 153] other than fate. The homogeneous combination of causal necessity and teleological necessity is made here in the following sense. Purposiveness, as what determines the present through the future, presupposes consciousness, as I said earlier (see p. 68), but between consciousness and the unconscious there opens a graded passage, and through this passage consciousness turns into the unconscious. In this way teleological necessity combines with causal necessity, and causal necessity with teleological necessity. Diogenes Laertius reports as a Stoic doctrine that "all things come about by fate (εἱμαρμένη), and fate is the *cause* (αἰτία) of existents, or the *reason* (λόγος) by which the world proceeds" (Diogenes Laertios, VII, 149),[^c3s12-13] and there is no harm in seeing here two moments contained within fate, teleological necessity as "reason" and causal necessity as "cause". Causal necessity and teleological necessity, at any rate, enter into homogeneous combination. As for what it means that teleological contingency and teleological necessity are combined in the concept of fate, the former concerns purposiveness on the horizon of human *Existenz*, whilst the latter concerns transcendent purposiveness. Fate, in short, is teleological contingency that combines with causal necessity and, through that mediation, with teleological necessity as well, and which, as the necessary- [p. 293] contingent, is at once pushed away into the infinitely distant and drawn into the infinitely near. For the logic of the understanding the concept of fate may perhaps be a "usurped concept", but it is a concept deeply rooted in human experience, provided originally, pre-conceptually as it were, by the humanity of the human being. Anyone who opens Mokuami's *Chijimiya Shinsuke*,[^c3s12-14] to say nothing of Sophocles' *Oedipus the King* or Shakespeare's *King Lear*, will refuse to surrender the acquired right of the concept of fate.

Kant himself offers a typical use of the concept of fate at the very opening of the preface to the first edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason*: "Human reason has a peculiar *fate* in one kind of its cognitions: it is troubled by questions that it cannot dismiss, but that it also cannot answer. It cannot dismiss them because they are imposed on reason by the nature of reason itself, and it cannot answer them because they transcend every capacity of human reason." What is fate, if not the necessary-contingent born as the lover of wisdom, the child of Poros and Penia, bearing a future of eternal suffering?[^c3s12-15] In the [p. 294] [コマ 154] first chapter of the *Principles of Moral Philosophy*[^c3s12-16] Kant also says: "Even if, through a special disfavour of *fate* or the scant provision of a stepmotherly nature, this will should wholly lack the capacity to carry through its purpose, and even if, for all its striving, nothing were accomplished by it and only the good will remained, it would still, like a jewel, shine truly by itself with a brilliant light, as something that holds its whole value within itself." Let me give one or two further examples of how the concept of fate is applied. "At each moment we are *face to face* with the absolutely irrational. We are face to face with absolute fact; there we are not act but sense; there we *touch* the fact of primordial history; it is what should be called the content of our *fate*" (Nishida Kitarō, *The Self-Conscious Determination of Nothingness*, pp. 187-188).[^c3s12-17] "There must be somewhere a place where the power of man and the work of man, everything human, at some point comes to an end, and we *come up against* a divine reality that confronts us with absolute authority. In revelation every resistance becomes vain, every like and dislike is set at nought, and a person is carried off with his whole personality, willy-nilly, into *unexpected* light, truth, blessedness and life. ... For the great [p. 295] religious figures revelation was not necessarily a path they liked or a work they relished. ... For them revelation was *fate*, holding infinite glory and joy, at any rate *fate*" (Hatano Seiichi, *Philosophy of Religion*, pp. 32-33).[^c3s12-18]

I said that fate is teleological contingency which, combining on the one hand with causal necessity and on the other with teleological necessity, has been expanded to the infinitely large and at the same time contracted to the infinitely small. If we force an analysis, the combination of teleological contingency with causal necessity appears as "blind fate", and the combination of teleological contingency with teleological necessity as "providence". According to Hegel, the πεπρωμένον and εἱμαρμένη of the ancients are blind fate,[^c3s12-19] whilst Christianity, by introducing purposiveness, teaches the providence of God; the former is "necessity that has not cast off its veil" (*unenthüllte Notwendigkeit*), the latter "necessity that has cast off its veil" (*enthüllte Notwendigkeit*), and the former is a blind and comfortless standpoint, the latter a foreseeing and consoling one (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 147, Zusatz, S. 197-200). Yet the distinctive feature of the concept of fate lies, I think, rather in its not permitting so sharp a distinction between two kinds of fate, since, as I have already said, between consciousness and the unconscious there is a graded and gradual [p. 296] [コマ 155] passage. Spengler too explains the inner connection of fate and chance, expressing the relation of antiquity and of the West to the two in these words: "If we may understand chance, for the Western soul, as fate of small content, then conversely, for the soul of antiquity, fate may be seen as chance raised to the enormous" (l. c. S. 189). On the point that the difference between fate and chance is a difference of degree in content, he says that "that Goethe came to Sesenheim is felt as chance, that he came to Weimar as fate; the former is seen as an episode (*Episode*), the latter as an epoch (*Epoche*)" (l. c. S. 179),[^c3s12-20] and he goes on to stress the opposition between them. Fate, however, should be seen as what has taken on the structure of the necessary-contingent as a result of contingency's heterogeneous combination with necessity, and to insist too much on an opposition between fate and chance hinders a proper understanding of both.

I have pointed out that teleological contingency enters into diagonal combination with teleological necessity (see the figure on p. 77 〔Figure 1〕)[^c3s12-21] because the former is teleological contingency for human existence whilst the latter is teleological necessity in transcendent existence. Teleological necessity in transcendent existence, as something external to the purposiveness of human existence itself, [p. 297] still does not cease to be teleological contingency. Under the concept of fate, however, there are cases in which teleological contingency and teleological necessity combine dynamically on the horizon of human existence, cases in which teleological contingency, as a sublated moment, lies within teleological necessity and conditions it, and such cases can be regarded as fate in the eminent sense. That the combination and the conditioning are then made, so to speak, "under the aspect of eternity"[^c3s12-22] goes without saying, since this is fate. In the ordinary concept of fate, teleological necessity is thought to condition teleological contingency within their combination; in the concept of fate in the eminent sense, conversely, teleological contingency conditions teleological necessity, or, in a certain sense, the two condition each other reciprocally. Here lies the difference between the ordinary concept of fate and the concept of fate in the eminent sense. Fate in the eminent sense appears, for example, in Heidegger's concept of fate (see *Iwanami Philosophy Series*, Kuki, "The Philosophy of Existence", pp. 81-82).[^c3s12-23] Fate becomes fate only when it is immanent in anticipatory resoluteness. Fate is the authentic happening of Dasein,[^c3s12-24] and accordingly it must be both thrownness (*Geworfenheit*) and projection (*Entwurf*), a handing down of oneself to oneself in a possibility that is inherited and yet [p. 298] [コマ 156] chosen. Fate lies in the combination of the superior power (*Übermacht*) and the powerlessness (*Ohnmacht*) of a resoluteness that hands itself down; the powerless superior power that passionately hands itself down in the face of the contingency of the disclosed situation is the place of fate. Fate is an existent, futural and at the same time originally having-been, taking over its own thrownness whilst giving possibility to itself in finitude. Jaspers's concept of fate is of the same kind.[^c3s12-25] As Jaspers puts it, fate is the case in which "chances are inwardly appropriated" (*Die Zufälle sind innerlich angeeignet*) (Jaspers, *Philosophie*, II. S. 218). "I and the situation have become one. Because historical consciousness is deeply aware of the self and the particularity of its own Dasein as identical, fortune and misfortune are no longer grasped merely as other, merely as what comes from outside, but are grasped, in the deeper thought called fate, as what belongs to me. I sink myself into my historical determinacy, and within this historical determinacy I affirm my Dasein as it is. It is not that I affirm Dasein merely as empirical objectivity; I affirm [p. 299] Dasein as existentially saturated objectivity. ... In this immersion I grasp fate not merely as something external but as my own, in 'love of fate' (*amor fati*). Since I become existentially aware only within fate, I love my fate as I love myself. In good contrast to the fading of the merely universal and of the whole, I experience existence existentially within what is objectively limitation. Historical consciousness as consciousness of fate is to think concrete Dasein seriously" (Ib., II. S. 218-219). "Historical determinacy in the limit situation, instead of being merely chance, becomes the appearance of a certain existence, an existence that my understanding does not grasp but that is confirmed to me as the eternal in time. The lover says to the beloved, 'You were my sister or my wife in a former life'" (Ib., II. S. 217).

The concept of fate in the eminent sense, in short, must be one in which the self, with passionate self-awareness, sinks itself into contingency and thereby brings itself originally to life. And contingency as fate is not infrequently grasped as the eternal present of recurrent metaphysical time.

[^c3s12-1]: Tr. Kuki joins 必然 and 偶然者 with a long printed rule, and 者 marks an entity. Rendered "the necessary-contingent"; the same pair without 者 is rendered "necessary-contingent" (connection) or "necessity-contingency" (complex). 實存性 is rendered "existentiality" and 實存 *Existenz*, keeping both apart from 存在, "existence".
[^c3s12-2]: Tr. εἱμαρμένη (*heimarmenē*), what is allotted, fate; εἰκῇ (*eikēi*), at random. The fragment numbers are Diels's.
[^c3s12-3]: Tr. "Transzendente Spekulation über die anscheinende Absichtlichkeit im Schicksale des Einzelnen" (1851), in *Parerga und Paralipomena*, vol. I.
[^c3s12-4]: Tr. The *Kyōgyōshinshō* (敎行信證) of Shinran (1173-1263), founder of the Jōdo Shinshū school of Pure Land Buddhism. 行信 (*gyōshin*) is practice and faith; 宿緣 (*shukuen*) are the karmic conditions formed in past lives.
[^c3s12-5]: Tr. The six teachers (六師) are the six non-Buddhist teachers contemporary with the Buddha. 宿命論 is the doctrine that all is fixed by past destiny; 無因論 the doctrine that things arise without cause. The *Madhyama Āgama* (中阿含經) is a Chinese collection of the Buddha's discourses, and the *Du jing* (度經) a sutra within it.
[^c3s12-6]: Tr. Wilhelm von Scholz (1874-1969), German writer; *Der Zufall: eine Vorform des Schicksals* (1924).
[^c3s12-7]: Tr. Kunikida Doppo (1871-1908), Japanese novelist; "Unmei ronja" (運命論者, 1903). Kuki's 甲 and 乙 are rendered A and B.
[^c3s12-8]: Tr. Mushanokōji Saneatsu (1885-1976), Japanese novelist and playwright; the title is 運命と碁をする男 (*Unmei to go o suru otoko*).
[^c3s12-9]: Tr. *Unvordenklich*: that which no thinking can get before; *Verhängnis*: destiny, doom.
[^c3s12-10]: Tr. *Fortuna Primigenia*, "first-born Fortune", was the cult title of Fortuna at Praeneste.
[^c3s12-11]: Tr. Kuki's 「權利は何ぞ」 renders Kant's *quid juris*, the question of right that a deduction must answer.
[^c3s12-12]: Tr. Oswald Spengler (1880-1936), German philosopher of history; *Der Untergang des Abendlandes* (*The Decline of the West*), 1918-1922.
[^c3s12-13]: Tr. Diogenes Laertius, *Lives of the Eminent Philosophers*, Book VII, on Zeno and the Stoics. αἰτία (*aitia*), cause; λόγος (*logos*), reason.
[^c3s12-14]: Tr. Kawatake Mokuami (1816-1893), kabuki playwright. *Chijimiya Shinsuke* is the popular name of his play about Shinsuke of the Chijimiya and the geisha Miyokichi (see Chapter 2, section 15).
[^c3s12-15]: Tr. In Plato's *Symposium* (203b-204b) Eros is the child of Poros (resource) and Penia (poverty), and as such a lover of wisdom. Kuki writes 愛智者 with the ruby フィロソフォス (*philosophos*).
[^c3s12-16]: Tr. Kuki's title is 道德哲學原論, "Principles of Moral Philosophy", for Kant's *Grundlegung zur Metaphysik der Sitten* (1785).
[^c3s12-17]: Tr. Nishida Kitarō (1870-1945); *Mu no jikakuteki gentei* (無の自覺的限定, 1932).
[^c3s12-18]: Tr. Hatano Seiichi (1877-1950), Japanese philosopher of religion; *Shūkyō tetsugaku* (宗敎哲學, 1935).
[^c3s12-19]: Tr. πεπρωμένον (*peprōmenon*), what has been allotted, destiny.
[^c3s12-20]: Tr. At Sesenheim in Alsace the student Goethe loved Friederike Brion (1770-1771); he settled in Weimar in 1775.
[^c3s12-21]: Tr. The figure is in Chapter 2, section 5.
[^c3s12-22]: Tr. *Sub specie aeternitatis*, Spinoza's phrase.

Heidegger's terms behind Kuki's Japanese: 先驅的決意性, *vorlaufende Entschlossenheit* (anticipatory resoluteness); 本來的な事件, *eigentliches Geschehen*; 自己交付, *sich überliefern* (handing oneself down); 超力 and 無力, *Übermacht* and *Ohnmacht*; 將來的, *zukünftig*; 既存的, *gewesend*. 現存在 is rendered "Dasein" here and in the Jaspers passage.
