[^c3s12-v2]: Tr. Kuki's text: 一切皆因宿命造. Our translation: "all is made by former destiny."
[^c3s12-v3]: Tr. Kuki's text: 一切皆無因無緣 (中阿含經、卷三、度經). Our translation: "all is without cause and without condition."

### 13. The metaphysical absolute

[p. 300] [コマ 157] Fate is one of the disjuncts, thrown out like the pips of a die, being grasped in lived experience into the core of *Existenz* even as it shakes *Existenz* in its whole breadth.[^c3s13-1] A disjunct presupposes the whole of the disjunctive possibilities, and the whole of possibilities leads, in the last analysis, to the concept of the metaphysical absolute. The absolute, because it is the absolute, is thought as absolutely one, and because it is absolutely one, it is thought as absolutely necessary; this absolute necessity can be called metaphysical necessity. Aristotle had already set out the concept in the *Metaphysics* (Aristoteles, *Metaphysica*, Λ. 7. 1072b).[^c3s13-2] The "unmoved mover", because it is unmoved, "never has the contingency of being otherwise" (οὐκ ἐνδέχεται ἄλλως ἔχειν οὐδαμῶς), and it is consequently "what exists of necessity" (ἐξ ἀνάγκης ὄν) (see p. 193). Everything other than the unmoved mover is moved by another, and so "has the contingency of being otherwise" (ἐνδέχεται ἄλλως ἔχειν); it is, in other words, metaphysical contingency as the [p. 301] negation of metaphysical necessity. These concepts of metaphysical necessity and metaphysical contingency are nothing other than the application of disjunctive necessity and disjunctive contingency to the metaphysical horizon, and in the Middle Ages they were taken up by Moses Maimonides, a venerator of Aristotle, and put to use as a moment in the "cosmological proof" of the existence of God.[^c3s13-3] Thomas Aquinas, following Maimonides, likewise inferred from the contingency of the world to "something that is necessary through itself" (*aliquid quod est per se necessarium*) (Thomas Aquinas, *Summa theologiae* I. 2. 3), and the relation between metaphysical necessity and metaphysical contingency also took the form of the opposition between "aseity" (*aseitas*) and "abaleity" (*abalietas*).[^c3s13-4] What Spinoza calls the "cause of itself" (*causa sui*) (Spinoza, *Ethica*, I, def. 1) is also nothing other than metaphysical absolute necessity: "God exists necessarily" (*Deus necessario existit*) (Ib., I, 11). By contrast, "so long as I find nothing that necessarily posits its existence or necessarily excludes it, I call individual things contingent (*contingentes*)" (Ib., IV, def. 3). Leibniz too, in the *Theodicy* and the *Monadology*, spoke of God as "the necessary existent" (*Être nécessaire*) and of the events of the world as "contingent existents" (*êtres contingents*) (Leibniz, *Opera philosophica*, ed. Erdmann, p. 506, 708).[^c3s13-5]

[p. 302] [コマ 158] We notice, then, that the meanings of necessity and contingency are directly reversed according to whether we stand on the empirical horizon or on the metaphysical horizon. The empirical horizon proceeds "from below", going back infinitely along the series of causal necessity until it arrives at primal contingency (see p. 183). The metaphysical horizon proceeds "from above", and obtains, as the negation of absolute necessity, the concept of contingency held captive within causality. In other words, being determined by causality is called necessary from the empirical standpoint and contingent from the metaphysical standpoint. Again, when the beginning of the causal series is grasped as an idea, it is called primal contingency from the empirical standpoint, whilst the same idea is called absolute necessity, in the form of disjunctive necessity, from the metaphysical standpoint. Within necessity, that is to say, there is a distinction between "absolute necessity" and "hypothetical necessity". Leibniz too drew the distinction between "absolute necessity" (*nécessité absolue*) and "hypothetical necessity" (*nécessité hypothétique*) (Leibniz, *Opera philosophica*, ed. Erdmann, p. 763), and according to Christian Wolff, "necessary existence is that whose existence is *absolutely* necessary (*absolute necessaria*); contingent existence is that which has the ground of its own existence outside itself", so that "the existence of contingent existence is merely *hypothetically* necessary (*hypothetice necessaria*)" (Christian Wolff, *Ontologia*, [p. 303] § 309f., § 316).[^c3s13-6] Kant too draws this distinction in the *Lectures on Metaphysics*: absolute necessity (*absolute Notwendigkeit*) is the case in which the existence of a thing is known a priori *simply* (*simpliciter*), and hypothetical necessity (*hypothetische Notwendigkeit*) the case in which the existence of a thing is known a priori *according to something* (*secundum quid*) (*Kant's Vorlesungen über die Metaphysik*, 2 Aufl. hrsg. v. Schmidt, S. 27). This metaphysical contingency, which is merely hypothetically necessary, can also not exist, since, as Aristotle had already shown, it exists only through some cause other than itself, and this is why Thomas Aquinas calls the contingent "what is possible not to exist" (*possibilia non esse*) (Thomas Aquinas, *Summa theologiae* I. 2. 3). Metaphysical contingency is, after all, nothing other than empirical necessity; yet even empirical necessity, insofar as it is witnessed over against the metaphysical necessity of the absolute, takes on the character of contingency.

We have seen that the relation of opposition between empirical necessity and primal contingency holds, as the relation of cause and effect, at the hypothetical standpoint, and that the relation of opposition between metaphysical contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity holds, as the relation of whole and part, at the disjunctive standpoint. We must next consider more closely the relation between primal [p. 304] [コマ 159] contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity, and the relation between empirical necessity and metaphysical contingency.

What, first, is the relation between primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity? Primal contingency is a concept obtained on the hypothetical horizon as the primal beginning of the causal series: going back infinitely along the causal series of empirical necessity, we arrived, as at an idea, at the concept of primal contingency. Primal contingency is the "primal event" (*Urereignis*), the "beginning of history" (*Anfang der Geschichte*) (Schelling, *Sämmtliche Werke* II, 2, 1857, S. 153). The absolute beginning of the necessary series conditioned by the causal chain can, however, also be thought as the whole that implicitly contains as its parts all the empirical necessities that are the members of the series. Absolute metaphysical necessity, on the other hand, is the whole that has as its parts all the metaphysical contingencies on the disjunctive horizon, and since absolute necessity as the whole can also be seen as conditioning the positing of the disjuncts as its parts, it can also be thought as the absolute beginning of the causal series. Insofar as the serial view and the extensional view can be exchanged for one another in this way, primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity must be one and the same. We might note, further, that on the hypothetical horizon [p. 305] the infinite causal series was represented in the form of a straight line, with primal contingency thought as its ideal beginning; but true infinity is represented in the form of a circle, in which the last of the series returns to the first, and absolute metaphysical necessity is then thought as the whole circle comprehending each of the circle's parts. From this point too it is clear that primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity are one and the same. Whether we say primal contingency or absolute necessity, we mean in the end the metaphysical absolute, and the Great Ultimate of the *Changes*, for instance, can likewise be thought, as the metaphysical absolute, both as primal contingency and as absolute necessity.[^c3s13-7] Absolute metaphysical necessity and primal contingency are, in short, no more than two sides of one thing, and Spinoza's "cause of itself" and Schelling's "what is contingent through itself" (*das durch sich selbst Zufällige*) in the end unite in one. The absolute must be thought as possessing the contradictory character of "the necessary-contingent".[^c3s13-8] As Jakob Böhme says, it is "Yes" (*Ja*) and at the same time "No" (*Nein*): "'Yes' and 'No' are simply one thing, only it separates itself into two beginnings and makes two centres" (Jacob Böhme, *Sämtliche Werke* 6, S. 597).[^c3s13-9] Primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity, whilst being one thing in the absolute, still make "two centres". Primal contingency is what was caught as the last idea from the empirical-hypothetical standpoint, which proceeds [p. 306] [コマ 160] "from below", and absolute necessity is what was set up as the first concept from the metaphysical-disjunctive standpoint, which proceeds "from above". Absolute metaphysical necessity consequently expresses what we might call the affirmative character of the absolute, and primal contingency its negative character. Absolute metaphysical necessity is the in-itself of the absolute; primal contingency is the otherness within the absolute.[^c3s13-10] Here too lies the possibility of thinking absolute metaphysical necessity as divine reality, and primal contingency as the beginning of the world or as a fall (*Zufall=Abfall*).[^c3s13-11] There is no harm in thinking that absolute necessity is the static side of the absolute and primal contingency its dynamic side. The words of the pre-critical Kant are worth savouring: "The first of things is the absolutely necessary. The first of states is the absolutely contingent" (*Das erste der Dinge ist das schlechterdings notwendige. Das erste der Zustände ist das schlechterdings zufällige.*) (Kant, *Reflexionen zur Metaphysik*, Akademie Ausgabe XVIII. S. 142).

If we think in this way, the close relation between primal contingency, as the absolute beginning of the causal series, and metaphysical contingency, posited as [p. 307] a disjunct, also becomes clear of itself. The a priori Ground of the possibility that a disjunct is posited as a contingent can be thought to lie in the contingency of primal contingency, which is nothing other than the absolute Ground that determines metaphysical disjunctive contingency, within variability, as contingency. The close relation between metaphysical necessity, as the whole of the parts, and empirical necessity, as a member of the causal series, is likewise clear; as I have already said, the relation between the two is the relation between absolute necessity and hypothetical necessity.

We still need to elucidate, in another respect, the character of the absolute as the necessary-contingent. Whole and part in general have their meaning in correlation: without parts there is no whole, and without a whole there are no parts. The necessity of absolute metaphysical necessity consequently cannot be thought without the contingency of metaphysical contingency, nor can the contingency of metaphysical contingency be thought without the necessity of absolute metaphysical necessity. The necessity of absolute necessity is the necessity possessed by a whole that has contingency as its parts. Necessity conditions contingency, and contingency conditions necessity. The absolute, in short, insofar as it is not an empty abstract whole [p. 308] [コマ 161] but a fulfilled concrete whole, is neither the merely necessary nor the merely contingent: it is the necessary-contingent, which has its meaning in the correlation of necessity and contingency. And since the absolute can be the absolute only through the relative, finite thing, which is to say that it acquires the concrete meaning of the absolute whole only by existing as other in contingent parts, there will be no harm in thinking the absolute as the necessary-contingent as the stage of the in-and-for-itself in the dialectic of the absolute. It goes without saying, moreover, that insofar as primal contingency can be thought as the whole that has each member of the causal series as its parts, exactly the same consideration can be made of the relation between primal contingency and empirical necessity as of the relation between metaphysical necessity and metaphysical contingency. I believe I have now made clear that primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity are two sides of the absolute as the necessary-contingent.

What, next, is the relation between empirical necessity and metaphysical contingency? Empirical necessity on the hypothetical horizon appears on the disjunctive horizon as metaphysical contingency. Leibniz, in the *Discourse on Metaphysics*, seeking to set up a distinction between the necessary (*nécessaire*) and the certain (*certain*), [p. 309] holds that the former is that whose opposite contains a contradiction and the latter that whose opposite does not, and that since only the absolutely necessary (*absolument nécessaire*) is *necessary* in the strict sense, the hypothetically necessary (*nécessaire ex hypothesi*) is no more than *certain*. That Caesar did not stop at the Rubicon but crossed it, for example, and that he did not lose but won the battle of Pharsalus, are not necessary but certain, and because they are certain but not necessary, Leibniz explains, they are contingent. The reason why something is certain and yet not necessary, that is, the reason why it is contingent, he Grounds in the will of God, which is to say in the freedom of choice (Leibniz, *Discours de métaphysique*, § 13).[^c3s13-12] Leibniz's concept of the "certain", in short, seeks to express empirical necessity, that is, metaphysical contingency. At any rate, each member of the series, insofar as it is conditioned by the absolute beginning, reflects the character of the absolute beginning, and in each part, insofar as it is a part of the whole, the character of the whole is projected. The character of the absolute as the necessary-contingent comes to take, in each member and each part, the form of fate as the necessary-contingent. Kant's so-called "lawfulness of the contingent" (*Gesetzlichkeit des Zufälligen*), that is, the concept of purposiveness (Kant, *Kritik der Urteils-* [p. 310] [コマ 162] *kraft*, § 76), is likewise nothing other than the necessary-contingent character possessed by a part determined by the whole.

Kant divides contingency into two kinds, empirical contingency (*empirische Zufälligkeit*) and intelligible contingency (*intelligibele Zufälligkeit*), and what Kant calls empirical contingency corresponds to what we call empirical necessity, whilst what he calls intelligible contingency corresponds to what we call metaphysical contingency. Of "empirical contingency" Kant says: "From the various changes in the world, empirical contingency, that is, the dependence of change on empirically determining causes, was inferred, and an ascending series of empirical conditions was obtained" (*Critique of Pure Reason* B. 486). It is also of empirical contingency that he speaks when he says that "what is conditioned in existence in general is called contingent, and what is unconditioned is called necessary" (Ib., B. 447), or that "we know contingency from the fact that some event can exist only as the effect of some cause; if something is therefore assumed to be contingent, that it has some cause is an analytic proposition" (Ib., B. 291). "Intelligible contingency" Kant also calls "contingency according to concepts of the pure understanding" (*Zufälligkeit nach Begriffen des* [p. 311] *reinen Verstandes*), and of this kind of contingency he says that "what is contingent in the pure sense of the category is that whose contradictory opposite is possible". A case in which "at the same time as there was motion, there could have been rest in place of motion" is intelligible contingency: when A exists, the case in which it is also possible that non-A exists in place of A is intelligible contingency. Kant calls metaphysical contingency on the disjunctive horizon, that is to say, intelligible contingency. The difference between empirical contingency and intelligible contingency may be said to be the difference between the case in which the opposite is possible *successively* and the case in which it is possible *simultaneously*. Kant holds that to move from empirical contingency to intelligible contingency is wholly impossible, being "a transition to another kind" (μετάβασις εἰς ἄλλο γένος) (Ib., B. 486-488),[^c3s13-13] and insofar as one is confined, at the critical standpoint, to knowledge of the world of appearances, it can be said to be natural to distinguish sharply between these two kinds of contingency and to rule out any passage from the one to the other. Yet to make empirical causal necessity disjunctively contingent and see it as metaphysical contingency, that is, through a conversion of dimension to see "empirical contingency" and "intelligible contingency" as one and the same at the root, must be permitted insofar as one stands on the metaphysical horizon. [p. 312] [コマ 163] Kant's so-called empirical contingency is causally conditioned, and to that extent it should naturally be called *empirical necessity*. Schopenhauer too, in his "Critique of the Kantian Philosophy", says that to think that everything contingent has a cause is "an evident contradiction",[^c3s13-14] though, as Schopenhauer also states, the idea did not begin with Kant but already has its source in Aristotle (Schopenhauer, *Sämtliche Werke*, hrsg. v. Deussen, I, S. 552-553). At any rate, in order to call *empirical necessity empirical contingency*, it should probably be thought that, at least in the motive of the naming, one has strayed unawares onto the metaphysical-disjunctive horizon and made necessity contingent. Empirical necessity, as hypothetical necessity, is necessary only *through the conditioning of another*, and is therefore called contingent; but for it to be called contingent, it is so called by thinking of absolute necessity, which is necessary *through itself*, and [p. 313] over against that. What is necessary only through the conditioning of another, moreover, is contingent because its contradictory opposite becomes possible as soon as the conditioning by the other is removed. To see empirical necessity as empirical contingency, in short, a making-contingent carried out in the metaphysical-disjunctive dimension must be presupposed at the root. In this way I believe I have made clear that empirical necessity and metaphysical contingency are the finite seen in different dimensions.

Let us represent the above considerations in the figure on the right 〔Figure 19〕.[^c3s13-15]

### 14. Being and nothingness

Contingency is an existence close to nothingness, and the correlation of necessity and contingency must make plain the correlation of being and nothingness. The disjunctive affirmation that something is A, and is B, and is C, and is D, carries on its reverse side the disjunctive negation that it is not A, not B, not C and not D. That the absolute is a "necessary-contingent entity"[^c3s14-1] tells us, in other words, that the absolute is absolute being and at the same time absolute nothingness. Contingency, in which A is, although it is also possible for A not to be, [p. 314] [コマ 164] is nothing other than a limit-existence that stands precariously on the boundary line between being and nothingness. Possibility is a non-actuality, lacking actuality, yet insofar as it is equipped with a tendency towards necessity it is deeply coloured by being; in the dimension of reality, that is, possibility stands with necessity in the relation of subalternation.[^c3s14-2] Contingency, by contrast, has actuality, yet insofar as it lies close to impossibility it has its fill of nothingness, and in the dimension of nihility[^c3s14-3] it stands with impossibility in the relation of subalternation. If we represent by a circle the character of being that necessity and possibility share, contingency is no more than the point of tangency at which the nothingness of impossibility happens to touch being (see p. 234). If we represent by a triangle the reality that necessity and possibility share, contingency stands as actuality at the single point of the triangle's apex and is at the same time located on the line of nothingness (see p. 236). Necessity has completely occupied the domain of being. Possibility stands facing being, as "possible existence", that is, as the "possibility of coming to be", whilst contingency stands facing nothingness, as the "possibility of the opposite", that is, as "possible non-existence". One who, in theory and in practice, always holds fast to necessity will seldom be conscious of nothingness. One who devotes himself solely to the pursuit of possibility will, in many cases, know nothingness [p. 315] conceptually, merely as "privation".[^c3s14-4] One who has the sense for witnessing contingency, on the other hand, intuits nothingness originarily.[^c3s14-5] The wonder that accompanies chance is the emotion that moves the question itself, when nothingness is taken as the background of being and the reason is asked for the transition from nothingness to being, or for the turn from being to nothingness. Chance means the possibility of nothingness.[^c3s14-6] And it is contingency, too, that dares the paradox of seizing impossibility from the core of nothingness and bringing it into contact with being.

![Figure 20](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-20-p315.png)

**Figure 20.** Being and nothingness: the mountain of possibility and the valley of contingency between necessity and impossibility (1948, p. 315). Key: Letters and signs, no Japanese: N necessity; I impossibility; P possibility; C contingency; R actuality; R′ non-actuality; + domain of being; − domain of nothingness.

The relation just described can be represented by the figure above 〔Figure 20〕.[^c3s14-7] In this figure N stands for necessity, I for impossibility, P for possibility, C for contingency, R for actuality and R′ for non-actuality, whilst + stands for the domain of being and − for the domain of nothingness (see pp. 238-239). R and R′ lie on the straight line and divide it equally into left and right. + and − have thickness, and between them they divide the mountain and the valley that the curve traces. N is R and at the same time +; I is R′ and at the same time −. P straddles R′ and +, so that whilst it is R′ it is +; C [p. 316] [コマ 165] straddles R and −, so that whilst it is R it is −. The arrows show the direction in which each tends.[^c3s14-8] The same relation can also be illustrated by coordinate axes, as on the left 〔Figure 21〕.[^c3s14-9]

![Figure 21](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-21-p316.png)

**Figure 21.** The modalities on coordinate axes of actuality and non-actuality (1948, p. 316). Key: Letters and signs, no Japanese, as in Figure 20; O origin; (I) to (IV) quadrants; a, b points on the curve.

Here the signs N, I, P, C, R, R′, + and − have the same meaning as in the previous figure. Let O be the origin, and take two fixed straight lines, ROR and R′OR′, that pass through O and intersect at right angles, with the right of O taken as positive and the left as negative, and above O positive and below negative. Of the coordinate axes, the horizontal axis ROR represents the domain of actuality and the vertical axis R′OR′ the domain of non-actuality. Necessity N lies on the horizontal axis in the positive direction and contingency C in the negative direction; possibility P lies on the vertical axis in the positive direction and impossibility I in the negative direction. The first quadrant (I) and the third quadrant (III) are the field of the relation of subalternation, the fourth quadrant (IV) the field of contrary opposition, and the second quadrant (II) the field of subcontrary opposition. If we consider the curve ab, then at point a possibility is small and contingency [p. 317] large, whilst at point b possibility is large and contingency small. The same relation can also be illustrated, from a somewhat different standpoint, as on the left 〔Figure 22〕.[^c3s14-10]

![Figure 22](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-22-p317.png)

**Figure 22.** Being and nothingness in the four modalities: the triangle of apodicticity, problematicity and assertoricity (1948, p. 317). Labels: 有 being; 無 nothingness; 必然 necessity; 可能 possibility; 偶然 contingency; 不可能 impossibility; 現實 actuality; 非現實 non-actuality; 確證性 apodicticity; 問題性 problematicity; 言明性 assertoricity. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

In this figure it goes without saying that the further contingency moves up the straight line of "problematicity" towards the possible, that is, towards being, the more the contingency of the contingent decreases, and that the further it moves down towards nothingness, the more marked its contingency becomes. Contingency is tied to necessity by "actuality", whilst "being" ties necessity and possibility together; possibility is bound to impossibility by "non-actuality", whilst "nothingness" envelops and extinguishes impossibility and contingency.[^c3s14-11]

Chance is without concept and without connection; it is without law, without order, without heed and without interest.[^c3s14-12] Chance has no purpose. It has no design, and it has no *yukari*.[^c3s14-13] Chance cannot be relied on. It is blind, without eyes. Chance, as Shakespeare says, "has no bottom", and as Hegel [p. 318] [コマ 166] says, it "has no ground".[^c3s14-14] In chance, nothingness has encroached deeply upon being, and to that extent chance is a fragile existence, one that merely ties a sharp-pointed, frail existence to "this place" and to "this moment". Every chance holds within itself, from the beginning, the fate of collapse and ruin: "Meeting has parting; all things pass away and perish" (*Nirvana Sutra*, vol. 2).[^c3s14-15] When actuality faces nothingness, and nothingness puts actuality in peril, we wonder, together with Milinda,[^c3s14-16] as though for the first time, and utter the question "why?".

[^c3s14-1]: Tr. 必然－偶然者 (*hitsuzen-gūzensha*). The 者 is "one who" or "that which"; "entity" is used to keep it apart from 存在 (existence) and 有 (being).
[^c3s14-2]: Tr. 大小對當 (*daishō taitō*): the relation of subalternation in the traditional square of opposition. Below, 反對對當 (*hantai taitō*) is contrary opposition and 小反對對當 (*shōhantai taitō*) subcontrary opposition.
[^c3s14-3]: Tr. 虛無性 (*kyomusei*): nihility, the modal character of what tends towards nothingness (無), set against 實在性 (*jitsuzaisei*), reality.
[^c3s14-4]: Tr. 缺如 (*ketsujo*): lack, privation.
[^c3s14-5]: Tr. 官能 (*kannō*), "sense", is a faculty of sense; 原的に (*genteki ni*), "originarily", means at first hand.
[^c3s14-6]: Tr. Kuki writes 無の可能 (*mu no kanō*), "the possible of nothingness", using 可能 (possible) where elsewhere he has 可能性 (possibility). The Conclusion's 無いことの可能 (*nai koto no kanō*), "the possibility of not being", has the same form.
[^c3s14-7]: Tr. The figure (p. 315): a horizontal line runs from N (left) to I (right), with R marked below its left half and R′ above its right half. From N an arc marked + rises above the line and comes down to P at the midpoint, just above the line; from P the curve crosses the line to C, just below it; from C an arc marked − runs below the line to I. An arrow on the upper arc points towards N, and one on the lower arc towards I.
[^c3s14-8]: Tr. 歸趨 (*kisū*): where something tends, its destination.
[^c3s14-9]: Tr. The figure (p. 316): coordinate axes crossing at O. The horizontal axis runs from −R (left) to +R (right), with C to the left of O and N to the right; the vertical axis runs from +R′ (top) to −R′ (bottom), with P above O and I below. The quadrants are (I) upper right, (II) upper left, (III) lower left, (IV) lower right. A curve rises from the third quadrant, crosses the horizontal axis to the left of C, passes through a in the second quadrant and b near the vertical axis, peaks on the vertical axis above P, and falls away in the first quadrant towards the horizontal axis. Lines drop from a and from b to each axis.
[^c3s14-10]: Tr. The figure (p. 317): an inverted triangle. Its top side, marked "being" (有), runs from "necessity" (必然, top left) to "possibility" (可能, top right); its lower vertex is "nothingness" (無). The left side, from necessity to nothingness, is marked "apodicticity" (確證性), and the right side, from possibility to nothingness, "problematicity" (問題性). A line marked "actuality" (現實) runs from necessity to "contingency" (偶然), which lies on the right side near the vertex; a line marked "non-actuality" (非現實) runs from possibility to "impossibility" (不可能), which lies on the left side near the vertex. The two lines cross, and "assertoricity" (言明性) is written in the centre.
[^c3s14-11]: Tr. 包消 (*hōshō*), joining "envelop" (包) and "extinguish" (消).
[^c3s14-12]: Tr. Each predicate in this run is formed with 無 (*mu*, nothingness, without): 無概念的, 無關聯的, 無法則, 無秩序, 無頓着, 無關心. The sentences that follow end in 無い (*nai*, "there is no"), and Kuki writes even the negative ending of 當てにならない, "cannot be relied on", with the character 無.
[^c3s14-13]: Tr. ゆかり (*yukari*): affinity, a tie of connection or relation. Compare *yukurinaku*, discussed in Chapter 2, §5.
[^c3s14-14]: Tr. Kuki gives no reference for either phrase.
[^c3s14-15]: Tr. The *Nirvana Sutra* (涅槃經, *Nehangyō*). Kuki quotes the classical Chinese, 合會有別離、一切皆遷滅.
[^c3s14-16]: Tr. 彌蘭 (*Miran*): King Milinda of the *Nāgasena Sūtra*, whose question Kuki discussed in Chapter 1, §6.

## Conclusion (結論) [コマ 166-172]

### 1. The core meaning of contingency

[p. 319] In the foregoing I have sought to elucidate contingency on three horizons, the categorical, the hypothetical and the disjunctive. Categorical contingency arose when, in a categorical judgement, the predicate signifies a non-essential mark in relation to the subject taken as concept; it is the case, that is, in which it has become clear that an assertoric judgement, lacking the identity of subject and predicate, has no apodicticity and consequently no necessity. Hypothetical contingency arose as what stands outside the relation of ground and consequent in the hypothetical judgement, which is to say as what lies outside the range of the apodicticity, and so of the necessity, determined by the identity of ground and consequent. Disjunctive contingency, we may say, arises when we regard a given categorical or hypothetical judgement as one member of the division in a disjunctive judgement,[^conc1-1] and think that besides it there exist several [p. 320] [コマ 167] other members. By regarding an assertoric or apodictic proposition as a member of a division that stands in a disjunctive relation, in other words, it stresses difference over against the identity of the divided concept, and at the same time turns assertoricity (actuality) and apodicticity (necessity) into problematicity.

Categorical contingency was the contingency that a non-essential mark, that is, a contingent mark, shows within categorical structure in relation to the identity of the concept. The relation between the necessary and the contingent marks of a concept was seen, in the distinction between analytic and synthetic judgements, in the identity on which the analytic judgement rests and the contingency that lurks at the root of the synthetic judgement; and in the distinction between universal and particular judgements we learned that necessity is expressed by the universal judgement and contingency stated by the particular judgement. We then considered how categorical contingency appears as isolated fact and further as exception. When the essential marks of a concept have the value of law, the contingency of a contingent mark, which can mean no more than an exception to the law, stresses contingency with particular force as exceptional contingency. In what, then, does the core meaning of categorical contingency lie? As we can gather from the fact that, among the forms of categorical contingency, exceptional contingency has a contingency of particular prominence, [p. 321] the core meaning of categorical contingency came down in the end to "the individual thing and the individual event" over against the general concept.

Hypothetical contingency, next, was the contingency shown by what lies outside the sphere of the hypothetical structure of groundedness, over against the identical necessity which that structure possesses. In the purely logical range it appeared as contingency of ground, and in the empirical world, in the form of an application of contingency of ground, as teleological and causal contingency, and each of these three modes of hypothetical contingency divided further into negative and positive contingency. Negative contingency is the case in which, with regard to a single event, the non-existence of groundedness, causality or purposiveness is negatively witnessed. Positive contingency is the case in which, between two or more events, we not only see the non-existence of a hypothetical necessary relation of groundedness, causality or purposiveness, but go further and positively witness the existence of some other relation. By virtue of its positivity, positive contingency shows a more marked contingency than negative contingency, and at the root of negative contingency some positive contingency is, moreover, always present. Insofar as it has the character of relative contingency, positive contingency shows the structure of "the Encounter of one series with another series"[^conc1-7], and this structure was indeed the [p. 322] [コマ 168] core meaning of hypothetical contingency. Teleological and causal contingency, we might add, could be called empirical contingency because they belong to the empirical world, and because they are empirical they have a temporal moment: empirical positive contingency is an encountering (*kaikō*, 邂逅) within time. It takes two forms, simultaneous and successive contingency, the latter being reduced to the former. And insofar as simultaneity is related to spatiality, the core meaning of contingency in the empirical world must take the form of the historical irrationality of "the Encounter in this place, at this moment".

Disjunctive contingency, finally, was the contingency that each possible disjunct shows within disjunctive structure over against the identity of the whole of the possible disjuncts. Once we came to know the relation between contingency and possibility, it became necessary to elucidate the position that contingency occupies within the system constituted by the modalities, and above all the special relation in which contingency stands to possibility. The minimum of possibility is the maximum of contingency, since a small possibility of being means a large possibility of not being.[^conc1-2] The core meaning of disjunctive contingency was its approach, as "the possibility of not being", towards "the necessary of not being". Contingency is an actuality that bears as its character the nothingness of [p. 323] impossibility, appearing as mere actuality in the present moment as though in play. Chance is this: to witness nothingness behind the actuality of a disjunct that has appeared in the present "now", and to wonder at it. The emotion of wonder, in turn, announces fate to *Existenz*.[^conc1-3] The whole of the possible disjuncts, moreover, signifies in the eminent sense the metaphysical absolute, and the metaphysical absolute is illuminated in its concreteness as the "necessary-contingent entity".[^conc1-4] And insofar as what joins the absolute and the finite is fate, fate too carries the character of the "necessary-contingent entity" and shakes *Existenz* to its core. To grasp that the correlation of necessity and contingency rests on the correlation of being and nothingness must form the foundation of our knowledge of contingency.

In sum, the core meaning of categorical contingency was "the individual thing and the individual event"; the core meaning of hypothetical contingency was "the Encounter of one series with another series"; and the core meaning of disjunctive contingency was "the possibility of not being". It was because it is an individual thing and an individual event that the contingent bore contingent marks over against the general concept. It was because it is the Encounter of one independent series with another that it stood outside the necessary relation of ground and consequent. And it was because it is the possibility of not being that it ran counter to the necessity [p. 324] [コマ 169] belonging to the whole of possibilities. These three meanings of contingency are by no means separate from one another; they are fused, undivided, into one. The core meaning of "the individual thing and the individual event" lies in "the Encounter of one series with another series", and the core meaning of Encounter lies in the fact that it is also possible not to encounter (*kaikō*, 邂逅), that is, in "the possibility of not being". The originary meaning of contingency, which primordially determines all of these, is the positing of an other over against necessity as the one.[^conc1-5] Necessity is nothing other than identity, the modality of the one, and contingency first exists only where there is the duality of the one and the other. This is precisely why Aristotle says that the contingent exists "not as itself but as another" (οὐχ ᾗ αὐτὸ ἀλλ' ᾗ ἕτερον) (Aristoteles, *Metaphysica*, Δ 30, 1025a), and why Hegel says that the contingent is in general what has the Ground of its existence "not in itself but in another" (nicht in sich selbst, sondern in Anderem) (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 145, Zusatz S. 193). The origin of the individual thing goes back to the dual positing of the other over against the one; Encounter is nothing other than the Encounter of two independent elements; and the possibility of not being presupposes duality, as something grounded in the choice of [p. 325] the one or the other. The philosophy of the Eleatics,[^conc1-6] which determined the meaning of being by the law of identity and took what runs counter to the law of identity to be nothingness, began from wonder at chance and ended in a tragic refusal of the dual positing of the other. And yet we cannot but acknowledge that the Eleatic philosophy holds a truth in one of its aspects. There, too, human suffering and joy lie hidden.

[^conc1-1]: Tr. 區分肢 (*kubunshi*), member of a division, and 被區分概念 (*hikubun gainen*), the divided concept, are terms from the logic of division. Kuki's 離接肢 (*risetsushi*) is rendered "disjunct".
[^conc1-2]: Tr. 有ること (*aru koto*) and 無いこと (*nai koto*), "being" and "not being", are the verbal forms of 有 (being) and 無 (nothingness). Kuki writes 可能 (possible) and 必然 (necessary) in 無いことの可能 and 無いことの必然, where elsewhere he has 可能性 and 必然性.
[^conc1-3]: Tr. 實存 (*jitsuzon*): *Existenz*, kept apart from 存在 (*sonzai*), existence. 實存者 is rendered "the finite *Existenz*" and 實存する "existing as *Existenz*".
[^conc1-4]: Tr. 開明 (*kaimei*), illumination; the word used in Japanese for Jaspers's *Erhellung*. On "entity" for 者, see note c3s14-1.
[^conc1-5]: Tr. 一者 (*issha*), the one, and 他者 (*tasha*), the other.
[^conc1-6]: Tr. The Eleatic school of Parmenides and Zeno.

[^conc1-7]: Tr. 邂逅 (*kaikō*) recurs throughout the Conclusion and is rendered "Encounter" each time. The capitals are frequent here. They cannot be dropped without losing the distinction Kuki draws at p. 148, where he chooses 邂逅 over 遭遇 (*sōgū*), "encounter", as the word of his definition of contingency. See the Translator's Note.

### 2. The internalisation of contingency

I have said that the problem of contingency, because it contains the question of nothingness, is in the strict sense a problem of metaphysics, and also that the sciences other than philosophy as metaphysics, because they exclude the question of nothingness, do not take contingency as a problem. Contingency, as the Encounter of two independent elements in "this place" and "this moment", stands at the peril of a sharp point and faces a boundless nothingness. For sciences that take universal thought as their model and pursue only the necessity, or else the probability, of law, contingency may well be thought an irrationality not worth a glance. Aristotle too spoke of chance as "contrary to [p. 326] [コマ 170] reason" (παράλογος) (Aristoteles, *Physica*, II, 5, 197a).[^conc2-1] Contingency forms a limit to scientific knowledge.

We must recognise, however, that for theoretical existentiality[^conc2-2] this limit has the significance of a beginning. Empirical knowledge must set out from contingency, the limit of knowledge, and must always remain conditioned by this limit. The originary meaning of the theoretical system that gives consistency and unity to experience lies in grasping the contingency of the other and, in its concreteness, assimilating it to and internalising it within the identity of the one. True judgement, in other words, must stand, within the correlation of contingency and necessity, on the contingency of fact, and must take the internalisation of contingency as its task. The law of identity, the basic principle of thought, is nothing other than the principle of internalisation: "A is A" is nothing other than "I am I". The essential meaning of judgement must be to deepen the "thou" that is encountered (*kaikō*, 邂逅) into the "I",[^conc2-3] and the idea of judgement is to identify the external thou concretely with the inner identity of the I. This must not, however, aim at the empty identity of Eleatic abstract universality. Internalisation by the law of identity must be a concrete internalisation, conditioned by the contingency of the thou encountered (*kaikō*, 邂逅) as fact. [p. 327] Here lies the foundation of the independence of the departments of science and of the graded organisation of scientific systems, and here too lies the guarantee of the concreteness and actuality of scientific labour within a single department. Mere identification, mere necessitation, leads by negating every thou and every contingency to acosmism.[^conc2-4] The ideal that theoretical knowledge should reach must not be mere necessity; it must be a "contingent-necessary entity" that has its fill of chance and is saturated with contingency.

Insofar as the temporal character of contingency is the present as immediacy,[^conc2-5] it is no wonder that contingency appears as irrationality for logic as the methodological system of necessary thought. Yet to let logical determination set out from the immediacy of the "thou", and to grasp the idea of scientific labour in the internalisation of the "thou", is the means by which life is brought to logic itself and concrete value conferred on science. To keep the domain of the identity of thought, "I = I", and the immediacy of the "I"'s experience of the "thou" in as close a relation as possible, and so to bring necessity and contingency into contact in an inseparable correlation, must be an axiomatic demand for theoretical *Existenz* as it presses on towards a logic of life.

[p. 328] [コマ 171] Just as contingency has no meaning for a science misled by abstract universality, so a rational and formal ethics that takes science as its model will have no room for chance. An ethical theory that seeks to make the moral law into something like a universal law of nature admitting no exceptions will, by pursuing abstract identity, arrive at "a will that wills nothing" and fall into a practical acosmism. The content of morality must be individualised by the contingency that the present offers, and the practical universality that morality takes as its task must not be abstract universality; it must be a concrete universal that, with chance as its moment, determines the whole intensively. If there were an ethical theory that sought to unify everything into formal identity, and someone rose up against its abstract universality saying, "I will lie, as Desdemona lied when dying; I will kill, as Timoleon did; I will take my own life, as Otho did; I will enter the temple and steal, as David did; and because I am hungry I will pluck ears of grain on the Sabbath" (Jacobi, *Werke* III, 1816, S. 37),[^conc2-6] that voice would be heard as the voice of conscience crying out in the inmost depths of the human being. The practical internalisation of contingency is nothing other than self-awareness of the relationships between part and part among the countless parts of a concrete whole.[^conc2-7] The one that exists in isolation, [p. 329] at the instant when, here or there, it unexpectedly encounters (*kaikō*, 邂逅) the other, must bind the whole suffering and joy of *Existenz* to the internalisation of the external thou into the depths of the I. Chance must be made to happen upon us (*kaikō*, 邂逅) as though it fell into the depths of the I. Heidegger too says that "what is called chance can befall only resoluteness, from the shared environing world" (Heidegger, *Sein und Zeit*, S. 300),[^conc2-8] and Jaspers that "beyond any thought that can be grasped, I experience terror in the limit situation, and so experience being one with the chance I have grasped as my own" (Jaspers, *Philosophie* II, S. 217).[^conc2-9] When the *Shuogua zhuan* says, "They observed the changes in yin and yang and established the trigrams; they brought forth the firm and the yielding and produced the lines; they accorded with the Way and its virtue and set in order what is right; they exhausted principle and fulfilled nature, and so arrived at fate,"[^conc2-v1] it too is setting out nothing other than the practical internalisation of contingency.[^conc2-10] The dual relativity that brings chance into being constitutes an originary sociality by disclosing intersubjectivity everywhere. Just as the meaning of judgement in theory lay in assimilating the thou of intersubjective sociality to the concrete identity of the I that exists as *Existenz*, and internalising it there, so the meaning of action in practice lies there too. If morality is to be no mere fiction and is to hold good in actuality as a force, [p. 330] [コマ 172] it must use the chance it is given as a springboard and stride up towards inwardness. Wonder at chance need not be grounded in the present alone, for we can ground the wonder of contingency inversely, from the future.[^conc2-11] Contingency is the point of tangency at which impossibility touches possibility. By grasping the minute possibility within contingency and nurturing a possibility for the future, we can unfold the curve of action and, turning back, understand inversely the productive meaning of present contingency. By letting "purpose without purpose" ripen in the production of the future, we can bring wonder to the "moment" of Encounter.[^conc2-14] To stress the wonder of all contingency by way of the future is to establish the correlation "contingency-necessity", and so to make contingency truly contingency. This must be the task given to finite *Existenz* and, at the same time, the salvation of the finite that exists as *Existenz*. When the *Treatise on the Pure Land* says, "Contemplating the power of the Buddha's original vow, of those who meet it none passes by in vain," this too is, in the end, probably what it means.[^conc2-12] What "meets" us is the contingency of the thou that encounters (*kaikō*, 邂逅) me in the present,[^conc2-13] and "none passes by in vain" has meaning only as the future possibility that the I, conditioned by the thou, holds with regard to the internalisation of the thou. Where a minute possibility close to the impossible [p. 331] becomes actual in contingency, where, firmly grasped as contingency, it gives birth to new possibility, and where possibility develops further into necessity, there we find both the Buddha's original vow as fate and the salvation of the human being. To give the meaning of an eternal fate to contingency, which holds nothingness within it and has the fate of perishing, there is no way but to make the moment live through the future, no way but to make the meaning of present contingency surge up through future possibility. To Milinda's "why?", within the sphere of theory we can answer only that contingency is the indispensable condition of concrete existence. In the domain of practice, however, we may fill the gap left by theory by giving ourselves the command: "Having met, do not pass by in vain."[^conc2-15]

[^conc2-1]: Tr. Kuki writes 背理 (*hairi*), contrary to reason, and gives Aristotle's παράλογος, unaccountable, beyond reckoning.
[^conc2-2]: Tr. 理論的實存性 (*rironteki jitsuzonsei*): literally "theoretical existentiality", *Existenz* as it engages in theory. Below, 理論的實存 is "theoretical *Existenz*".
[^conc2-3]: Tr. 汝 (*nanji*), thou; 我 (*ware*), I.
[^conc2-4]: Tr. 無宇宙論 (*muuchūron*), acosmism: the denial of the reality of the world.
[^conc2-5]: Tr. 直態 (*chokutai*), literally "direct state", rendered "immediacy".
[^conc2-6]: Tr. Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi (1743-1819); the passage comes from his open letter to Fichte (1799). Desdemona, dying in Shakespeare's *Othello*, lies to shield Othello; Timoleon of Corinth had his brother, the tyrant Timophanes, killed; the Roman emperor Otho killed himself in 69 CE; David took the consecrated bread from the house of God (1 Samuel 21); the plucking of grain on the Sabbath is in Matthew 12.
[^conc2-7]: Tr. 間柄 (*aidagara*): relationship, betweenness; the term is central to Watsuji Tetsurō's ethics.
[^conc2-8]: Tr. Kuki uses 偶然する (*gūzen suru*), "to chance", as a verb.
[^conc2-9]: Tr. 限界狀況 (*genkai jōkyō*): Jaspers's *Grenzsituation*, limit situation.
[^conc2-10]: Tr. The *Shuogua zhuan* (說卦傳, "Discussion of the Trigrams"), one of the Ten Wings, the commentaries on the *Book of Changes*. Kuki quotes the classical Chinese. 命 (*ming*), rendered "fate", also means mandate or allotted life.
[^conc2-11]: Tr. 倒逆的に (*tōgyakuteki ni*): in reverse, running backwards from the future to the present.
[^conc2-12]: Tr. The *Jōdoron* (淨土論, *Treatise on the Pure Land*), attributed to Vasubandhu and translated into Chinese by Bodhiruci. Kuki quotes the classical Chinese, 觀佛本願力、遇無空過者. 本願 (*hongan*) is the original vow of Amida Buddha.
[^conc2-13]: Tr. 遇ふ (*au*), to meet, takes up the sutra's 遇; Kuki pairs it with 邂逅 (*kaikō*), encounter. His closing command, 遇うて空しく過ぐる勿れ, recasts the sutra's line as an imperative.
[^conc2-14]: Tr. 「目的なき目的」, "purpose without purpose", is Kuki's own phrase, used in Chapter 2 for positive teleological contingency. It recalls Kant's *Zweckmäßigkeit ohne Zweck*, "purposiveness without purpose", from the Analytic of the Beautiful in the *Critique of Judgement*, but Kuki writes 目的 twice and not Kant's term, 合目的性 (purposiveness). His verb, 醸す (*kamosu*), is to brew, as sake is brewed, and so to bring something about by slow working.
[^conc2-15]: Tr. 遇うて空しく過ぐる勿れ. John C. Maraldo renders the line "Let not your encounters occur in vain" (Maraldo 2008) and "Let not your encounters pass in vain" (*Japanese Philosophy in the Making 2*, 2019, pp. 270-71), and his note there (pp. 238-39) gives other translators' versions. His renderings make the encounters the subject and use "encounter" for 遇ふ. The version here keeps as its subject the one who has met, as the Japanese does, and keeps "meet" for 遇ふ, so that the line answers the *Treatise on the Pure Land*'s "of those who meet it none passes by in vain".
[^conc2-v1]: Tr. Kuki's text: 觀變於陰陽而立卦、發揮於剛柔而生爻、和順於道德而理於義、窮理盡性以至於命. Our translation: "They observed the changes in yin and yang and established the trigrams; they brought forth the firm and the yielding and produced the lines; they accorded with the Way and its virtue and set in order what is right; they exhausted principle and fulfilled nature, and so arrived at fate."

---

*End of the text (p. 331). The colophon (コマ 173) gives the first printing as 15 December 1935 and this fourth printing as 20 April 1948, Iwanami Shoten.*

---

## Appendix A. Notes on Translation Choices

These notes record the decisions taken in this translation, with the alternatives considered, so that other translators of Kuki can see where and why they might choose differently. Page references are to the 1948 printing.

### 1. The text and the two versions

The translation was made from the fourth printing of 偶然性の問題 (Iwanami Shoten, 20 April 1948; first printing 15 December 1935), using the National Diet Library's digitised images (pid/1037966). The 1935 and 1948 printings have not been compared, and the text has not yet been checked against volume 2 of the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū* (KSZ), which is the edition scholars normally cite. A concordance of 46 fixed points between the 1948 pages and KSZ vol. 2, drawn from Graham Mayeda, Takeshi Morisato and John C. Maraldo, is kept with the working files; the whole book corresponds to KSZ 2:9-260.

There are two versions. The literal version follows Kuki's sentences one by one, is the version to quote from, and is published alongside the readable text. The readable version joins his short declarative sentences into continuous argument, using reformulations such as "that is" and "this is to say" where Kuki restates, and it is meant to be read as philosophy in English. The readable version is not supposed to say anything the literal version does not.

### 2. The three modes

偶然性 is "contingency" and 偶然 "the contingent", or "chance" where Kuki is discussing *hasard* or *Zufall*. 必然性 is "necessity". The three modes are 定言的 "categorical", 假說的 "hypothetical" and 離接的 "disjunctive". Kuki deliberately sets aside the standard Japanese logical terms 假言的 and 選言的 for the second and third (p. 8): the characters of 假說的 stress supposition, and those of 離接的 join separation (離) and connection (接). English has one word for each pair, so the change cannot be shown in the text and is explained in a footnote. 樣態 is "mode" and 樣相 or 樣相性 "modality".

### 3. Existence, being and nothingness

Kuki uses two pairs of terms that English translations often merge: 存在 and 非存在, and 有 and 無. They are kept apart here: 存在 is "existence", 非存在 "non-existence", 有 "being" and 無 "nothingness". This follows Takeshi Morisato's translation of the Introduction and agrees with Maraldo's usage. The first draft of this translation rendered both 存在 and 有 as "being", which hid the distinction on the opening page, where contingency is an existence "interposed at the surface of contact between being and nothingness" (p. 1).

The decision has consequences elsewhere:
- 眞の存在 (Kuki's gloss on Plato's ὄντως ὄν) is "true existence", and 極限的存在 is "limit-existence".
- 存在論 and 存在論的 remain "ontology" and "ontological".
- 實存, in the passages on Heidegger and Jaspers, is *Existenz*, with 實存性 "existentiality", so that it does not collide with 存在. 現存在 is "Dasein".
- 虛無性 is "nihility", to keep it apart from 無; 實在性 is "reality", and 實在理由 (*ratio essendi*) is "ground of reality".
- 個體 is "the individual", never "the individual being".
- 有る and 無い as ordinary verbs remain "to be" and "not to be".
- Chapter 3 §14, 有と無, is "Being and Nothingness".

### 4. Encounter

Kuki's central definition of contingency is 獨立なる二元の邂逅, rendered "the Encounter of two independent elements" (pp. 146-148). 二元 is "elements" because it is the plainest rendering of 元; Mayeda, working from Omodaka's French, has "two independent principles", which another translator may prefer. Kuki uses both 邂逅 and 遭遇 for the encounter of independent series of events (Cournot's *séries indépendantes*), and at pp. 146 and 148 writes 遭遇または邂逅 before choosing 邂逅 for the definition. 邂逅 is "Encounter" and 遭遇 "encounter", with the Japanese given where he sets them side by side and where 邂逅 is a verb (see the Translator's Note). 遇ふ, in the Conclusion's Buddhist passages, is "meet", kept apart from 邂逅. The closing imperative 遇うて空しく過ぐる勿れ is rendered "Having met, do not pass by in vain", which keeps as its subject the one who has met. Maraldo's "Let not your encounters occur in vain" (2008) and "Let not your encounters pass in vain" (*Japanese Philosophy in the Making 2*) are smoother but use "encounter" for 遇ふ.

Kuki also proposes a sign for contingency, )(, two arcs touching at their midpoints, which he reads as a picture of this Encounter (p. 73). It is reproduced as )( wherever he uses it.

### 5. Ground, cause and purpose

理由 is "ground", following the German *Grund*, and 根據, which Kuki also uses for *Grund*, is "Ground" (see the Translator's Note). 理由律 is "law of ground" and 充足理由律 "law of sufficient reason", to match "law of identity" (同一律) and "law of contradiction" (矛盾律); the translators first divided between "law" and "principle", and "law" was chosen throughout. 理由的偶然 is "contingency of ground", and 認識理由 and 實在理由 are "ground of knowledge" and "ground of reality".

In Chapter 2, 因果的 is "causal" and 目的的 "teleological", with 因果性 "causality" and 目的性 "purposiveness". 目的 is "purpose", or "end" where the sense is telos. 無目的 is "purposelessness" and 反目的 "counter-purpose". 目的なき目的, Kuki's version of a Kantian formula, is "purpose without purpose". 故意 is "intention" and 意圖 "design", kept apart because Kuki distinguishes them in discussing Aristotle's *automaton* and *tychē*. 消極的 and 積極的 are "negative" and "positive".

### 6. Logic and judgement

徵表 is "mark" (*Merkmal*). 樣相性, modality as a category, is "Modality"; 樣相, a modality or the modalities, is "modality"; 樣態 is "mode". In Lotze's distinction, 全稱判斷 is the "universal judgement" (*universale Urteil*) and 普遍的判斷 the "generic judgement" (*generelle Urteil*); the first draft confused them (p. 7). 特稱判斷 is "particular judgement". The square of opposition follows standard English logic: 矛盾 "contradictory", 反對 "contrary", 小反對 "subcontrary" and 大小對當 "subalternation". 確證性, 言明性 and 問題性 are "apodicticity", "assertoricity" and "problematicity". Kuki's 甲, 乙 and 丙 are rendered A, B and C. His symbolic notations (Couturat's algebra in Chapter 1, C. I. Lewis's signs in Chapter 2, Oskar Becker's dot-and-stroke notation in Chapter 3) are transcribed as printed.

### 7. Probability

Kuki uses both 確率 and 蓋然性. Both are rendered "probability", since English has no settled pair that maps onto them, and the Japanese is given where he sets them side by side (p. 246). A translator who wants to keep them apart could use "probability value" for 確率.

### 8. Wonder and the emotions

驚異 is "wonder", the emotion Kuki assigns to contingency. 情緒 is "emotion" and 感情 "feeling", kept apart throughout Chapter 3 §10. 感情當價 is "affective equivalent". 不思議 is "the marvellous", so that it does not compete with "wonder".

### 9. Japanese words for chance, and poetry

Kuki builds arguments on Japanese words: *yukurinaku*, *hashinakumo* and *futo* (Chapter 2 §5), and *wakuraba ni*, *tamatama*, *tama*, *mama* and *ma* (Chapter 1 §5). These are kept in romanisation, with the sense given in the sentence where Kuki gives it and the rest in footnotes. In the readable version, the poems Kuki quotes and his classical Chinese passages (the *Liezi*, the *Nāgasena Sūtra*, the *Book of Changes*, Buddhist texts) appear in the body in English, with romanisation where the argument depends on sound (rhyme, acrostic, pivot word, palindrome); a footnote then gives Kuki's text exactly as printed, Japanese or Chinese, with our close translation. Chinese is translated as Kuki quotes it, and his kunten reading marks are not reproduced. The literal version gives the originals in the body.

### 10. Quotation, citation and emphasis

All quotations from other authors, European, Chinese and Japanese, are translated from Kuki's Japanese, not from the original texts, because his renderings may shape his argument. They should not be cited as translations of those authors. Kuki's references stay inline, as in the original. The literal version keeps them exactly as printed ("Aristoteles, Metaphysica", "Ib.", "3 Aufl."); the readable version gives authors' names and titles in their usual English form and italicises titles, keeping Kuki's editions and page numbers, and has not yet been made fully consistent in this. His emphasis marks (圏点) are rendered as italics. Japanese era dates are converted to Western years. His long dashes are rendered with commas, colons or parentheses.

Kuki's examples of race, skin colour and disability use the terms of the 1930s. They are translated plainly, without softening, and the dated Japanese terms are identified in footnotes.

### 11. Marking the translator's contributions

Every footnote in the readable version is the translator's and begins "Tr.", since Kuki gives his own references in the body of the text. Anything inserted into Kuki's text, whether a supplied word, a gloss or a note, is set in tortoise-shell brackets, 〔 〕, following the Japanese editorial convention for insertions by an editor or by the person quoting. Kuki's own sign )( was considered for this purpose and rejected, because he uses it in his argument.

### 12. Misprints, figures and open questions

Apparent misprints and doubtful references in the 1948 printing have been kept as printed and noted, among them Δ 7 for Λ 7 of the *Metaphysics* (p. 70), odds of 60,480 where Kuki's own example implies one in a million (p. 165), 大極 for 太極 (p. 305), and a cross-reference on p. 266 that does not match this pagination. Shestov's *La philosophie de la tragédie* is cited as Paris 1926 in this printing; Morisato has 1929. Kuki's 22 figures are reproduced from the National Diet Library's full-resolution scan of the 1948 printing, with contrast adjusted so that the print reads clearly and no marks removed; each has a caption and an English key to its Japanese labels, and Kuki's own references to a figure are followed by 〔Figure N〕.

Attributions and dates in the footnotes that the translators could not confirm have been left out or flagged in the working notes, and need checking before citation.

### 13. Alternatives attested in Omodaka's French

Omodaka Hisayuki's French translation has not yet been consulted. Graham Mayeda quotes it in his own English in "Is There a Method to Chance?" (2008), mainly from the Introduction and the last pages of the book. A comparison of about twenty of his quotations with this translation found agreement in substance throughout, and in three places the Japanese supports our reading against his (pp. 324, 324-325 and 330-331). Where his renderings differ they are recorded here as alternatives, marked "via Mayeda (2008), from Omodaka (1966)", since a difference may come from either translator:
- 一者: "the same", where we have "the one". Kuki defines necessity through 同一性, identity, the modality of 一者, and "the one" keeps the link to 一 that "the same" loses.
- 個物および個々の事象: "individuality and individual phenomenality", where we have "the individual thing and the individual event", which keeps Kuki's concrete nouns.
- 無いことの可能: "possible nothingness", where we have "the possible of not being", since Kuki uses the verbal 無いこと and not the noun 無.
- 形而上的理由: "metaphysical sense", where we have "metaphysical grounds", since 理由 is ground or reason.
- 實存: "existence", where we have *Existenz* (see section 3).
- 內面化: "interiorization", where we have "internalisation".
- 極微: "infinitesimal", where we have "minute".
- 二元: "principles", where we have "elements" (see section 4).

Mayeda's quotations are his English and should not be cited as evidence of Omodaka's wording.

## Appendix C. Concordance with the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū*

This concordance ties the pages of the 1948 printing, used in this translation, to volume 2 of the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū* (KSZ), the edition normally cited. It is built from 46 fixed points taken from Graham Mayeda, Takeshi Morisato and John C. Maraldo. It has not been checked against a physical copy of KSZ, and only rows marked HIGH or MEDIUM should be used for citation, after checking.

### Editions and sources

- **1948 printing**: 九鬼周造『偶然性の問題』, Iwanami Shoten, 1948 printing (331 pp.). Its pages are the `[p. N]` markers in our translation (`Kuki_Problem-of-Contingency_literal.md`, `Kuki_Problem-of-Contingency_translation.md`, and `ch3-parts/partH-` to `partN-literal.md`). Introduction pp. 1-11; Chapter 1 pp. 13-46; Chapter 2 pp. 47-184; Chapter 3 pp. 185-318; Conclusion pp. 319-331.
- **KSZ**: 『九鬼周造全集』 (*Kuki Shūzō zenshū*), vol. 2 (Tokyo: Iwanami Shoten, 1980; Mayeda gives 1981), as cited by Mayeda. The text runs KSZ 2:9-260.
- **French**: Kuki Shûzô, *Le problème de la contingence*, trans. Omodaka Hisayuki (University of Tokyo Press, 1966), as cited by Mayeda.

Anchors come from:
1. Graham Mayeda, *Ethics and Difference* (PhD thesis, University of Toronto, 2001), Chapter 5 endnotes, which cite "Kuki, *Le problème de la contingence*, [French page]; KSZ, vol. 2, [KSZ page]". Each note was matched to Mayeda's body passage, then to the sentence in our literal translation. Many of Mayeda's notes reproduce the Japanese passage (checked against the thesis PDF, pp. 291-295), which fixes the match; these are marked "Japanese quoted in note".
2. KSZ page breaks marked in Takeshi Morisato's translation (KSZ 2:10, 2:11, 2:12).
3. John C. Maraldo, *Frontiers of Japanese Philosophy* 2 (2008), for KSZ 2:258, 2:259, 2:260 in the Conclusion.

Where a passage crosses a 1948 page break, the 1948 column gives the range. The KSZ page is as Mayeda gives it; this file has not been checked against a physical copy of KSZ. Use only HIGH and MEDIUM rows for citation, and only after checking the page in KSZ. LOW rows are recorded for completeness.

Confidence: HIGH = a distinctive sentence clearly the same (most are confirmed by the Japanese Mayeda quotes); MEDIUM = Mayeda paraphrases, and the page is likely; LOW = doubtful.

Mayeda notes with no KSZ anchor: nn. 38-39, 63, 68, 70, 75-76, 84 (other authors or commentary); nn. 41-43 (glosses on 手間, 間 and the *ma* expressions, 1948 pp. 36-37, with no page citation); n. 65 gives only the French page (row included, no KSZ value).

### Monotonicity check

KSZ pages increase with 1948 pages across every row except one pair:

| 1948 page | KSZ page (Mayeda) | French page | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| 51 | 51 | 31 | n. 45 |
| 53 | 50 | 32 | n. 46 |

The French pages (31, 32) run in order with the 1948 pages, so the passages are in the right sequence and one of Mayeda's KSZ numbers is wrong. The suspect value is **n. 45 (KSZ 51)**. Interpolating between the HIGH anchors at 1948 pp. 41-42 (KSZ 40) and pp. 84-85 (KSZ 73-74) puts 1948 p. 51 at about KSZ 47-48 and p. 53 at about KSZ 49, so n. 46 (KSZ 50) is within a page of expectation and n. 45 is about three pages too high. It may be a slip that repeats the 1948 page number (the passage opens 1948 p. 51). The n. 45 row is rated LOW for this reason; the passage match itself is secure.

No other row breaks the sequence. Rows that share a KSZ page (e.g. 1948 pp. 267 and 268 at KSZ 211; pp. 297 and 298 at KSZ 234; pp. 142 and 143 at KSZ 116) fit the rate of about 0.76 KSZ pages per 1948 page.


### Locating guide (for finding a page in KSZ, not for citation)

Derived from HIGH anchors only. Across the whole book the HIGH anchors fit **KSZ ≈ 0.76 × (1948 page) + 8**, within 2 pages at every HIGH anchor (the largest deviations, about 1.5 pages, fall in 1948 pp. 50-90, where KSZ runs slightly ahead of the formula). Use this to open KSZ near the right page, then find the passage.

| Part of the 1948 printing | 1948 pages | Implied KSZ vol. 2 range | HIGH anchors in range |
|---|---|---|---|
| Introduction | 1-11 | 2:9 to ~2:17 | pp. 1-4 = KSZ 9-12 (exact breaks) |
| Chapter 1. Categorical contingency | 13-46 | ~2:19 to ~2:44 | p. 30 = 32; pp. 41-42 = 40 |
| Chapter 2. Hypothetical contingency | 47-184 | ~2:45 to ~2:148 | p. 53 = 50; pp. 84-85 = 73-74; p. 89 = 77; p. 94 = 80; p. 134 = 110; pp. 142-143 = 116-117; p. 148 = 120; p. 164 = 132; pp. 168-169 = 136 |
| Chapter 3. Disjunctive contingency | 185-318 | 2:149 to ~2:250 | pp. 185-186 = 149; p. 206 = 165; pp. 212-213 = 170; pp. 220-221 = 176; pp. 236-237 = 187-188; p. 263 = 208; p. 267 = 211; p. 274 = 216; pp. 289-290 = 227-228; pp. 297-298 = 234; pp. 317-318 = 249 |
| Conclusion | 319-331 | ~2:251 to 2:260 | p. 323 = 254; p. 324 = 254-255; p. 328 = 257; pp. 328-329 = 258; p. 330 = 259; p. 331 = 260 |

The chapter-boundary values marked ~ are interpolated; KSZ may begin chapters on a new page, which would shift a boundary by a page.

## Appendix D. Index of Persons and Works Cited

This index lists the persons and works that Kuki names in *The Problem of Contingency*, with page numbers of the 1948 printing (Iwanami Shoten, fourth printing, 20 April 1948). It covers Kuki's text only; the translator's notes are not indexed. Persons named inside Kuki's quotations are included when they are historical; fictional, legendary and mythological figures are included only where Kuki discusses them, and are marked as such. Editors and translators named in Kuki's references are included. Works are given in the form Kuki cites them, editions included, and have not been checked against the editions; where a reference looks wrong it is kept as printed and marked "[as printed]". Dates and identifications are given only where certain; Shōwa-era dates are followed by the Western year in square brackets.

## Glossary

| Japanese | Reading | English |
|---|---|---|
| 偶然性 | gūzensei | contingency |
| 偶然 | gūzen | the contingent; chance (where Kuki is discussing *hasard*, *Zufall*) |
| 必然性 / 必然 | hitsuzensei / hitsuzen | necessity / the necessary |
| 必ず然か有る | kanarazu shika aru | being necessarily so |
| 偶々然か有る | tamatama shika aru | happening to be so |
| 定言的 | teigenteki | categorical |
| 假說的 | kasetsuteki | hypothetical |
| 離接的 | risetsuteki | disjunctive |
| 樣態 | yōtai | mode |
| 樣相 / 樣相性 | yōsō / yōsōsei | modality / Modality |
| 存在 / 非存在 | sonzai / hisonzai | existence / non-existence (存在論 ontology) |
| 有 / 無 | u / mu | being / nothingness |
| 極限的存在 | kyokugenteki sonzai | limit-existence |
| 接觸面 | sesshokumen | surface of contact |
| 根據 | konkyo | Ground |
| 理由 / 歸結 | riyū / kiketsu | ground / consequent |
| 概念 / 徵表 | gainen / chōhyō | concept / mark (*Merkmal*) |
| 全體 / 部分 | zentai / bubun | whole / part |
| 離接肢 | risetsushi | disjunct |
| 全稱判斷 | zenshō handan | universal judgement (Lotze's *universale Urteil*) |
| 普遍的判斷 | fuhenteki handan | generic judgement (Lotze's *generelle Urteil*) |
| 特稱判斷 | tokushō handan | particular judgement |
| 綜合的 / 分析的判斷 | sōgōteki / bunsekiteki handan | synthetic / analytic judgement |
| 虛無性 / 實在性 | kyomusei / jitsuzaisei | nihility / reality |
| 認識理由 / 實在理由 | ninshiki riyū / jitsuzai riyū | ground of knowledge / ground of reality (*ratio cognoscendi* / *ratio essendi*) |
| 實體 / 屬性 | jittai / zokusei | substance / attribute (accident, in Hegel and Aristotle) |
| 孤立的事實 | koritsuteki jijitsu | isolated fact |
| 個物 / 個體 | kobutsu / kotai | individual thing / individual |
| 理由律 / 充足理由律 | riyūritsu / jūsoku riyūritsu | law of ground / law of sufficient reason |
| 理由的偶然 | riyūteki gūzen | contingency of ground |
| 消極的 / 積極的 | shōkyokuteki / sekkyokuteki | negative / positive |
| 因果的 / 目的的偶然 | ingateki / mokutekiteki gūzen | causal / teleological contingency |
| 因果性 / 目的性 | ingasei / mokutekisei | causality / purposiveness |
| 無目的 / 反目的 | mumokuteki / hanmokuteki | purposelessness / counter-purpose |
| 目的なき目的 | mokuteki naki mokuteki | purpose without purpose |
| 故意 / 意圖 | koi / ito | intention / design |
| 邂逅 | kaikō | Encounter (Japanese given where Kuki pairs it with 遭遇, and where it is a verb) |
| 獨立なる二元の邂逅 | dokuritsu naru nigen no kaikō | the Encounter of two independent elements |
| 系列 | keiretsu | series |
| 交叉點 | kōsaten | point of intersection |
| 複合的偶然 | fukugōteki gūzen | compound contingency |
| 絶對的 / 相對的偶然 | zettaiteki / sōtaiteki gūzen | absolute / relative contingency |
| 同時的 / 繼起的偶然 | dōjiteki / keikiteki gūzen | simultaneous / successive contingency |
| 原始偶然 | genshi gūzen | primal contingency (*Urzufall*) |
| 一回性 | ikkaisei | once-only character |
| 確證性 / 言明性 / 問題性 | kakushōsei / genmeisei / mondaisei | apodicticity / assertoricity / problematicity |
| 驚異 / 驚き | kyōi / odoroki | wonder |
| 周易 | Shūeki | the *Book of Changes* (*Zhouyi*) |
| 實存 / 實存性 | jitsuzon / jitsuzonsei | *Existenz* / existentiality (kept apart from 存在) |
| 現存在 | genzonzai | Dasein (Heidegger, Jaspers) |
| 矛盾 / 反對 / 小反對 / 大小對當 | mujun / hantai / shōhantai / daishō taitō | contradictory / contrary / subcontrary opposition / subalternation |
| 確率 / 蓋然性 | kakuritsu / gaizensei | probability (both; Japanese given where Kuki sets them side by side) |
| 遊戲 | yūgi | play |
| 感情當價 | kanjō tōka | affective equivalent |
| 情緒 / 感情 | jōcho / kanjō | emotion / feeling |
| 不思議 | fushigi | the marvellous |
| 運命 / 宿命 | unmei / shukumei | fate / destiny |
| 形而上的絕對者 | keijijōteki zettaisha | the metaphysical absolute |
| 限界狀況 | genkai jōkyō | limit situation (Jaspers) |
| 遇ふ | au | meet (kept apart from 邂逅, Encounter) |
| 內面化 | naimenka | internalisation |
| 遭遇 | sōgū | encounter |
| 目撃する | mokugeki suru | to witness |
| 闡明 | senmei | elucidation |
| 形而上學 | keijijōgaku | metaphysics |
| 勝義における哲學 | shōgi ni okeru tetsugaku | philosophy in the eminent sense |
| 優勢的命名法 | yūseiteki meimeihō | naming from the predominant feature (*denominatio a potiori*) |
